# Political Theory Flashcards

> One flashcard per examinable item in the repository Political Theory owners (basic + advanced), plus a concept card per owner file. IDs: PT-1001 onward by agent block.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia and other pages that loaded while building; confirmed items carry a ✅ source tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the cards. Time-sensitive facts carry as-of dates from the owner.

---

## POLITICAL THEORY — Part 1: Nature of Political Theory, Ideology, Liberalism, Marxism and Other Ideologies

Tags: (✅ owner NN) = grounded in the repository owner file NN (basic and advanced); (✅ <source>) = confirmed from a fetched page on 2026-11-01; ⚠️ = unverified, analytical synthesis or memory-only.

### GROUP — 01 Nature and Significance of Political Theory

### Flashcard PT-1001 — The political (what makes a matter "political")

**Front:** What does "political" mean in the opening definition of political theory, and what is excluded from it?

**Definition (learn):** The political is the public or common sphere of the whole community: authoritative rule-making, collective decision and regulation (✅ owner 01; Wolin, Gauba pp.15-16).

**Breakdown:**
- Core test: is the matter common to the whole community and does it need authoritative regulation?
- Not every social or cultural activity is political; it becomes political only when regulation for public order or safety becomes necessary (✅ owner 01).
- Sheldon Wolin is the named anchor for "what is common to the whole community" (✅ owner 01).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, Fifth Edition (2009), PDF pp.15-16 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1002 (political theory defined); PT-1009 (Wolin); PT-1026 (public vs private trap in the same owner).

**Trap:** Do not define "political" as "anything involving power in a family, firm or community". The owner's test is public and common regulation; informal power is a later critical-theory debate (owner 01 Unit 1 limitation).

**Mains hook:** Use the public/common test to justify why a dispute is political, then note the feminist and critical objection that informal private power also matters. Conclusion: the political is best defined by collective authority, while its boundary remains contested.

**Back:** *"POLITICAL = COMMON + AUTHORITATIVE: what binds the whole community, not what happens privately."*

### Flashcard PT-1002 — Political theory as systematic knowledge

**Front:** How does Gauba define political theory, and what does the definition commit it to?

**Definition (learn):** Political theory is systematic knowledge of political phenomena (✅ owner 01; Gauba PDF p.16).

**Breakdown:**
- "Systematic" means organised, reasoned and testable knowledge rather than opinion.
- It must include factual explanation and value-judgment; it cannot be confined to value-free science alone (✅ owner 01).
- It has two components: political science and political philosophy (see PT-1006 and PT-1007).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.16-17 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1001; PT-1003 to PT-1005 (three statement types); PT-1008 (integration thesis).

**Trap:** "Systematic" does not mean "scientific only". Positivists and behaviouralists restrict valid knowledge to empirical and logical statements; Gauba rejects that restriction (✅ owner 01).

**Mains hook:** Define theory, then state that a theory of politics that excludes evaluative statements cannot account for why policies are judged fair or unjust.

**Back:** *"THEORY = SYSTEMATIC, not merely SCIENTIFIC: facts plus values, organised by reason."*

### Flashcard PT-1003 — Empirical statement

**Front:** What is an empirical statement in Gauba's three-way classification?

**Definition (learn):** A statement based on observation through sense-experience alone (✅ owner 01; Gauba PDF p.16).

**Breakdown:**
- Verifiable through observation; the typical claim of political science.
- Example pattern: "riots occur where X" is an empirical causal claim (✅ owner 01 illustrative example).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.16 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1002; PT-1004 (logical); PT-1005 (evaluative); PT-1006 (political science).

**Trap:** Do not confuse "empirical" with "true". An empirical claim is checkable by observation, not automatically correct.

**Mains hook:** Use it to show which parts of a policy debate (e.g. output data) can be settled by evidence and which cannot.

**Back:** *"EMPIRICAL = EYES (sense-experience)."*

### Flashcard PT-1004 — Logical statement

**Front:** What is a logical statement, and how does it differ from an empirical one?

**Definition (learn):** A statement based on reasoning, such as formal or inferential relations (✅ owner 01; Gauba PDF p.16).

**Breakdown:**
- Its validity comes from internal consistency and inference, not from observation.
- It is one of the three statement types the owner lists alongside empirical and evaluative (✅ owner 01).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.16 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1003; PT-1005.

**Trap:** Logic can show an argument is consistent, but cannot by itself tell you which ends are good.

**Mains hook:** A chain of valid inference from a premise about ends still needs a value premise to reach a policy verdict.

**Back:** *"LOGICAL = REASON (inference and consistency)."*

### Flashcard PT-1005 — Evaluative statement

**Front:** What is an evaluative statement, and why does Gauba still treat it as rational?

**Definition (learn):** A statement based on value-judgment about what is right, good or desirable (✅ owner 01; Gauba PDF p.16).

**Breakdown:**
- Central to political philosophy and to any question of justice or legitimacy.
- Gauba treats evaluative claims as rationally discussable through dialogue and reasoned justification, not as mere emotion (✅ owner 01; PDF pp.16-17, 20).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.16-17, 20 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1002; PT-1007 (political philosophy); PT-1024 (trap: evaluative equals irrational).

**Trap:** Saying "values cannot be known, so they are not knowledge" is the positivist objection that Gauba answers by treating values as debatable through reasoning (✅ owner 01 Chain 1).

**Mains hook:** Public policy needs a standard for "fair" or "humane"; evaluative statements supply it, and their rational discussability is what allows democratic debate.

**Back:** *"EVALUATIVE = VALUE: what SHOULD be, argued by reason, not settled by emotion."*

### Flashcard PT-1006 — Political science (the factual component)

**Front:** What does political science contribute to political theory, and which methods does Gauba attribute to it?

**Definition (learn):** The component of theory concerned with facts, observation, explanation and generalised propositions about political behaviour (✅ owner 01; Gauba PDF pp.17-18).

**Breakdown:**
- Methods: observation, generalisation, explanation, prediction and prescription (✅ owner 01; PDF p.18).
- Behaviouralism focuses on actual behaviour, not merely formal institutions (✅ owner 01; PDF pp.18-19).
- George Catlin: science studies control and facts (PT-1010).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.17-19 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1007; PT-1021 (explanation vs justification); PT-1016 (Easton 1953); PT-1025 (behaviouralism trap).

**Trap:** Political science is not the whole of theory. It answers "what is and why", not "what ought to be".

**Mains hook:** A purely descriptive political science can measure participation but cannot judge whether that participation is meaningful freedom; that judgement needs philosophy.

**Back:** *"SCIENCE = WHAT IS, WHY, AND WHAT TENDS TO HAPPEN."*

### Flashcard PT-1007 — Political philosophy (the evaluative component)

**Front:** What does political philosophy add to political theory, and what is its characteristic function?

**Definition (learn):** The component of theory concerned with justification, criticism, concept-clarification and the ends of political life (✅ owner 01; Gauba PDF pp.17-20).

**Breakdown:**
- Functions named by D.D. Raphael: critical and justificatory function, plus concept-clarification (PT-1012).
- Not only ideal blueprints; it performs critical evaluation and seeks justification rather than explanation alone (✅ owner 01; PDF pp.19-20).
- Example: "what is a good society?" is a philosophical question (✅ owner 01).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.17-20 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1006; PT-1012 (Raphael); PT-1021; PT-1023 (trap: merely normative).

**Trap:** Do not reduce political philosophy to "designing the ideal state". Gauba's account is criticism and concept clarification.

**Mains hook:** Freedom can mean non-interference or self-development; clarifying which is philosophical work that prevents rhetorical misuse of the word.

**Back:** *"PHILOSOPHY = ENDS, JUSTIFICATION, CONCEPTS: why, not just how."*

### Flashcard PT-1008 — Political theory as an integrated enterprise (concept card)

**Front:** What is the integrated model of political theory, and what is the "one-line answer spine"?

**Definition (learn):** Political theory as a whole combines political science (facts, means) and political philosophy (values, ends), so that facts are interpreted through concepts and judged against defensible values (✅ owner 01 §8, §11).

**Breakdown:**
- Political science: what happens and why. Political philosophy: what ought to be and on what grounds. Theory as a whole: which means fit which ends.
- Gauba's closing formula: science strengthens means, philosophy judges ends (✅ owner 01 §12.4).
- One-line spine (⚠️ owner synthesis): political theory is the systematic study of political life that explains reality through science and judges goals through philosophy.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.16-18, 27 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1006; PT-1007; PT-1029 (qualified thesis); PT-1021.

**Trap:** The integration is a task, not a fixed balance; the weighting between science and philosophy has itself been contested (owner 01 §13).

**Mains hook:** Conclude that theory needs both components, while conceding that how much weight each deserves in a concrete policy dispute is a historical and practical question.

**Back:** *"SCIENCE SHOWS THE MEANS, PHILOSOPHY JUDGES THE ENDS."*

### Flashcard PT-1009 — Sheldon S. Wolin (the political as the common)

**Front:** What is Sheldon Wolin's contribution cited in the owner on the nature of the political?

**Definition (learn):** The political is uniquely concerned with what is common to the whole community (✅ owner 01 §6; Gauba PDF p.15).

**Breakdown:**
- Anchors the definition in PT-1001.
- The owner does not name a Wolin book or year; do not add one (owner 01 §19).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.15 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1001; PT-1026.

**Trap:** Wolin's "common" concern is not the same as a public-opinion survey; it concerns collective authority.

**Mains hook:** Wolin's test lets an answer explain why a neighbourhood quarrel is not political while a law on public order is.

**Back:** *"WOLIN: the political is the COMMON concern."*

### Flashcard PT-1010 — George Catlin (two-part division of theory)

**Front:** How does George Catlin divide political theory, and what does each part study?

**Definition (learn):** Catlin divides political theory into political science and political philosophy; science studies control and facts, philosophy studies ends and values (✅ owner 01 §6; Gauba PDF p.17).

**Breakdown:**
- Political science: control and facts.
- Political philosophy: ends and values.
- Owner gives no Catlin book title or year (⚠️ do not add one).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.17 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1006; PT-1007; PT-1008.

**Trap:** Do not present Catlin's division as the only possible classification; Gauba uses it as the founding frame, not as the end of debate.

**Mains hook:** The Catlin split is the cleanest starting frame for a "discuss" question on theory's components.

**Back:** *"CATLIN: CONTROL and FACTS (science) / ENDS and VALUES (philosophy)."*

### Flashcard PT-1011 — Andrew Hacker (part scientist, part philosopher)

**Front:** What does Andrew Hacker claim about the political scientist, and why is it useful?

**Definition (learn):** Every political scientist is part scientist and part philosopher; theory needs factual understanding and a view of political goals (✅ owner 01 §6; Gauba PDF pp.17-18).

**Breakdown:**
- Grounds the integration thesis of PT-1008.
- Used to reject one-sided answers (owner 01 Unit 2).
- Limitation: does not say how much weight science or philosophy should get in a concrete dispute (owner 01 Unit 2).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.17-18 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1008; PT-1029.

**Trap:** Hacker's claim is about the political scientist's dual role, not a licence for any single political view.

**Mains hook:** "Every political scientist is part philosopher" lets an answer show that even a data-driven study carries implicit value choices.

**Back:** *"HACKER: every political scientist is PART SCIENTIST, PART PHILOSOPHER."*

### Flashcard PT-1012 — D.D. Raphael (explanation vs justification)

**Front:** What distinction does D.D. Raphael draw, and what is the function of political philosophy in his account?

**Definition (learn):** Science seeks explanation; philosophy seeks justification. Political philosophy is recognised by its critical and justificatory function plus concept-clarification (✅ owner 01 §5-6; Gauba PDF pp.19-20).

**Breakdown:**
- Explanation: why something happens.
- Justification: why something is right or defensible.
- Concept-clarification is the third function (✅ owner 01).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.19-20 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1021 (the distinction card); PT-1007.

**Trap:** Many real questions (e.g. "does democracy increase legitimacy?") straddle both, so a clean split is not always possible (owner 01 Unit 3).

**Mains hook:** Use Raphael's criterion to classify a question first, then answer both parts if it straddles.

**Back:** *"RAPHAEL: SCIENCE EXPLAINS, PHILOSOPHY JUSTIFIES."*

### Flashcard PT-1013 — David Held (the cost of no systematic theory)

**Front:** What does David Held argue happens when political theory is not systematically pursued?

**Definition (learn):** Politics may be left to ignorance, self-interest or mere will to power (✅ owner 01 §6; Gauba PDF p.23).

**Breakdown:**
- A normative reason why theory matters.
- Usable as an opening or conclusion (owner 01 Unit 4).
- Limitation: the claim is itself a value-judgment, not neutral empirical proof (owner 01 Unit 4).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.23 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1028 (significance); PT-1008.

**Trap:** Do not present Held's claim as a neutral finding; it is a normative argument for theory.

**Mains hook:** Argue that theory matters because unexamined politics drifts toward self-interest or naked power; then note that this premise is itself evaluative.

**Back:** *"HELD: no theory, and politics goes to IGNORANCE, SELF-INTEREST or WILL TO POWER."*

### Flashcard PT-1014 — Alfred Cobban (decline with continuing need for judgment)

**Front:** What did Alfred Cobban claim about political theory in the mid-twentieth century?

**Definition (learn):** Cobban described a decline of political theory under both capitalist and communist systems, yet insisted politics needs criteria of judgment that narrow social-science methodology cannot supply (✅ owner 01 §12.1; Gauba PDF p.25).

**Breakdown:**
- Two systems, one diagnosis: decline under both.
- Still asserts the need for normative criteria.
- Owner gives no Cobban book title (do not add one).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.25 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1016 (Easton); PT-1015 (Lipset); PT-1022 (decline of a mode).

**Trap:** Cobban's decline claim is about a mode of theorising, not the end of political theory (PT-1022).

**Mains hook:** Cobban shows that the decline debate predates behaviouralism and that the need for judgment was argued from within the field.

**Back:** *"COBBAN: theory DECLINED under both systems, but JUDGMENT was still needed."*

### Flashcard PT-1015 — Seymour Martin Lipset (the good-society thesis, first version)

**Front:** What is Lipset's claim in the decline debate, and what is the formulation issue flagged by the owner?

**Definition (learn):** In mid-twentieth-century United States, the long search for the good society had substantially ended because liberal democracy approximated it in operation (✅ owner 01 §12.1; Gauba PDF p.25).

**Breakdown:**
- Ch.1 weaker wording: US democracy as "the closest approximation to the good society itself in operation" (✅ owner 02 §12; Gauba PDF pp.25, 37-38).
- Ch.2 stronger wording: democracy "is the good society itself in operation" (✅ owner 02 §13).
- Both are historically situated, not interchangeable quotations (✅ owner 02).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mid-twentieth-century US context (✅ owner 01, 02).

**Links:** PT-1016; PT-1050 (Lipset in end-of-ideology debate); PT-1022.

**Trap:** Do not treat Lipset as proving that politics has no further normative tasks; his thesis is contextual to Western democracy (✅ owner 01 §12, advanced §12).

**Mains hook:** Lipset's claim lets an answer test whether liberal democracy has settled ends, and argue that the answer depends on context.

**Back:** *"LIPSET 1: democracy = the GOOD SOCIETY in operation (a contextual, mid-century claim)."*

### Flashcard PT-1016 — David Easton (1953 behavioural critique)

**Front:** What were the four core complaints David Easton made against traditional political theory in 1953, and what was his remedy?

**Definition (learn):** Traditional theory was speculative, tied to classics and history of ideas, lacked causal research tools, and had no outstanding philosopher after Marx and Mill. The remedy was behavioural political science that suspends the quest for values to explain behaviour causally (✅ owner 01 §12.1; Gauba PDF p.24).

**Breakdown:**
- Complaints: speculation; no acute observation of reality; classics-bound; little contemporary relevance (✅ owner 01 advanced §3).
- Failure: no tools to explain fascism, communism or wartime policy (✅ owner 01 advanced §3).
- Remedy: causal theory, integration with other social sciences, suspension of values (✅ owner 01 advanced §3).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1953; Gauba PDF p.24 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1017 (Easton 1969); PT-1025; PT-1006.

**Trap:** The 1953 critique targets a mode of theory, not theory as such (PT-1022).

**Mains hook:** Easton's critique is historically real and partly correct; its remedy was incomplete, which his own 1969 turn shows.

**Back:** *"EASTON 1953: theory was SPECULATIVE, HISTORICAL and CAUSALLY WEAK, so study behaviour instead."*

### Flashcard PT-1017 — David Easton (1969 post-behavioural turn)

**Front:** What did Easton's 1969 post-behavioural turn change in his own 1953 position?

**Definition (learn):** Political science should become practically relevant to a crisis-ridden society and restore concern with values without discarding scientific method for facts (✅ owner 01 §12.2; Gauba PDF pp.24-25).

**Breakdown:**
- Relevance to crisis plus renewed value concern.
- Science retained for facts.
- Shows the behavioural remedy was incomplete (✅ owner 01 Unit 5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1969; Gauba PDF pp.24-25 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1016; PT-1019 (Germino); PT-1020 (Marcuse).

**Trap:** Do not merge 1953 and 1969 into one position; Gauba presents a distinct later correction (✅ owner 01 advanced §12).

**Mains hook:** A question asking "why does Easton's own turn complicate his attack" is answered by pointing to the 1969 correction.

**Back:** *"EASTON 1969: science for FACTS, values for ENDS, relevance for CRISIS."*

### Flashcard PT-1018 — Leo Strauss (positivism as symptom of decline)

**Front:** What does Leo Strauss argue against positivist political science?

**Definition (learn):** A positivist political science modelled on natural science ignores normative questions, treats all social values as equally important, and denies that some things are intrinsically high while others are low (✅ owner 01 §12.3; Gauba PDF pp.25-26).

**Breakdown:**
- Three charges: ignores norms; flattens values; denies intrinsic hierarchy.
- Positivist political science is itself a symptom of decline (✅ owner 01 advanced §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.25-26 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1016; PT-1019; PT-1020; PT-1026.

**Trap:** The strongest revival argument can be overextended to say empirical work is worthless; Gauba does not endorse that (owner 01 Unit 6 limitation).

**Mains hook:** Strauss's critique is a corrective to scientism, not a case against evidence.

**Back:** *"STRAUSS: positivism FLATTENS values and DENIES any higher and lower."*

### Flashcard PT-1019 — Dante Germino (revival as recovery of political philosophy)

**Front:** How does Dante Germino define the revival of political theory, and what distinction does he draw about detachment?

**Definition (learn):** Revival means recovery of political philosophy as critical inquiry into the right political order, distinct from reductionist behaviouralism and from ideology. Detachment is not the same as ethical neutrality (✅ owner 01 §12.3; Gauba PDF pp.26-27).

**Breakdown:**
- Critical inquiry into right order.
- Rejects reductionist behaviouralism and ideology alike.
- Detached yet engaged, not neutral (✅ owner 01 advanced §4).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.26-27 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1020; PT-1018; PT-1058 (ideology vs theory).

**Trap:** "Detached" can be misread as "neutral". Germino's point is that critical distance does not mean having no ethical position.

**Mains hook:** Use Germino to show that a theorist can be disciplined by evidence yet still judge ends.

**Back:** *"GERMINO: REVIVAL = critical PHILOSOPHY of right order; DETACHED is not NEUTRAL."*

### Flashcard PT-1020 — Herbert Marcuse (measurement can become control)

**Front:** What warning does Herbert Marcuse give about a natural-science style social science?

**Definition (learn):** Converting critique into measurable administrative categories can make social science an instrument of social control rather than emancipation (✅ owner 01 §12.3; Gauba PDF p.27).

**Breakdown:**
- The mechanism: critique turned into administrative metrics.
- Result: status quo normalised.
- Owner: "over-measured social science can normalize the status quo" (✅ owner 01 advanced §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.27 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1017; PT-1019; PT-1058.

**Trap:** Marcuse is a critic of a particular use of measurement, not of measurement itself; the advanced owner says he is a corrective to scientism (✅ owner 01 advanced §12).

**Mains hook:** A scientifically measured politics may still fail as a critical politics if it measures only what the existing order can count.

**Back:** *"MARCUSE: measurable CRITIQUE becomes administrative CONTROL."*

### Flashcard PT-1021 — Explanation vs justification (distinction card)

**Front:** What is the difference between explanation and justification, and which discipline pursues each?

**Definition (learn):** Explanation seeks causal regularities (science); justification seeks defensible grounds for what ought to be (philosophy) (✅ owner 01 §5; Raphael, Gauba PDF p.19).

**Breakdown:**
- Explanation: what causes what; generalisations and prediction.
- Justification: why an arrangement is right, fair or legitimate.
- Concept-clarification supports both.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Raphael, Gauba PDF p.19 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1012; PT-1006; PT-1007; PT-1029.

**Trap:** A question that explains why a policy is adopted is not a question about whether it is justified; answers that merge them lose marks on "discuss" and "evaluate" directives.

**Mains hook:** Separate the two questions explicitly in the body of an answer, then show where they meet.

**Back:** *"EXPLAIN = WHY IT HAPPENS; JUSTIFY = WHY IT SHOULD."*

### Flashcard PT-1022 — Decline of a mode, not disappearance of theory

**Front:** What is the single most important distinction in the decline/revival debate?

**Definition (learn):** Cobban, Lipset and Easton (1953) describe the decline of one mode of political theorising (classical, speculative, historicist, explicitly normative), not the disappearance of political theory as an enterprise (✅ owner 01 §12.2; ⚠️ owner synthesis).

**Breakdown:**
- Three separate claims: Cobban (mode declined under systems), Lipset (good society found), Easton 1953 (speculation lacks tools).
- The revival re-admits values without discarding evidence (✅ owner 01 §12.4).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.24-27 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1014; PT-1015; PT-1016; PT-1023.

**Trap:** Conflating "decline" with "disappearance" is the most common error on this topic (owner 01 §12.2).

**Mains hook:** Defensible verdict: political theory did not die; a narrow, ahistorical, facts-only variant lost credibility.

**Back:** *"DECLINE OF A MODE ≠ DEATH OF THEORY."*

### Flashcard PT-1023 — Trap: flat chronology of decline and revival

**Front:** Why is "theory ended in the 1950s and was revived in the 1970s" an examiner trap?

**Definition (learn):** It flattens three distinct claims into one timeline and ignores Easton's own 1969 correction, which began revival from inside behaviouralism (✅ owner 01 §12.4).

**Breakdown:**
- Cobban, Lipset and Easton (1953) differ in scope.
- Easton 1969 predates the 1970s consolidation.
- Gauba's closing statement records political science and philosophy testing each other since the 1970s (✅ owner 01 advanced §3).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Easton 1953 and 1969; Gauba PDF pp.24-27 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1022; PT-1017.

**Trap:** The trap itself is the flat chronology; the correct answer gives the three claims with their scope.

**Mains hook:** Structure a decline/revival answer around scope, not around decades.

**Back:** *"NOT A STORY OF DATES, BUT OF SCOPE: three claims, one debate."*

### Flashcard PT-1024 — Trap: political theory is merely normative

**Front:** Why is "political theory is merely normative" wrong?

**Definition (learn):** Gauba explicitly includes political science as a component of theory, so theory includes factual explanation as well as value-judgment (✅ owner 01 §9; Gauba PDF pp.17-18).

**Breakdown:**
- Correct: theory = science + philosophy.
- Wrong: theory = normative only.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.17-18 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1002; PT-1006; PT-1008.

**Trap:** This is a one-sided answer, which a "discuss" question rewards you for avoiding.

**Mains hook:** Open with the integration thesis to avoid the one-sided error.

**Back:** *"NOT JUST VALUES: theory also EXPLAINS."*

### Flashcard PT-1025 — Trap: behaviouralism made political theory unnecessary

**Front:** Why is "behaviouralism made political theory unnecessary" wrong?

**Definition (learn):** Post-behaviouralism restores values while keeping scientific method for facts; Gauba's summary treats the revival as compatible with science (✅ owner 01 §9, advanced §12).

**Breakdown:**
- Easton's own 1969 turn is the evidence.
- The behavioural challenge targets speculation and historicism, not all theory (✅ owner 01 advanced §2).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Easton 1969; Gauba PDF pp.18-19, 23 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1016; PT-1017; PT-1022.

**Trap:** Do not present behaviouralism and theory as opposites; the owner treats them as overlapping debates.

**Mains hook:** Behaviouralism exposed real weakness in traditional theory, and its own successor re-admitted values.

**Back:** *"BEHAVIOURALISM CRITIQUED THEORY; IT DID NOT ABOLISH IT."*

### Flashcard PT-1026 — Public vs private (what counts as political)

**Front:** When does a social or cultural activity become "political" in Gauba's account?

**Definition (learn):** Politics concerns the public sphere; an activity becomes political when regulation for public order or safety becomes necessary (✅ owner 01 §5; Gauba PDF p.16).

**Breakdown:**
- Public and common: political.
- Private: not political unless regulation becomes necessary.
- Owner flags later feminist critique that informal power matters (owner 01 Unit 1).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.16 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1001; PT-1009; cross-owner: see Feminism file (owner 06, not in this fragment).

**Trap:** Do not claim "everything is political" nor "nothing private is political". Both are one-sided.

**Mains hook:** A boundary test (is public order at stake?) gives a clear criterion for classifying a debate.

**Back:** *"PUBLIC NEEDS REGULATION; PRIVATE DOES NOT, UNLESS ORDER IS AT STAKE."*

### Flashcard PT-1027 — Vague political language as a tool of manipulation

**Front:** What warning does Gauba give about words like liberty, equality and democracy?

**Definition (learn):** Vague political language allows demagogues and autocrats to exploit these words for emotional manipulation or legitimation (✅ owner 01 §7; Gauba PDF p.22).

**Breakdown:**
- Political philosophy's concept-clarifying function answers this.
- Example: whether "freedom" means non-interference or self-development (✅ owner 01 §7).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.22 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1007; PT-1028.

**Trap:** Concept-clarification is not pedantry; it is a defence against manipulation.

**Mains hook:** Use the example of a regime calling itself "democratic" to show why clarifying the concept is a political act.

**Back:** *"CLEAR WORDS DEFEND AGAINST DEMAGOGUES."*

### Flashcard PT-1028 — Significance of political theory (Gauba's four functions)

**Front:** What does Gauba list as the significance of political theory?

**Definition (learn):** Control of social life; social criticism and reconstruction; clarification of concepts; and encouragement of mutual respect and toleration (✅ owner 01 §4 item 7; Gauba PDF pp.21-23).

**Breakdown:**
- Control: theory guides collective ends.
- Criticism and reconstruction: judges the status quo.
- Clarification: defeats rhetoric.
- Mutual respect and toleration: an ethical function.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.21-23 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1013 (Held); PT-1027; PT-1030 (spine).

**Trap:** Do not add "constitutional drafting" as a theory function; the owner says the constitutional detail belongs to Polity files.

**Mains hook:** Four functions, one question: does theory help people live together justly?

**Back:** *"CONTROL, CRITICISE, CLARIFY, TOLERATE."*

### Flashcard PT-1029 — Qualified thesis and directive decoding (Nature of Political Theory)

**Front:** What is the qualified thesis and directive decoding for an unfamiliar question on the nature of political theory?

**Definition (learn):** Political theory is neither pure science nor pure philosophy; it uses science to explain political reality and philosophy to judge its ends, and the weighting has itself been historically contested (✅ owner 01 §13; ⚠️ owner synthesis).

**Breakdown:**
- "Discuss": both components and their relation, plus a verdict on integration.
- "Examine": test the claim against Easton 1953 and Lipset.
- "Critically analyse": weigh behavioural critique against revival, then conclude.
- "Elucidate": a paired illustration (riot causation versus the good-society question).
- "Comment": judgment-bearing paragraph, not neutral summary.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner directive table (✅ owner 01 §13).

**Links:** PT-1008; PT-1021; PT-1022; PT-1030.

**Trap:** Describing only one component, or narrating the definition without testing it.

**Mains hook:** Match depth to marks: 10 marks for one thesis, one example and a verdict; 20 marks for thinkers on both sides and an objection-reply chain.

**Back:** *"THESIS: SCIENCE EXPLAINS, PHILOSOPHY JUDGES, THE BALANCE IS CONTESTED."*

### Flashcard PT-1030 — Premises, mechanism and conclusion (Nature of Political Theory)

**Front:** What is the premises-mechanism-conclusion spine for "Can political theory be value-free? Discuss"?

**Definition (learn):** Premises: the political is public and authoritative; knowledge has three statement types; positivists restrict valid knowledge to empirical and logical. Mechanism: political life generates both factual and value questions, so science explains and philosophy justifies. Conclusion: theory must integrate both (✅ owner 01 §14).

**Breakdown:**
- Premises (a), (b), (c) as in the owner.
- Mechanism: two angles on the same subject-matter.
- Conclusion: significance of control, criticism, clarification and toleration.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.15-23 (✅ owner 01).

**Links:** PT-1001 to PT-1005; PT-1008; PT-1028; PT-1029.

**Trap:** Treating the spine as a verbatim summary of Gauba; it is an answer structure built from his argument (owner 01 §19).

**Mains hook:** Use the spine to answer any unfamiliar question in the owner's topic without memorising the chapter.

**Back:** *"POSITIVISTS CUT, FACTS AND VALUES BOTH ASKED, THEORY MUST INTEGRATE."*

### GROUP — 02 Ideology and End of Ideology

### Flashcard PT-1031 — Ideology: its double meaning

**Front:** What are the two meanings of "ideology" that Gauba distinguishes at the start of the chapter?

**Definition (learn):** Ideology is (a) a set of ideas accepted by a group, party or nation as true without further testing, used to justify or denounce an arrangement; and (b) a science of ideas that studies how ideas are formed, distorted and purified (✅ owner 02 §1, §3; Gauba PDF pp.28-31).

**Breakdown:**
- Sense (a): action-oriented, mobilising, justificatory.
- Sense (b): reflective, the study of formation and distortion (Destutt de Tracy, PT-1033).
- Theory is the critical evaluator; ideology is the committed claim (PT-1058).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.28-31 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1032; PT-1033; PT-1058.

**Trap:** Using only sense (a) when a question asks for the "science of ideas" sense loses a mark; using only (b) misses the mobilising role.

**Mains hook:** Ideology both motivates action and invites critical testing; the two senses explain why it cannot be dismissed or celebrated.

**Back:** *"IDEOLOGY = IDEAS THAT MOBILISE, and the SCIENCE that tests how such ideas form."*

### Flashcard PT-1032 — Ideology as a set of accepted ideas

**Front:** Define ideology in its first sense and name its three main functions.

**Definition (learn):** A set of ideas accepted as true by a group, party or nation without further examination, often used to justify or denounce a social or political arrangement (✅ owner 02 §3; Gauba PDF pp.28-29).

**Breakdown:**
- Functions: justify rule, attack opponents, motivate sacrifice or transformation (✅ owner 02 §4).
- Action-orientation is the key feature.
- Examples: nationalism can mobilise sacrifice for freedom; communalism can mobilise hatred (✅ owner 02 §7).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.28-29 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1031; PT-1058; PT-1059 (trap: ideology is falsehood).

**Trap:** Ideology is not automatically false. Owner 02 says oppressed groups' ideologies may express legitimate claims (PT-1059).

**Mains hook:** Nationalism versus communalism shows that the same mobilising form can serve liberation or hatred; the test is the content and its scrutiny.

**Back:** *"IDEOLOGY = ACCEPTED IDEAS THAT JUSTIFY, ATTACK, MOBILISE."*

### Flashcard PT-1033 — Destutt de Tracy and the science of ideas

**Front:** Who first used "ideology" as the science of ideas, and what did he tie valid knowledge to?

**Definition (learn):** Destutt de Tracy, who first used ideology as the science of ideas, tied valid knowledge to empirical learning (✅ owner 02 §6; Gauba PDF pp.30-31).

**Breakdown:**
- The older, reflective meaning of "ideology".
- Valid knowledge is empirically grounded.
- Owner gives no work title or year for Tracy (⚠️ do not add one).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.30-31 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1031; PT-1034 (Bacon).

**Trap:** Do not use Tracy's sense to mean the modern "partisan worldview"; that is sense (a) in PT-1032.

**Mains hook:** Starting with Tracy shows that the word began as a neutral science and acquired its partisan sense later.

**Back:** *"TRACY: IDEOLOGY = the SCIENCE OF IDEAS, grounded in experience."*

### Flashcard PT-1034 — Francis Bacon and the idols of distorted knowledge

**Front:** What did Francis Bacon warn about knowledge, and why does Gauba link him to later ideology critique?

**Definition (learn):** Knowledge can be distorted by false impressions or "idols", anticipating later critique of distorted ideas (✅ owner 02 §6; Gauba PDF p.31).

**Breakdown:**
- "Idols" are false impressions that mislead the mind.
- Anticipates Marx and Mannheim (PT-1039, PT-1043).
- Owner gives no Novum Organum title or date (⚠️ do not add).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.31 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1033; PT-1039; PT-1036.

**Trap:** Do not present "idols" as a modern Marxist term.

**Mains hook:** Bacon shows that a critique of distorted thought is older than the sociology of knowledge.

**Back:** *"BACON: IDOLS distort the mind before any ideology does."*

### Flashcard PT-1035 — Obscurantism

**Front:** What is obscurantism in Gauba's account?

**Definition (learn):** A deliberate effort to keep things obscure so that people do not know the truth (✅ owner 02 §3; Gauba PDF p.29).

**Breakdown:**
- Deliberate, not accidental.
- Contrasts with open inquiry (PT-1047 open society).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.29 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1032; PT-1047; PT-1046.

**Trap:** Obscurantism is intentional concealment; ideology can be sincere and still distorted.

**Mains hook:** Use obscurantism to separate deliberate deception from sincere but distorted belief.

**Back:** *"OBSCURANTISM = keeping people in the DARK on purpose."*

### Flashcard PT-1036 — Sociology of knowledge (Mannheim's inquiry)

**Front:** What is the sociology of knowledge, and with which thinker does Gauba link it?

**Definition (learn):** Inquiry into how knowledge is socially determined, conditioned or distorted; Gauba links the term especially with Karl Mannheim (✅ owner 02 §3; Gauba PDF p.31).

**Breakdown:**
- Studies social conditioning of ideas as such (✅ owner 02 §12).
- Mannheim's aim is a more objective synthesis, not relativism (PT-1043).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.31, 33-34 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1043; PT-1038; PT-1039.

**Trap:** Do not treat sociology of knowledge as proof that objective truth is impossible (owner 02 §12 and §17 Chain 3).

**Mains hook:** Mannheim shows both the power of situated-knowledge critique and its risk of confusing origin with validity.

**Back:** *"SOCIOLOGY OF KNOWLEDGE = ideas are SOCIALLY SITUATED; the task is SYNTHESIS, not surrender."*

### Flashcard PT-1037 — Critical theory

**Front:** How does Gauba define critical theory?

**Definition (learn):** A stream of thought that studies society from the angle of deviation from rational human emancipation, rather than copying natural-science paradigms (✅ owner 02 §3; Gauba PDF p.31).

**Breakdown:**
- Emancipatory orientation.
- Method: critique of deviation from rational emancipation.
- Links to Frankfurt School (PT-1107 in the Marxism group).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.31 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1036; PT-1107; PT-1020 (Marcuse).

**Trap:** Critical theory is not simply a method of measurement; the owner contrasts it with the natural-science copy.

**Mains hook:** Critical theory is the form of social inquiry that asks what emancipation requires, not only what is.

**Back:** *"CRITICAL THEORY = society judged against RATIONAL EMANCIPATION, not copied from physics."*

### Flashcard PT-1038 — Mannheim: ideology versus utopia

**Front:** How does Mannheim contrast ideology with utopia, and what is the effect of each?

**Definition (learn):** Ideology conserves the status quo; utopia energises change through an unrealisable projection; both are forms of false consciousness in Mannheim's treatment (✅ owner 02 §5, §6; Gauba PDF p.34).

**Breakdown:**
- Ideology: conserving.
- Utopia: an unrealisable but fascinating vision that agitates for change.
- Owner describes both as false consciousness (✅ owner 02 advanced §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.34 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1036; PT-1043; PT-1058.

**Trap:** Do not map utopia onto "good" and ideology onto "bad"; Mannheim treats both as partial.

**Mains hook:** A status-quo ideology and a change-seeking utopia can both distort, so theory must test each.

**Back:** *"MANNHEIM: IDEOLOGY CONSERVES, UTOPIA AGITATES, BOTH DISTORT."*

### Flashcard PT-1039 — Karl Marx: ideology as distorted consciousness

**Front:** How does Gauba reconstruct Marx's account of ideology?

**Definition (learn):** Ideology arises from material conditions, is distorted consciousness, and serves dominant-class interest by making a sectional arrangement appear natural or universal (✅ owner 02 §6, §12; Gauba PDF pp.32-33).

**Breakdown:**
- Consciousness lags behind material development (✅ owner 02 §12).
- The superstructure reflects the economic structure (✅ owner 02 advanced §5).
- Gauba's account is a reconstruction, not a verbatim Marx quotation (owner 02 §20).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.32-33 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1040 (false consciousness provenance); PT-1041 (Lenin); PT-1042 (Lukács); PT-1104 (Marx and Engels in the Marxism group).

**Trap:** Ideology as false consciousness is a Marxist claim about class domination, not a claim that every person lies.

**Mains hook:** Distorted consciousness explains how exploitation can look natural without anyone deceiving deliberately.

**Back:** *"MARX: IDEOLOGY = consciousness that makes CLASS RULE look NATURAL."*

### Flashcard PT-1040 — "False consciousness": phrase provenance

**Front:** Who wrote the phrase "false consciousness", and what is the verified source?

**Definition (learn):** The explicit phrase is Friedrich Engels's, not Marx's; it comes from Engels's letter to Franz Mehring of 14 July 1893 (✅ owner 02 §5, §13; ✅ marxists.org Engels to Mehring, 14 Jul 1893, fetched 2026-11-01).

**Breakdown:**
- The fetched letter reads that ideology is a process accomplished by the thinker consciously but with a false consciousness.
- Gauba's Marx reconstruction is retained as a source account only (owner 02 §21).
- Do not present the phrase as a verified Marx quotation.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Engels to Mehring, 14 July 1893, London (✅ marxists.org, fetched).

**Links:** PT-1039; PT-1041; PT-1062 (trap: Marx and Lenin same sense).

**Trap:** Attributing "false consciousness" to Marx. Answer by naming Engels and the 1893 letter.

**Mains hook:** Precision on provenance earns credit; an answer can use the concept and still name its author correctly.

**Back:** *"FALSE CONSCIOUSNESS = ENGELS, to MEHRING, 14 JULY 1893, not MARX's own phrase."*

### Flashcard PT-1041 — Lenin: proletarian ideology

**Front:** Why does Lenin, in Gauba's account, insist that the proletariat also needs ideology?

**Definition (learn):** The working class needs scientific socialism because class struggle persists in the socialist phase (✅ owner 02 §6; Gauba PDF p.33).

**Breakdown:**
- Ideology is not only bourgeois distortion; it can organise emancipatory consciousness (✅ owner 02 §16 Unit 2).
- Limitation: can blur the line between critiquing and using ideology (owner 02 Unit 2).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.33 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1039; PT-1042; PT-1059.

**Trap:** Treating Lenin as identical to Marx on ideology is the error in PT-1062.

**Mains hook:** Lenin shows ideology can be necessary for struggle, which complicates the claim that all ideology is simple falsehood.

**Back:** *"LENIN: the PROLETARIAT needs IDEOLOGY too: scientific socialism."*

### Flashcard PT-1042 — Georg Lukács: class consciousness

**Front:** What does Lukács add to the Marxian account of consciousness?

**Definition (learn):** Consciousness is always class-situated, and the proletariat's structural position uniquely opens the route to a more universal consciousness (✅ owner 02 §12; Gauba PDF p.33).

**Breakdown:**
- Bourgeois ideology is false because bourgeois class-situation is structurally limited (✅ owner 02 advanced §5).
- Marxism is the proletarian expression, but ideology must not replace class struggle (✅ owner 02 advanced §5).
- Reification (⚠️ supplementary vocabulary, not page-anchored in Gauba): social relations appear as fixed natural relations between things.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.33 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1039; PT-1041; PT-1043.

**Trap:** Reification is an analytical gloss in this owner, not a verified Gauba claim; do not attach a book title or year to it (owner 02 §20).

**Mains hook:** Lukács relocates falsity from ideology as a genre to a standpoint's structural limits.

**Back:** *"LUKÁCS: CONSCIOUSNESS is CLASS-SITUATED; the PROLETARIAT can see further."*

### Flashcard PT-1043 — Karl Mannheim: situated thought and the intelligentsia

**Front:** What does Mannheim claim about all thought, and what caution does the owner attach to his synthesis?

**Definition (learn):** All thought is socially situated; a free-floating stratum of intellectuals might move between perspectives toward more objective synthetic knowledge (✅ owner 02 §5, §12; Gauba PDF pp.33-34).

**Breakdown:**
- Generalises ideology critique beyond accusing an opponent (✅ owner 02 §16 Unit 4).
- ⚠️ Total versus particular conception of ideology, and relational objectivity, are supplementary vocabulary not page-anchored to Gauba (owner 02 §12).
- Caution: this can confuse the social origin of knowledge with its validity and slide into expert authority (owner 02 §12, Gauba PDF p.34).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.33-34 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1036; PT-1038; PT-1042.

**Trap:** Do not present Mannheim's intelligentsia as an unproblematic solution; end any Mannheim answer with the caution.

**Mains hook:** Is Mannheim a path to objectivity or a doorway to relativism? Answer: a path if the synthesis stays accountable, a doorway if it becomes unchecked expertise.

**Back:** *"MANNHEIM: ALL THOUGHT IS SITUATED; SYNTHESIS needs a CAUTIOUS INTELLIGENTSIA."*

### Flashcard PT-1044 — Karl Popper: ideology and the open society

**Front:** What is Popper's contrast between ideology and the open society?

**Definition (learn):** Ideology belongs to totalitarian societies that claim final truth; open societies permit criticism and revision and reject a monopoly on truth (✅ owner 02 §6; Gauba PDF p.35).

**Breakdown:**
- Totalitarian: claims final truth, mobilises through ideology.
- Open: free flow of information, criticism, policy revision (PT-1047).
- Open societies have strong values of openness and criticism (✅ owner 02 advanced §12).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.35 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1045 (Arendt); PT-1046; PT-1047; PT-1061.

**Trap:** Popper does not say politics must be ideology-free; his target is a closed claim to truth.

**Mains hook:** A threshold test: is criticism allowed and can policy be revised?

**Back:** *"POPPER: IDEOLOGY + FINAL TRUTH = closed society; OPEN = criticisable."*

### Flashcard PT-1045 — Hannah Arendt: totalitarianism as ideology plus terror

**Front:** How does Arendt connect ideology, terror and mass uprootedness?

**Definition (learn):** Totalitarianism is marked by ideology plus terror and depends on social conditions of mass uprootedness (✅ owner 02 §6; Gauba PDF p.35).

**Breakdown:**
- Conditions named in the advanced owner: anti-Semitism, imperialism and uprooted masses in modern Europe (✅ owner 02 advanced §5).
- Total domination built through ideology and terror.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.35 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1044; PT-1046; PT-1061.

**Trap:** Do not generalise the Arendtian model to every ideological politics (owner 02 advanced §8).

**Mains hook:** Arendt explains why totalitarianism needs both an ideology and a terror apparatus, and why uprootedness makes it possible.

**Back:** *"ARENDT: IDEOLOGY + TERROR + UPROOTED MASSES = TOTAL DOMINATION."*

### Flashcard PT-1046 — Totalitarianism (definition)

**Front:** Define totalitarianism as Gauba uses the term.

**Definition (learn):** A regime-form seeking to direct all aspects of life toward state-defined goals and suppressing criticism or alternative aims (✅ owner 02 §3; Gauba PDF pp.35-36).

**Breakdown:**
- Scope: all of life, not only politics.
- Suppression of criticism and rival aims.
- Used in the fascist variant in PT-1144.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.35-36 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1044; PT-1045; PT-1047; PT-1144.

**Trap:** Totalitarianism is not the same as any authoritarian rule; the owner stresses total direction of life.

**Mains hook:** Use the total-direction test to distinguish totalitarian from merely authoritarian regimes.

**Back:** *"TOTALITARIAN = ALL LIFE directed to STATE GOALS, CRITICISM silenced."*

### Flashcard PT-1047 — Open society (definition)

**Front:** What defines an open society?

**Definition (learn):** A society with free flow of information, criticism, policy revision and no monopoly claim to truth (✅ owner 02 §3; Gauba PDF p.35).

**Breakdown:**
- Three features: information, criticism, revisability.
- Absence of monopoly on truth.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.35 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1044; PT-1046; PT-1035.

**Trap:** An open society is not value-free; it has strong values of openness (owner 02 advanced §12).

**Mains hook:** Openness is the institutional guard against ideology becoming totalitarian.

**Back:** *"OPEN SOCIETY = INFORMATION FLOWS, CRITICISM ALLOWED, POLICY REVISABLE, NO MONOPOLY ON TRUTH."*

### Flashcard PT-1048 — Daniel Bell and the end-of-ideology thesis

**Front:** What did Daniel Bell argue, and in which book did he publish the thesis?

**Definition (learn):** Mid-century Western convergence around the welfare state, decentralised power, mixed economy and pluralism was read as the end of sharp Left-Right ideological conflict (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF pp.37-38).

**Breakdown:**
- Bell's book: *The End of Ideology: On the Exhaustion of Political Ideas in the Fifties*, 1960; second edition 1962 (✅ Wikipedia "End of ideology", fetched 2026-11-01).
- Thesis is historically situated, not a permanent law (owner 02 §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1960 (first edition); 1962 second edition (✅ Wikipedia).

**Links:** PT-1049; PT-1050; PT-1057; PT-1060.

**Trap:** Do not present Bell's thesis as established contemporary fact; it is a mid-twentieth-century claim (owner 02 §12 factual-risk).

**Mains hook:** Bell is the anchor for the "end of ideology" side; the critics in PT-1053 to PT-1056 are the other side.

**Back:** *"BELL 1960: the IDEOLOGICAL AGE was declared EXHAUSTED."*

### Flashcard PT-1049 — Ralph Dahrendorf and isolated industrial conflict

**Front:** What does Dahrendorf argue about industrial conflict in post-capitalist society?

**Definition (learn):** Post-capitalist society institutionally isolated industrial (workplace) conflict from wider political conflict, weakening the applicability of Marx's total-conflict framework to Western societies (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF p.37).

**Breakdown:**
- Workplace conflict contained by institutions.
- Consequence: Marx's total-conflict frame applies less well.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.37 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1048; PT-1039.

**Trap:** Dahrendorf does not claim conflict has vanished; he claims it is institutionally contained.

**Mains hook:** Isolation of conflict is an institutional argument, testable against the strength of unions and courts.

**Back:** *"DAHRENDORF: industrial CONFLICT was CONTAINED, not ended."*

### Flashcard PT-1050 — Lipset in the end-of-ideology debate (stronger formulation)

**Front:** How does Lipset's end-of-ideology argument differ from his earlier formulation?

**Definition (learn):** Lipset's Chapter 2 stronger claim: democracy "is the good society itself in operation"; Chapter 1 weaker claim: US democracy was "the closest approximation". Both are historically situated (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF pp.25, 37-38).

**Breakdown:**
- Stronger form implies shallow Left-Right differences in affluent democracies (✅ owner 02 advanced §5).
- Weaker form is a contextual approximation (PT-1015).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.25, 37-38 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1015; PT-1048; PT-1057.

**Trap:** Treating the two Lipset formulations as one quotation. The owner warns they are not interchangeable.

**Mains hook:** The weaker wording is more defensible; the stronger one needs the critics' reply.

**Back:** *"LIPSET: 'IS the good society' (strong) vs 'CLOSEST APPROXIMATION' (weak)."*

### Flashcard PT-1051 — W.W. Rostow and stage-based development

**Front:** How did W.W. Rostow relate development to ideology?

**Definition (learn):** Economic development is a universal, stage-based process not determined by which political ideology a society professes (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF p.38).

**Breakdown:**
- Development stages apply across ideologies.
- Advanced owner says Gauba presents a five-stage process (✅ owner 02 advanced §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.38 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1052; PT-1048.

**Trap:** Rostow's stages are a convergence claim, not a verdict that ideology has ended in every country.

**Mains hook:** Rostow's argument is that growth, not doctrine, shapes institutions; critics ask whether growth is ideologically neutral.

**Back:** *"ROSTOW: DEVELOPMENT stages, not DOCTRINE, shape societies."*

### Flashcard PT-1052 — J.K. Galbraith and technocratic managerialism

**Front:** What is technocratic managerialism, and which thinker is its clearest exponent in the owner?

**Definition (learn):** Industrial societies converge through centralisation, bureaucratisation and technocratisation; power shifts from ideological leadership to a bureaucratic-technocratic managerial elite (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF p.38).

**Breakdown:**
- Policy justified through technical expertise and efficiency, not open ideological argument.
- Debate appears to move "beyond ideology" while a managerial distribution of power is naturalised (✅ owner 02 §13).
- Owner names J.K. Galbraith as the clearest statement.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.38 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1048; PT-1057; PT-1058.

**Trap:** Technocracy does not remove ideology; it relocates it into administrative language (owner 02 §13).

**Mains hook:** Ask which interests become harder to contest once a decision is framed as purely technical.

**Back:** *"GALBRAITH: IDEOLOGY hides in EXPERTISE, the managerial elite rules."*

### Flashcard PT-1053 — Richard Titmuss (objection to end-of-ideology)

**Front:** What objection does Titmuss raise against the end-of-ideology thesis?

**Definition (learn):** Titmuss points to monopolistic concentration of economic power, social disorganisation and cultural deprivation (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF p.39).

**Breakdown:**
- Supplies evidence omitted by the proponents.
- Used in the reply chain of PT-1057.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.39 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1048; PT-1054; PT-1057.

**Trap:** Owner names him as an objector, not as an end-of-ideology proponent.

**Mains hook:** Concentrated economic power and social disorganisation show that conflict has not disappeared.

**Back:** *"TITMUSS: MONOPOLY, DISORGANISATION, DEPRIVATION: conflict still there."*

### Flashcard PT-1054 — C. Wright Mills (ideology of complacency)

**Front:** How does C. Wright Mills characterise the end-of-ideology thesis?

**Definition (learn):** An ideology of political complacency that acquiesces in or justifies the status quo (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF p.39).

**Breakdown:**
- The thesis is itself an ideological position.
- Links to the owner's self-refuting argument (PT-1057).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.39 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1053; PT-1055; PT-1056; PT-1057.

**Trap:** Mills's charge concerns function (complacency), not that the thesis is factually false.

**Mains hook:** A claim of neutrality can defend the status quo.

**Back:** *"MILLS: 'END OF IDEOLOGY' = COMPLACENCY dressed as neutrality."*

### Flashcard PT-1055 — C.B. Macpherson (market society and equitable distribution)

**Front:** What does C.B. Macpherson attack in the end-of-ideology position?

**Definition (learn):** He attacks the attempted solution of equitable distribution within market society (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF p.39).

**Breakdown:**
- The problem is structural: distribution within a market order.
- Owner gives no Macpherson book title (⚠️ do not add one).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.39 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1053; PT-1054; PT-1057.

**Trap:** Do not confuse Macpherson's market critique with Hayek's market defence (see PT-1077).

**Mains hook:** Equitable distribution inside a market order may be impossible without changing the order itself.

**Back:** *"MACPHERSON: equity INSIDE a MARKET society is the wrong fix."*

### Flashcard PT-1056 — Alasdair MacIntyre (the thesis as ideology of its time)

**Front:** What does MacIntyre argue about the end-of-ideology thesis?

**Definition (learn):** The end-of-ideology thesis may itself express the ideology of its own time and place (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF p.39).

**Breakdown:**
- Self-referential critique: the thesis is historically situated.
- Strongest form of the critique of the thesis.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.39 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1054; PT-1057; PT-1043 (Mannheim situated thought).

**Trap:** MacIntyre does not claim ideology is still dominant in the same form; he claims the thesis is itself situated.

**Mains hook:** The end-of-ideology thesis is one more situated claim, to be tested like any other.

**Back:** *"MACINTYRE: the END OF IDEOLOGY is an IDEOLOGY of its TIME."*

### Flashcard PT-1057 — The end-of-ideology thesis: proponents, critics and the self-refutation point

**Front:** Who are the proponents and critics of the end-of-ideology thesis in the owner, and what is the self-refutation argument?

**Definition (learn):** Proponents: Bell, Dahrendorf, Lipset, Rostow, Galbraith (mid-century historical claims about affluent industrial societies). Critics: Titmuss, Mills, Macpherson, MacIntyre (✅ owner 02 §13; Gauba PDF pp.37-39).

**Breakdown:**
- Self-refutation: a claim of neutrality can defend a particular distribution of power (⚠️ owner 02 §13 synthesis).
- Technocratic managerialism relocates ideology rather than removing it (PT-1052).
- Thesis is a historical claim, not a settled fact (owner 02 §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.37-39 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1048 to PT-1056; PT-1060.

**Trap:** Presenting the thesis without its critics loses the "critically examine" marks (owner 02 §14).

**Mains hook:** Test the thesis against its critics, then give a verdict on whether ideology declined or changed form.

**Back:** *"BELL, DAHRENDORF, LIPSET, ROSTOW, GALBRAITH say it ENDED; TITMUSS, MILLS, MACPHERSON, MACINTYRE say it RELOCATED."*

### Flashcard PT-1058 — Ideology versus political theory

**Front:** What is the difference between ideology and political theory?

**Definition (learn):** Ideology is interested, action-oriented and justificatory; theory is disinterested, critical and validity-testing (✅ owner 02 §5; Gauba PDF pp.29-30).

**Breakdown:**
- Ideology asserts from commitment; theory examines origin, strength, weakness and validity.
- Owner: the better distinction is critical testing versus interested justification, not originless versus situated (owner 02 advanced §12).
- Theory is not socially originless.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.29-30 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1031; PT-1059; PT-1061.

**Trap:** Do not describe theory as socially originless. Both can be socially situated.

**Mains hook:** Distinguish by function (testing versus justifying), not by origin.

**Back:** *"IDEOLOGY JUSTIFIES FROM COMMITMENT; THEORY TESTS CLAIMS FOR VALIDITY."*

### Flashcard PT-1059 — Trap: ideology always means falsehood

**Front:** Why is "ideology always means falsehood" wrong?

**Definition (learn):** Gauba criticises the blanket view and says oppressed groups also have ideologies that may express legitimate claims and emancipatory movements (✅ owner 02 §9; Gauba PDF p.36).

**Breakdown:**
- Lenin and Lukács show ideology can organise emancipatory politics (PT-1041, PT-1042).
- Accurate generalisation: ideology is action-oriented belief, not automatically falsehood (owner 02 §17).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.36 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1032; PT-1041; PT-1039.

**Trap:** The falsehood reading is a one-sided reading of Marx.

**Mains hook:** Ideology can be both mobilising and distorting; the task is to tell which in each case.

**Back:** *"NOT ALL IDEOLOGY IS LIES: some ideas MOBILISE JUSTLY."*

### Flashcard PT-1060 — Trap: end of ideology is an established fact

**Front:** Why is "the end of ideology is an established contemporary fact" wrong?

**Definition (learn):** In Gauba it is a historically situated thesis raised in the 1950s and 1960s about industrial Western societies, and critics reject it (✅ owner 02 §9; Gauba PDF pp.36-39).

**Breakdown:**
- Time-bound: mid-twentieth-century.
- Contested by critics (PT-1057).
- Ideologies continue as value-systems and movements (✅ owner 02 advanced §12).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.36-39 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1048; PT-1057.

**Trap:** Treating the thesis as the verdict of political science.

**Mains hook:** Call it a thesis and test it.

**Back:** *"END OF IDEOLOGY = a THESIS, not a FACT."*

### Flashcard PT-1061 — Trap: Popper and Arendt want ideology-free politics

**Front:** Why is "Popper and Arendt say all politics must be ideology-free" wrong?

**Definition (learn):** Their target is ideology as a totalising, closed claim to truth in totalitarian systems, not political commitment as such (✅ owner 02 §9; Gauba PDF pp.35-36).

**Breakdown:**
- Open societies have values (PT-1047).
- The threshold is monopoly on truth and closure to criticism.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.35-36 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1044; PT-1045; PT-1047.

**Trap:** Universalising the totalitarian model (owner 02 advanced §8).

**Mains hook:** The question is whether criticism remains possible, not whether conviction is present.

**Back:** *"POPPER/ARENDT target CLOSED TRUTH, not CONVICTION."*

### Flashcard PT-1062 — Trap: Marx and Lenin use ideology in the same sense

**Front:** Why are Marx's and Lenin's uses of ideology not the same?

**Definition (learn):** Gauba reconstructs Marx's ideology critique as distorted consciousness tied to domination, while Lenin allows a proletarian ideology for socialist struggle (✅ owner 02 §9; Gauba PDF pp.32-33).

**Breakdown:**
- Marx: distortion and domination.
- Lenin: proletarian scientific socialism as organised consciousness.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.32-33 (✅ owner 02).

**Links:** PT-1039; PT-1041; PT-1040.

**Trap:** Presenting both as one undifferentiated "ideology is distortion".

**Mains hook:** The difference is the test for "distinguish" questions.

**Back:** *"MARX: DISTORTION. LENIN: PROLETARIAN CONSCIOUSNESS."*

### Flashcard PT-1063 — Answer architecture for ideology (qualified thesis and directive)

**Front:** What is the qualified thesis and mark-weight architecture for an unfamiliar ideology question?

**Definition (learn):** Ideology is neither pure falsehood nor a spent historical category; it is a group-accepted, action-oriented idea-set that can mobilise legitimate claims as well as dominant-class distortion, and theory must test it, including the end-of-ideology thesis itself (✅ owner 02 §14; ⚠️ owner synthesis).

**Breakdown:**
- "Distinguish": mobilisation (Marx/Lenin/Lukács) vs totalitarian closure (Popper/Arendt) vs declining category (end-of-ideology).
- "Critically examine": Bell/Lipset/Galbraith against Titmuss/Mills/Macpherson/MacIntyre.
- 10 marks: one thesis, Marx and Lenin, verdict. 20 marks: four or five units plus two chains.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner directive table (✅ owner 02 §14, §19).

**Links:** PT-1031; PT-1057; PT-1058; PT-1059.

**Trap:** Describing the definition without the debate.

**Mains hook:** Ideology persists in new forms, including technocratic language.

**Back:** *"IDEOLOGY IS TESTED, NOT CELEBRATED OR DISMISSED."*

### GROUP — 03 Liberalism and Neoliberalism

### Flashcard PT-1064 — Liberalism (definition)

**Front:** How does Gauba define liberalism?

**Definition (learn):** A political principle that places the liberty of the individual at the centre of public policy, especially freedom from authoritarian restraint (✅ owner 03 §3; Gauba PDF p.40).

**Breakdown:**
- Liberalism is an evolving movement, not one frozen doctrine (✅ owner 03 §1).
- Arose in the West against feudal privilege and for the entrepreneurial class (✅ owner 03 §4).
- Core tenets: rational individuality, natural rights, consent, civil society and state as artificial institutions, primacy of procedure, civil liberties, contract and group bargaining (✅ owner 03 advanced §3).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.40-41 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1065; PT-1066; PT-1067; PT-1068; PT-1070.

**Trap:** Liberalism is not anti-state by definition (PT-1087).

**Mains hook:** Liberalism's common core is the individual's liberty against arbitrary power; its economic programme is contested.

**Back:** *"LIBERALISM = LIBERTY of the INDIVIDUAL against ARBITRARY AUTHORITY."*

### Flashcard PT-1065 — Authoritarian state (the liberal target)

**Front:** What is an authoritarian state in Gauba's definition?

**Definition (learn):** A state in which authority is treated as unquestionable and its directives must be obeyed without challenge (✅ owner 03 §3; Gauba PDF p.40).

**Breakdown:**
- Authority is unquestionable.
- Liberalism opposes it through limits on power and individual rights (✅ owner 03 §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.40 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1064; PT-1070.

**Trap:** Authoritarian is not the same as totalitarian (PT-1046 for totalitarian in the ideology group).

**Mains hook:** Liberal opposition is to unquestioned, unlimited power.

**Back:** *"AUTHORITARIAN = AUTHORITY NOT TO BE QUESTIONED."*

### Flashcard PT-1066 — Classical or negative liberalism

**Front:** What is classical (negative) liberalism, and who are its founders in Gauba's account?

**Definition (learn):** The laissez-faire strand that confines the state to protecting life, property, contract and minimal common services (✅ owner 03 §3; Gauba PDF p.41).

**Breakdown:**
- The state is a necessary evil; the individual is the end (✅ owner 03 §4).
- Founders named: Locke, Smith and Bentham (PT-1071 to PT-1073).
- Minimal services are those private enterprise would not undertake (✅ owner 03 advanced Unit 1).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.41 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1064; PT-1067; PT-1071; PT-1072; PT-1073.

**Trap:** Do not use "classical" to mean "old-fashioned" in general; it names this laissez-faire strand.

**Mains hook:** Classical liberalism names the state's minimal functions; its gap is the market-generated deprivation it does not address.

**Back:** *"CLASSICAL = STATE as NECESSARY EVIL: PROTECT LIFE, PROPERTY, CONTRACT."*

### Flashcard PT-1067 — Welfare or positive liberalism

**Front:** What is welfare (positive) liberalism, and how does it differ from the classical strand?

**Definition (learn):** The strand that gives the state a positive role in securing a dignified life, welfare and self-development (✅ owner 03 §3; Gauba PDF p.41).

**Breakdown:**
- Liberty needs supportive conditions, not only absence of restraint (✅ owner 03 §9).
- Welfare liberalism's contributors: Mill, Green, Hobhouse, Laski, Tawney (PT-1074 to PT-1076).
- Owner ⚠️ notes a finer split is analytical, not Gauba's own label.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.41-42 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1066; PT-1068; PT-1074; PT-1075; PT-1076.

**Trap:** Welfare liberalism did not abandon liberty for equality (owner 03 §9).

**Mains hook:** Welfare liberals ask what background conditions liberty needs.

**Back:** *"POSITIVE LIBERALISM = STATE SECURES the CONDITIONS of LIBERTY."*

### Flashcard PT-1068 — Welfare state (definition)

**Front:** Define the welfare state as Gauba uses it.

**Definition (learn):** A state that provides social security, education, health and other public services to secure social well-being (✅ owner 03 §3; Gauba PDF p.41).

**Breakdown:**
- Services: social security, education, health.
- Expansion of state responsibility is the target of neoliberal rollback (PT-1069).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.41 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1067; PT-1069; PT-1077.

**Trap:** Do not conflate the welfare state with socialism; the owner treats it as a liberal-welfare institution.

**Mains hook:** The welfare state is the institutional object that neoliberals challenge on coercion grounds.

**Back:** *"WELFARE STATE = SECURITY, EDUCATION, HEALTH by PUBLIC SERVICE."*

### Flashcard PT-1069 — Neoliberalism, neo-classical liberalism and libertarianism (umbrella)

**Front:** What umbrella does Gauba use for neoliberalism, and what precision caveat applies?

**Definition (learn):** Gauba groups neoliberalism, neo-classical liberalism and libertarianism as a restoration of laissez-faire and welfare-state rollback (✅ owner 03 §3; Gauba PDF pp.42-43).

**Breakdown:**
- Market neoliberalism (Hayek, Friedman) and rights-based libertarianism (Nozick) overlap but are not identical (⚠️ owner 03 §3).
- The file uses "neoliberalism" as an umbrella term for pedagogy (owner 03 advanced §2).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.42-43 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1077; PT-1078; PT-1079; PT-1088.

**Trap:** Treating the three as one doctrine (PT-1089).

**Mains hook:** Name the umbrella, then distinguish its three arguments.

**Back:** *"NEOLIBERAL UMBRELLA: ROLL BACK the STATE; three ROUTES, not one DOCTRINE."*

### Flashcard PT-1070 — Constitutionalism (liberalism's durable core)

**Front:** Why does Gauba say liberalism survives most durably as constitutionalism?

**Definition (learn):** Organisation of the state according to a constitution so that power is structured and limited rather than arbitrary (✅ owner 03 §3; Gauba PDF p.45).

**Breakdown:**
- Gauba: liberalism is invoked both as a theory of capitalism and as a theory of constitutionalism; the second has more lasting value (✅ owner 03 §5).
- Limited offices and rule-bound power (✅ owner 03 advanced §7).
- Owner warns constitutionalism has distributive consequences too (owner 03 Unit 6).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.45 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1064; PT-1067; PT-1069; PT-1093.

**Trap:** Neoliberalism is not constitutionalism. Gauba separates the capitalist and constitutional strands (advanced §12).

**Mains hook:** The safest verdict on liberalism is that its enduring contribution is limits on arbitrary power.

**Back:** *"CONSTITUTIONALISM = POWER LIMITED BY RULES, the most DURABLE liberal gift."*

### Flashcard PT-1071 — John Locke

**Front:** What role does John Locke play in the owner's account of liberalism?

**Definition (learn):** Father of liberalism; early defender of rights, consent and limited government within classical liberalism (✅ owner 03 §6; Gauba PDF p.41).

**Breakdown:**
- Named with Smith and Bentham as classical founders (PT-1066).
- Owner gives no Locke work title or year (⚠️ do not add).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.41 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1066; PT-1070.

**Trap:** Locke's rights and consent are not the same as Nozick's entitlement (PT-1079).

**Mains hook:** Consent and limited government are Locke's contribution to the constitutional core.

**Back:** *"LOCKE: RIGHTS, CONSENT, LIMITED GOVERNMENT."*

### Flashcard PT-1072 — Adam Smith

**Front:** What does Adam Smith contribute to classical liberalism in the owner?

**Definition (learn):** Early liberal defender of laissez-faire and market society (✅ owner 03 §6; Gauba PDF pp.41-42).

**Breakdown:**
- Market society as the model of order.
- State limited in economic life (✅ owner 03 advanced §5).
- Owner gives no book title or date (⚠️ do not add).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.41-42 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1066; PT-1081 (market coordination).

**Trap:** Smith as defender of laissez-faire is the owner's claim; do not attribute neoliberal knowledge arguments to him.

**Mains hook:** Smith's market society is the classical benchmark that welfare liberals revise.

**Back:** *"SMITH: MARKET SOCIETY, LAISSEZ-FAIRE, LIMITED STATE."*

### Flashcard PT-1073 — Jeremy Bentham

**Front:** What role does Jeremy Bentham play in the owner's liberal account?

**Definition (learn):** Founder of utilitarianism focused on the greatest happiness, still part of early liberal development (✅ owner 03 §6; Gauba PDF p.42).

**Breakdown:**
- Utilitarian stream within liberalism.
- Later modified by Mill (PT-1074).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.42 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1074; PT-1085 (individualism versus utilitarianism).

**Trap:** Do not present Bentham as a rights theorist.

**Mains hook:** Bentham shows liberalism's two early roots: rights and happiness.

**Back:** *"BENTHAM: GREATEST HAPPINESS, the utilitarian root of liberalism."*

### Flashcard PT-1074 — J.S. Mill (liberty and welfare turn)

**Front:** How does Mill change utilitarianism and open the welfare turn?

**Definition (learn):** Mill modifies utilitarianism, elevates liberty and opens the road toward taxation for welfare and a positive state role (✅ owner 03 §6; Gauba PDF pp.41-42).

**Breakdown:**
- Qualitative distinctions in pleasure (✅ owner 03 advanced §5).
- Liberty central.
- Taxation for the poor (✅ owner 03 advanced §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.41-42 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1073; PT-1075; PT-1067.

**Trap:** Mill is not a classical laissez-faire thinker in the owner's account.

**Mains hook:** Mill is the bridge from classical to welfare liberalism.

**Back:** *"MILL: UTILITARIANISM tilted toward LIBERTY and WELFARE."*

### Flashcard PT-1075 — T.H. Green (moral dimension of welfare liberalism)

**Front:** What does T.H. Green add to liberal thought?

**Definition (learn):** He adds a moral dimension and a fuller theory of welfare liberalism (✅ owner 03 §6; Gauba PDF p.41).

**Breakdown:**
- Liberty needs conditions; a positive state role (⚠️ owner 03 Unit 2 analytical restatement).
- Owner calls the "new liberalism" label analytical, not Gauba's (PT-1092).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.41 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1067; PT-1074; PT-1076; PT-1092.

**Trap:** Green's moral argument is not the same as the institutional welfare state of Laski and Tawney.

**Mains hook:** Green supplies the moral case for positive liberty-enabling state action.

**Back:** *"GREEN: MORAL DUTY makes the STATE ENABLE LIBERTY."*

### Flashcard PT-1076 — Hobhouse, Laski and Tawney (welfare-state tradition)

**Front:** What do Hobhouse, Laski and Tawney contribute?

**Definition (learn):** They develop the welfare-state tradition in the first half of the twentieth century (✅ owner 03 §6; Gauba PDF p.41).

**Breakdown:**
- Institutional social security, education and redistribution (✅ owner 03 §12.2).
- Tawney also appears as critic of sacralised property (✅ owner 03 advanced §5; Gauba PDF p.44).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.41, 44 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1075; PT-1067; PT-1084.

**Trap:** Do not merge Tawney's property critique into neoliberal property defence.

**Mains hook:** Tawney's critique of unqualified property is a welfare-liberal argument.

**Back:** *"HOBHOUSE, LASKI, TAWNEY: the WELFARE STATE tradition."*

### Flashcard PT-1077 — F.A. Hayek (spontaneous order and knowledge)

**Front:** What is Hayek's place in neoliberal argument, as the owner presents it?

**Definition (learn):** A chief exponent of neoliberal rollback, associated with spontaneous order and market coordination against state planning (✅ owner 03 §6, advanced §5; Gauba PDF p.42).

**Breakdown:**
- Gauba groups him with Friedman and Nozick; the knowledge argument is attributed to "all neo-liberals", not to Hayek alone (✅ owner 03 §13).
- Spontaneous order: needs emerge through unpredictable transactions (PT-1083).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.42 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1081; PT-1083; PT-1078; PT-1079.

**Trap:** Hayek's spontaneous-order claim does not by itself prove that every welfare instrument is central planning (owner 03 §13).

**Mains hook:** The informational argument against planning is strong against command planning, weaker against all welfare action.

**Back:** *"HAYEK: SPONTANEOUS ORDER, DISPERSED KNOWLEDGE, MARKETS over PLANS."*

### Flashcard PT-1078 — Milton Friedman (market coordination)

**Front:** What role does Milton Friedman play in the neoliberal group?

**Definition (learn):** Named with Hayek as a major neoliberal defender of market coordination over state intervention (✅ owner 03 §6; Gauba PDF p.42).

**Breakdown:**
- Market freedom and market coordination.
- Gauba's neoliberal "market democracy" claim is reported without individual attribution (✅ owner 03 §13).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.42-43 (✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1077; PT-1082; PT-1081.

**Trap:** Friedmanite market freedom is a distinct argument from Hayekian spontaneous order (owner 03 advanced §2).

**Mains hook:** Market freedom and spontaneous order reach limited-government conclusions by different routes.

**Back:** *"FRIEDMAN: MARKET CHOICE over STATE CONTROL."*

### Flashcard PT-1079 — Robert Nozick (minimal state and entitlement)

**Front:** What is Nozick's entitlement triad, and how is it verified?

**Definition (learn):** Nozick's historical entitlement theory in *Anarchy, State, and Utopia* (1974) requires just initial acquisition, just transfer and rectification of injustice in holdings (✅ owner 03 §14; ✅ Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, "Nozick's Political Philosophy", fetched 2026-11-01).

**Breakdown:**
- The SEP entry identifies Part II, chapter 7 ("Distributive Justice") as the place for the historical entitlement conception (✅ SEP, fetched).
- Minimal state limited to life, liberty, property and contract; no more extensive state can be justified (✅ SEP, fetched).
- Gauba names Nozick only as a rollback exponent (✅ owner 03).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1974; Part II Chapter 7 (✅ SEP, fetched; ✅ owner 03).

**Links:** PT-1080; PT-1090; PT-1078; PT-1077.

**Trap:** Do not attribute the entitlement triad to Gauba's text (owner 03 §14 caution).

**Mains hook:** Nozick's minimal state is not indifferent to injustice; rectification is part of his test.

**Back:** *"NOZICK: JUST ACQUISITION, JUST TRANSFER, and RECTIFICATION."*

### Flashcard PT-1080 — Rawls's welfare-liberal reply (cross-owner)

**Front:** What is the standard welfare-liberal reply to a procedural entitlement theory?
