**Definition (learn):** Burke's position is that a political order without any means of change lacks the means of its own preservation; the conservative objection is to total, abstract, simultaneous and irreversible change (⚠️ owner 05 §22.2; Burke, *Reflections* 1790 as cited by owner).

**Breakdown:**
- Reform in order to conserve (PT-1168).
- Method critique, not motive critique (PT-1179).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Burke, *Reflections on the Revolution in France* (1790) (✅ owner 05 §21; not fetched).

**Links:** PT-1166; PT-1168; PT-1180.

**Trap:** Stating conservatism as opposition to all change.

**Mains hook:** Start any conservatism answer with this correction.

**Back:** *"CONSERVATISM ≠ NO CHANGE: REFORM to CONSERVE."*

### Flashcard PT-1168 — Edmund Burke: inheritance, prescription and prudential reform

**Front:** What are Burke's three core ideas on inheritance, prescription and reform?

**Definition (learn):** A political order is an intergenerational trust rather than a contract renegotiable by the living alone; long usage is evidence of workability; prescription creates a presumption, not immunity, in favour of what exists (✅ owner 05 §21, §22.3; ⚠️ owner analytical; *Reflections on the Revolution in France* (London: J. Dodsley, 1790), owner-cited archive.org link, not fetched).

**Breakdown:**
- Inheritance: trust across generations (⚠️ owner 05 §22.3).
- Prescription: survival plus adjustment (⚠️ owner 05 §22.3).
- Prudential reform: timely, particular, remedial (⚠️ owner 05 §22.3).
- Rights rooted in inherited custom (Gauba PDF pp.346-347, cross-owner; ✅ owner 05 §22.3).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1790 (✅ owner 05 §21; not fetched).

**Links:** PT-1167; PT-1169; PT-1170; PT-1178.

**Trap:** Do not put Burke's famous "partnership" formulation in quotation marks without verification (owner 05 §22.3).

**Mains hook:** Burke's method critique: wholesale abstract redesign destroys accumulated capital.

**Back:** *"BURKE 1790: INHERITED TRUST, PRESCRIPTION, PRUDENT REPAIR."*

### Flashcard PT-1169 — Burke's "prejudice" in its older sense

**Front:** What does Burke mean by "prejudice", and what is the trap?

**Definition (learn):** A settled pre-judgment carried in habit and manners that makes right conduct habitual under pressure, where fresh reasoning would be slow and uncertain (⚠️ owner 05 §22.3).

**Breakdown:**
- Older sense: pre-judgment, not bigotry.
- Latent wisdom argument (owner 05 §22.3).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Burke, 1790 (owner 05 §21; not fetched).

**Links:** PT-1168; PT-1178.

**Trap:** Rendering prejudice as a defence of bigotry is a marks-losing modern equivocation (owner 05 §22.3).

**Mains hook:** Distinguish older and modern senses of the same word.

**Back:** *"PREJUDICE (OLD SENSE) = HABIT THAT GUIDES, not HATRED THAT BLINDS."*

### Flashcard PT-1170 — Burke's trustee model of representation

**Front:** What is Burke's representative trustee model?

**Definition (learn):** The representative owes deliberative judgment to constituents, not mechanical instruction (⚠️ owner 05 §22.3; Speech to the Electors of Bristol, 3 November 1774, owner-cited).

**Breakdown:**
- Same disposition as prescription, applied to representation (owner 05 §22.3).
- Cross-link to the democracy file (owner 05 §22.3).
- Labelling of representation models carries a provenance hedge (owner 05 §22.3).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 3 November 1774 (✅ owner 05 §21; not fetched).

**Links:** PT-1168; PT-1171.

**Trap:** Do not equate the trustee model with a claim that representatives may ignore constituents altogether.

**Mains hook:** Deliberative judgment, not mechanical instruction, is the representative's duty in Burke's model.

**Back:** *"BURKE 1774: REPRESENTATIVE = JUDGMENT, not a MESSENGER."*

### Flashcard PT-1171 — Michael Oakeshott: "Rationalism in Politics"

**Front:** What is Oakeshott's critique of rationalism in politics?

**Definition (learn):** Rationalism treats political problems as technical problems soluble by applying an abstract doctrine, and is suspicious of anything not justifiable from first principles; political knowledge is not exhausted by manuals (⚠️ owner 05 §22.4).

**Breakdown:**
- Technical knowledge (formulable in rules) versus practical knowledge (acquired in the doing) (⚠️ owner 05 §22.4).
- Rationalist error: assuming technical knowledge is the whole (⚠️ owner 05 §22.4).
- Essay first published in *Cambridge Journal*, 1947; collected 1962 (✅ owner 05 §21, §22.4; not fetched).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1947 essay; 1962 collection (✅ owner 05, not fetched).

**Links:** PT-1172; PT-1168; PT-1179.

**Trap:** Do not attach a page or verbatim sentence to the essay (owner 05 §22.4).

**Mains hook:** Oakeshott supplies the epistemological argument for Burke's historical one.

**Back:** *"OAKESHOTT 1947: POLITICS IS NOT A MANUAL; practical knowledge lives in DOING."*

### Flashcard PT-1172 — Oakeshott's "intimations" and the Burke relation

**Front:** What positive alternative does Oakeshott's standard vocabulary offer, and how does he relate to Burke?

**Definition (learn):** Political activity pursues the intimations already present in a society's arrangements, making consistent what a tradition half-contains; Oakeshott supplies the epistemological argument where Burke supplies the historical and prudential one (⚠️ owner 05 §22.4).

**Breakdown:**
- "Intimations" is standard vocabulary, not a quoted sentence (⚠️ owner 05 §22.4).
- Oakeshott is more sceptical about all political doctrine, including conservatism (owner 05 §22.4).
- Do not claim a Hayekian rejection of economic planning in Oakeshott (owner 05 §22.4).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner beyond 1947/1962.

**Links:** PT-1171; PT-1168; PT-1166.

**Trap:** Over-extending Oakeshott into a policy platform (owner 05 §22.4).

**Mains hook:** Complementary, not identical, to Burke.

**Back:** *"OAKESHOTT: BUILD FROM INTIMATIONS in the TRADITION, not from BLUEPRINTS."*

### Flashcard PT-1173 — The four pillars of conservatism

**Front:** What are the four conservative pillars, and what objection stands against each?

**Definition (learn):** Order, authority, tradition and property. Each has a claim, a mechanism and a standing objection (⚠️ owner 05 §22.5).

**Breakdown:**
- Order: a fragile achievement; the weakest lose most (⚠️ owner 05 §22.5). Objection: order invoked to criminalise protest.
- Authority: legitimated by performance and continuity; economises on coercion (⚠️). Objection: circularity of endurance.
- Tradition: compressed tested experience (⚠️). Objection: survival evidences power, not value.
- Property: stake in order (⚠️). Objection: concentrated property defends the few (cross-link to the liberty owner, Tawney's functionless-property test in the liberty owner, owner 18 file).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Named-scholar reconstruction (owner 05 §22).

**Links:** PT-1166; PT-1168; PT-1174.

**Trap:** Presenting a pillar without its objection.

**Mains hook:** Each pillar's objection shows where the presumption is rebutted.

**Back:** *"ORDER · AUTHORITY · TRADITION · PROPERTY: each NEEDS a CHECK."*

### Flashcard PT-1174 — Strands of conservatism (kept distinct)

**Front:** What are the three conservative strands, and what is the classification caution on New Right?

**Definition (learn):** Traditional or Burkean (prescription and prudential reform); paternalistic or "one nation" (advantage owes obligation); New Right (market plus authority, a composite) (⚠️ owner 05 §22.6).

**Breakdown:**
- Traditional: base case.
- Paternalistic: Disraeli's "two nations" framing in *Sybil* (1845); "One Nation" is a later shorthand (⚠️ owner 05 §22.6).
- New Right: classification contested; economic half is liberal in origin (⚠️ owner 05 §22.6).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *Sybil; or, The Two Nations* (London: Henry Colburn, 1845) (✅ owner 05 §21; not fetched).

**Links:** PT-1173; PT-1175; PT-1176.

**Trap:** Filing Hayek or Friedman under conservatism (PT-1175).

**Mains hook:** Conservatism is internally plural.

**Back:** *"STRANDS: BURKEAN, PATERNALIST (ONE NATION), NEW RIGHT (CONTESTED)."*

### Flashcard PT-1175 — Hayek: "Why I Am Not a Conservative"

**Front:** Why does Hayek disown the conservative label, and why does it matter for classification?

**Definition (learn):** In the postscript "Why I Am Not a Conservative" to *The Constitution of Liberty* (Chicago, 1960), Hayek disowns conservatism because it resists change without a principled alternative (✅ owner 05 §22.6; owner-cited, not fetched).

**Breakdown:**
- Hayek is a market liberal (owner 05 §22.6; PT-1077).
- Filing Hayek under conservatism is a misclassification (owner 05 §22.6).
- Owner notes the sociological alliance of market and traditionalist politics is a separate question (owner 05 Unit C5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1960 (✅ owner 05 §21; not fetched).

**Links:** PT-1077; PT-1174; PT-1069.

**Trap:** Treating New Right market content as conservative.

**Mains hook:** Classification control shows what the doctrine is and is not.

**Back:** *"HAYEK 1960: NOT a CONSERVATIVE, a MARKET LIBERAL."*

### Flashcard PT-1176 — Thomas Paine: inherited authority is no authority

**Front:** What is Paine's antagonistic claim against Burke?

**Definition (learn):** No generation may bind its successors, and inherited authority is not authority at all (✅ owner 05 §22.8 Chain 1; *Rights of Man*, Part I, London: J. S. Jordan, 1791, owner-cited).

**Breakdown:**
- Canonical opposing evidence unit (⚠️ owner 05 Unit C6).
- Paine's own account needs a theory of stability for a re-founded order (owner 05 Unit C6).
- Written against Burke (owner 05 §22.8).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1791 (✅ owner 05 §21; not fetched).

**Links:** PT-1168; PT-1178; PT-1174.

**Trap:** Presenting Paine as a conservative critic.

**Mains hook:** Paine supplies the named antagonist for a "critically examine" answer.

**Back:** *"PAINE 1791: EACH GENERATION OWNS ITS OWN WORLD."*

### Flashcard PT-1177 — Trap: conservatism equals fascism equals reaction

**Front:** Why are conservatism, fascism and reaction different?

**Definition (learn):** Reaction seeks to restore a superseded order; conservatism seeks to conserve and repair the existing one; fascism seeks a leader-centred totalitarian reconstruction that overrides inherited constitutional restraint (⚠️ owner 05 §22.2; ❌ owner trap).

**Breakdown:**
- Fascism rejects the leader-limiting restraint conservatism defends (owner 05 §22.2).
- Liberty and state rows show the split (owner 05 §13 and §22.2).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 05 §22.2; §§2-9 fascism content.

**Links:** PT-1130; PT-1166; PT-1133.

**Trap:** Calling conservatism a mild fascism.

**Mains hook:** Use the leader principle as the test that separates the two.

**Back:** *"CONSERVATISM ≠ FASCISM: CONSERVE the RESTRAINTS fascism DESTROYS."*

### Flashcard PT-1178 — Prescription creates a presumption, not an immunity

**Front:** Why does conservatism's prescription give a presumption, not an immunity, and how does this answer the hierarchy objection?

**Definition (learn):** Prescription shifts the burden of proof; an inherited arrangement that demonstrably fails those subject to it loses the presumption (⚠️ owner 05 §22.8 Chain 1).

**Breakdown:**
- Objection: prudence is what the well-placed recommend to the badly placed (owner 05 §22.8).
- Reply: relocate the test; the paternalistic strand treats deep social division as a conservative obligation (owner 05 §22.6).
- Residual weakness: who decides when the presumption is rebutted (owner 05 §22.8).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 05 §22.8.

**Links:** PT-1168; PT-1176; PT-1174; PT-1179.

**Trap:** Treating prescription as conclusive.

**Mains hook:** Concede the residual weakness in a graded verdict.

**Back:** *"PRESUMPTION, NOT IMMUNITY: a FAILING ORDER LOSES its CLAIM."*

### Flashcard PT-1179 — The conservative critique of abstract redesign

**Front:** What is the conservative premises-mechanism-conclusion argument against wholesale redesign?

**Definition (learn):** P1 (epistemic): knowledge is dispersed, tacit and carried in practices. P2 (institutional): institutions encode forgotten solutions. P3 (consequential): comprehensive redesign is simultaneous and irreversible. Mechanism: designers underestimate what they destroy and cannot undo errors. Conclusion: reform should be particular, evidenced, reversible and continuous (⚠️ owner 05 §22.7).

**Breakdown:**
- Attacks the method, not the motive (owner 05 §22.7).
- Demands reform, does not forbid it (owner 05 §22.7).
- Rules out justifying an institution purely by age (owner 05 §22.7).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 05 §22.7.

**Links:** PT-1171; PT-1168; PT-1178; PT-1167.

**Trap:** Claiming any reform is unconservative merely because it is a reform.

**Mains hook:** Conclusion: piecemeal, evidenced, reversible reform.

**Back:** *"P1 KNOWLEDGE, P2 FORGOTTEN REASONS, P3 IRREVERSIBLE: REDESIGN FAILS, REPAIR WORKS."*

### Flashcard PT-1180 — Three anti-revolutionary routes: Burke, Fabian and Gandhi

**Front:** How do Burkean prudential reform, Fabian incrementalism and Gandhian change differ?

**Definition (learn):** All three reject revolution. Burke: no designed end-state; reform repairs an existing order. Fabian: gradualism with a designed egalitarian end-state (✅ owner 05 §12.1, Gauba PDF pp.57-59). Gandhi: non-violent means with a morally transformed radical end (✅ owner 05 §4, Gauba PDF pp.79-83) (⚠️ Burke row, owner 05 §22.9).

**Breakdown:**
- Pace: gradual (Burke, Fabian), disciplined non-violent (Gandhi).
- Warrant for change: demonstrated malfunction (Burke); reasoned planning (Fabian); truth and ahimsa (Gandhi).
- State: inheritance (Burke), instrument (Fabian), redundant machine (Gandhi).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 05 §22.9.

**Links:** PT-1124; PT-1158; PT-1166; PT-1168.

**Trap:** Treating Burkean and Fabian as identical because both are gradualist; the designed end-state is the discriminator.

**Mains hook:** Fabianism is gradualism with a blueprint; Burkean reform is gradualism because it distrusts blueprints.

**Back:** *"BURKE REPAIRS, FABIAN BUILDS, GANDHI TRANSFORMS MORALLY."*

### Flashcard PT-1181 — Conservatism and India: a caution, not an application

**Front:** What limits apply when using Burke or Oakeshott on Indian material, and what is the caste hard limit?

**Definition (learn):** The vocabulary is European and transfers only as an analytical lens; no Indian factual claim is made in the owner (⚠️ owner 05 §22.11). Prescription must never defend caste hierarchy, since those it burdened never accepted it (owner 05 §22.11 hard limit, §22.8 Chain 2).

**Breakdown:**
- Lens use: the nineteenth-century social-reform debates pose the presumption question (owner 05 §22.11).
- Do not label contemporary Indian parties as conservative, New Right or one-nation without dated sourcing (owner 05 §22.11).
- Route caste to Citizenship file §13, Caste-Gandhi-Ambedkar file and Social-Justice files (owner 05 §22.11).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None asserted in owner.

**Links:** PT-1178; PT-1166; PT-1162.

**Trap:** Using tradition as a defence of caste.

**Mains hook:** Correctly stated, the conservative argument does not run in favour of caste hierarchy.

**Back:** *"TRADITION NEVER SHIELDS CASTE: IT WAS NEVER ACCEPTED BY THOSE IT BURDENED."*

## POLITICAL THEORY — Part 2: Feminism, Politics, Approaches, State, Sovereignty and Globalisation

### GROUP — 06 Feminism, Sex and Gender (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard PT-2001 — Feminism as protest against patriarchal subordination
**Front:** How does Gauba define feminism, and what is its core protest?
**Definition (learn):** Feminism is concern with the status and role of women in relation to men, combined with protest against injustice suffered because of sex; its target is the inferior status patriarchy assigns to women.
**Breakdown:**
- Diagnosis: women's inferior status is social and cultural, not naturally justified.
- Programme: equal rights for women and men plus improvement of women's position in society.
- Feminism is not a denial that biological difference exists.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, 5th ed. (2009), Ch.2, PDF pp.84–86 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** PT-2002 (patriarchy); PT-2003 (sex vs gender); PT-2009/PT-2012/PT-2014 (three streams).
**Trap:** Feminism is not one doctrine; "feminism says women are biologically determined" is a misreading (see PT-2010 for Firestone's position).
**Mains hook:** Feminism diagnoses subordination as socially produced and therefore alterable; the answer must show both the diagnosis and the feminist remedy.
**Back:** *"Feminism = protest + remedy: unjust female subordination is social, so it can change."*

### Flashcard PT-2002 — Patriarchy (rule of the father to wider male domination)
**Front:** What is patriarchy and how did its meaning widen?
**Definition (learn):** Literally "rule of the father"; now used for male domination in the household, labour market and wider social life.
**Breakdown:**
- Original sense: rule of the father within the family.
- Extended sense: domination extending from household authority into labour market and public life.
- Political sense: a power system, not merely a family arrangement.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.84 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** PT-2001; PT-2011 (Millett: patriarchy as political power); PT-2019 (formal citizenship and patriarchy).
**Trap:** Patriarchy is not the same as "sex"; it names a system of domination, which is why feminism treats it as a political category.
**Mains hook:** Argue why patriarchy is a political category and not merely a family arrangement: it structures law, labour, education and representation.
**Back:** *"Patriarchy: from father's rule at home to male rule in the market and the polity."*

### Flashcard PT-2003 — Sex vs gender: the foundational distinction
**Front:** Distinguish sex from gender.
**Definition (learn):** Sex is biological difference (reproductive function, secondary characteristics); gender is the culturally constructed images, expectations and status-distinctions attached to women and men.
**Breakdown:**
- Sex = natural / biological.
- Gender = cultural / social; open to change.
- The distinction lets biology be separated from socially assigned inferiority.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.84–86 (✅ owner 06). Later standard provenance for the terminology (Oakley, Rubin) is ⚠️ — see PT-2021, PT-2022.
**Links:** PT-2004; PT-2005; PT-2006; PT-2020 (de Beauvoir ⚠️).
**Trap:** Sex and gender do not mean the same thing; equating them lets "biology is destiny" slip into the argument.
**Mains hook:** Use sex/gender to show why gender hierarchy is alterable even though sex difference is real; conclude that the hierarchy, not the biology, is the problem.
**Back:** *"Sex is what nature gives; gender is what society adds — and society can change it."*

### Flashcard PT-2004 — Gender roles as instruments of social control
**Front:** Which traits does Gauba link to men and women as cultural roles?
**Definition (learn):** Gender roles such as aggressiveness and dominance for men, and submissiveness and expressiveness for women, are treated as cultural instruments of social control.
**Breakdown:**
- Masculinity: aggressiveness, dominance.
- Femininity: submissiveness, expressiveness.
- Socialisation: girls taught obedience; boys taught assertiveness.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.85 (✅ owner 06); socialisation example PDF p.87 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** PT-2003; PT-2005; PT-2015 (legal vs substantive equality).
**Trap:** These traits are coded as natural to justify domination; the feminist move is to show they are learned, not innate.
**Mains hook:** Socialisation explains how inequality reproduces without any formal law forcing it.
**Back:** *"Roles are taught, not born: obedience for girls, assertiveness for boys, control for society."*

### Flashcard PT-2005 — Natural vs conventional inequality (Rousseau via Gauba)
**Front:** Why does Gauba invoke Rousseau's natural/conventional inequality distinction?
**Definition (learn):** Natural inequality is grounded in nature; conventional inequality is produced by social agreement or custom and is therefore open to criticism and alteration.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural inequality: differences of nature (not a basis for political hierarchy by itself).
- Conventional inequality: created by social arrangement; alterable.
- Gauba places gender inequality in the second category.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.86 (✅ owner 06). Advanced restatement: PDF p.86 (✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2003; PT-2006 (biology does not justify hierarchy); PT-2016.
**Trap:** Do not say Gauba rejects all natural differences; he rejects treating gender hierarchy as natural.
**Mains hook:** Rousseau's distinction lets the answer say "difference is natural, hierarchy is conventional", which is the core anti-essentialist move.
**Back:** *"Nature gives difference; convention gives rank — gender rank is convention, so it can change."*

### Flashcard PT-2006 — Biology does not justify inferior status (trap pair)
**Front:** Does biology justify women's inferior status, according to Gauba?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba explicitly rejects the claim that biological differences justify hierarchy; the main causes lie in social arrangements and cultural conditioning.
**Breakdown:**
- Rejected: biology justifies hierarchy.
- Accepted: biological differences exist.
- Cause identified: social arrangement and conditioning.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.86–87 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** PT-2005; PT-2003; PT-2010 (Firestone is the exception that gives biology a strong role).
**Trap:** Examiners set "biology justifies hierarchy" as a ❌ option; the correct answer for Gauba is the reverse.
**Mains hook:** Biological difference does not entail political inferiority; the burden of proof lies on whoever claims a natural basis.
**Back:** *"Biology is a difference, not a verdict."*

### Flashcard PT-2007 — Mary Wollstonecraft (rational women, equal rights)
**Front:** What does Wollstonecraft argue, and when was her key work published?
**Definition (learn):** Wollstonecraft argues that women are rational individuals and should enjoy equal rights, education, property and civic standing.
**Breakdown:**
- Thesis: rationality is common to both sexes, so rights should be equal.
- Demands: education, property, civic rights.
- Early-rights stream in Gauba's reconstruction.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.88 (✅ owner 06). Work: *A Vindication of the Rights of Woman*, published January 1792 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
**Links:** PT-2008 (Mill); PT-2018 (early rights agenda); PT-2009 (liberal feminism).
**Trap:** Do not confuse Wollstonecraft's 1792 work with Mill's 1869 essay; both are early liberal arguments but separated by almost eight decades.
**Mains hook:** Wollstonecraft shows that the feminist demand began as a claim of reason and rights, not as a radical critique of the family.
**Back:** *"Wollstonecraft 1792: reason is common; so rights must be common."*

### Flashcard PT-2008 — J.S. Mill (women not inferior in talent)
**Front:** What does Mill argue about women's talent and rights?
**Definition (learn):** Mill argues that women are not inferior in talent and deserve full legal and political rights.
**Breakdown:**
- Thesis: no natural inferiority in talent.
- Demand: full legal and political equality.
- Cited by Gauba at PDF pp.84, 88.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.84, 88 (✅ owner 06). *The Subjection of Women* first published 1869; Mill worked on it until completion in 1861 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
**Links:** PT-2007; PT-2009; PT-2018.
**Trap:** The 1869 date is publication; the 1861 date is when Mill finished writing. Do not swap them.
**Mains hook:** Mill's argument moves the case for women from charity to justice: their exclusion is an injustice, not a natural fact.
**Back:** *"Mill 1869: women are not less able, so they must not be less free."*

### Flashcard PT-2009 — Liberal feminism (equal opportunity and formal barriers)
**Front:** What is liberal feminism's core claim, obstacle and remedy?
**Definition (learn):** Liberal feminism seeks equality of opportunity, equal pay, removal of formal barriers and better representation.
**Breakdown:**
- Core claim: women deserve equal opportunity and equal rights in all walks of life.
- Obstacle: formal legal, educational and professional barriers.
- Remedy: equal pay, representation, reform of discriminatory laws.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.88 (✅ owner 06); comparison table PDF pp.88–89 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** PT-2007; PT-2008; PT-2015; PT-2016; PT-2033 (three-school comparison).
**Trap:** Reducing feminism to legal reform alone is a ❌ caricature; liberal feminism is one stream, not the whole movement.
**Mains hook:** Liberal feminism asks whether barriers are open; its limit is that open barriers do not by themselves change power.
**Back:** *"Liberal = open the doors: equal pay, equal law, equal representation."*

### Flashcard PT-2010 — Shulamith Firestone and the reproductive-biology thesis
**Front:** What is Firestone's radical-feminist thesis?
**Definition (learn):** Firestone ties women's subordination to reproductive biology and sees technological change as the route to release from that dependence.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: reproductive biology is the historical basis of female subordination.
- Remedy: technology enables escape from reproductive dependence.
- Gauba's treatment: a strong biology-centred radical account, not feminism's consensus.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.89 (✅ owner 06). *The Dialectic of Sex* published October 1970 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched). Advanced: strong explanatory role for biology (PDF p.89, ✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2011; PT-2012; PT-2006 (biology justifies hierarchy is the rejected view, while Firestone is the strongest exception); PT-2029.
**Trap:** Generalising Firestone's biological emphasis to all feminism is a ❌ error; her view sits inside radical feminism only.
**Mains hook:** Firestone shows that once biology is granted an explanatory role, feminism must decide whether technology or politics removes subordination.
**Back:** *"Firestone 1970: reproduction made women dependent; technology can free them."*

### Flashcard PT-2011 — Kate Millett and patriarchy as political power
**Front:** How does Millett read relations between the sexes?
**Definition (learn):** Millett reads male-female relations as political power relations sustained by ideology, authority and economic force.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: relations between sexes are political, not private.
- Mechanisms: ideology, authority, economic force.
- Gauba places her in the radical stream.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.89 (✅ owner 06). *Sexual Politics* published 1970 by Doubleday (✅ Wikipedia, fetched). Millett's definition of patriarchy as "the birthright priority whereby males rule females" (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
**Links:** PT-2002; PT-2010; PT-2012; PT-2019.
**Trap:** Millett does not rely on biology as the main explanation; she treats dominance as social constructs.
**Mains hook:** Millett converts patriarchy from a family arrangement into a political system, which is why the answer must treat it as a power question.
**Back:** *"Millett 1970: the personal is political; patriarchy is power, not nature."*

### Flashcard PT-2012 — Radical feminism (sex-power and primary oppression)
**Front:** What does radical feminism claim, obstacle, remedy?
**Definition (learn):** Radical feminism holds that women's oppression is primary and cannot be reduced to some deeper system alone.
**Breakdown:**
- Core claim: women's oppression is primary.
- Obstacle: patriarchal sexual power and reproductive dependence.
- Remedy: transform sex-power relations and dismantle domination.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.89 (✅ owner 06); comparison table (✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2010; PT-2011; PT-2014; PT-2029 (objection to radical feminism).
**Trap:** Radical feminism is not the same as "biology determines everything"; its primacy claim is about power, with biology as one route.
**Mains hook:** The radical case is strongest when it explains why sexual and reproductive domination is analytically distinct from class or race domination.
**Back:** *"Radical = patriarchy first: sex-power must be dismantled, not just regulated."*

### Flashcard PT-2013 — Sheila Rowbotham and socialist feminism
**Front:** Who is Rowbotham and what does she link?
**Definition (learn):** Rowbotham is a socialist feminist who links women's liberation with struggle against capitalism and other oppressions.
**Breakdown:**
- Link: women's liberation + anti-capitalist struggle.
- Advanced: also the recovery of women's hidden history.
- Gauba cites her at PDF pp.89–90.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.89–90 (✅ owner 06); advanced PDF pp.89–90 (✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2014; PT-2023 (Marxist feminism ⚠️); PT-2030.
**Trap:** Socialist feminism is not simply Marxist feminism; Rowbotham's dual-systems view treats patriarchy and capitalism as two mutually reinforcing systems.
**Mains hook:** Rowbotham's synthesis explains why class analysis alone misses patriarchal control of sexuality and labour.
**Back:** *"Rowbotham: women's liberation needs both anti-patriarchy and anti-capitalism."*

### Flashcard PT-2014 — Socialist feminism (patriarchy + capitalism)
**Front:** What is socialist feminism's core claim, obstacle and remedy?
**Definition (learn):** Socialist feminism links women's oppression both to patriarchy and to capitalism; it combines patriarchal analysis with Marxist class analysis.
**Breakdown:**
- Core claim: oppression linked to both systems.
- Obstacle: wage labour, domestic labour and class exploitation together.
- Remedy: link women's liberation to anti-capitalist and anti-oppression struggles.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.89–90 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** PT-2013; PT-2023; PT-2012; PT-2033.
**Trap:** Collapsing gender into class loses feminist specificity; an answer must keep patriarchy as a separate analytic axis.
**Mains hook:** Socialist feminism widens explanation but must avoid submerging gendered experience inside general anti-capitalist politics.
**Back:** *"Socialist = patriarchy and capital, two locks, one key to unlock."*

### Flashcard PT-2015 — Legal equality vs substantive equality
**Front:** Why is legal equality not the end of the feminist question?
**Definition (learn):** Early feminism attacked barriers to education, property and vote; later feminism asks whether equal law alone changes social power.
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: legal exclusion (education, property, suffrage, divorce).
- Stage 2: substantive equality (representation, social status, participation in public power).
- Gauba shows representation remains unequal after suffrage.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.88–93 (✅ owner 06); advanced PDF pp.91–93 (✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2018; PT-2019; PT-2016; PT-2009.
**Trap:** "If women receive equal legal rights, feminism has no further issue" is a ❌ misreading of Gauba.
**Mains hook:** Equal voting rights did not automatically create equal political power; the gap lies between citizenship in law and actual representation.
**Back:** *"Law opens the door; substance fills the room."*

### Flashcard PT-2016 — Equal treatment vs compensatory support
**Front:** What are the two broad views on how to achieve gender equality?
**Definition (learn):** One view insists on the sameness of the legal regime; the other allows maternity-related protections and compensatory measures.
**Breakdown:**
- Equal rights view: same laws, same treatment.
- Compensatory view: differentiated protections and representation-enhancing measures.
- Gauba presents both without declaring one final.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.92–93 (✅ owner 06); advanced PDF pp.92–93 (✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2015; PT-2005 (conventional inequality is alterable, so compensation can be justified); PT-2033.
**Trap:** Compensatory protection is not a concession that women are naturally weaker; the justification is correcting a social disadvantage.
**Mains hook:** A strong answer shows that equal treatment can reproduce unequal starting points, so some measures must be compensatory.
**Back:** *"Same rule vs fair start: equal law, sometimes unequal support."*

### Flashcard PT-2017 — Dated statistics trap (Gauba's 1980 UN figure)
**Front:** Can Gauba's 1980 UN numerical claim be quoted as current fact?
**Definition (learn):** No. The 1980 UN figure on women's work, income and property, and the line saying the condition remained unchanged, are book-period illustrations and must not be repeated as present-day fact.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: Gauba, 5th ed. (2009), PDF p.86.
- Status: historical citation.
- Related: late-twentieth-century representation percentages (PDF pp.91–92) are also historical.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1980 UN statistic (✅ owner 06, PDF p.86). Representation figures for Western Europe, UK, US and Scandinavia are late-twentieth-century (✅ owner 06 advanced, PDF pp.91–92).
**Links:** PT-2019; PT-2031 (Indian application must be hedged); PT-2034 (dated indices).
**Trap:** Old numbers quoted as present reality is a ❌ trap; the card's rule is to label every number with its date.
**Mains hook:** Use feminist theory for the conceptual argument; use dated data only as illustration and state the date.
**Back:** *"Old numbers stay in their year: 1980 is history, not news."*

### Flashcard PT-2018 — Early feminist rights agenda
**Front:** What did early feminism demand, and why?
**Definition (learn):** The early rights agenda focused on education, property, divorce and suffrage, because these exclusions had no necessary connection with female biology.
**Breakdown:**
- Education: access to schooling.
- Property: right to hold and control property.
- Divorce: exit from marriage.
- Suffrage: right to vote.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.87–88 (✅ owner 06); advanced rights history PDF pp.87–88 (✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2007; PT-2008; PT-2015; PT-2009.
**Trap:** These exclusions were conventional, not biological; a rights agenda is the first feminist move to show that.
**Mains hook:** The rights agenda proved that exclusion was social; the later agenda asks whether that proof is enough.
**Back:** *"Early feminism asked for school, property, divorce and vote: exclusions with no biological reason."*

### Flashcard PT-2019 — Formal citizenship vs gendered representation (advanced)
**Front:** Why is formal citizenship insufficient from a feminist standpoint?
**Definition (learn):** Formal citizenship grants rights in law; feminist critique asks whether those rights translate into representation, status and power in public life.
**Breakdown:**
- Citizenship in law: equal voting, equal legal rights.
- Actual representation: unequal legislative participation even after suffrage.
- Social power: gender-coded roles still shape public life.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.90–93 (✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2015; PT-2002; PT-2017; PT-2024 (Pateman, ⚠️).
**Trap:** A ✅ law does not guarantee a ✅ outcome; do not say formal equality has been fully achieved.
**Mains hook:** The answer should move from citizenship-in-law to actual representation, and end with the unfinished substantive-equality agenda.
**Back:** *"Citizen on paper is not citizen in power."*

### Flashcard PT-2020 — Simone de Beauvoir (womanhood as social becoming) ⚠️
**Front:** What is de Beauvoir's founding proposition for the sex/gender split?
**Definition (learn):** Womanhood is a social becoming rather than a fixed biological given; this is the philosophical root of the sex/gender distinction that Gauba later states in textbook form.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *The Second Sex*, 1949 (⚠️ standard knowledge, not fetched).
- Claim: "one is not born, but rather becomes, a woman" (paraphrased proposition; verbatim not confirmed here).
- Not in Gauba's chapter.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged extension (⚠️ owner 06 §13). Year 1949 ⚠️.
**Links:** PT-2003; PT-2021; PT-2022.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the sex/gender terminology to de Beauvoir as a formal coinage; she supplies the proposition, not the later textbook terms.
**Mains hook:** De Beauvoir shows that the sex/gender distinction has a philosophical lineage that predates the terminology.
**Back:** *"Beauvoir: womanhood is becoming, not being born."*

### Flashcard PT-2021 — Ann Oakley and the sex/gender terminology ⚠️
**Front:** Who popularised the working sex/gender terminology in sociology?
**Definition (learn):** Ann Oakley is usually credited with popularising sex as biological and gender as the culturally learned, historically variable content attached to it.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *Sex, Gender and Society*, 1972 (⚠️ owner-flagged, standard).
- Concept: sex (biology) vs gender (learned content).
- Caveat: the distinction has multiple co-formulators.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1972 ⚠️ (owner 06 §13, not fetched).
**Links:** PT-2003; PT-2020; PT-2022.
**Trap:** Avoid a single uncontested "origin story"; the distinction was consolidated gradually.
**Mains hook:** Cite Oakley as a populariser, not the sole originator, when explaining why gender is social.
**Back:** *"Oakley 1972: sex is biology, gender is what society does with it."*

### Flashcard PT-2022 — Gayle Rubin and the sex/gender system ⚠️
**Front:** What mechanism does Rubin supply for turning sex into gender?
**Definition (learn):** The "sex/gender system": social arrangements that transform biological sex into a hierarchy of gendered obligation, kinship exchange and constrained sexuality.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: "The Traffic in Women", 1975 (⚠️ owner-flagged).
- Mechanism: kinship exchange and obligation.
- Gap filled: Gauba asserts the transformation but does not mechanise it.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1975 ⚠️ (owner 06 §13; not fetched).
**Links:** PT-2003; PT-2020; PT-2021; PT-2024.
**Trap:** Rubin explains how sex becomes gender; do not reduce her to the definition alone.
**Mains hook:** The mechanism question is what distinguishes a thin sex/gender split from a full theory of gender power.
**Back:** *"Rubin 1975: kinship turns sex into gender obligations."*

### Flashcard PT-2023 — Marxist feminism: domestic labour and social reproduction ⚠️
**Front:** What is the Marxist-feminist claim, and how does it differ from socialist feminism?
**Definition (learn):** Women's oppression is generated principally by the capitalist mode of production, especially unpaid domestic and reproductive labour that reproduces the workforce free of charge.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: unpaid housework reproduces labour-power for capital.
- Evidence: the 1970s domestic-labour debate treated housework as unwaged labour.
- Significance: family labour becomes a class question.
- Limit: class reduction cannot explain patriarchal violence or sexuality control that predates capitalism.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1970s domestic labour debate ⚠️ (owner-flagged, not fetched).
**Links:** PT-2013; PT-2014; PT-2024.
**Trap:** Marxist and socialist feminism overlap but are not synonyms: lead with social reproduction for "Marxist", with dual-systems for "socialist".
**Mains hook:** Marxist feminism relocates the household inside political economy; its limit is the explanation of patriarchy beyond capital.
**Back:** *"Marxist feminism: the unpaid home is the factory of labour-power."*

### Flashcard PT-2024 — Carole Pateman and the sexual contract ⚠️
**Front:** What does Pateman's sexual contract thesis claim?
**Definition (learn):** Classical social-contract theory (Hobbes, Locke, Rousseau) silently assumes a prior sexual contract securing men's access to women's bodies and labour, on which the public civic contract rests.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: unmentioned private contract of subordination underlies public contract.
- Evidence: contract narratives are silent on marriage and domestic subordination.
- Significance: liberal equality rests on an unexamined patriarchal base.
- Limit: an interpretive lens, not a documented event; do not present as a real signed contract.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *The Sexual Contract*, 1988 ⚠️ (owner 06 §13; not fetched).
**Links:** PT-2019; PT-2025 (Okin); PT-2022.
**Trap:** Do not claim a real historical contract was signed; it is a reconstruction of silences.
**Mains hook:** Pateman's argument shows that civic equality depends on a private hierarchy that liberal theory does not name.
**Back:** *"Pateman 1988: the civic contract hides a sexual one."*

### Flashcard PT-2025 — Susan Moller Okin and justice in the family ⚠️
**Front:** What does Okin argue about justice and the family?
**Definition (learn):** Theories of justice, which Okin targets via Rawls, wrongly treat the family as a pre-political given rather than a site justice must examine.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: family must be inside the scope of justice.
- Evidence: unequal domestic burden limits women's access to primary goods (time, income, position).
- Significance: extends justice beyond state and market into the household.
- Limit: risk of over-extending justice into private life; syntheses preserve some family autonomy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *Justice, Gender, and the Family*, 1989 ⚠️ (owner 06 §13).
**Links:** PT-2024; PT-2019; PT-2030 (justice in advanced owners 20).
**Trap:** Okin does not say all family life should be state-regulated; her claim is about gender-just background conditions.
**Mains hook:** Justice cannot stop at the public sphere if the household distributes time and income unequally.
**Back:** *"Okin 1989: justice must enter the home."*

### Flashcard PT-2026 — Judith Butler: gender as performativity ⚠️
**Front:** What is Butler's performativity thesis?
**Definition (learn):** Gender is not a cultural coating on stable sex but is performative: produced through repeated, stylised acts (speech, gesture, dress, behaviour) that create the appearance of a natural gendered self.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *Gender Trouble*, 1990 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Claim: gender sustained through "iterative repetitions".
- Radical move: even "sex" is partly constituted through gendered discourse.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1990 ✅ (Wikipedia, fetched); performativity claim ✅ (Wikipedia); later and more radical than Gauba's textbook split (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-2027; PT-2028; PT-2003.
**Trap:** Do not present performativity as Gauba's position; do not claim Butler denies bodies exist.
**Mains hook:** Butler shows that category stability is itself a political issue, which must be paired with the objection-reply chain (PT-2028).
**Back:** *"Butler 1990: gender is repeated doing, not a coat on a fixed body."*

### Flashcard PT-2027 — Kimberlé Crenshaw: intersectionality ⚠️/✅
**Front:** What does Crenshaw's intersectionality claim, and what case did she use?
**Definition (learn):** Treating race and sex discrimination as separate, additive categories fails women who face a distinct, compounded disadvantage at the intersection.
**Breakdown:**
- Paper: "Demarginalizing the Intersection of Race and Sex", 1989 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Case cited: *DeGraffenreid v. General Motors*, 413 F. Supp. 142 (E.D. Mo. 1976) (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Court held race and sex claims separately and dismissed the case.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1989 paper (✅ fetched); DeGraffenreid 1976 (✅ fetched); Black women's hiring claim.
**Links:** PT-2026; PT-2029; PT-2012 (radical's universalism critique).
**Trap:** The 1976 case is a federal district court case, not a Supreme Court judgment; do not inflate its court.
**Mains hook:** Intersectionality forces feminism to ask which women a claim describes; its limit is analytic sprawl without a clear political question.
**Back:** *"Crenshaw 1989: race and sex added separately erases the Black woman."*

### Flashcard PT-2028 — Objection-reply: performativity vs category stability
**Front:** If gender is performative, what happens to the category "woman"?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: if gender is socially constructed, feminism loses a stable category around which to organise and claim rights. Reply: strategic essentialism; political claims can use provisionally stabilised categories while accepting historical construction.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: loss of stable subject for rights.
- Reply: strategic essentialism (provisional category).
- Pragmatic reply: legal sex-discrimination categories work regardless of deeper philosophical instability.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged contested debate (⚠️ owner 06 §14).
**Links:** PT-2026; PT-2027.
**Trap:** Present as a live debate, not a settled result.
**Mains hook:** Good answers show awareness of the debate: strategic use does not prove ontological stability.
**Back:** *"Performativity asks 'who is woman?'; strategy answers 'for now, a usable category'."*

### Flashcard PT-2029 — Objection-reply: radical feminism and undifferentiated "women"
**Front:** What is the objection to radical feminism's primacy claim, and the reply?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: treating patriarchy as the primary, near-universal oppression erases race, class and caste differences among women. Reply: intersectionality and socialist feminism correct the map, while keeping the radical insight that sexual and reproductive domination are analytically distinct.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: undifferentiated "women" as one class.
- Reply 1: Crenshaw's intersectionality.
- Reply 2: Marxist/socialist attention to class.
- Retained radical insight: sexual domination is distinct.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged (⚠️ owner 06 §14).
**Links:** PT-2012; PT-2010; PT-2027; PT-2014.
**Trap:** Do not say the reply replaces radical feminism; it strengthens it.
**Mains hook:** Caste and class reshape who is subordinated in India; the answer should show compounding rather than a single axis.
**Back:** *"Radical sees the wall; intersectionality shows every door opens differently."*

### Flashcard PT-2030 — Savitribai Phule and early girls' education (Indian application) ⚠️
**Front:** What Indian illustration does the owner offer for early challenge to gendered exclusion?
**Definition (learn):** Savitribai Phule, with Jyotirao Phule, is widely associated with pioneering girls' education in nineteenth-century Maharashtra; an early, home-grown challenge to gendered exclusion from education.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: "early advocate of women's education against caste-and-gender exclusion".
- Caution: no specific school dates or counts without verification.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 19th-century Maharashtra (⚠️ owner 06 §15; not fetched).
**Links:** PT-2018; PT-2031; PT-2013.
**Trap:** Do not attach unverified school-opening dates or numbers.
**Mains hook:** Indian feminism has roots in caste reform; the answer should note the intersection of caste and gender.
**Back:** *"Phule: girls' schooling as caste-and-gender reform (keep dates out unless verified)."*

### Flashcard PT-2031 — Ambedkar and women's legal and social equality ⚠️
**Front:** How does the owner link Ambedkar to women's equality?
**Definition (learn):** Ambedkar is widely associated with linking women's legal and social equality to social democracy, including reform of Hindu personal law.
**Breakdown:**
- Orientation: social democracy and legal equality.
- Caution: no bill provisions, sections or dates asserted here.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged general orientation (⚠️ owner 06 §15).
**Links:** PT-2030; PT-2015; PT-2017.
**Trap:** Do not cite specific Hindu Code Bill sections or years from this file; they are outside its verified base.
**Mains hook:** Ambedkar's line shows that legal reform and social democracy are linked; verify specific legislation separately (Polity).
**Back:** *"Ambedkar: law and social democracy must go together for women."*

### Flashcard PT-2032 — Gauba's answer architecture for feminism (10/15/20 marks)
**Front:** How should a feminism answer be structured for 10, 15 and 20 marks?
**Definition (learn):** 10 marks: thesis, sex/gender plus one stream, one limit, conclusion. 15 marks: adds doctrine reconstruction, 2–3 streams, one objection-reply, one Indian illustration. 20 marks: full doctrine, all streams, Pateman/Okin extension, two objection-replies, Indian application, graded conclusion.
**Breakdown:**
- Directive decoding: "Discuss" needs diagnosis and response; "Distinguish" needs analysis of why; "Critically examine" needs thesis, comparison, objection and verdict.
- Each claim: claim → evidence → significance → limit.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner architecture (⚠️ owner 06 §16).
**Links:** PT-2001; PT-2003; PT-2028; PT-2029; PT-2033.
**Trap:** Describing patriarchy without the feminist response is an incomplete answer.
**Mains hook:** Structure follows the directive; the verdict must be reasoned, not neutral.
**Back:** *"Thesis, doctrine, streams, objection, Indian case, verdict."*

### Flashcard PT-2033 — Three-school comparison: formal equality, sexual power, political economy
**Front:** How do liberal, radical and socialist feminism differ by obstacle?
**Definition (learn):** Organise the comparison by obstacle identified: liberal = formal exclusion; radical = patriarchal sexual power; socialist = combined patriarchy and capitalism.
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal: law, education, profession, representation.
- Radical: reproduction, ideology, domination.
- Socialist: wage labour, domestic labour, history of oppression.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Comparison matrix (✅ owner 06, PDF pp.88–90; advanced §8 ✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2009; PT-2012; PT-2014; PT-2032.
**Trap:** Treating any one school as "the" feminist position produces caricature.
**Mains hook:** Use the obstacle axis to compare schools; the richest contrast is formal equality vs sexual power vs political economy.
**Back:** *"Liberal: the door. Radical: the bedroom and the body. Socialist: the factory and the home."*

### Flashcard PT-2034 — Dated indices (GDI/GEM 1995) and book-period figures
**Front:** How should UNDP GDI/GEM references in Gauba be handled?
**Definition (learn):** The 1995 UNDP GDI and GEM references are historical illustrations inside a 2009 text and must be labelled with their date if used.
**Breakdown:**
- GDI and GEM: UNDP gender indices, 1995 references (✅ owner 06 advanced, PDF pp.92–93).
- Rule: any index value must carry its year and source.
- Current values are not supplied by this file.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1995 (✅ owner 06 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2017; PT-2019.
**Trap:** Quoting 1995 indices as current is a ❌ error.
**Mains hook:** Using dated indices as illustration is acceptable only with the date stated.
**Back:** *"Index years are labels, not updates."*

### GROUP — 07 Nature of Politics and Communitarianism (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard PT-2035 — Politics as a public process (concept of the chapter)
**Front:** How does Gauba define politics beyond elections or party intrigue?
**Definition (learn):** Politics is a public process involving conflict, its resolution, and authoritative allocation of values in society; it is studied as a social process, not merely as the organisation of government.
**Breakdown:**
- Lay image rejected: public meetings, electoral intrigue, "dirty game".
- Redefined as a discipline of a particular kind of human activity (conflict + settlement).
- Liberal, Marxist and communitarian readings differ on the image of society behind this process.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, Ch.3, PDF pp.94–99 (✅ owner 07). Advanced restatement PDF pp.94–95 (✅ owner 07 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2036 (political situation); PT-2037 (Easton); PT-2042 (three images of society).
**Trap:** Politics is not a synonym for party competition; Gauba widens it to public conflict-resolution.
**Mains hook:** Politics read as conflict-resolution, domination or common-good pursuit depends on the assumed image of society.
**Back:** *"Politics = public conflict + authoritative settlement, not just votes."*

### Flashcard PT-2036 — Political situation: public conflict, not private quarrel, not war
**Front:** What makes a situation political in Gauba's account?
**Definition (learn):** A political situation is a public conflict among major groups that calls for a universal or public solution, not a private settlement; war is a failure of political solution, not politics itself.
**Breakdown:**
- Public, not private: affects broad groups.
- Requires universal/public resolution.
- Stops short of war as the method of settlement.
- Any quarrel is not political; only public conflicts requiring binding settlement are.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.96 (✅ owner 07); advanced restatement PDF p.96 (✅ owner 07 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2035; PT-2038 (authority vs force); PT-2044 (compromise vs cooperation).
**Trap:** "Any quarrel becomes political" is a ❌ misreading; "politics = war" is also wrong per Gauba.
**Mains hook:** Use the political-situation test to show when a dispute enters the public domain requiring state or institutional settlement.
**Back:** *"Political = public, binding, universal; war is failed politics."*

### Flashcard PT-2037 — David Easton: authoritative allocation of values
**Front:** How does Easton define politics?
**Definition (learn):** Politics is the authoritative allocation of values for society: decisions distribute valued things and are treated as binding because they carry authority.
**Breakdown:**
- Allocation: distribution of scarce valued things.
- Authoritative: decisions treated as binding.
- Gauba cites the formula at PDF pp.96–99.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Easton's formulation, PDF pp.96–99 (✅ owner 07). Easton's book year not in owner file (⚠️ not stated here).
**Links:** PT-2038; PT-2035; PT-2036; PT-2043.
**Trap:** Easton's "authoritative" is not the same as "forceful"; binding decisions secure obedience through willing compliance, legitimacy, fear, or both (advanced, PDF pp.96–99).
**Mains hook:** Reserved seats and taxation show how authoritative allocation binds beneficiaries and contributors alike.
**Back:** *"Easton: politics = who gets what, binding because authorised."*

### Flashcard PT-2038 — Authority vs brute force (trap pair)
**Front:** How does authority differ from brute force in politics?
**Definition (learn):** Authoritative allocation secures obedience because the decision is treated as binding; simple violence is not itself a political solution.
**Breakdown:**
- Authority: binding by recognised rules; obedience by legitimacy or acceptance.
- Brute force: compulsion without a binding claim.
- Gauba: violence alone does not make politics.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.96–97, 99 (✅ owner 07). Advanced: compliance by legitimacy, fear or both (✅ owner 07 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2037; PT-2036; PT-2032 (Polity/Political Theory answer frames in other owners).
**Trap:** The option "politics is the exercise of force" is a ❌ pair; authority requires recognition.
**Mains hook:** A decision is political when it binds because it has authority, not merely because it can be enforced.
**Back:** *"Authority binds by recognition; force only compels."*

### Flashcard PT-2039 — Liberal view of politics: reconciliation of interests
**Front:** How does liberal theory read politics?
**Definition (learn):** Politics is group-and-state activity aimed at reconciling competing interests and promoting a common good compatible with them, leading to order, justice and welfare.
**Breakdown:**
- Image of society: plurality of groups and individuals with reconcilable interests.
- Mechanism: accommodation through legitimate rules and state-backed decisions; legitimate coercion.
- Outcome: order, justice and welfare.
- Liberal-pluralist strand traced to Locke-linked liberalism (Gauba).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.101–102 (✅ owner 07); liberal pluralism PDF pp.100–102 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** PT-2042; PT-2045; PT-2043 (Liberty); PT-2052 (neutrality objection).
**Trap:** Liberalism does speak of a common good, but as something worked out through accommodation (PDF p.102); "liberalism ignores common good" is a ❌ trap.
**Mains hook:** Liberalism treats politics as reconciliation; its limit is that compromise may not remove domination (Marxist critique, PT-2044).
**Back:** *"Liberal politics = reconcile many interests through just rules."*

### Flashcard PT-2040 — Marxist view of politics: class domination and suppression
**Front:** How does Marxist theory read politics?
**Definition (learn):** Politics is an effect of class division; the dominant class uses it to hold down the dependent class until class society is abolished.
**Breakdown:**
- Image of society: antagonistic classes structured by ownership and exploitation.
- Mechanism: state as organised power of the dominant class; compromise imposed within domination.
- End-point: revolution, then classless society where politics withers away.
- Marx and Engels: politics and the state arise from class division (PDF pp.102–104).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.102–104 (✅ owner 07); end-point PDF pp.104, 107 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** PT-2042; PT-2044; PT-2056 (book-period USSR/China); PT-2043 (Equality).
**Trap:** Marxism does not treat every visible compromise as genuine reconciliation; it reads compromise as often imposed (PDF pp.103–106).
**Mains hook:** The Marxist answer to "can politics end?" is withering-away; Gauba adds caution about subtle dominance.
**Back:** *"Marx: politics = class domination until the classes disappear."*

### Flashcard PT-2041 — Communitarian view of politics: embedded self and common good
**Front:** How does communitarian theory read politics?
**Definition (learn):** Persons are socially constituted, owe duties to the community and seek their good through the good of the community; politics pursues the common good through cooperation and belonging.
**Breakdown:**
- Image of society: interconnected persons whose identity is formed within community.
- Nature of politics: identification and pursuit of the common good.
- Outcome: social solidarity, cooperation, belonging.
- Organising ideal: fraternity (PT-2043).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.101, 104–107 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** PT-2042; PT-2045; PT-2043; PT-2047; PT-2048; PT-2049.
**Trap:** Communitarianism is not total unanimity or denial of real tensions; Gauba says it is futile where disparities of power and opinion persist (PDF pp.106–107).
**Mains hook:** Communitarianism gives a shared-purpose reading of politics; the test is whether it can handle real power inequality.
**Back:** *"Communitarian politics = the good of the whole, shared through belonging."*

### Flashcard PT-2042 — Three rival images of society: individual, class, community
**Front:** Which image of society underlies the liberal, Marxist and communitarian views?
**Definition (learn):** Liberalism imagines a plurality of groups (reconcilable); Marxism imagines antagonistic classes; communitarianism imagines an ethically connected community.
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal: unit = individual-in-groups; conflict limited.
- Marxist: unit = class; conflict deep until class society ends.
- Communitarian: unit = community-embedded person; no basic conflict within a true community.
- Advanced: identify the image of society before discussing institutions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.100–107 (✅ owner 07); comparative matrix PDF p.107 (✅ owner 07 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2039; PT-2040; PT-2041; PT-2043.
**Trap:** Mixing unit of analysis: liberal reconciliation is not Marxist suppression, and both differ from communitarian pursuit of the common good.
**Mains hook:** The first move in any answer on the nature of politics is naming the social ontology.
**Back:** *"Individuals reconcile; classes clash; communities share."*

### Flashcard PT-2043 — Liberty, equality, fraternity: the organising-ideal matrix
**Front:** Which organising ideal does Gauba link to each view?
**Definition (learn):** Gauba's synoptic table aligns liberalism with liberty, Marxism with equality, and communitarianism with fraternity.
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal: liberty; end-point order, justice, welfare.
- Marxist: equality; end-point classless society.
- Communitarian: fraternity; end-point social solidarity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.107 (✅ owner 07; advanced ✅ owner 07 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2042; PT-2039; PT-2040; PT-2041; PT-2058 (fraternity caution).
**Trap:** Do not map fraternity to liberalism or liberty to communitarianism; the matrix is one-to-one as Gauba presents it.
**Mains hook:** Use the three ideals as a conceptual frame for social cohesion rather than three slogans.
**Back:** *"Liberty for liberals, equality for Marxists, fraternity for communitarians."*

### Flashcard PT-2044 — Compromise vs cooperation (Marxist critique of liberalism)
**Front:** What is the Marxist objection to liberal compromise?
**Definition (learn):** Marxists accuse liberalism of conflating negotiated compromise with genuine cooperation; compromise can occur without removing domination, whereas cooperation requires freedom from dominance and dependence.
**Breakdown:**
- Compromise: negotiated settlement within existing power relations.
- Cooperation: removal of dominance and dependence.
- Source: Miliband discussion, via Gauba (PDF pp.105–106).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.105–106 (✅ owner 07; advanced ✅ owner 07 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2036; PT-2040; PT-2039.
**Trap:** Do not claim Gauba endorses the Marxist view as final; he records it as a criticism.
**Mains hook:** Liberal bargaining may manage antagonism without ending it; the answer should test which kind of settlement is at stake.
**Back:** *"Compromise is a truce; cooperation needs freedom from domination."*

### Flashcard PT-2045 — Liberal vs communitarian common good (trap pair)
**Front:** How does the liberal common good differ from the communitarian common good?
**Definition (learn):** Liberals see a common good emerging through reconciling interests (an equilibrium); communitarians see a shared good prior to isolated self-interest, through which persons discover their own good.
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal: balanced outcome accommodating contending interests (PDF p.102).
- Communitarian: shared good comprehending each member's interest (PDF p.107).
- Liberal = equilibrium; communitarian = shared purpose.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.102, 104–105, 107 (✅ owner 07 and advanced).
**Links:** PT-2039; PT-2041; PT-2048; PT-2049.
**Trap:** Liberalism is not "no common good"; the difference is where the good comes from.
**Mains hook:** The contrast is between common good as result (liberal) and common good as starting point (communitarian).
**Back:** *"Liberal common good = result of accommodation; communitarian = starting point of belonging."*

### Flashcard PT-2046 — Aristotle and political association
**Front:** What does Aristotle contribute to this chapter?
**Definition (learn):** Politics begins with the polis; the communitarian intuition is that political association is necessary for a good life and that the individual is incomplete apart from the political whole.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: polis as starting point (PDF p.95).
- Revived later by communitarian strands (PDF p.104).
- Individual incomplete apart from political whole (advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.95, 104 (✅ owner 07 and advanced).
**Links:** PT-2041; PT-2050 (Rousseau); PT-2051 (Green).
**Trap:** Aristotle is not a modern communitarian; he is an early source of the intuition.
**Mains hook:** Use Aristotle to show that the communitarian claim has ancient roots, while noting modern differences.
**Back:** *"Aristotle: man is a political animal; the polis completes the good life."*

### Flashcard PT-2047 — Alasdair MacIntyre: practices, internal goods, tradition (After Virtue)
**Front:** What is MacIntyre's communitarian mechanism?
**Definition (learn):** The self is intelligible only within the narrative of a community and moral tradition; virtues are acquired through "practices" with internal standards of excellence.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *After Virtue*, first published 1981 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Practice: activity with internal standards (e.g. medicine, craft, scholarship).
- Internal goods: excellence achieved through the activity itself.
- Limit: relativism risk; how to judge rival traditions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1981 ✅ (Wikipedia, fetched); owner-flagged (⚠️ owner 07 §13). Gauba mention PDF pp.101, 105 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** PT-2048; PT-2049; PT-2041.
**Trap:** Do not present MacIntyre as a generic "community matters" thinker; his mechanism is practices and internal goods.
**Mains hook:** MacIntyre argues that morality is always reasoning within an inherited tradition; the answer must address the relativism objection.
**Back:** *"MacIntyre 1981: virtue lives inside practices and their inherited traditions."*

### Flashcard PT-2048 — Charles Taylor: dialogical self and recognition
**Front:** What is Taylor's communitarian claim?
**Definition (learn):** The self is dialogical: identity is formed through language and relationships with significant others, and depends partly on adequate recognition by others.
**Breakdown:**
- Works: *Sources of the Self* (1989); "The Politics of Recognition" (1994) (⚠️ owner-flagged, standard).
- Mechanism: misrecognition inflicts real harm; grounds recognition of minority cultures.
- Limit: how far recognition can be accommodated without flattening disagreement or fragmenting citizenship.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates 1989/1994 ⚠️ (owner 07 §13, not fetched). Gauba names Taylor PDF p.105 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** PT-2047; PT-2049; PT-2045.
**Trap:** Taylor is not Sandel; the recognition thesis is his, not the unencumbered-self critique.
**Mains hook:** Taylor's politics of recognition is a moral case for community, not merely a sociological one.
**Back:** *"Taylor: identity is dialogical; misrecognition wounds."*

### Flashcard PT-2049 — Michael Sandel: the unencumbered self
**Front:** What does Sandel target in Rawls, and in which work?
**Definition (learn):** Sandel targets Rawls's original position and veil of ignorance, calling the chooser the "unencumbered self", detached from its ends and attachments.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *Liberalism and the Limits of Justice*, first appeared 1982; second edition 1998 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Claim: persons are constituted by their ends and attachments.
- Consequence: justice cannot be derived from a purely neutral standpoint.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1982 ✅ (fetched); owner PDF p.105 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** PT-2053 (reply); PT-2047; PT-2048.
**Trap:** Sandel's "unencumbered" is a critique of the self in Rawls, not a claim that Rawls believes persons are literally unattached.
**Mains hook:** Use Sandel to show that the liberal device behind the veil can be read as a metaphysical claim.
**Back:** *"Sandel 1982: the Rawlsian self is unencumbered — and that is the problem."*

### Flashcard PT-2050 — Rousseau as a cautious communitarian precursor ⚠️
**Front:** Why does Gauba count Rousseau among communitarian indications?
**Definition (learn):** Because of his stress on the general will and civic unity over private or particular interest; the common good is prior to and different from the sum of private wills.
**Breakdown:**
- Gauba: PDF pp.101, 107 (✅ owner 07).
- Classification caution: Rousseau is more properly social-contract/popular sovereignty theory (⚠️ owner 07 §14; see PT-2095 area in Sovereignty).
- Use as precursor only.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged precursor (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-2046; PT-2051; PT-2041.
**Trap:** Do not call Rousseau a card-carrying contemporary communitarian; this is an overclaim.
**Mains hook:** The general-will anticipation supports the common-good claim, but its tension with individual liberty must be discussed.
**Back:** *"Rousseau: general will before private will — a precursor, not a communitarian."*

### Flashcard PT-2051 — T.H. Green as idealist liberal precursor ⚠️
**Front:** How is T.H. Green a precursor of communitarian thought?
**Definition (learn):** Green's idealist liberalism ties individual self-realisation to membership in a moral community and to the common good, rather than to isolated possession of rights; he remains inside liberalism.
**Breakdown:**
- Gauba: precursor by linking self-realisation to social membership (PDF p.101, ✅ owner 07 advanced).
- Boundary: liberal tradition, not communitarian proper (⚠️ owner 07 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-2050; PT-2039; PT-2041.
**Trap:** Overstating Green as communitarian is a taxonomic overclaim.
**Mains hook:** Green shows that liberal thought already contained a social conception of the self.
**Back:** *"Green: self-realisation inside a moral community, still a liberal."*

### Flashcard PT-2052 — Objection-reply: liberal neutrality and the autonomous self
**Front:** Why do communitarians say liberal neutrality is impossible, and how do liberals reply?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: liberal politics presupposes and promotes the autonomous rights-bearing chooser while claiming neutrality. Reply (Rawls-style): neutrality is a political device for fair terms of cooperation among people who reasonably disagree; it does not claim that no view of the self is presupposed.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: presupposed self, not neutral.
- Reply: procedural fairness across competing comprehensive doctrines.
- Status: ongoing, unresolved debate.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged chain (⚠️ owner 07 §15).
**Links:** PT-2039; PT-2049; PT-2053.
**Trap:** Present as contested, not as a settled winner.
**Mains hook:** A balanced answer shows both the presupposition objection and the procedural reply.
**Back:** *"Neutral? Communitarians say no; Rawls says fair terms, not a full self."*

### Flashcard PT-2053 — Objection-reply: Sandel's charge and the heuristic veil
**Front:** What is the standard liberal reply to Sandel's unencumbered-self charge?
**Definition (learn):** The veil of ignorance is a heuristic for identifying fair principles under reasonable disagreement, not a claim that actual persons are unencumbered; only the choice of basic principles is modelled behind the veil.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection (Sandel): theory rests on a metaphysically false self.
- Reply: methodological device, not metaphysics.
- Counter-reply: even as heuristic, the device smuggles in an individualist picture of practical reasoning.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged (⚠️ owner 07 §15).
**Links:** PT-2049; PT-2052; PT-2032 (answer chain).
**Trap:** Sandel's critique can be confused with a claim that Rawls endorses atomism; check the heuristic/metaphysical distinction.
**Mains hook:** Show both moves: the heuristic reply and the second-order communitarian rejoinder.
**Back:** *"Veil = method, not metaphysics — but Sandel asks whether method hides a self."*

### Flashcard PT-2054 — Gandhian resonance: village community and trusteeship (analogy) ⚠️
**Front:** What Indian parallel does the owner draw to communitarian thought?
**Definition (learn):** Gandhi's gram sabha or village-community emphasis, trusteeship and duty-based swaraj are a commonly drawn analogy to communitarian stress on embedded selfhood over atomised individualism.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: analogy, not a claim that Gandhi was an academic communitarian.
- Cross-check: Gandhi material in `Political-Theory/basic/05_...` (outside this card's source base).
- Do not import unverified detail here.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-flagged analogy (⚠️ owner 07 §16).
**Links:** PT-2041; PT-2057; PT-2058.
**Trap:** Do not present Gandhi as an academic communitarian; use analogy only.
**Mains hook:** Use the analogy to explain why Indian thinkers often reject individualism without endorsing forced unanimity.
**Back:** *"Gandhi's village is a moral community — an analogy, not a label."*

### Flashcard PT-2055 — Trap: communitarianism is not forced unanimity
**Front:** Does communitarianism deny all conflict or require unanimity?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba presents communitarianism as stressing common interest and cooperation; he holds that it is futile where wide disparities of power and opinion persist.
**Breakdown:**
- Common interest: stressed.
- Conflict: Gauba says no basic conflict within a true community (advanced PDF pp.106–107), not that inequality disappears.
- Boundary: not a defence of suppressing dissent.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.106–107 (✅ owner 07; advanced ✅ owner 07 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2041; PT-2058; PT-2057.
**Trap:** "Communitarianism means total unanimity" and "Gauba endorses suppression of dissent" are both ❌.
**Mains hook:** A strong answer separates communitarian common good from coercive conformity.
**Back:** *"Community is not conformity; the common good is not a muzzle."*

### Flashcard PT-2056 — Trap: Marxist withering away and book-period evidence
**Front:** Can Marxism's classless-society promise be treated as achieved, using Gauba?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba notes the historical difficulty of realising the classless society, citing the experience of socialist revolutions such as the former USSR and China, as book-period perspective.
**Breakdown:**
- USSR: 1917 (✅ owner 07 advanced, PDF p.106).
- PRC: 1949 (✅ owner 07 advanced).
- Status: historical illustrations, not live comparative data.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1917 and 1949 (✅ owner 07 advanced, PDF p.106).
**Links:** PT-2040; PT-2057; PT-2060.
**Trap:** Do not treat 20th-century socialist regimes as current evidence; the owner forbids unlabelled use.
**Mains hook:** Use Marxism's theory and its historical test separately; Gauba's caution is that politics may persist at subtle levels.
**Back:** *"Withering away is a theory; USSR 1917 and China 1949 are dated tests."*

### Flashcard PT-2057 — Scope conditions: liberalism, Marxism and communitarianism fit different societies
**Front:** Under what social conditions does Gauba say each view fits?
**Definition (learn):** Liberalism fits relatively moderate disparities; Marxism fits sharply polarised class societies; communitarianism fits societies ready for cooperation and social satisfaction.
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal: moderate disparities.
- Marxist: sharply polarised classes.
- Communitarian: cooperation-ready societies.
- Gauba: the views are condition-sensitive, not a linear progression (PDF p.106).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.106 (✅ owner 07 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2042; PT-2055; PT-2056.
**Trap:** Do not treat one view as a universal key or as a stage-model of progress.
**Mains hook:** The answer should match the theory to the social condition the question describes.
**Back:** *"Moderate gap: liberal. Polarised classes: Marxist. Ready for cooperation: communitarian."*

### Flashcard PT-2058 — Caution: fraternity detached from liberty and equality
**Front:** What is the advanced caution about the fraternity ideal?
**Definition (learn):** If fraternity is detached from liberty and equality, communitarian language can slide into conformity; if liberty is detached from fraternity, liberal politics can thin out social belonging.
**Breakdown:**
- Fraternity alone: risk of conformity.
- Liberty alone: risk of thin social belonging.
- Balance: the three ideals together.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner advanced caution (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §7).
**Links:** PT-2043; PT-2055; PT-2041.
**Trap:** Presenting fraternity as an independent good without liberty and equality invites the conformity objection.
**Mains hook:** Social cohesion needs liberty and equality as checks on fraternity.
**Back:** *"Fraternity without liberty becomes conformity; liberty without fraternity becomes isolation."*

### Flashcard PT-2059 — Application: one labour dispute, three analyses (advanced)
**Front:** How does each view analyse the same labour dispute?
**Definition (learn):** Liberal: what institutional bargain reconciles employer and worker? Marxist: which class controls production and how does the bargain reproduce domination? Communitarian: do workplace relations and social norms sustain a shared purpose or produce alienation?
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal question: institutional bargain.
- Marxist question: control of production and domination.
- Communitarian question: shared purpose vs alienation.
- Easton: distributive decisions bind unequal groups despite disagreement (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner application (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §9); no current-affairs data.
**Links:** PT-2039; PT-2040; PT-2041; PT-2037.
**Trap:** Do not answer the dispute from one view alone; the three questions expose different assumptions.
**Mains hook:** Use the three questions to structure a labour or reservation answer.
**Back:** *"Same dispute, three questions: bargain, control, belonging."*

### Flashcard PT-2060 — Politics as conflict, cooperation or power: answer discipline
**Front:** How should an answer on "is politics conflict-resolution or common-good pursuit?" be structured?
**Definition (learn):** Start with Gauba's public-conflict definition, then compare liberal, Marxist and communitarian answers; end with a graded conclusion tied to the social condition.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1: public-conflict definition (PT-2035/2036).
- Step 2: three images of society (PT-2042).
- Step 3: scope conditions (PT-2057).
- Step 4: graded verdict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner prompt and architecture (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §10; basic §17).
**Links:** PT-2042; PT-2057; PT-2032.
**Trap:** Describing institutions before identifying the social ontology weakens the answer.
**Mains hook:** A graded answer shows that politics can be read in more than one way depending on the question's social setting.
**Back:** *"Define the conflict, name the society, match the condition, then judge."*

### GROUP — 08 Approaches, Behaviouralism and Post-Behaviouralism (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard PT-2061 — Method vs approach (Van Dyke)
**Front:** How does a method differ from an approach?
**Definition (learn):** A method is the procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge is obtained; an approach is a wider frame that includes the method plus criteria for selecting problems, questions and relevant data.
**Breakdown:**
- Method: how to inquire (procedure, tools).
- Approach: how and what to inquire into (selection of problems and data).
- Van Dyke clarifies the distinction (PDF pp.108–109).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.108–109 (✅ owner 08); Van Dyke (✅ owner 08 advanced, PDF pp.108–109, 113).
**Links:** PT-2062; PT-2063; PT-2064.
**Trap:** Method and approach are not synonyms; approach is wider.
**Mains hook:** Methodological disputes are often prior to ideological ones; name the approach before evaluating the method.
**Back:** *"Method = how to look; approach = what to look at and why."*

### Flashcard PT-2062 — Traditional/contemporary is not normative/empirical
**Front:** Does "traditional" always mean normative and "contemporary" always mean empirical?
**Definition (learn):** No. Traditional and contemporary are historical labels for dominant styles of inquiry (pre- and post-Second World War), and they do not coincide with normative and empirical.
**Breakdown:**
