- Traditional: philosophy, history, law, institutions.
- Contemporary: behavioural, post-behavioural and analytical models.
- Gauba explicitly rejects the mechanical equation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.109–110 (✅ owner 08); advanced: "historical distinction, not a perfect facts-values binary" (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2061; PT-2063; PT-2087 (trap cases Aristotle, Marx, Rawls, Hayek ⚠️ prompt in advanced §10).
**Trap:** The ❌ option "traditional = normative; contemporary = empirical" is wrong per Gauba.
**Mains hook:** Show that a contemporary study can be normative and a classic can be empirical; the labels describe styles, not content.
**Back:** *"Traditional and contemporary are dates of style, not switches of fact versus value."*

### Flashcard PT-2063 — Empirical vs normative by content, not grammar
**Front:** How do you tell an empirical statement from a normative one?
**Definition (learn):** Empirical statements claim an observable situation that can be tested or verified by repeated observation; normative statements express a preference, duty or moral principle about what ought to be valued or prescribed.
**Breakdown:**
- Empirical: observable, testable.
- Normative: preferred order, moral principle, intrinsic value.
- Form vs content: "is" can be normative (justice definition); "ought" can be empirical (instrumental condition).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.110–112 (✅ owner 08); examples PDF p.110 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2062; PT-2061; PT-2081 (value-neutral trap); PT-2082.
**Trap:** "Any sentence with 'ought' is normative" is a ❌ misreading.
**Mains hook:** Test the content of a claim; the verb form does not settle the classification.
**Back:** *"Test the content: observable and testable is empirical; preferred or prescribed is normative."*

### Flashcard PT-2064 — Philosophical approach and Leo Strauss
**Front:** What does the philosophical approach do, and how does Strauss defend it?
**Definition (learn):** The philosophical approach clarifies concepts and evaluates institutions against standards of the good, right and just; Strauss defends political philosophy as inseparable from facts and values, and criticises value-free behaviouralism.
**Breakdown:**
- Van Dyke: philosophical analysis = conceptual clarification plus evaluation (PDF pp.108–109, 113).
- Strauss: knowledge of facts and standards of the good (PDF pp.112, 114, 121).
- Limit: cannot supply behavioural verification (⚠️ owner 08 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.112–114 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2061; PT-2078; PT-2080.
**Trap:** Strauss is a critic of behaviouralism's value-neutrality; do not cite him as a behaviouralist.
**Mains hook:** The philosophical approach gives standards; the question is whether standards need behavioural evidence.
**Back:** *"Philosophy asks what is right; Strauss asks what good politics is."*

### Flashcard PT-2065 — Historical approach
**Front:** What does the historical approach study?
**Definition (learn):** It studies either historical laws or processes, or the history of political thought in relation to its context.
**Breakdown:**
- Object: past events and texts (PDF pp.114–115).
- Strength: restores context and continuity.
- Risk: living on inherited ideas without new synthesis (PDF p.115).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.114–115 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2064; PT-2066; PT-2080.
**Trap:** Historical approach is not the same as the legal approach; history explains processes, law explains valid authority.
**Mains hook:** A historical approach shows why a political idea arose in its context.
**Back:** *"History restores context; its trap is repeating inherited ideas."*

### Flashcard PT-2066 — Legal approach
**Front:** What questions does the legal approach ask?
**Definition (learn):** It studies politics through law, the constitutional framework and legally valid authority, asking what laws and constitutions authorise.
**Breakdown:**
- Object: legal provisions, procedures, judgments (PDF pp.115–116).
- Strength: binding framework and validity.
- Limit: misses extra-legal behaviour and informal forces (PDF p.116).
- Indian example: what constitutional provisions authorise institutions (⚠️ owner 08 basic §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.115–116 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2067; PT-2064; PT-2061.
**Trap:** The legal approach is valuable, but it is incomplete for informal power.
**Mains hook:** A legal answer on India must add institutional and behavioural analysis to explain practice.
**Back:** *"Legal = what the rules authorise, not what people do."*

### Flashcard PT-2067 — Institutional approach
**Front:** What does the institutional approach map?
**Definition (learn):** It studies the organisation and functioning of government, parties and related institutions: legislature, executive, judiciary, parties and their formal relations.
**Breakdown:**
- Object: organs of government and parties (PDF pp.116–117).
- Strength: gives politics disciplinary identity (PDF p.116).
- Limit: over-description; neglects informal processes and individuals (PDF p.117).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.116–117 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2066; PT-2068; PT-2073 (systems); PT-2074.
**Trap:** Institutional description alone misses how people behave inside institutions.
**Mains hook:** Institutions tell you the formal map; behavioural analysis tells you how it moves.
**Back:** *"Institutional = who sits where; its trap is forgetting who acts."*

### Flashcard PT-2068 — Early forerunners of behaviouralism: Wallas, Bentley, Merriam, Lasswell, Catlin
**Front:** Who were the early precursors of the behavioural turn?
**Definition (learn):** Graham Wallas (psychology against an overly rational image of man), Arthur Bentley (informal group processes), Charles Merriam (scientific rigour, interdisciplinarity), Harold Lasswell and G.E.G. Catlin (value-free power analysis; politics as who gets what, when, how).
**Breakdown:**
- Wallas: attention to actual behaviour (PDF p.118).
- Bentley: group processes over institutions (PDF p.118).
- Merriam: scientific, interdisciplinary, policy-science orientation (PDF pp.118–119).
- Lasswell and Catlin: power analysis (PDF p.119).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.118–119 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2069; PT-2070; PT-2086.
**Trap:** Forerunners are not behaviouralists in the strict tenet sense; they shifted attention.
**Mains hook:** Behaviouralism has roots before the post-war movement.
**Back:** *"Wallas: psychology; Bentley: groups; Merriam: science; Lasswell: who gets what."*

### Flashcard PT-2069 — Easton's eight behavioural tenets (list)
**Front:** Name the eight tenets of behaviouralism in order.
**Definition (learn):** (1) Regularities; (2) Verification; (3) Techniques; (4) Quantification; (5) Values; (6) Systematisation; (7) Pure science; (8) Integration.
**Breakdown:**
- Sequence: observable regularity → testable verification → technique → quantification.
- Values: analytic separation of empirical from ethical (value-neutrality as method).
- Systematisation: theory and data interdependent.
- Pure science: explanation before application.
- Integration: borrowing from other social sciences.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Easton's "intellectual foundation stones" (✅ Wikipedia, fetched; ⚠️ owner 08 §13 notes a pedagogical summary, not verbatim). Owner: PDF pp.120–121 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2070; PT-2071; PT-2079; PT-2081.
**Trap:** Listing them as "Easton's tenets" is fine; quoting one verbatim sentence is not verified.
**Mains hook:** Name at least four and pair each with one illustration (quantification → voting surveys; integration → psychology in leadership studies).
**Back:** *"Really Value-Tested Quantified Systems Integrate Science: R-V-T-Q-V-S-P-I."*

### Flashcard PT-2070 — Tenet: Values (value neutrality as method, not indifference)
**Front:** What does the "Values" tenet of behaviouralism mean?
**Definition (learn):** An analytic separation between empirical explanation and ethical evaluation; value-neutrality is a method, not indifference.
**Breakdown:**
- Empirical explanation: what is.
- Ethical evaluation: what ought to be.
- Separation is analytical, not moral indifference.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §13 (⚠️ pedagogical tenet); Easton list (✅ Wikipedia). Owner PDF p.121 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2069; PT-2081; PT-2082; PT-2078.
**Trap:** Value-neutral (method) ≠ value-indifferent (ignoring consequences).
**Mains hook:** Behaviouralism's value-neutrality answers the question of method, not of responsibility; post-behaviouralism raised the second.
**Back:** *"Values tenet: separate explanation from evaluation; separate is not ignore."*

### Flashcard PT-2071 — Tenet: Pure science before application
**Front:** What does "pure science" mean in the behavioural tenets?
**Definition (learn):** Understanding and explanation of political behaviour is logically prior to its application in solving urgent social problems.
**Breakdown:**
- Priority: explanation first, application later.
- Critic: post-behaviouralism (PT-2077) argues that this priority left urgent problems unaddressed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §13 (⚠️ tenet); Easton list (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-2069; PT-2077; PT-2082.
**Trap:** "Pure science" is not "science for its own sake leads to activism"; it is the claimed order of priority.
**Mains hook:** Pure-science priority is the point of contention that post-behaviouralism reopens.
**Back:** *"Pure science: explain first, apply later — the order post-behaviouralism questioned."*

### Flashcard PT-2072 — David Easton: systems analysis
**Front:** What is Easton's systems model?
**Definition (learn):** The political system is an open system in a wider environment, processing inputs (demands and supports) through a conversion process (legislatures, parties, bureaucracy) into outputs (policies, decisions), generating feedback that reshapes future demands and supports.
**Breakdown:**
- Inputs: demands and supports.
- Conversion: legislatures, parties, bureaucracy.
- Outputs: policies, decisions.
- Feedback: reshapes demands and supports.
- Stability: supports (loyalty, legitimacy, taxes, compliance).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §15 (⚠️ reconstruction); Gauba PDF pp.122–126 (✅ owner 08 advanced) — "systems analysis" listed PDF pp.125–126 for weakness.
**Links:** PT-2073; PT-2074; PT-2075; PT-2076; PT-2080.
**Trap:** Systems analysis is weak on power distribution and mass behaviour such as voting (✅ owner 08 advanced PDF pp.125–126).
**Mains hook:** Use systems analysis for cross-polity comparison; add power analysis for conflict.
**Back:** *"Easton systems: inputs → conversion → outputs → feedback."*

### Flashcard PT-2073 — Gabriel Almond: structural-functional approach
**Front:** What functions does Almond identify for every political system?
**Definition (learn):** Every political system performs universal functions — political socialisation and recruitment, interest articulation, interest aggregation, rule-making, rule-application, rule-adjudication, and communication — performed by different structures in different systems.
**Breakdown:**
- Example: interest articulation via parties in one system; via kinship or patron-client networks in another.
- Strength: compares a parliamentary democracy and a chiefdom on the same functional grid.
- Developing polities: widens comparison beyond formal constitutions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §15 (⚠️ reconstruction); Gauba PDF pp.127–129 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2072; PT-2074; PT-2083 (Western benchmark objection); PT-2090.
**Trap:** Almond's functions are fixed categories; structures vary. Do not invert.
**Mains hook:** Informal structures perform functions in many systems; compare by function, not label.
**Back:** *"Almond: same functions, different structures."*

### Flashcard PT-2074 — Karl Deutsch: communication and cybernetic approach
**Front:** What is Deutsch's cybernetic model of politics?
**Definition (learn):** Government functions as a steering mechanism; decision-making depends on the flow of information through communication channels, and feedback loops correct the system's course toward its goals.
**Breakdown:**
- Concepts: information load, lag, gain and lead (owner §15).
- Strength: learning, error-correction, adaptive capacity.
- Limit: mechanistic metaphor understates power, ideology and class conflict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.129–131 (✅ owner 08 advanced); reconstruction (⚠️ owner 08 §15).
**Links:** PT-2072; PT-2073; PT-2075.
**Trap:** Cybernetics here is a communication model, not a claim that politics is machinery.
**Mains hook:** Use Deutsch for bureaucratic responsiveness and policy feedback; add power analysis to explain conflict.
**Back:** *"Deutsch: government as steering through information and feedback."*

### Flashcard PT-2075 — Decision-making approach
**Front:** What does the decision-making approach explain?
**Definition (learn):** Outcomes are explained by identifying decision-makers, alternatives before them, and the information, values and organisational setting that shape the choice.
**Breakdown:**
- Stages (foreign-policy analysis): problem recognition, search for alternatives, evaluation, choice, implementation.
- Strength: explains why one alternative was chosen.
- Limit: narrow to the moment of decision; elite bias.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §15 (⚠️ reconstruction); Gauba PDF pp.131–133 (✅ owner 08 advanced, approximate range).
**Links:** PT-2072; PT-2074; PT-2076; PT-2089.
**Trap:** Decision-making is not the same as systems analysis; it focuses on choosers, not outputs.
**Mains hook:** Trace a specific choice through its stages and organisational setting.
**Back:** *"Decision-making: who chose, what options, under which setting."*

### Flashcard PT-2076 — Marxian approach: superstructure and base
**Front:** How does the Marxian approach analyse politics?
**Definition (learn):** Politics has no autonomous existence; institutions and ideas form a superstructure resting on the economic base (mode of production and class relations); the state in class society is an instrument of the dominant class.
**Breakdown:**
- Base: mode of production.
- Superstructure: law, bureaucracy, ideology.
- Method: who owns and controls the means of production.
- Limit: reductionist; struggles with variation among capitalist states; revolutionary predictions contested.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.133–134 (✅ owner 08 advanced); owner §15 (⚠️ reconstruction).
**Links:** PT-2040; PT-2072; PT-2080.
**Trap:** Marxian analysis refuses autonomy of politics; systems analysis does not. Keep distinct.
**Mains hook:** The Marxian lens exposes hidden class interest in neutral institutions; its limit is explanatory reductionism.
**Back:** *"Marx: base decides, superstructure reflects, state serves the dominant class."*

### Flashcard PT-2077 — Post-behaviouralism: relevance and action (Easton's correction)
**Front:** What two demands does post-behaviouralism add to behaviouralism?
**Definition (learn):** Relevance (political science must address urgent public problems: war, poverty, discrimination, injustice) and action (intellectuals must apply knowledge for social betterment).
**Breakdown:**
- Keeps: rigour, verification, technique.
- Adds: relevance and action.
- Corrective within science, not a licence for untested moralism.
- Easton's internal self-correction (⚠️ owner §14; owner advanced PDF pp.121–125).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.121–122 (✅ owner 08). Easton 1969 post-behavioural intervention per owner (✅ owner 08 advanced §12; not confirmed by Wikipedia, which gives no year).
**Links:** PT-2069; PT-2070; PT-2071; PT-2082.
**Trap:** Post-behaviouralism is not abandoning science for activism.
**Mains hook:** Relevance without disciplined evidence collapses into moralism.
**Back:** *"Post-behaviouralism: keep the science, add relevance and action."*

### Flashcard PT-2078 — Leo Strauss criticises value-free behaviouralism
**Front:** How does Strauss criticise behaviouralism?
**Definition (learn):** Strauss reasserts political philosophy as knowledge of facts and standards of the good, and rejects a value-free political science.
**Breakdown:**
- Political philosophy inseparable from facts and values (PDF p.112).
- Criticises value-free behaviouralism (PDF pp.114, 121).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.112, 114, 121 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2064; PT-2070; PT-2080.
**Trap:** Strauss is not a post-behaviouralist; he is a critic from philosophy.
**Mains hook:** Use Strauss to show the value-free aspiration is itself contested.
**Back:** *"Strauss: no value-free politics; philosophy must judge the good."*

### Flashcard PT-2079 — Bernard Crick's critique of the American science of politics (1959)
**Front:** Which 1959 critique challenged behaviouralism?
**Definition (learn):** Bernard Crick's *The American Science of Politics* (1959) challenged six basic premises of the approach.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: 1959 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched, as stated on the behavioralism page).
- Premises: six basic premises challenged (page-stated count).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1959 ✅ (Wikipedia, fetched). Not in owner files (⚠️ owner-absent).
**Links:** PT-2069; PT-2078; PT-2077.
**Trap:** Do not attribute this critique to Easton; it is Crick's.
**Mains hook:** The critique of behaviouralism started inside the discipline, not only from outside.
**Back:** *"Crick 1959: the American science of politics, challenged."*

### Flashcard PT-2080 — Comparative matrix of the five analytical models
**Front:** Compare systems, structural-functional, communications, decision-making and Marxian approaches.
**Definition (learn):** Each model highlights a different variable: stability (Easton), function (Almond), communication (Deutsch), choice (decision-making), class (Marxian).
**Breakdown:**
- Systems: unit = inputs-conversion-outputs-feedback; strength = cross-system comparison; weak on power.
- Structural-functional: unit = functions; strength = informal structures; weak = conservative bias.
- Communication: unit = information flow; strength = learning; weak = understates power.
- Decision-making: unit = decision-makers; strength = specific choices; weak = elite bias.
- Marxian: unit = base and class; strength = hidden class interest; weak = reductionism.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 table (⚠️ owner 08 basic); Gauba PDF pp.122–134 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2072–PT-2076.
**Trap:** No single model is "correct"; choose by question.
**Mains hook:** Match the model to the variable the question asks about.
**Back:** *"Five lenses: stability, function, feedback, choice, class — none is the master key."*

### Flashcard PT-2081 — Trap: value-neutral vs value-indifferent
**Front:** What is the difference between value-neutral and value-indifferent inquiry?
**Definition (learn):** Value-neutral: a method that separates empirical explanation from ethical evaluation. Value-indifferent: ignoring consequences of what one chooses to study. Behaviouralism aimed at the first; post-behaviouralism criticised the second.
**Breakdown:**
- Neutral = method (PT-2070).
- Indifferent = irresponsibility, a charge post-behaviouralism raised.
- Behavioural inquiry can shape what is studied and ignored (PDF pp.121–122).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner advanced §4 (⚠️ synthesis); Gauba PDF pp.121–122 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2070; PT-2077; PT-2082.
**Trap:** "Value-free means politically consequence-free" is a ❌ trap.
**Mains hook:** Neutral method does not excuse indifference to consequences.
**Back:** *"Neutral method is not indifferent politics."*

### Flashcard PT-2082 — Objection-reply: value-neutrality and complicity
**Front:** What is the objection to behaviouralism's value-neutrality, and the post-behavioural reply?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: choosing what to study or ignore is value-laden, so value-neutral political science is complicit in ignoring injustice. Reply: post-behaviouralism keeps verification and rigour; action is added, not substituted for evidence standards.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: complicity by omission.
- Reply: relevance + evidence.
- Status: presented as balanced debate (⚠️ owner 08 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.121–122 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2077; PT-2081; PT-2070.
**Trap:** The reply does not license unsupported moralism.
**Mains hook:** Good answers show the cost of neutral silence without abandoning disciplined evidence.
**Back:** *"Complicity objection: fix by relevance, not by dropping evidence."*

### Flashcard PT-2083 — Objection-reply: structural-functional Western benchmark
**Front:** What is the objection to structural-functionalism, and the reply?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: comparative studies measure non-Western polities against an implicit Western liberal-democratic template and label divergence as underdevelopment. Reply: the functional categories are designed to be filled by any structure, so the bias is a failure of application.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: implicit benchmark; conservative bias.
- Reply: function-neutral categories.
- Critics: correction is inconsistently applied (⚠️ owner 08 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.129 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2073; PT-2080.
**Trap:** Do not resolve the debate as settled.
**Mains hook:** Use the debate to test any comparative claim about developing polities.
**Back:** *"Almond's function is neutral; its application often is not."*

### Flashcard PT-2084 — Trap: Marxian predictions are book-period theory
**Front:** Should Marxian revolutionary predictions be treated as settled fact?
**Definition (learn):** No. The historical predictions about revolutionary transition remain contested and should be treated as book-period theoretical claims.
**Breakdown:**
- Marxian reductionism: denies institutions independent weight.
- Variation: capitalist states with similar economies differ.
- Caution: owner notes PDF pp.133–134.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §15 (⚠️ owner 08 basic); Gauba PDF pp.133–134 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2076; PT-2056 (USSR and China book-period); PT-2080.
**Trap:** Do not claim the Marxian model has been validated or refuted by history.
**Mains hook:** Use the Marxian lens for hidden class interest; test its predictions against evidence.
**Back:** *"Marxian prediction is theory, not a dated result."*

### Flashcard PT-2085 — Application: one university election through six approaches (advanced)
**Front:** How would the six approaches analyse the same university election?
**Definition (learn):** Philosophical: what fair representation requires. Historical: how the institution evolved. Legal: rules, eligibility, procedures. Institutional: offices, committees, roles. Behavioural: turnout, attitudes, campaigning, peer influence. Post-behavioural: does knowledge help solve exclusion or distrust. Systems and decision-making: inputs/outputs; who chose what.
**Breakdown:**
- Each approach asks a different question.
- Same case, different variables.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner application (⚠️ owner 08 advanced §9); no real case data.
**Links:** PT-2061; PT-2066; PT-2067; PT-2069; PT-2072; PT-2075.
**Trap:** Do not claim one approach captures the election; the answer should switch lenses explicitly.
**Mains hook:** Method pluralism is an answer strategy, not a weakness.
**Back:** *"One election, six questions — switch lens, keep rigour."*

### Flashcard PT-2086 — The behavioural revolution: origin of the term and Easton's role
**Front:** Who coined and popularised "behaviouralism", and when did it peak?
**Definition (learn):** Dwight Waldo probably coined the word in a 1956 book; Easton popularised it and first distinguished it from behaviourism (cited to his 1953 work). Gained support from 1942 through the 1970s and peaked in the 1960s and 1970s.
**Breakdown:**
- Term: Waldo (1956, probable) → Easton (popularised).
- Easton's 1953 work cited on distinction.
- Almond extended approach into comparative politics in 1954–63.
- Post-war American movement (Gauba, PDF pp.118–121).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Waldo 1956 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched, "probably"); Easton 1953 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched); Almond 1954–63 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched). Owner dates not given for these (⚠️ owner-absent).
**Links:** PT-2068; PT-2069; PT-2073; PT-2077.
**Trap:** "Behaviouralism" and "behaviourism" are different; the first is a discipline's movement, the second a psychological theory.
**Mains hook:** Behavioural revolution dates to the post-war American period; the term's history is contested.
**Back:** *"Waldo coined, Easton popularised, the 1960s–70s peaked."*

### Flashcard PT-2087 — Application: Indian decision-making analysis (⚠️)
**Front:** How can the decision-making approach be applied to an Indian policy choice?
**Definition (learn):** Trace a Cabinet or Cabinet Committee decision from problem recognition to implementation, without asserting unverified specifics.
**Breakdown:**
- Stages: problem recognition, search, evaluation, choice, implementation.
- Use: as a type of analysis, not as claims about a real decision.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner application (⚠️ owner 08 basic §18).
**Links:** PT-2075; PT-2089.
**Trap:** Avoid unverified claims about any named decision.
**Mains hook:** Use the stages to organise the answer.
**Back:** *"Decision in India: problem to choice to implementation."*

### Flashcard PT-2088 — Application: Indian structural-functional interest articulation (⚠️)
**Front:** How can structural-functionalism compare India's formal and informal interest articulation?
**Definition (learn):** Compare formal parliamentary structures with informal channels (caste associations, kinship networks, patron-client ties) performing the same function in different areas.
**Breakdown:**
- Function: interest articulation.
- Structures: formal vs informal.
- Use as a type of comparison, not with unverified statistics.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner application (⚠️ owner 08 basic §18).
**Links:** PT-2073; PT-2083.
**Trap:** Do not use unverified figures for caste associations.
**Mains hook:** Informal structures are functional, not merely pre-modern.
**Back:** *"Almond in India: parties and caste networks both articulate interests."*

### Flashcard PT-2089 — Application: systems model and a farmers' agitation (⚠️)
**Front:** How can the systems model trace a public demand in India?
**Definition (learn):** A sustained demand (e.g. a farmers' agitation) moves through input-conversion-output-feedback into policy.
**Breakdown:**
- Input: demand.
- Conversion: legislature, parties, bureaucracy.
- Output: policy.
- Feedback: new demands.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner application (⚠️ owner 08 basic §18); no specific outcome asserted.
**Links:** PT-2072; PT-2075.
**Trap:** Do not assert any specific outcome without verification.
**Mains hook:** The systems model shows persistence; add power analysis to explain conflict.
**Back:** *"Easton on the farm: demand in, policy out, feedback back."*

### Flashcard PT-2090 — Trap: post-behaviouralism is not a licence for activism
**Front:** Does post-behaviouralism abandon science for activism?
**Definition (learn):** No. Easton presents it as retaining behavioural gains while reconnecting them to problem-solving.
**Breakdown:**
- Retains: rigour, verification, technique.
- Adds: relevance and action.
- Risk: vagueness if relevance is asserted without discipline.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.121–122 (✅ owner 08); advanced §7 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2077; PT-2082.
**Trap:** Post-behavioural ≠ untested moralism.
**Mains hook:** Relevance needs analytical discipline.
**Back:** *"Post-behavioural: science with a conscience, not a sermon."*

### Flashcard PT-2091 — Criticisms of behaviouralism: micro-focus and value distance
**Front:** What criticisms does Gauba record against behaviouralism?
**Definition (learn):** It privileges reliable micro-level questions over broader but less tractable questions; it is method-heavy, micro-biased and value-neutral in a way that distances it from urgent crises.
**Breakdown:**
- Micro-focus: reliable but narrow.
- Method-fetish: method over relevance.
- Distance: from war, dictatorship, nuclear threat, crises (PDF pp.121–122).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.121–122 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2077; PT-2069; PT-2081.
**Trap:** Criticism is not rejection; post-behaviouralism keeps the gains.
**Mains hook:** Behaviouralism's gains are real; its limits are about questions asked.
**Back:** *"Behaviouralism: precise on small questions, silent on big ones."*

### GROUP — 09 Interdisciplinary Political Analysis (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard PT-2092 — Interdisciplinary approach: definition and three functions
**Front:** What is the interdisciplinary approach, and what does it do for political science?
**Definition (learn):** Use of more than one discipline to study a problem, especially where common or overlapping phenomena are involved.
**Breakdown:**
- Function 1: use other disciplines' findings.
- Function 2: verify political theories through them.
- Function 3: contribute back to those disciplines.
- Social sciences are especially suited because they study overlapping aspects of human society.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.136–137 (✅ owner 09); advanced §3 (✅ owner 09 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2093; PT-2094; PT-2123.
**Trap:** Interdisciplinary is not free borrowing; the purpose must be political understanding.
**Mains hook:** Justify interdisciplinarity by its three functions, not by a list of subject names.
**Back:** *"Borrow, verify, give back — always for a political question."*

### Flashcard PT-2093 — Purposeful vs random borrowing (trap)
**Front:** Must interdisciplinary borrowing be purposeful?
**Definition (learn):** Yes. Gauba warns that expansion across disciplines must be well-conceived; labour relations or crowd psychology matter only where they illuminate political outcomes.
**Breakdown:**
- Purposeful: serves a political question.
- Random: borrowing for its own sake.
- Test: does the borrowed evidence change the political explanation?
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.137 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
**Links:** PT-2092; PT-2094; PT-2109.
**Trap:** "Broader scope" is permitted only while the focus stays political (advanced §4 ❌ row).
**Mains hook:** The discipline is defined by its question, not by its borrowed tools.
**Back:** *"Borrow for the question; never for the sake of borrowing."*

### Flashcard PT-2094 — Integration without merger (trap)
**Front:** Does interdisciplinarity dissolve political theory into economics or sociology?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba concludes that integration keeps politics central; single-discipline approaches are partial, but merger into one master science is neither the aim nor the result.
**Breakdown:**
- Integration: relevant disciplines combined.
- Merger: rejected.
- Organising centre: the political question — who gets valued things, through what authoritative process.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.146 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
**Links:** PT-2092; PT-2116; PT-2123.
**Trap:** ❌ "Interdisciplinary = dissolving political theory" is a wrong option.
**Mains hook:** Argue for integration without merger: the political question keeps identity while evidence crosses boundaries.
**Back:** *"Integrate the evidence; keep the question; never merge the discipline."*

### Flashcard PT-2095 — Empirical approach: facts, explanation, prediction
**Front:** What does the empirical approach do?
**Definition (learn):** It focuses on observable facts, explanation, correlation, generalisation and prediction.
**Breakdown:**
- Object: observable facts.
- Tools: correlation, generalisation, prediction.
- Contrast: normative approach focuses on values, prescriptions and what ought to be done (PDF p.138).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.138 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** PT-2063 (empirical vs normative by content); PT-2096; PT-2105.
**Trap:** Empirical is not "contemporary" by definition (see PT-2062).
**Mains hook:** The empirical push displaced ideal-state speculation and forced borrowing from other sciences.
**Back:** *"Empirical: observe, explain, predict; normative: value, prescribe."*

### Flashcard PT-2096 — History: verification through time, and "history is past politics"
**Front:** What does history contribute, and what older formula does Gauba reject?
**Definition (learn):** History supplies verification through past events, sequences and long-term causation; the formula "history is past politics" is an oversimplification.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: test theory against historical cases (rule-and-exception).
- Limit: history alone can become chronicle.
- Political science has an independent identity now.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.141 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Links:** PT-2095; PT-2108; PT-2065.
**Trap:** ❌ "History and political science are the same" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** History verifies a theory of democracy or state formation, but it does not settle present moral worth.
**Back:** *"History checks claims over time; it is not politics told in the past tense."*

### Flashcard PT-2097 — Political economy: production, distribution and political conflict
**Front:** What is political economy and what does it contribute?
**Definition (learn):** Analysis of the political aspects of economic policy-making and of the mutual impact of economic forces and political decisions.
**Breakdown:**
- Contributions: production, distribution, exchange, scarcity, economic conflict (PDF pp.142–143).
- Uses: welfare state, class conflict, planning, development.
- Limit: cannot alone settle legitimacy or justice.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.142–144 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** PT-2107; PT-2109; PT-2119.
**Trap:** Economics explains mechanisms; political economy explains political conflict over them. Do not confuse the two.
**Mains hook:** Use political economy to show why economic facts alone do not decide political outcomes.
**Back:** *"Political economy: who gains from economic decisions, and how politics decides."*

### Flashcard PT-2098 — Political sociology and Lipset on political development
**Front:** What does political sociology study, and what does Lipset add?
**Definition (learn):** Study of political institutions, processes and ideologies in relation to social structures and modes of thought.
**Breakdown:**
- Contributions: group life, status, attitudes, participation, political culture (PDF pp.143–144).
- Lipset: political development depends on social mobilisation, value change, kinship patterns, caste and class relations (PDF p.140).
- Limit: reduces politics to social structure alone.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.140, 143–144 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2104 (Lipset); PT-2110; PT-2111; PT-2120.
**Trap:** Political development is not only constitutions or legal forms; Gauba's point (PDF p.140).
**Mains hook:** Political development needs social mobilisation, not only constitutional design.
**Back:** *"Political sociology: institutions read through society; Lipset: mobilise, change values, mind caste and class."*

### Flashcard PT-2099 — Political psychology: attitudes, leadership and propaganda
**Front:** What does political psychology analyse?
**Definition (learn):** Use of psychological models and techniques to analyse political behaviour, attitudes, learning, socialisation, leadership and propaganda.
**Breakdown:**
- Object: attitudes, personality, learning, leadership, propaganda (PDF pp.144–145).
- Applications: communication, propaganda, charismatic leadership, public opinion (PDF pp.144–145).
- Limit: cannot alone explain institutions or economic conflict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.144–145 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** PT-2103 (Eulau); PT-2110; PT-2122.
**Trap:** Psychology explains actors, not structural constraints (psychological reductionism).
**Mains hook:** Use psychology for why citizens follow extremist appeal; use institutions for what constrains them.
**Back:** *"Psychology: why people feel, follow and learn — not why institutions bind."*

### Flashcard PT-2100 — Political philosophy and Comte: science is not opposed to philosophy
**Front:** What is political philosophy's role, and why does Gauba soften the science/philosophy split?
**Definition (learn):** Inquiry into the moral and substantive dimensions of politics: values, obligation, good life, worth of political systems. Auguste Comte is used to show that science itself is rooted in philosophy.
**Breakdown:**
- Science: helps find facts and operational tools.
- Philosophy: determines goals of public policy.
- Comte: science originates in philosophy and needs philosophical validation of ends.
- Philosophy regained prominence after post-behaviouralism (PDF pp.145–146).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.145–146 (✅ owner 09; advanced §7 ✅).
**Links:** PT-2077 (post-behaviouralism); PT-2112; PT-2113.
**Trap:** ❌ "Empirical political science made philosophy irrelevant" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Policy goals need philosophy; policy means need science.
**Back:** *"Science finds the means; philosophy names the ends."*

### Flashcard PT-2101 — Aristotle: politics as master science
**Front:** How did Aristotle treat politics, per Gauba?
**Definition (learn):** The classical "master science" approach treated politics as the master science, without sharply separating normative from empirical analysis or politics from wider social life.
**Breakdown:**
- Classical unity of fact and value.
- Modern separation came later.
- Contrast with behaviouralist separation (PT-2070).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.146 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §5).
**Links:** PT-2100; PT-2070; PT-2046.
**Trap:** Aristotle's "master science" is not modern interdisciplinary method; it is an older unity.
**Mains hook:** The classical unity shows why modern division was a choice, not a given.
**Back:** *"Aristotle: politics is the master science, facts and values together."*

### Flashcard PT-2102 — Marx and Engels: early systematic interdisciplinarians
**Front:** Why does Gauba credit Marx and Engels with an early interdisciplinary approach?
**Definition (learn):** They located political behaviour in socio-economic factors such as production, technology and class structure.
**Breakdown:**
- Factors: production, technology, class structure.
- Gauba: PDF p.137.
- Limit: economic reductionism (PT-2109).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.137 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §5).
**Links:** PT-2076; PT-2109; PT-2107.
**Trap:** Marx's interdisciplinary move is also a reductionist one if used alone.
**Mains hook:** Credit Marx for the method and critique the reduction in the same answer.
**Back:** *"Marx: politics read through production and class."*

### Flashcard PT-2103 — Heinz Eulau: behaviour in wider social, cultural and personal context
**Front:** What does Eulau argue about political behaviour?
**Definition (learn):** Political behaviour analysis must be interdisciplinary because political action occurs in a wider social, cultural and personal context.
**Breakdown:**
- Social context: structure.
- Cultural context: values, norms.
- Personal context: psychology.
- Gauba: PDF p.139.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.139 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §5).
**Links:** PT-2099; PT-2077; PT-2098.
**Trap:** Eulau is a behaviouralist who argues for interdisciplinarity; do not treat behaviouralism as purely micro without context.
**Mains hook:** Behaviour is explained by context, not by isolated individuals.
**Back:** *"Eulau: behaviour lives in society, culture and person."*

### Flashcard PT-2104 — Seymour Martin Lipset: political development and social mobilisation
**Front:** What does Lipset say political development depends on?
**Definition (learn):** Political development cannot be studied without social mobilisation, value change, kinship patterns, caste and class relations.
**Breakdown:**
- Social mobilisation.
- Value change.
- Kinship patterns.
- Caste and class.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.140 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §5).
**Links:** PT-2098; PT-2110.
**Trap:** Lipset's point is not that constitutions do not matter; it is that they are insufficient alone.
**Mains hook:** Use the mobilisation-value-kinship-class checklist in a development answer.
**Back:** *"Lipset: development = mobilise + change values + mind kin, caste and class."*

### Flashcard PT-2105 — Easton's system framework as a borrowed model
**Front:** Where does Easton's system concept come from, per Gauba?
**Definition (learn):** Easton imports the "system" concept from sociology and general systems theory into political science, treating politics as inputs-conversion-outputs-feedback.
**Breakdown:**
- Borrowed: general systems and sociology.
- Result: political-system framework (PT-2072).
- Gauba: PDF p.140 (✅ owner 09).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.140 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §5).
**Links:** PT-2072; PT-2107; PT-2095.
**Trap:** Systems analysis is borrowed, not a native political concept.
**Mains hook:** Borrowed models help comparison and carry their own reductionism.
**Back:** *"Easton borrowed the system from sociology and made it political."*

### Flashcard PT-2106 — Lasswell's problem-solving model
**Front:** What is Lasswell's problem-solving model as Gauba uses it?
**Definition (learn):** An example of psychology and decision theory feeding political analysis: identify problems, generate alternatives, evaluate outcomes.
**Breakdown:**
- Steps: problem identification, alternatives, evaluation.
- Link: decision-making approach (PT-2075).
- Gauba: PDF p.140 (✅ owner 09).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.140 (✅ owner 09; advanced ✅ owner 09 advanced §5).
**Links:** PT-2075; PT-2099; PT-2107.
**Trap:** Lasswell also appears as a power analyst (PT-2068); keep both roles.
**Mains hook:** Use the problem-solving model to structure a policy-choice answer.
**Back:** *"Lasswell's loop: problem, alternatives, evaluation."*

### Flashcard PT-2107 — Borrowed models: elite theory, base-superstructure, structural-functionalism, bargaining analogy
**Front:** Which borrowed models does Gauba name, and from which disciplines?
**Definition (learn):** Elite theory; base-superstructure analysis (Marxian political economy); structural-functionalism (sociology/anthropology); problem-solving or decision models (psychology and organisation theory); market and bargaining analogies (economics).
**Breakdown:**
- Elite theory: elite-led politics.
- Base-superstructure: economic base drives political superstructure.
- Structural-functionalism: functions and structures.
- Decision models: psychology and organisation.
- Bargaining: economics.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.139–140 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** PT-2076; PT-2105; PT-2106; PT-2073; PT-2109.
**Trap:** Each borrowed model has a donor discipline and a reductionism risk.
**Mains hook:** Name the donor discipline and the risk for any borrowed model used.
**Back:** *"Five borrowed tools: elite, base, function, decision, bargain."*

### Flashcard PT-2108 — Evidence-routing matrix: which discipline answers what
**Front:** Which discipline routes which kind of political claim?
**Definition (learn):** History verifies claims over time; political economy tests material interests; sociology tests fit with social basis; psychology traces mass appeal; philosophy tests coherence and defensibility of concepts.
**Breakdown:**
- History: whether theory survives cases; cannot settle present moral worth.
- Political economy: material interests; cannot settle legitimacy.
- Sociology: social basis; cannot settle a single actor's motive.
- Psychology: mechanisms of appeal; cannot settle structural explanation.
- Philosophy: concepts and ends; cannot explain behaviour alone.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner advanced §8 (⚠️ owner 09 advanced synthesis, PDF pp.141–146).
**Links:** PT-2096; PT-2097; PT-2098; PT-2099; PT-2100.
**Trap:** Each route has a "cannot settle alone" limit; the matrix forbids single-discipline verdicts.
**Mains hook:** Match the evidence to the missing piece in your argument.
**Back:** *"Each lens answers one question and is blind to the others."*

### Flashcard PT-2109 — Economic reductionism
**Front:** What is economic reductionism and what does it miss?
**Definition (learn):** Treating all politics as market bargaining or class interest; it misses legitimacy, identity and normative claims that are not reducible to material interest.
**Breakdown:**
- Risk: economics alone settles politics.
- Miss: legitimacy, identity, normative claims.
- Gauba: economics does not settle analysis alone (PDF pp.140, 142–143, 146).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §14 (⚠️ synthesis); Gauba PDF pp.140, 142–146 (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Links:** PT-2097; PT-2102; PT-2107.
**Trap:** ❌ treating class as the only explanation.
**Mains hook:** Use economics for conflict and welfare; use philosophy for legitimacy.
**Back:** *"Economics explains interests, not all of legitimacy."*

### Flashcard PT-2110 — Psychological reductionism
**Front:** What does psychological reductionism miss?
**Definition (learn):** Treating all politics as individual attitude or personality misses structural, institutional and economic constraints operating independently of any single actor.
**Breakdown:**
- Risk: psychology alone.
- Miss: institutions, economy.
- Use: psychology explains appeal, not structure.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §14 (⚠️ synthesis).
**Links:** PT-2099; PT-2108.
**Trap:** Psychology is not the only route to mass behaviour; structure matters.
**Mains hook:** Pair psychological appeal with institutional constraint.
**Back:** *"Psychology sees the person; institutions bind the person."*

### Flashcard PT-2111 — Sociological and functional reductionism
**Front:** What does sociological or functional reductionism risk?
**Definition (learn):** Treating politics as maintenance of social structure risks a conservative bias that under-explains conflict, agency and deliberate institutional change.
**Breakdown:**
- Risk: function maintenance.
- Miss: conflict, agency, change.
- Link: structural-functional critique (PT-2083).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §14 (⚠️ synthesis); Gauba PDF p.129 (✅ owner 08 advanced).
**Links:** PT-2083; PT-2098; PT-2073.
**Trap:** Functional systems tend towards equilibrium, which can hide conflict.
**Mains hook:** Add conflict and agency to any structural account.
**Back:** *"Function explains order; it under-explains conflict and change."*

### Flashcard PT-2112 — Legal reductionism
**Front:** What does legal reductionism miss?
**Definition (learn):** Treating politics as fully captured by formal rules misses informal power and non-compliance.
**Breakdown:**
- Risk: rules alone.
- Miss: informal power, bargaining, non-compliance.
- Use: law gives constraints, not the whole story.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §14 (⚠️ synthesis); legal approach limit PDF p.116 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** PT-2066; PT-2108; PT-2100.
**Trap:** Law is evidence, but not the complete picture.
**Mains hook:** Pair legal text with sociology and psychology for compliance.
**Back:** *"Law says what must happen; sociology says what does."*

### Flashcard PT-2113 — Political anthropology: order without the modern state (⚠️)
**Front:** What does political anthropology add to interdisciplinary analysis?
**Definition (learn):** Studies political organisation in small-scale, kinship-based or non-state societies (lineage systems, tribal councils, chieftaincy), showing that authority and order can exist without a centralised state.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: order does not require a Weberian state.
- Evidence: kinship and lineage dispute-resolution in non-state societies.
- Significance: tests authority, legitimacy and sovereignty against non-state cases.
- Limit: cannot be mechanically transplanted to large modern states.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §13 extension (⚠️ owner 09 basic §13); not fetched.
**Links:** PT-2112; PT-2115; PT-2117.
**Trap:** Anthropology is not a substitute for state theory; it sharpens it.
**Mains hook:** Use non-state cases to test whether the state is the only unit of analysis.
**Back:** *"Anthropology: authority without the state, tested on small scales."*

### Flashcard PT-2114 — Law as data source (⚠️)
**Front:** How does law serve interdisciplinary political analysis, distinct from the legal approach?
**Definition (learn):** Constitutional text, statutes and judicial reasoning are primary evidence of how authority is formally allocated and constrained.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: legal materials are indispensable evidence.
- Evidence: constitutional provisions and case law define powers, limits and remedies.
- Significance: grounds institutional claims in verifiable text.
- Limit: cannot explain informal power or bargaining.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §13 extension (⚠️ owner 09 basic §13).
**Links:** PT-2066; PT-2112; PT-2116.
**Trap:** Distinguish law as a source of evidence from the legal approach as a method (PT-2066).
**Mains hook:** Use constitutional text for formal authority and sociology for practice.
**Back:** *"Law is evidence of formal power, not proof of actual power."*

### Flashcard PT-2115 — Political geography: territory, resources and federalism (⚠️)
**Front:** What does political geography contribute?
**Definition (learn):** Studies the spatial dimension of politics: territory, boundaries, resource distribution, federalism, regional and electoral geography.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: spatial location shapes conflict and design.
- Evidence: boundary disputes, resource imbalance, federal responses to diversity.
- Significance: explains regionalism, federalism, centre-state and inter-state conflict.
- Limit: geography constrains but does not determine outcomes.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §13 extension (⚠️ owner 09 basic §13).
**Links:** PT-2113; PT-2121; PT-2120.
**Trap:** Do not treat geography as destiny.
**Mains hook:** Combine geography with identity (sociology) and resources (economics).
**Back:** *"Geography sets the stage; it does not write the script."*

### Flashcard PT-2116 — Objection-reply: subject-matter dissolution
**Front:** Does borrowing from other social sciences dissolve political science?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: borrowing core models risks dissolving political science into social science in general. Reply: integration borrows evidence and models without merging; the political question (who gets valued things, through what authoritative process) remains the organising centre.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: loss of distinct subject-matter.
- Reply: Gauba's integration without merger (PDF p.146).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.146 (✅ owner 09 basic §15).
**Links:** PT-2094; PT-2037; PT-2123.
**Trap:** Present as the chapter's answer, not a settled debate.
**Mains hook:** The political question defines the discipline; evidence is shared.
**Back:** *"Shared evidence, distinct question, no merger."*

### Flashcard PT-2117 — Objection-reply: over-generalising small-scale or spatial evidence
**Front:** Does anthropological or geographical evidence transfer mechanically to modern states?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: such evidence over-generalises from small-scale or spatial contexts to large democracies. Reply: use it to test and illustrate a claim, not to assert mechanical transfer.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: scale mismatch.
- Reply: weigh each discipline's evidence for relevance.
- Status: extension of Gauba's purposeful-borrowing standard (⚠️ owner 09 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️ owner 09 basic §15); not a book quotation.
**Links:** PT-2113; PT-2115; PT-2116.
**Trap:** Do not treat anthropological evidence as a universal template.
**Mains hook:** Evidence is weighed for relevance, not imported wholesale.
**Back:** *"Test, illustrate, do not transplant."*

### Flashcard PT-2118 — Application: why one democracy survives and another collapses (advanced)
**Front:** How would integrated analysis explain democratic survival or collapse?
**Definition (learn):** Route through history for antecedents, economics for inequality, sociology for cleavages, psychology for authoritarian appeal, philosophy for legitimacy.
**Breakdown:**
- History: antecedents.
- Economics: inequality.
- Sociology: cleavages.
- Psychology: authoritarian appeal.
- Philosophy: legitimacy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Hypothetical application (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §9); no real case asserted.
**Links:** PT-2108; PT-2097; PT-2098; PT-2099; PT-2100.
**Trap:** Do not pile disciplines; ask which discipline supplies the missing evidence.
**Mains hook:** Build the answer from missing evidence, not from a checklist.
**Back:** *"Ask which discipline fills the gap, not which one is listed."*

### Flashcard PT-2119 — Indian application: political-economy lens (⚠️)
**Front:** How can political economy illustrate Indian policy conflict?
**Definition (learn):** Debates on economic liberalisation, welfare-scheme design or federal fiscal transfers show political theory using economic evidence to analyse policy conflict.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: type of application.
- Caution: no unverified figures, scheme names or years.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 (⚠️ owner 09 basic).
**Links:** PT-2097; PT-2107; PT-2121.
**Trap:** Do not assert scheme figures without verification.
**Mains hook:** Use a general debate type with a qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Indian policy conflict: interests, rules, and who bears the cost."*

### Flashcard PT-2120 — Indian application: caste and identity in political sociology (⚠️)
**Front:** How does caste or community identity illustrate political sociology?
**Definition (learn):** Caste, community and regional identity are a standard illustrative anchor for the sociology-politics link in Indian party mobilisation and political culture.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: general, well-known pattern.
- Caution: no unverified electoral statistics.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 (⚠️ owner 09 basic).
**Links:** PT-2098; PT-2104; PT-2115.
**Trap:** Avoid turning a pattern into a statistic.
**Mains hook:** Caste shapes mobilisation, but the answer must add institutions and economics.
**Back:** *"Caste mobilises; it does not alone explain politics."*

### Flashcard PT-2121 — Indian application: federal reorganisation and resource disputes (⚠️)
**Front:** How do territory and resource disputes illustrate political geography in India?
**Definition (learn):** India's federal reorganisation of states and continuing regional and resource-sharing disputes (river-water disputes, hill-state demands) show territory and resources shaping conflict.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: type of illustration.
- Route exact dates, tribunal names or case detail to Polity or Geography.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 (⚠️ owner 09 basic); not fetched.
**Links:** PT-2115; PT-2119.
**Trap:** Do not assert tribunal names or dates here.
**Mains hook:** Territory plus resources explains many centre-state conflicts.
**Back:** *"Territory and water: the map that makes disputes."*

### Flashcard PT-2122 — Trap: behaviouralist reductionism and the revival of normative theory
**Front:** Did behaviouralism's focus on observable behaviour end normative theory?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba records that behaviouralism corrected older formalism, but political philosophy later revived justice, liberty, equality and community.
**Breakdown:**
- Behavioural gain: corrected formalism.
- Behavioural limit: narrow focus.
- Revival: normative theory returned (PDF pp.145–146).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.145–146 (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Links:** PT-2077; PT-2100; PT-2091.
**Trap:** ❌ "Behaviouralism ended philosophy" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Normative theory returned because empirical methods could not define ends.
**Back:** *"Behaviouralism sharpened facts; philosophy returned for ends."*

### Flashcard PT-2123 — Answer spine: integration without merger
**Front:** What is the answer spine for the interdisciplinary question?
**Definition (learn):** Politics is an open, socially embedded process; therefore political analysis borrows evidence and models from history, economics, sociology, psychology and philosophy, while keeping the political question central.
**Breakdown:**
- Define interdisciplinarity (PT-2092).
- Show why empirical and systems analysis pushed outward (PT-2095, PT-2072).
- Give one line per discipline (PT-2096 to PT-2100).
- End: integration without merger (PT-2094).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.136–146 (✅ owner 09); spine (⚠️ owner 09 basic §11).
**Links:** PT-2092; PT-2094; PT-2108.
**Trap:** Do not replace the spine with a list of subjects.
**Mains hook:** Move from evidence to explanation to normative evaluation.
**Back:** *"Evidence, explanation, evaluation — borrow, integrate, never merge."*

### GROUP — 10 State, Civil Society, Nation and Internationalism (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard PT-2124 — Weber's definition: monopoly of legitimate physical force
**Front:** How does Weber define the modern state, per Gauba?
**Definition (learn):** The modern state is defined sociologically by its successful claim to the monopoly of legitimate physical force within a territory; the definition rests on means, not a fixed moral end.
**Breakdown:**
- Means-based definition (not moral end).
- Claim: successful, within a territory.
- Anchor for the rest of the chapter.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.148 (✅ owner 10; advanced ✅ owner 10 advanced §2).
**Links:** PT-2125; PT-2138 (Machiavelli); PT-2134 (sovereignty).
**Trap:** Weber's definition is about legitimate force, not any particular regime type.
**Mains hook:** Begin any state answer with the means-based definition; it avoids moral overload.
**Back:** *"Weber: the state claims legitimate force over a territory."*

### Flashcard PT-2125 — Four constituent elements of the state
**Front:** List the four elements of the state.
**Definition (learn):** Population, territory, government and sovereignty.
**Breakdown:**
- Population: essential but not racially, linguistically or religiously homogeneous (PDF p.150).
- Territory: fixed jurisdiction and internationally intelligible boundaries (PDF pp.150–151).
- Government: concrete administrative organ (PDF pp.151, 156).
- Sovereignty: final authority to make and enforce binding decisions (PDF p.152).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.149–152 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2126; PT-2127; PT-2129; PT-2134; PT-2128.
**Trap:** International recognition is not a fifth element (PT-2128).
**Mains hook:** Use the four elements to show what a state needs and what it does not.
**Back:** *"P-T-G-S: people, land, government, sovereignty."*

### Flashcard PT-2126 — Population: no racial, linguistic or religious homogeneity required
**Front:** Does a state require a homogeneous population?
**Definition (learn):** No. Population is essential to the state, but the state does not require racial, linguistic or religious homogeneity.
**Breakdown:**
- Essential: a population.
- Not required: homogeneity.
- Link to nation: nationhood can contain multiple nationalities (PT-2131).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.150 (✅ owner 10 advanced §4).
**Links:** PT-2125; PT-2131; PT-2132; PT-2150.
**Trap:** Do not equate state with a single nation.
**Mains hook:** Diversity within a state is compatible with statehood; the question is how the state treats it.
**Back:** *"A state can hold many peoples; homogeneity is not a requirement."*

### Flashcard PT-2127 — Territory: fixed jurisdiction and intelligible boundaries
**Front:** Why is territory essential to the state?
**Definition (learn):** A state needs fixed jurisdiction and internationally intelligible boundaries.
**Breakdown:**
- Jurisdiction: where authority applies.
- Boundaries: recognisable to others.
- Contrast: nation without a state (PT-2132).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.150–151 (✅ owner 10 advanced §4).
**Links:** PT-2125; PT-2132.
**Trap:** A people with a territory is not automatically a state.
**Mains hook:** Territory anchors sovereignty in space.
**Back:** *"Territory: the place where the state's law stops at the edge."*

### Flashcard PT-2128 — International recognition is not constitutive of statehood (trap)
**Front:** Is international recognition an element of the state?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba treats recognition as incidental to sovereignty; Bangladesh is used as the illustration.
**Breakdown:**
- Element list: population, territory, government, sovereignty only.
- Recognition: follows sovereignty; not a constituent element.
- Illustration: Bangladesh (PDF p.152).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.152 (✅ owner 10; advanced ❌ row ✅ owner 10 advanced §4).
**Links:** PT-2125; PT-2134.
**Trap:** ❌ "Recognition is a fifth element" is a wrong option.
**Mains hook:** Statehood is factual control; recognition is diplomatic acknowledgement.
**Back:** *"Recognition follows sovereignty; it does not create it."*

### Flashcard PT-2129 — Government vs state (MacIver and the trap of confusion)
**Front:** How does government differ from the state?
**Definition (learn):** Government is the operating agency of the state, administrative or machinery through which common policies are made; the state is wider and more enduring.
**Breakdown:**
- Government: changeable office-holders; concrete.
- State: abstract, enduring political community.
- Gauba: government may rise and fall without destroying the state (PDF pp.151–152, 156–157).
- MacIver: government is the organ that speaks with the voice of law (PDF pp.149, 156).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.151, 156 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2125; PT-2140 (MacIver); PT-2166 (idealist danger).
**Trap:** ❌ "State = government" is wrong. Suppression of a people's legitimate government can destroy or suspend the state's identity (PDF pp.156–157).
**Mains hook:** The distinction protects citizens from fusion of rulers with the state.
**Back:** *"Government changes hands; the state stays."*

### Flashcard PT-2130 — Society vs state: state depends on society
**Front:** How is society related to the state?
**Definition (learn):** Society is the wider association of human beings meeting the whole range of life's needs, of which the state is only one organised part.
**Breakdown:**
- Society: custom, morality, family, non-political ties.
- State: one organised part.
- Society can exist without a state; the state depends on society (PDF pp.153–154).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.153–154 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2129; PT-2133; PT-2142.
**Trap:** Do not inflate the state into the whole of social life (authoritarian confusion).
**Mains hook:** State authority is partial; society is wider.
**Back:** *"Society is the whole web; the state is one knot in it."*

### Flashcard PT-2131 — Nation vs nationality vs state
**Front:** How do nation, nationality and state differ?
**Definition (learn):** Nation: people in a defined territory united by common political aspirations, interests, history and destiny. Nationality: a feeling of unity often based on race, language, religion, culture and common historical development. State: political organisation with sovereignty.
**Breakdown:**
- Nation: political identity and destiny.
- Nationality: feeling of unity from cultural markers.
- State: the institutional political organisation.
- Nation may include different nationalities within one state.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.157–158 (✅ owner 10); nationality PDF p.157 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2132; PT-2135; PT-2126.
**Trap:** ❌ "Nation = state" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Nation-state is a fit, not an identity; mismatch is the main source of conflict.
**Back:** *"Nation: shared destiny. Nationality: shared feeling. State: shared power."*

### Flashcard PT-2132 — Nation without a state; state without a nation (Kurds, multi-ethnic states)
**Front:** Give Gauba's examples of nation-state mismatch.
**Definition (learn):** A nation need not have its own state (Kurds); states can lack full national integration (book-period cases: former USSR, Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia, Ethiopia).
**Breakdown:**
- Kurds: nation without state (PDF p.167).
- Former USSR, Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia, Ethiopia: weak national integration (PDF p.167).
- Lebanon and Cyprus appear in advanced §12 (✅ owner 10 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.167 (✅ owner 10); book-period illustrations (⚠️ not current status).
**Links:** PT-2131; PT-2127; PT-2160.
**Trap:** Do not update these cases into current geopolitical claims (⚠️ owner).
**Mains hook:** Use mismatch cases to show that nation and state are analytically separate.
**Back:** *"Kurds: a nation with no state; Yugoslavia: a state with too many nations."*

### Flashcard PT-2133 — Civil society: layered meanings and the NGO trap
**Front:** What does "civil society" mean, and is it just NGOs?
**Definition (learn):** In contemporary usage, the sphere of associations and public activity outside direct state control; Gauba presents older meanings where it was equated with the state.
**Breakdown:**
- Ancient/state-equated sense.
- Hegelian, Marxian, Gramscian senses (PDF pp.154–155).
- Contemporary associational-democratic sense (PDF pp.173–176).
- NGOs: one contemporary component only.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.154–155, 173–176 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2142; PT-2143; PT-2144; PT-2145; PT-2147; PT-2158.
**Trap:** ❌ "Civil society simply means NGOs" is a wrong option.
**Mains hook:** Civil society has conceptual lineages; NGOs are a subset.
**Back:** *"Civil society: many lineages; NGOs are one branch."*

### Flashcard PT-2134 — Sovereignty: supreme power to make and enforce binding law
**Front:** Define sovereignty as Gauba does.
**Definition (learn):** The supreme power of the state to make and enforce binding laws and decisions over population and territory; the final authority to authorise legitimate force.
**Breakdown:**
- Supreme: final authority.
- Binding: laws and decisions.
- Enforce: legitimate force.
- Population and territory: the domain.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.152 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2124; PT-2128; PT-2159 (Laski objection); sovereignty cards in file 11.
**Trap:** Sovereignty is not the same as government; sovereignty belongs to the state.
**Mains hook:** Pluralists challenge the moral supremacy of sovereignty, not its coercive function.
**Back:** *"Sovereignty: final say, binding, enforceable over land and people."*

### Flashcard PT-2135 — Nationalism: sentiment and ideology
**Front:** What is nationalism?
**Definition (learn):** Both a sentiment of attachment to one's nation and an ideology that political organisation should rest on nationhood.
**Breakdown:**
- Sentiment: attachment.
- Ideology: state organised on nationhood and self-determination.
- Unit: nation and national interest (PDF pp.164–165).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.164–165 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2131; PT-2136; PT-2137.
**Trap:** Nationalism is not merely love of one's country; its ideological claim is that the political unit should match the nation.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish sentiment from the ideology; the ideology creates the nation-state demand.
**Back:** *"Nationalism: feeling, then a claim that the state must match the nation."*

### Flashcard PT-2136 — Internationalism: cooperation and restraint on sovereignty
**Front:** What is internationalism, per Gauba?
**Definition (learn):** A view that mankind needs larger cooperative organisations and reasonable restraints on sovereignty for common human interests.
**Breakdown:**
- Unit: humanity and common interests beyond one nation (PDF pp.166–167).
- Implication: larger cooperation; sovereignty restrained.
- Tension: states must concede autonomy (PT-2161).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.166–167 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2135; PT-2137; PT-2161.
**Trap:** Internationalism is not the same as abolishing states.
**Mains hook:** Internationalism asks states to give up some autonomy for shared problems.
**Back:** *"Internationalism: common human interest, with sovereignty kept reasonable."*

### Flashcard PT-2137 — Nationalism and internationalism: opposites or overlap? (trap)
**Front:** Are nationalism and internationalism exact opposites with no overlap?
**Definition (learn):** Gauba presents them as conflicting orientations; modern states can combine national organisation with international cooperation.
**Breakdown:**
- Conflict: loyalty unit (nation vs humanity).
- Overlap: states can be national and cooperative.
- Owner caution flagged ⚠️ (basic §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.164–167 (✅ owner 10); overlap (⚠️ owner 10 basic §9).
**Links:** PT-2135; PT-2136.
**Trap:** ❌ "Exact opposites with no overlap" is a wrong framing.
**Mains hook:** Argue the tension and the possible synthesis.
**Back:** *"Nationalism and internationalism conflict in loyalty, yet can share a state."*

### Flashcard PT-2138 — Machiavelli: the state as authority over men
**Front:** What does Gauba credit Machiavelli with for the modern state?
**Definition (learn):** Machiavelli's focus on power having authority over men gives an early modern articulation of the state as authority rather than a purely moral ideal.
**Breakdown:**
- Authority over men.
- Non-moral focus.
- Modern concept anchor (PDF p.148).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.148 (✅ owner 10 and advanced).
**Links:** PT-2124; PT-2125.
**Trap:** Machiavelli is not a defender of tyranny by definition; the card records only the concept.
**Mains hook:** The modern state begins as power that commands, then acquires legitimacy.
**Back:** *"Machiavelli: the state is power over men, not a moral ideal first."*

### Flashcard PT-2139 — MacIver: state as organ of law and whole people
**Front:** How does MacIver define the state?
**Definition (learn):** The state embraces the whole people in a territory and acts through government as the organ that speaks with the voice of law.
**Breakdown:**
- Whole people in territory.
- Government: administrative organ.
- Law: voice of the state.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.149, 156 (✅ owner 10 and advanced §5).
**Links:** PT-2129; PT-2131.
**Trap:** MacIver's "whole people" is about membership; it does not deny government's changeability.
**Mains hook:** Use MacIver to separate state from government.
**Back:** *"MacIver: the people, the law's voice, the government as organ."*

### Flashcard PT-2140 — Laski: pluralist critic of sovereignty (correction and date conflict)
**Front:** What is Laski's position on sovereignty, and why is the owner's date to check?
**Definition (learn):** Laski is a pluralist critic of absolute sovereignty; the state is one association among many (churches, unions, family), and authority must be earned through service rather than by definitional fiat.
**Breakdown:**
- Early point: residence brings binding obedience (PDF p.149) — definitional, not monist.
- Federal society: authority must be coordinative (advanced §5).
- Works: *A Grammar of Politics*.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner conflict: advanced §5 gives 1938; Wikipedia (fetched, Harold Laski page) gives 1925 as first publication and lists 1925 in the bibliography; no 1938 edition confirmed. Pluralism (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
**Links:** PT-2159; PT-2129; PT-2134.
**Trap:** Do not read Laski as a monist; the basic owner's "monist-sounding" line is corrected in §6.
**Mains hook:** Laski's pluralism challenges moral supremacy; the state must earn obedience.
**Back:** *"Laski: one association among many — the state must earn obedience."*

### Flashcard PT-2141 — Hegel: civil society as sphere of self-interest
**Front:** How does Hegel distinguish civil society from the state?
**Definition (learn):** Civil society is the sphere of universal egoism or self-interest (economic life beyond family); the state is the sphere of universality. Hegel idealised the state.
**Breakdown:**
- Civil society: needs and self-interest.
- State: universal; idealised.
- Risk: over-subordination of the individual (advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.154, 156–157 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** PT-2133; PT-2142; PT-2166.
**Trap:** Hegel's civil society is not the same as Tocqueville's associational sphere.
**Mains hook:** Hegel shows civil society as a distinct sphere, but his state is over-elevated.
**Back:** *"Hegel: civil society is self-interest; the state is universal."*

### Flashcard PT-2142 — Marx on civil society and law as class instrument
**Front:** How does Marx treat civil society?
**Definition (learn):** Marx accepted Hegel's description of civil society as an economic sphere but treated law and state as serving the capitalist class; under capitalism civil society is legally equal but materially dominated.
**Breakdown:**
- Accepts: civil society = economic sphere.
- Rejects: state neutrality; law serves capital.
- Civil society: formal equality, material domination.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.155 (✅ owner 10 and advanced).
**Links:** PT-2141; PT-2143; PT-2040.
**Trap:** Marx is not a pluralist on civil society; he sees class as the core.
**Mains hook:** The Marxian critique exposes legal equality as class-biased.
**Back:** *"Marx: legal equality, material domination."*

### Flashcard PT-2143 — Gramsci: consent, coercion and hegemony
**Front:** How does Gramsci divide civil society and political society?
**Definition (learn):** Civil society is structures of legitimation (family, school, church) manufacturing consent; political society is structures of coercion; together they form structures of domination.
**Breakdown:**
- Civil society: consent and legitimation.
- Political society: coercion.
- Together: domination.
- Counter-hegemonic struggle as a hint (advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.155 (✅ owner 10 and advanced).
**Links:** PT-2133; PT-2158; PT-2168.
**Trap:** Gramsci's civil society is not automatically liberating.
**Mains hook:** Use Gramsci for domination through consent, not only coercion.
**Back:** *"Gramsci: civil society wins consent; political society enforces."*

### Flashcard PT-2144 — Tocqueville: associations as counterweights and schools of democracy
**Front:** What does Tocqueville say about voluntary associations?
**Definition (learn):** Intermediate voluntary associations guard liberty, publicise interests and act as schools of democracy; they counterweight state power.
**Breakdown:**
- Liberty guard.
- Publicity of interests.
- School of democracy (civic virtue).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF pp.173–174 (✅ owner 10 and advanced).
**Links:** PT-2133; PT-2147; PT-2148.
**Trap:** Tocqueville is the democratic-renewal pole of civil society; Gramsci is the domination pole.
**Mains hook:** Use Tocqueville for the democratic-renewal reading.
**Back:** *"Tocqueville: associations check power and teach democracy."*

### Flashcard PT-2145 — Marcuse: civil society eclipses the state, and the reverse (One-Dimensional Man, 1964)
**Front:** What does Marcuse argue about civil society and the state?
**Definition (learn):** Under modern capitalism civil society eclipses the state; under modern communism the state eclipses civil society; freedom needs independence of both.
**Breakdown:**
- Capitalism: civil society dominates.
- Communism: state dominates.
- Freedom: independence of both.
- Critique: advanced industrial society absorbs opposition via "repressive desublimation" (⚠️ owner §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, PDF p.155 (✅ owner 10 advanced); *One-Dimensional Man*, 1964 (⚠️ owner §13, not fetched).
**Links:** PT-2141; PT-2143; PT-2146.
**Trap:** Do not present Marcuse as a democratic renewal theorist; he is the pessimistic pole.
**Mains hook:** Civil society is not automatically free; it can be captured.
**Back:** *"Marcuse: capitalism swallows civil society; communism swallows the state; freedom needs both apart."*

### Flashcard PT-2146 — Marcuse counterpoint: new social movements (⚠️)
**Front:** What counterpoint is raised against Marcuse?
**Definition (learn):** Critics argue Marcuse underestimates civil society's mobilising capacity; the civil-rights, feminist, environmental and anti-war movements show advanced societies are not closed to opposition.
**Breakdown:**
- Thesis: absorption and pacification.
- Counter: mobilisation and new social movements.
- Cohen-Arato and Putnam partly reply to this pessimism (⚠️ owner §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §13 (⚠️ owner 10 basic, not fetched).
**Links:** PT-2145; PT-2147; PT-2148.
**Trap:** Present as contested, not settled.
**Mains hook:** Use the counterpoint to test Marcuse.
**Back:** *"Marcuse pessimism meets movements: pacified or still mobilising?"*

### Flashcard PT-2147 — Robert Putnam: social capital and democratic performance (⚠️ owner, ✅ year)
**Front:** What is Putnam's social-capital claim?
**Definition (learn):** Dense networks of civic associational life generate social capital (norms of reciprocity and trust), making collective action easier and improving democratic performance.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *Making Democracy Work* (1993) (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Finding: strong civic-engagement traditions correlate with better regional government in Italy (20 regional governments since 1970; North-Central vs South) (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Limit: bonding vs bridging; causation direction unsettled.
- *Bowling Alone* (2000) (⚠️ owner-flagged).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1993 ✅ (fetched); Italian regional study ✅ (fetched); 2000 ⚠️.
**Links:** PT-2144; PT-2148; PT-2158; PT-2164.
**Trap:** Association density does not prove causation; check direction.
**Mains hook:** Social capital gives civil society an empirical link to democracy.
**Back:** *"Putnam 1993: civic traditions make better government."*

### Flashcard PT-2148 — Cohen and Arato: civil society as a third sphere (⚠️)
**Front:** What three-sphere model do Cohen and Arato propose?
**Definition (learn):** Civil society is a distinct sphere (associations, public sphere, social movements and culture) analytically separate from the state and the economy, mediated by political society (parties, parliaments) and economic society (regulated markets, trade unions).
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *Civil Society and Political Theory*, 1992 (⚠️ owner-flagged).
- Mediators: political society and economic society.
- Limit: separation understates state and market penetration (funding, co-optation).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1992 ⚠️ (owner §13; not fetched).
**Links:** PT-2147; PT-2133; PT-2158.
**Trap:** Do not collapse civil society into political society.
**Mains hook:** Three spheres explain how movements reach power without becoming parties.
**Back:** *"Cohen-Arato: civil society, political society and economic society — three spheres, two bridges."*

### Flashcard PT-2149 — Civil-society variants comparison
**Front:** Compare liberal-associational, Hegelian, Marxian, Gramscian and contemporary democratic civil society.
