**Mains hook: 15-marker** — use Sassen for how state control weakens in specific domains (capital, labour) while legal authority remains.
**Back:** *"Sassen: global cities, lost control, denationalised bits."*

### Flashcard PT-2258 — Gauba's balanced appraisal: promises and dangers
**Front:** How does Gauba evaluate globalisation?
**Definition (learn):** Gauba presents globalisation with both promises and dangers. It may spread knowledge, communication and environmental awareness, and may also deepen domination and inequality.
**Breakdown:**
- Promise: knowledge, communication, environmental awareness, cooperation.
- Danger: domination, cultural erosion, unequal gains.
- Verdict: mixed, neither celebration nor rejection.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.217-218 (✅ owner 12 basic §6; advanced §5, §7).
**Links:** PT-2246; PT-2262; PT-2267 (globalisation as neo-colonialism).
**Trap:** Do not write Gauba as a globalisation opponent or booster; the appraisal is balanced.
**Mains hook:** A graded verdict must mirror this balance.
**Back:** *"Gauba: globalisation gives and takes; judge by who gains."*

### Flashcard PT-2259 — Trap: globalisation is not only economic
**Front:** Is globalisation only an economic process in the owner's account?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba links it also to communication, culture and environmental spillovers, which do not respect borders.
**Breakdown:**
- Economic: markets, finance, production.
- Communication: media networks.
- Cultural and environmental: global culture and spillovers.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.214-217 (✅ owner 12 basic §9; advanced §7).
**Links:** PT-2246; PT-2260; PT-2264.
**Trap:** Flattening globalisation into economics loses an examiner-marked dimension.
**Mains hook:** Use the four domains to show how sovereignty is challenged on several fronts.
**Back:** *"Globalisation: money, media, meaning, environment."*

### Flashcard PT-2260 — Transnational threats: a sealed-container view is untenable
**Front:** Which border-crossing threats does the owner use to show external sovereignty cannot be sealed?
**Definition (learn):** Environmental damage, terrorism, energy dependence, electronic finance and communication shocks do not respect borders, so external sovereignty cannot operate as if states were sealed containers.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner illustrations: Iraq-Kuwait 1990, Chernobyl 1986, ozone concerns, transnational media shock (PDF pp.214-216).
- 11 September 2001: a valid dated illustration; exact Gauba page not verified (⚠️).
- Lesson: insulation from outside is not possible.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Iraq-Kuwait 1990 and Chernobyl 1986 (✅ owner 12 basic §7). 11 September 2001 is a dated historical event; no Gauba page is asserted (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-2259; PT-2249; PT-2261 (historical caution).
**Trap:** Do not cite a Gauba page for 9/11, and do not present the examples as current affairs.
**Mains hook:** Border-crossing threats are the strongest reason external sovereignty must be revised.
**Back:** *"Chernobyl, Kuwait, ozone, media: borders leak."*

### Flashcard PT-2261 — Historical caution: book-period examples and Cold War vocabulary
**Front:** How should the chapter's Cold War and Third World examples be used in an answer?
**Definition (learn):** NATO, Warsaw Pact, "third world", non-alignment, Iraq-Kuwait 1990 and Chernobyl 1986 are book-period or historical illustrations from a 2009 text. The owner notes that Alfred Sauvy coined "third world" and Walter Lippmann popularised "Cold War" (⚠️ owner-only attribution, not verified here).
**Breakdown:**
- Treat as: dated historical illustrations.
- Vocabulary: "third world" (Sauvy) and "Cold War" (Lippmann), both owner-attributed (⚠️).
- Do not present: as current affairs.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.212-216 (✅ owner 12 advanced §5, §12). Sauvy and Lippmann attributions are ⚠️ owner-only.
**Links:** PT-2242; PT-2243; PT-2260; PT-2162 (pt_2 caution).
**Trap:** Presenting Cold War categories as today's order is an examiner-flagged error.
**Mains hook:** Good theory answers label illustrations by period.
**Back:** *"Book-period, not today's news."*

### Flashcard PT-2262 — Trap: globalisation has not ended sovereignty
**Front:** Does globalisation mean sovereignty has disappeared?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba presents challenge, constraint and revision, not automatic extinction of the state. Legal sovereignty remains the precondition for participation in pooling, delegation and treaty commitments.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner verdict: constrained and reconstituted.
- Precondition: legal sovereignty authorises commitments.
- Test: is the constraint sufficient to extinguish sovereignty, or only to constrain its exercise?
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.214-218 (✅ owner 12 basic §9, §13A; advanced §12).
**Links:** PT-2234; PT-2249; PT-2252.
**Trap:** This is the headline trap of the chapter; no owner source supports extinction.
**Mains hook:** The "to what extent" directive demands a graded verdict, not a yes or no.
**Back:** *"Not ended: constrained, reconstituted, still legal."*

### Flashcard PT-2263 — Neo-colonial evidence: Latin America as book-period illustration
**Front:** What example does Gauba use to show that formal independence coexists with dependency?
**Definition (learn):** Gauba describes Chile, Brazil, Panama and other Latin American states remaining sources of cheap raw materials and labour for US industry despite political independence.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: formal independence, economic dependency.
- Evidence: raw materials and labour supply.
- Limit: pre-2009 book-period illustration.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.211 (✅ owner 12 basic §13E). Not a current description (owner 12 advanced §12).
**Links:** PT-2239; PT-2248; PT-2251.
**Trap:** Do not present the Latin American example as a current description of any named country.
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — claim, the Latin American evidence, significance, and the book-period limit.
**Back:** *"Free flag, sold raw materials: a book-period picture."*

### Flashcard PT-2264 — Comparison matrix: colonialism, neo-colonialism, power blocs, globalisation
**Front:** How do the four mechanisms differ in legal status, real constraint and conceptual effect?
**Definition (learn):** Colonialism suppresses sovereignty. Neo-colonialism hollows autonomy while formal independence remains. Power blocs narrow choice through security pressure. Globalisation transforms and revises sovereignty through interdependence.
**Breakdown:**
- Colonialism: no full sovereign equality; direct control; suppression.
- Neo-colonialism: formal independence; trade, capital, MNCs; hollowing.
- Power blocs: statehood remains; security and alliance demands; strategic narrowing.
- Globalisation: sovereignty recognised; interdependence in finance, environment, culture; transformation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.209-218 (✅ owner 12 basic §8; advanced §8).
**Links:** PT-2236; PT-2239; PT-2242; PT-2246.
**Trap:** Do not place power blocs under neo-colonialism; they are security, not economic, pressure.
**Mains hook:** The matrix shows the sequence from suppression to transformation.
**Back:** *"Suppress, hollow, narrow, transform."*

### Flashcard PT-2265 — State, market and citizenship effects of globalisation (with limits)
**Front:** How does globalisation affect the state, the market and the citizen, and what limits each effect?
**Definition (learn):** The state's policy autonomy narrows under IMF/World Bank conditionality, WTO commitments and delegated procedures, but the state authorises and may withdraw. The market's control over its own economic space erodes through MNCs, but developing-country coalitions can renegotiate. Citizenship frames widen beyond the territorial state, but rights are still delivered by territorial states.
**Breakdown:**
- State: narrowed autonomy; limit: the state authorises and can withdraw.
- Market: MNCs erode control; limit: OPEC-type coalitions (PT-2245).
- Citizenship: wider frame (⚠️ synthesis); limit: rights are territorial.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.210-218 (⚠️ owner 12 basic §8a, analytical synthesis of Gauba's discussion).
**Links:** PT-2250; PT-2251; PT-2245; PT-2262.
**Trap:** "Global citizenship" is aspirational, not a substitute for national legal status (owner 12 §8a).
**Mains hook:** Use the three dimensions to test a claim that globalisation ends the state.
**Back:** *"State narrows, market leaks, citizen widens, rights stay local."*

### Flashcard PT-2266 — Objection-reply: is consent to conditionality free?
**Front:** If policy space is narrowed so far that IMF/World Bank conditions dictate choices, is formal consent meaningful?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: when conditionality dictates choices, consent is not free. Reply (owner): conditionality may be formally accepted yet substantively unequal, so it is a consent-and-power problem, not an example of delegated sovereign administration.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: asymmetry makes consent hollow.
- Reply: consent is formal but unequal.
- Verdict: a power problem, not a delegation problem.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.216-217 (✅ owner 12 basic §13C; "dictated" terms from Gauba's critics'-view paragraph).
**Links:** PT-2250; PT-2252; PT-2245.
**Trap:** Do not classify conditionality as delegation to escape the objection.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — objection-reply on conditionality with a verdict.
**Back:** *"Consent given, but the price was set elsewhere."*

### Flashcard PT-2267 — Objection-reply: is globalisation just new neo-colonialism?
**Front:** Is globalisation merely a new name for neo-colonialism?
**Definition (learn):** Case for: economic dependence, MNC capital and conditionality reproduce the neo-colonial asymmetry. Case against: globalisation also spreads knowledge and communication, and developing-country coalitions can resist (OPEC). The owner's balance: it may widen access and cooperation, but may also reproduce domination.
**Breakdown:**
- Continuity: asymmetry in capital and terms.
- Difference: wider interdependence in media, environment and culture.
- Counter-case: collective bargaining and renegotiation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.210-218 (✅ owner 12 advanced §6, §7; prompt 4 ⚠️).
**Links:** PT-2239; PT-2245; PT-2258; PT-2262.
**Trap:** Answering "yes, it is the same" or "no, it is entirely new" loses the verdict. Use "partly, under conditions".
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — balanced verdict: continuity in economic asymmetry, novelty in interdependence, resistance as the limit.
**Back:** *"Neo-colonial in part; new in reach; contestable in practice."*

### Flashcard PT-2268 — Directive decoder and mark-scaled architecture (globalisation)
**Front:** How should "examine", "to what extent" and "critically evaluate the neo-colonialism thesis" be answered?
**Definition (learn):** "Examine": sequence imperialism to neo-colonialism to power blocs to globalisation, each with mechanism and limit. "To what extent": graded verdict (constrained vs abolished). "Critically evaluate the neo-colonialism thesis": Nkrumah's mechanisms, the popularised-not-coined correction, and a named case (OPEC).
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: thesis, one mechanism, one example, verdict.
- 15 marks: two mechanisms, Nkrumah correction, one objection-reply, verdict.
- 20 marks: full three-part spine, typology lens, cautious Indian application, verdict on legal persistence vs practical constraint.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner basic 12 §13A, §13B, §13F (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** PT-2240; PT-2241; PT-2254; PT-2270.
**Trap:** Do not conclude sovereignty has ended; do not describe only economic globalisation.
**Mains hook:** The verdict is always graded.
**Back:** *"Examine the sequence, grade the verdict, never end it."*

### Flashcard PT-2269 — Quotation and proposition method (five moves)
**Front:** How should a stem that gives an attributed line on sovereignty be handled?
**Definition (learn):** Five moves: identify the proposition and the concept it targets; reconstruct the strongest argument for it; test the counterargument and conditions (hyperglobalist, sceptic, transformationalist triad); apply named evidence and label its period; give a graded verdict (holds, holds under conditions, fails).
**Breakdown:**
- Move 1: restate as one testable claim.
- Move 2: P1, P2, C.
- Move 3: necessary vs sufficient.
- Move 4: named evidence unit (Nkrumah 1965, OPEC 1960, NATO 1949 and Warsaw Pact 1955, IMF/World Bank, MNCs, WTO ⚠️, non-alignment).
- Move 5: graded verdict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner basic 12 §13H (✅ owner 12, local implementation of the master framework).
**Links:** PT-2254; PT-2268; PT-2240.
**Trap:** Never treat an unverified quotation as verbatim; use the three safe registers (verified; reliable attribution; uncertain attribution).
**Mains hook:** The method keeps quotation-based answers honest and structured.
**Back:** *"Restate, rebuild, test, evidence, grade."*

### Flashcard PT-2270 — Indian application: 1991 balance-of-payments crisis (cautious)
**Front:** How may India's 1991 external-payments crisis be used in a globalisation answer?
**Definition (learn):** India's 1991 balance-of-payments crisis and subsequent IMF-linked liberalisation is a commonly cited illustration of externally conditioned policy choice. Use it only as a general, widely known historical fact.
**Breakdown:**
- Event: 1991 balance-of-payments crisis (⚠️ general knowledge; not verified against a source here).
- Link: IMF-linked liberalisation (⚠️).
- Limit: Gauba's 2009 text does not detail the Indian case.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner basic 12 §G (⚠️ owner, not source-verified). Do not attach clauses, figures or tariff lines (owner 12 §G).
**Links:** PT-2247 (policy globalisation); PT-2250 (IMF conditionality); PT-2268.
**Trap:** Do not extend the answer into tariff lines or FTAs; those belong to Economy and IR files.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — one Indian illustration, labelled as cautious, applied to the conditionality objection.
**Back:** *"1991: India borrowed, and liberalised with conditions."*

### Flashcard PT-2271 — Application: a small state between two superpower camps
**Front:** Why can a formally independent small state still have constrained foreign policy?
**Definition (learn):** A small state placed between two hostile superpower camps may be forced to seek military guarantees, showing that formal independence can coexist with constrained foreign policy.
**Breakdown:**
- Setting: two hostile camps.
- Pressure: security guarantees.
- Result: constrained choice despite independence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner advanced 12 §9 (⚠️ hypothetical application, not a dated current anchor).
**Links:** PT-2242; PT-2243; PT-2244.
**Trap:** Hypotheticals are for explanation; do not present them as present-tense facts.
**Mains hook:** Use the hypothetical to show security dependence narrowing sovereignty.
**Back:** *"Between two camps: independent flag, borrowed guarantees."*

### Flashcard PT-2272 — Graduated sovereignty: legally equal, materially unequal
**Front:** What is the strongest analytical conclusion about sovereignty in the owner's advanced file?
**Definition (learn):** Sovereignty becomes graduated in capacity while remaining formally equal in law. Legal equality of states and actual inequality of power, capital, technology and security dependence coexist.
**Breakdown:**
- Legal: equal and recognised.
- Material: capacity graded by resources and dependence.
- Lesson: judge capacity, not only status.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner advanced 12 §7 (⚠️ analytical synthesis); juridical equality vs unequal capability (owner advanced 12 §1).
**Links:** PT-2248; PT-2234; PT-2262.
**Trap:** Do not say legal equality is meaningless; it is the precondition for the graded picture.
**Mains hook:** The juridical-material gap is the core advanced debate.
**Back:** *"Equal on paper, graded in power."*

### Flashcard PT-2273 — Figgis: churches and the pluralist state (outside owners 11-12)
**Front:** What is John Neville Figgis's pluralist contribution and his key work?
**Definition (learn):** Figgis is a source of the English pluralist state idea: associations such as churches have a standing of their own, not merely as creatures of the state. Laski and Cole took up his ideas. His key pluralist text is *Churches in the Modern State*.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *Churches in the Modern State* (1913) (✅ Wikipedia, Figgis, Works list).
- Influence: Figgis partly adapted the pluralist-state idea from Otto von Gierke; Laski and Cole took it up (✅ Wikipedia).
- Position: associational life beyond the state's command.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1913 (✅ Wikipedia). Death 13 April 1919 (✅ Wikipedia). The argument of *Churches in the Modern State* is not described on the page (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-2215 (Laski); PT-2224 (pluralism); PT-2274 (Cole); PT-2275 (Hirst).
**Trap:** Figgis is not in owners 11-12; cite him only as a precursor of the pluralist line discussed in owner 11.
**Mains hook:** Figgis gives the church-and-association case that makes the pluralist critique concrete.
**Back:** *"Figgis 1913: churches are not the state's servants."*

### Flashcard PT-2274 — G.D.H. Cole: guild socialism and functional democracy (outside owners 11-12)
**Front:** What is G.D.H. Cole's contribution to the pluralist and associational line?
**Definition (learn):** Cole theorised guild socialism, with production organised through worker guilds, and rejected both state socialism and syndicalism as risks of tyranny. He wanted democratic workplace and community units, with the state protecting consumers' rights and civil liberties.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *Social Theory* (1920); *Guild Socialism Restated* (1920); *Self-Government in Industry* (1917, revised 1920) (✅ Wikipedia bibliography).
- Position: rejects state socialism and syndicalism.
- Role of state: protects consumers and civil liberties.
- Later: first Chichele Professor of Social and Political Theory at Oxford, 1944 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1917, 1920 and 1944 years (✅ Wikipedia, G. D. H. Cole). Figgis as an influence on Cole is shown in the infobox, citing a 2017 chapter (✅ Wikipedia). The page does not use the term "pluralism" for Cole (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-2273 (Figgis); PT-2215 (Laski); PT-2224 (pluralism).
**Trap:** Cole's guild socialism is a theory of economic association, not an anti-state anarchism.
**Mains hook:** Cole shows the associational critique applied to industry and to the state's role as protector of rights.
**Back:** *"Cole: guilds run work, the state guards rights."*

### Flashcard PT-2275 — Paul Hirst: associative democracy and the statism-pluralism line (outside owners 11-12)
**Front:** What does Paul Hirst's associationalism say about the state, and when was *Associative Democracy* published?
**Definition (learn):** Hirst developed associationalism in the late 1980s and early 1990s as an alternative to state socialism and free-market liberalism, turning to the English political pluralists (Figgis, Cole, Laski). His *From Statism to Pluralism* (1997) carries the same argument.
**Breakdown:**
- Work: *Associative Democracy* (listed as 1993, Polity) (✅ Wikipedia bibliography; ⚠️ see conflict).
- Work: *From Statism to Pluralism* (1997) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Position: associations deliver public functions under democratic governance.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Source conflict: Wikipedia lists *Associative Democracy* as 1993; the year commonly given for the Polity edition is 1994 (⚠️ memory, not fetched). Verify before a dated citation. 1990 *Representative Democracy and its Limits* (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-2273; PT-2274; PT-2224 (pluralism); PT-2226 (limits).
**Trap:** Hirst's associationalism is not Laski's pluralism; it adds a proposal for democratic governance through associations.
**Mains hook:** Hirst is the contemporary bridge from the pluralist critique to a constructive associative model, but still needs a state.
**Back:** *"Hirst: from statism to associations, with the state still in the room."*

## POLITICAL THEORY — Part 3: State Perspectives, Obligation, Power, Citizenship and Rights

> Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` = repository owner text (basic unless marked "adv"); `(✅ Wikipedia: …)` = fetched page confirmation; `⚠️` = unverified, memory-only, or owner-vs-source conflict flagged inside the card.
> Time-sensitive counts (e.g. treaty parties) carry the as-of date of the page fetched (2025-12 snapshot).

### GROUP — 13 Diverse Perspectives on the State

### Flashcard PT-3001 — The State as a Contested Concept (six-test grid)
**Front:** Why does Gauba refuse to give a final verdict on the state, and what six tests make perspectives comparable?
**Definition (learn):** The state is a contested concept: origin, purpose, liberty, inequality, civil society and route to change are the six tests used to compare every theory of the state.
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: natural growth / contract / class division / colonial history / association.
- Purpose: good life / rights protection / market order / welfare / class rule / community / decolonisation / self-rule / gender justice / coordination.
- Liberty: obedience / civil liberty / negative / positive / emancipation / swaraj / freedom from patriarchy.
- Relation to society: above society / umpire / class instrument / coercive machine / one association among many.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba's chapter is Ch.10, PDF pp.220–279, Fifth Edition (2009) (✅ owner 13). The aim is to show complexity, not to pronounce a final verdict (PDF p.220) (✅ owner 13 adv).
**Links:** PT-3002 (organic), PT-3008 (laissez-faire), PT-3011 (Marxist), PT-3016 (Gandhian), PT-3021 (grid comparison), PT-3022 (neutral-umpire trap).
**Trap:** Do not present the liberal stream as one position; split it into contractarian, laissez-faire and welfare-liberal strands (PT-3010).
**Mains hook:** A 20-mark answer opens with "no single true description": the state is ethical whole, protector, welfare enabler, class instrument, patriarchal structure or coordinator, depending on the dimension foregrounded.
**Back:** *"Six tests, no verdict: origin, purpose, liberty, inequality, society, change."*

### Flashcard PT-3002 — Organic / Idealist Theory of the State
**Front:** What does the organic theory say about the state's nature and the individual's place in it?
**Definition (learn):** The state is a natural and ethical organism; individuals are its organs, and their worth depends on the whole (✅ owner 13).
**Breakdown:**
- State is natural (grows with civilised life) and ethically exalted, not a mere coercive device.
- Freedom is realised through obedience and functional differentiation.
- Often blurs state and society (state above society).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.221–225 (✅ owner 13). Gauba's critique: it subordinates conscience and can end in official absolutism (PDF pp.223–225) (✅ owner 13 adv).
**Links:** PT-3003 (Aristotle), PT-3004 (Burke, Hegel), PT-3026 (totalitarian risk), PT-3019 (pluralist contrast).
**Trap:** "Organic theory simply means patriotism" is wrong; it is a strong claim that the state is prior to and higher than the individual (✅ owner 13).
**Mains hook:** Organic theory explains social interdependence but naturalises hierarchy; its strongest problem is absorption of the person into the state.
**Back:** *"Organ in the body: state first, self through the whole."*

### Flashcard PT-3003 — Aristotle: the State for the Good Life
**Front:** Why, for Aristotle, does the state exist, and why is man intelligible only within the polis?
**Definition (learn):** The state exists for the sake of life and continues for the sake of the good life; the individual is not self-sufficient outside the political whole (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.221–222).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural institution: the polis is the framework of a complete human life.
- Man is a political animal, intelligible only within the polis.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.221–222 (✅ owner 13). Ancient polis citizenship was restricted (see the citizenship and rights cards (PT-3201 to PT-3278)).
**Links:** PT-3002 (organic), Aristotle's citizen, Greek exclusion.
**Trap:** Aristotle's "natural" state is not the modern liberal artificial state; it is the ancestor of the organic image.
**Mains hook:** Use Aristotle as the earliest "ethical end" theory of the state against later instrumental (contract, Marxist) accounts.
**Back:** *"Polis for life, polis for the good life."*

### Flashcard PT-3004 — Burke and Hegel: Historical Growth and Ethical Elevation
**Front:** How do Burke and Hegel differ in their treatment of the state as organism?
**Definition (learn):** Burke stresses the state's historical growth like an organism that cannot be dissected mechanically; Hegel raises the state's ethical image to an almost sacred level (✅ owner 13, PDF p.222).
**Breakdown:**
- Burke: historical, prescriptive, anti-mechanical; grows like an organism (also in rights theory, the citizenship and rights cards (PT-3201 to PT-3278)).
- Hegel: highest point of modern idealist elevation of the state; by identifying obedience with divine reason in the state, he tends to unlimited obligation (PDF p.284, PT-3038).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.222 (✅ owner 13); Hegel on obligation PDF p.284 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3002, PT-3038 (Hegel obligation), Burke rights.
**Trap:** Do not treat Burke's organicism as Hegel's: Burke is gradualist-conservative, Hegel is absolute-idealist.
**Mains hook:** Hegel's ethical state is the high-water mark of organic theory; Gauba's warning links it to fascistic demands for blind obedience (PT-3026).
**Back:** *"Burke grows, Hegel ascends."*

### Flashcard PT-3005 — Hobbes: Artificial Sovereign for Security
**Front:** What does Hobbes's social-contract theory of the state claim, and what liberty does it surrender?
**Definition (learn):** The state is an artificial device created by agreement to end the insecurity of the state of nature; absolute sovereignty secures peace by surrender of natural liberty (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.227–231).
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: contract out of the state of nature.
- Purpose: security and order.
- Liberty: narrowed for security (security-first).
- Route to change: none within the contract; revolt reopens anarchy (see PT-3035).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.227–236 (✅ owner 13 and adv).
**Links:** PT-3006 (Locke), PT-3007 (Rousseau), PT-3035 (Hobbes obligation), PT-3069 (trap: contract theorists).
**Trap:** "All contract theorists defend limited government" is false; Hobbes does not (PDF pp.230–234) (✅ owner 13).
**Mains hook:** Hobbes solves order by absolute sovereignty; the cost is that liberty is narrowed to security.
**Back:** *"Hobbes: peace bought with the whole liberty."*

### Flashcard PT-3006 — Locke: Trust, Limited Government and Resistance
**Front:** What is Locke's contract theory of the state, and when may people resist?
**Definition (learn):** Government is a trust to protect life, liberty and property; when the trust is seriously breached, people may resist and replace it (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF pp.231–232).
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: consensual, rights-preserving contract.
- Purpose: protect natural rights (life, liberty, property).
- Liberty: rights preserved, not surrendered.
- Route to change: replacement of government when trust fails.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Two Treatises of Government: first published December 1689, title page dated 1690; Laslett placed most composition in 1679–1683 (Exclusion Crisis) (✅ Wikipedia: Two Treatises of Government). Gauba cites Glorious Revolution 1688 as example (PDF p.232) (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** PT-3023 (dating trap), PT-3005 (Hobbes), PT-3036 (Locke obligation), Locke and citizenship, Locke rights.
**Trap:** Do not write that Locke wrote the treatises "to celebrate 1688"; use "associated with," "came to justify" or "published and read as vindicating" (⚠️ owner caution, confirmed by Wikipedia dating).
**Mains hook:** Locke gives the most sustainable consent-based basis for limited government and a right of resistance; it is the anchor for constitutional correctives.
**Back:** *"Locke: a trust, not a surrender."*

### Flashcard PT-3007 — Rousseau: General Will and Civil Liberty
**Front:** How does Rousseau locate sovereignty, and how does he change natural liberty?
**Definition (learn):** Sovereignty resides in the people as a collective whole (general will); civil liberty replaces natural liberty and is higher, yet the general will can absorb individual dissent (✅ owner 13 and adv, PDF pp.227–236).
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: contract; sovereignty of the collective.
- Liberty: natural liberty transformed into civil liberty.
- Obligation: to one's higher, civic self (PT-3037).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.227–234 (✅ owner 13 adv).
**Links:** PT-3005, PT-3006, PT-3037, citizenship.
**Trap:** Rousseau is popular-sovereign but absolutist in an important sense; do not classify him as a limited-government theorist like Locke (✅ owner 13).
**Mains hook:** Rousseau's general will is the strongest case for popular sovereignty but raises the problem of majoritarian absorption.
**Back:** *"Rousseau: free by obeying the general will."*

### Flashcard PT-3008 — Laissez-faire Liberal-Individualism (Night-watchman State)
**Front:** What is the laissez-faire view of the state, and who represents it?
**Definition (learn):** The state is a necessary evil whose task is protection, contract enforcement, law and order, not economic regulation (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.236–244).
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: artificial and limited.
- Purpose: protect life, property, contract and external order.
- Liberty: negative liberty and market freedom.
- Inequality: accepted if produced by free acquisition and exchange; Spencer radicalises this.
- Representatives: Adam Smith, Bentham, James Mill, Spencer, Nozick (PDF pp.237–244).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.236–244 (✅ owner 13); night-watchman / libertarian label (✅ owner 13 adv).
**Links:** PT-3010 (three liberal strands), PT-3009 (welfare revision), Nozick citizenship, Nozick rights.
**Trap:** "Liberalism always means minimal state" is wrong; Gauba separates laissez-faire from welfare liberalism (PDF pp.243–260) (✅ owner 13).
**Mains hook:** Laissez-faire is destabilised by its own outcomes (child labour, slums, insecurity), which pushed liberalism to welfare revision (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF pp.245–247).
**Back:** *"Night-watchman: protect, enforce, and keep out."*

### Flashcard PT-3009 — Welfare / Positive Liberal State
**Front:** What does the welfare or positive liberal view claim about the state and liberty?
**Definition (learn):** The state should create conditions for moral freedom and social welfare, especially for vulnerable sections; positive liberty means real capacity to develop and act well (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.245–260).
**Breakdown:**
- Thinkers: J.S. Mill, T.H. Green, Hobhouse, Laski, Maclver (PDF pp.249–260).
- Purpose: remove obstacles to self-development; secure welfare, education, social justice.
- Liberty: positive (enabling conditions).
- Society is morally prior to the state in Green and Maclver.
- Route: incremental democratic reform and welfare expansion.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.245–260 (✅ owner 13); Green: rights from moral community, state removes obstacles to self-realisation (PDF pp.251–255) (✅ owner 13 adv).
**Links:** PT-3008, PT-3010, PT-3039 (Green obligation), Laski rights.
**Trap:** Welfare liberalism can still understate deeper capitalist domination (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF p.260).
**Mains hook:** Use welfare liberalism to show that liberalism itself was revised; the Gauba "tragedy" is that welfare procedures can legitimise a capitalist order.
**Back:** *"Welfare liberal: enable, don't merely fence."*

### Flashcard PT-3010 — Three Strands of Liberal Individualism (contract / laissez-faire / welfare)
**Front:** Why must the broad liberal stream be split into three strands for exam answers?
**Definition (learn):** Gauba's broad liberal-individualist stream contains social-contract legitimacy, laissez-faire protection and welfare-liberal revision; these strands make different claims about neutrality, property and welfare.
**Breakdown:**
- Contractarian: origin by agreement; purpose security or rights (Hobbes/Locke/Rousseau).
- Laissez-faire: minimal state; property and market freedom.
- Welfare-liberal: positive liberty; redistribution and welfare regulation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Splitting is an analytical device, flagged ⚠️ in owner 13 (§5 and §8 notes).
**Links:** PT-3001, PT-3005–3009, PT-3021.
**Trap:** Do not collapse "the liberal state" into one position; do not treat welfare liberals as laissez-faire theorists.
**Mains hook:** A 15-mark liberal-vs-Marxist answer should use the three-strand split before comparing with class theory.
**Back:** *"Contract, cage, care: three liberal strands."*

### Flashcard PT-3011 — Marxist / Class Theory of the State
**Front:** What does the class theory of the state claim about origin, purpose and liberty?
**Definition (learn):** The state arises with private property and class division and serves dominant-class power; liberal freedom is formal and real freedom needs class emancipation (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.261–266).
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: private property and class division (PDF pp.261–263).
- Purpose: preserve dominant-class rule and suppress class conflict (PDF pp.262–266).
- Route: revolution, proletarian rule in transition, then withering away of the state (PDF pp.263–266).
- Thinkers: Marx, Engels, Lenin, Gramsci, Miliband, Poulantzas (PDF pp.261–267).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.261–267 (✅ owner 13); Leninist theory distinct from Marx (✅ owner 17 adv).
**Links:** PT-3012 (Gramsci), PT-3013 (Miliband vs Poulantzas), PT-3040 (Marxist anti-obligation), Marx on rights, PT-3079 (Marx on power).
**Trap:** Marxist and Gandhian both want a stateless end but differ in means and premises (PT-3028).
**Mains hook:** The 20-mark Marxist answer needs classical Marx first, then neo-Marxist refinement (relative autonomy), since most marks lie there (✅ owner 13 §13B).
**Back:** *"Class makes the state; revolution unmakes it."*

### Flashcard PT-3012 — Gramsci: Political Society and Civil Society
**Front:** How does Gramsci depart from classical Marxism on the state?
**Definition (learn):** Gramsci was the first neo-Marxist to concede relative autonomy of the state, distinguishing political society (force, direct domination through "juridical" government) from civil society (consent or hegemony through family, school and church) (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF p.266).
**Breakdown:**
- Political society: coercion.
- Civil society: consent; hegemony via family, school, church.
- Civil society can reproduce hegemony even under capitalism (PDF pp.266–267).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.266 (✅ owner 13); Gramsci's hegemony also used in power theory (PT-3080).
**Links:** PT-3011, PT-3013, PT-3080 (Gramsci power), PT-3095 (power over/to).
**Trap:** "Gramsci only repeats classical Marxism" is wrong; he gives relative autonomy to the superstructure (see PT-3099).
**Mains hook:** Hegemony explains why capitalist rule survives through consent, not only coercion.
**Back:** *"Force in political society, consent in civil society."*

### Flashcard PT-3013 — Miliband vs Poulantzas: Instrumentalism vs Structuralism
**Front:** What are Miliband's and Poulantzas's rival theories of the capitalist state, and what limit does Gauba add?
**Definition (learn):** Miliband (instrumentalism): the state is an instrument of class power because officials share the capitalist class background, the capitalist class wields direct economic power, and officials have vested interests. Poulantzas (structuralism): class domination is not automatically state power; the capitalist state has relative autonomy to secure legitimacy by invoking "the people" (✅ owner 13 §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Claim (Miliband, *The State in Capitalist Society*, 1969): instrumentalist mechanisms (1) class background of officials, (2) direct economic leverage, (3) vested interest in the system.
- Objection (Poulantzas, *Political Power and Social Classes*, French 1968; English 1973): structural dependence plus relative autonomy; the state becomes an arena of class struggle.
- Limit (Gauba's own caution): too much autonomy risks departing from Marxism's original tenets (PDF p.266–267).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Miliband 1969 (✅ Wikipedia). Poulantzas French 1968, English 1973 (✅ Wikipedia). Gauba's reconstruction PDF pp.266–267 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** PT-3011, PT-3012, PT-3014 (communitarian contrast not needed), PT-3021 (grid).
**Trap:** "Miliband and Poulantzas say the same thing" is wrong; Poulantzas explicitly rejects instrumentalism (✅ owner 13 §9).
**Mains hook:** Use claim → objection → limit as a template for the Marxist state; it shows that "the state serves capital" can mean capture or functional service.
**Back:** *"Miliband: state as tool. Poulantzas: state as arena, with leash."*

### Flashcard PT-3014 — Communitarian Perspective on the State
**Front:** What does communitarianism claim about the state, and who are its main thinkers?
**Definition (learn):** The self is socially embedded, so political order must attend to shared values, common good and community, not rights alone (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.267–270).
**Breakdown:**
- Attacks the atomistic, liberal "unencumbered" self.
- Purpose: sustain common good, shared identity and moral responsibility.
- Community, not mere association, is central.
- Thinkers: MacIntyre, Sandel, Walzer, Taylor (PDF pp.267–270).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.267–270 (✅ owner 13 and adv).
**Links:** PT-3027 (weakness), communitarian citizen, MacIntyre on rights, PT-3007 (general will contrast).
**Trap:** MacIntyre does not simply subordinate every right to community (✅ owner 17 adv).
**Mains hook:** Communitarianism restores moral seriousness but lacks a robust political mechanism (PT-3027).
**Back:** *"Self-in-community, not self-alone."*

### Flashcard PT-3015 — Post-colonial State and Macaulay (1835)
**Front:** How does the post-colonial perspective understand the state, and what example does Gauba give?
**Definition (learn):** The post-colonial state must be understood through colonial domination, neo-colonial dependence, nation-building and state-building (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.270–273).
**Breakdown:**
- Shaped by conquest, inherited borders, elites, institutions.
- Purpose: nation-building, state-building, developmental fulfilment.
- Freedom beyond formal independence: decolonising values and institutions.
- Macaulay's 1835 educational minute: colonial institutions could modernise and dominate at once (PDF pp.271–272).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Macaulay's minute 1835 (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.271–272).
**Links:** PT-3029 (paradox), illustration, PT-3016 (Gandhi on the state), Kymlicka context not needed.
**Trap:** Do not present colonial modernisation as pure benevolence; Gauba ties it to stabilising rule and neo-colonial continuity (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF pp.271–273).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — colonial inheritance and nation-building burden, kept conceptual (no constitutional article detail; owner 13 §13G).
**Back:** *"Empire leaves the state, and its habits."*

### Flashcard PT-3016 — Gandhian State: Coercive Machine and Swaraj
**Front:** Why does Gandhi regard the state as morally inferior, and what replaces it?
**Definition (learn):** The state is a coercive, centralised machine; the ideal is self-rule, self-discipline and decentralised swaraj, with the state at best a temporary means (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.273–277).
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: coercive and centralised political power.
- Purpose: at best a temporary means; ideal is self-regulated swaraj.
- Liberty: self-rule, self-restraint, capacity to resist abused authority.
- Inequality: moral as well as economic; answered by trusteeship and dignity of labour (PDF pp.274–275).
- Route to change: non-violent reform, self-purification, decentralised reconstruction.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.273–277 (✅ owner 13). Soulless machine of coercion (✅ owner 13 adv).
**Links:** PT-3028 (Gandhi vs Marxism), PT-3042 (civil disobedience), PT-3094 (swaraj as power to), PT-3025 (not anarchist).
**Trap:** Gandhi is not "just another anarchist"; his premises are ahimsa, religion, swaraj and satyagraha (✅ owner 13 §9).
**Mains hook:** Gandhian critique supplies moral limits on centralised coercion, but scaling decentralised swaraj to complex modern states is hard (✅ owner 13 adv, §6).
**Back:** *"Gandhi: state is a machine; swaraj is self-rule."*

### Flashcard PT-3017 — Kate Millett: Personal Is Political
**Front:** What is Kate Millett's feminist contribution to the state debate?
**Definition (learn):** Millett redefines politics as "power-structured relationships" and pushes analysis into the personal/intimate sphere; domination, not eroticism, is the subject of sexual relations (✅ owner 13 §6, PDF p.277).
**Breakdown:**
- Entry point: personal/intimate sphere and cultural patriarchy.
- Slogan: "the personal is political."
- Work: *Sexual Politics*.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives 1971 for *Sexual Politics* (✅ owner 13); ⚠️ conflict: Wikipedia states first appearance in 1970 (Doubleday, U.S.) (✅ Wikipedia: Sexual Politics). Flag the owner year.
**Links:** PT-3018 (Eisenstein), PT-3024 (distinct, not merged), PT-3090 (patriarchy), feminist critique of citizenship.
**Trap:** Do not present Millett and Eisenstein as making the identical argument (see PT-3024).
**Mains hook:** Use Millett for the personal-is-political / cultural-patriarchy dimension of the state's gender critique.
**Back:** *"Millett: the bedroom is a power structure."*

### Flashcard PT-3018 — Eisenstein: Liberal-State Neutrality and Socialist Feminism
**Front:** What does Zillah Eisenstein criticise in the liberal state?
**Definition (learn):** Eisenstein targets the liberal state's claimed neutrality: it presents itself as autonomous and equally open without examining women's share of power; her socialist-feminist extension argues capitalist welfare policy increases women's dependence and relies on women's unpaid domestic work (✅ owner 13 §6, PDF pp.277–278).
**Breakdown:**
- Entry point: political economy and claimed neutrality.
- Target: liberal state's neutrality.
- Socialist-feminist extension: welfare policy, dependence, unpaid labour.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *The Radical Future of Liberal Feminism* (1981) (✅ owner 13 §6). Not fetched; ✅ owner.
**Links:** PT-3017, PT-3024, PT-3087 (agenda).
**Trap:** Do not attribute to Eisenstein the personal-is-political slogan; that is Millett's entry point.
**Mains hook:** Use Eisenstein for the neutrality-critique and political-economy dimension of feminism.
**Back:** *"Eisenstein: neutral state, unpaid labour."*

### Flashcard PT-3019 — Pluralist State: Coordinator of Associations
**Front:** What is the pluralist view of the state, and who develops it?
**Definition (learn):** The state is not the sole centre of power; it coordinates and arbitrates among multiple associations and interests, justified by justice across groups (✅ owner 13, PDF pp.278–279).
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: modern democracy produces multiple centres of power.
- Purpose: coordinate, arbitrate, justify authority through justice across groups.
- Liberty: freedom of association indispensable.
- Civil society: bulwark of democratic order.
- Thinkers: Duguit, Laski, Maclver, Dahl and Lindblom (polyarchy) (PDF pp.278–279).
- Solidarity (Poland) and Eastern European environmental groups illustrate associational pressure (PDF p.278).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.278–279 (✅ owner 13 and adv). Dahl and Lindblom, *Politics, Economics and Welfare* (1953) (✅ owner 15, cross-used).
**Links:** PT-3020 (concession), PT-3030 (Solidarity), PT-3086 (power-chapter pluralism), PT-3002 (organic contrast).
**Trap:** "Pluralism proves the state is neutral" is wrong; even Gauba's pluralism admits unequal groups (✅ owner 13 §9).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — pluralism's coordinator role versus organic and class views, using the six tests.
**Back:** *"Pluralist state: umpire among many players."*

### Flashcard PT-3020 — Dahl and Lindblom: Pluralism's Own Concession (adv)
**Front:** How do later pluralists qualify the early pluralist picture of the state?
**Definition (learn):** Early pluralism underestimated unequal bargaining power; Dahl and Lindblom later admit privileged business or property positions in market-dominated polyarchies (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF p.279).
**Breakdown:**
- Early claim: power dispersed; groups bargain on near-equal footing.
- Qualification: unequal political resources; business privilege.
- Consequence: Dahl's recommendation of redistributive policies (see PT-3086).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.279 (✅ owner 13 adv); Lindblom *Politics and Markets* (1977) and Dahl *Dilemmas of Pluralist Democracy* (1982) (✅ owner 15 adv).
**Links:** PT-3019, PT-3086, PT-3098.
**Trap:** Do not describe pluralism as proof that all groups bargain equally (✅ owner 13 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** The concession shows pluralism can self-correct; the question is whether correction is enough.
**Back:** *"Pluralists admit: bargaining is not equal."*

### Flashcard PT-3021 — Comparison Grid: Six Tests Across Perspectives
**Front:** How do the perspectives compare on origin, purpose, liberty, inequality, civil society and route to change?
**Definition (learn):** A comparative matrix places organic, contract, laissez-faire, welfare-liberal, Marxist, communitarian, post-colonial, Gandhian, feminist and pluralist perspectives on the six tests (✅ owner 13 §8, owner 13 adv §8).
**Breakdown:**
- Organic: natural-historical origin; good life; obedience; state-society merge; organic growth.
- Contract: deliberate agreement; security/rights; narrowed or preserved liberty; route is constitutional replacement (Locke).
- Laissez-faire: minimal artificial protector; market freedom; accepted market inequality; roll back intervention.
- Welfare-liberal: interdependence origin; positive liberty; inequality corrected; democratic reform.
- Marxist: property/class origin; organise class domination; emancipation; revolution then withering away.
- Communitarian: embedded selves; common good; community; moral reconstruction.
- Post-colonial: empire and inherited elites; nation-building; decolonisation.
- Gandhian: coercive origin; self-rule; swaraj; non-violent reform.
- Feminist: gendered origin; protection from patriarchy; family political; legal reform plus transformation.
- Pluralist: dispersed power; coordination; association; democratic correction.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner tables (✅ owner 13 and adv).
**Links:** PT-3001, PT-3010, PT-3011, PT-3016, PT-3019.
**Trap:** Marking every row ✅ with no evidence; the owner marks some cells ⚠️ as analytical inference (use those flags).
**Mains hook:** 15-marker: pick three perspectives and run the six-test grid; end with a graded verdict.
**Back:** *"Six tests, ten lenses, one grid."*

### Flashcard PT-3022 — Trap: "The Liberal State Is Neutral"
**Front:** Is liberal neutrality a settled description of liberal states?
**Definition (learn):** No. Neutrality is a contested liberal aspiration, not an uncontested description; welfare, Marxist, feminist and pluralist critiques show bias and structural inequality persist (✅ owner 13 §5, PDF pp.245–279).
**Breakdown:**
- Claim (liberal neutrality): the state is an impartial umpire.
- Evidence: welfare debate, Marxist class bias, Eisenstein's neutrality critique, pluralist unequal groups.
- Verdict: the aspiration is partly valid, the descriptive claim is not.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 13 §5 and §13E (✅).
**Links:** PT-3001, PT-3018, PT-3019, PT-3020.
**Trap:** This card is itself the trap; do not answer with "the state is neutral" as fact.
**Mains hook:** A qualified line: liberal neutrality is an aspiration whose limits the critics expose.
**Back:** *"Umpire is a hope, not a fact."*

### Flashcard PT-3023 — Trap: Locke Wrote After 1688 to Celebrate It?
**Front:** Did Locke write the Two Treatises after 1688 to celebrate the Glorious Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** No. Most of the text was composed c.1679–1683, before the Revolution; it was published in 1689 (title page 1690) and read as the Revolution's philosophical defence (⚠️ owner 13 §7; ✅ Wikipedia: Two Treatises of Government).
**Breakdown:**
- Composition: Laslett's dating, c.1679–83, Exclusion Crisis.
- Publication: December 1689; title page 1690.
- Later reception: read as vindicating the Revolution Settlement.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1679–83 composition; 1689 publication; 1688 Glorious Revolution (✅ owner 13; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3006, PT-3036.
**Trap:** This is the trap itself; the correct exam phrasing is "came to justify" or "read as vindicating."
**Mains hook:** Use Locke's dating to show that consent theory preceded the event it was later used to explain.
**Back:** *"Written before, printed after, read as the defence."*

### Flashcard PT-3024 — Trap: Millett and Eisenstein Are Not the Same Argument
**Front:** How do Millett and Eisenstein differ within feminist critiques of the state?
**Definition (learn):** Millett's entry point is the personal/intimate sphere and "power-structured relationships" (personal is political); Eisenstein's is the liberal state's claimed neutrality and a socialist-feminist critique of capitalist welfare policy (✅ owner 13 §6, §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Millett: cultural patriarchy; intimate domination.
- Eisenstein: neutrality claim; political economy; unpaid labour.
- Use Millett for the personal dimension, Eisenstein for the liberal-state dimension.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Millett owner year 1971 (⚠️ Wikipedia 1970); Eisenstein 1981 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** PT-3017, PT-3018, PT-3090.
**Trap:** Merging the two into one "feminist view of the state" loses marks at 15 and 20 marks.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish Millett from Eisenstein explicitly in a feminist-critique answer.
**Back:** *"Millett: the home. Eisenstein: the neutral office."*

### Flashcard PT-3025 — Trap: Organic Theory Is Not Patriotism; Gandhi Is Not an Anarchist
**Front:** Why are "organic theory means patriotism" and "Gandhi was just another anarchist" both wrong?
**Definition (learn):** Organic theory is a strong claim that the state is natural, ethically prior and superior to the individual; Gandhi shares suspicion of coercive authority but grounds it in ahimsa, religion, swaraj and satyagraha, not Bakunin's or Kropotkin's premises (✅ owner 13 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Organic: state prior to individual; obedience as freedom.
- Gandhi: stateless aspiration shared with anarchists but different means, moral anthropology and religion (PDF pp.274–276).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.221–223; PDF pp.274–277 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** PT-3002, PT-3016, PT-3028, PT-3041 (anarchist).
**Trap:** The owner text itself names this as the mistake; never reduce Gandhi to anarchism.
**Mains hook:** Use the distinction to show that the anti-state tradition is not one doctrine.
**Back:** *"Organic is not patriot; Gandhi is not anarchist."*

### Flashcard PT-3026 — Organic / Hegelian Statism: Totalitarian Risk (adv)
**Front:** Why does Gauba treat organic and Hegelian statism as dangerous?
**Definition (learn):** The organic view subordinates conscience, confuses state with society and can end in official absolutism; Hegelian statism collapses moral reason into the state and can be recruited by fascistic demands for blind obedience (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF pp.223–225; owner 14 adv PDF p.284).
**Breakdown:**
- Organic risk: absorption of the person into the whole.
- Hegelian risk: state = divine reason; obedience as freedom.
- Historical warning: Nazi and Fascist experiences (PDF p.224) (✅ owner 13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.223–225, p.224 (Nazi/Fascist warning) (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** PT-3002, PT-3004, PT-3038 (Hegel obligation).
**Trap:** Do not present organic theory as the same as totalitarianism; it is the nearest ancestor of the risk.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — state the organic image's strength (social interdependence) and its absorption risk.
**Back:** *"Whole swallows the part."*

### Flashcard PT-3027 — Communitarian Weakness: No Political Mechanism (adv)
**Front:** Why does Gauba see communitarianism as stronger in moral philosophy than in political philosophy?
**Definition (learn):** Communitarianism restores moral seriousness but lacks a robust institutional mechanism to guarantee its ethics (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF p.270).
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: corrects atomistic liberalism; community and shared meanings.
- Weakness: no institutional guarantee; hard to scale to large plural societies.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.270 (✅ owner 13 adv).
**Links:** PT-3014, communitarian citizen.
**Trap:** Do not present communitarianism as "community overrides every right" (see the citizenship and rights cards (PT-3201 to PT-3278)).
**Mains hook:** Communitarianism's answer must add institutional design, else it remains moral advice.
**Back:** *"Community without a lever is a sermon."*

### Flashcard PT-3028 — Gandhi and Marx: Shared Stateless Aspiration, Different Means (adv)
**Front:** Where do Gandhi and Marxism converge and diverge on the state?
**Definition (learn):** Both aspire to a stateless or state-diminished order, but differ radically in means, moral anthropology, property, labour and religion (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF pp.274–276).
**Breakdown:**
- Shared: critique of centralised coercion; stateless aspiration.
- Divergent: Gandhi's non-violence and self-purification vs Marxist revolution; religion vs materialism; trusteeship vs class abolition.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.274–276 (✅ owner 13 adv).
**Links:** PT-3011, PT-3016, PT-3025.
**Trap:** Do not claim Gandhi and Marx use the same means.
**Mains hook:** Use the divergence to answer a "compare Marxist and Gandhian criticisms of centralised power" question.
**Back:** *"Same horizon, different road."*

### Flashcard PT-3029 — Post-colonial Paradox: Subjugation and Nationalism (adv)
**Front:** How can colonial institutions both subjugate and create the conditions for nationalism?
**Definition (learn):** Colonial institutions dominated subjects but unintentionally generated modern consciousness and nationalism through education and administration (✅ owner 13 adv, PDF pp.271–273).
**Breakdown:**
- Subjugation: stabilised rule, elite inheritance.
- Generation: colonial education and administrative ideas used against the empire.
- Post-independence: inherited bureaucracy and elites; civil society can inherit elite bias.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.271–273 (✅ owner 13 adv); Macaulay 1835 (PDF pp.271–272).
**Links:** PT-3015, PT-3030.
**Trap:** Do not treat colonial modernisation as pure benevolence (see PT-3015).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — paradox used to show colonial legacy is a mixed inheritance, not one-sided.
**Back:** *"Empire taught the lesson it did not intend."*

### Flashcard PT-3030 — Solidarity (Poland) and Associational Pressure (pluralist illustration)
**Front:** How does Gauba illustrate pluralist associational pressure on authoritarian systems?
**Definition (learn):** Solidarity in Poland and environmental groups in Eastern Europe are book-period illustrations of associational pressure helping authoritarian systems give way to pluralist democracy (✅ owner 13, PDF p.278).
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: independent associations as the bulwark of civil society.
- Outcome (book-period): pressure toward pluralist democracy.
- Caveat: book-period illustration, not a present-tense claim.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.278 (✅ owner 13). Not independently re-dated here (⚠️ book-period).
**Links:** PT-3019, PT-3029.
**Trap:** Do not present the Solidarity example as an undated current fact.
**Mains hook:** Use it as evidence for the pluralist claim that civil society is a democratic bulwark.
**Back:** *"Associations push; authoritarians bend."*

### GROUP — 14 Political Obligation, Resistance and Law

### Flashcard PT-3031 — Political Obligation: Spectrum and Separate Concepts
**Front:** What is political obligation, and why must obedience, legitimacy, resistance and legal validity be kept apart?
**Definition (learn):** Political obligation is the duty to obey political authority and the laws needed to maintain institutions; the question is how far, when and why one must obey (✅ owner 14, PDF p.280).
**Breakdown:**
- Unlimited views: force, divine right, strong idealist or absolutist consent.
- Limited views: Locke, Green, cautious constitutional resistance, Gandhi.
- Anti-obligation: Marxist and anarchist positions against coercive class-state or organised authority.
- Separate: obedience (behaviour), moral legitimacy, resistance, legal validity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba Ch.11–12, PDF pp.280–300 (✅ owner 14); *Socio-Political Philosophy* PDF pp.164–167 for punishment (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3032 to PT-3041 (positions), PT-3046 (modes of resistance), PT-3068 (validity vs justice).
**Trap:** "Political obligation, resistance, civil disobedience and revolution are interchangeable" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — identify the rung of the obligation ladder the question's scenario occupies, then evaluate.
**Back:** *"Why, when, how far: unlimited, limited, or none."*

### Flashcard PT-3032 — Force-based Obligation (Force Theory)
**Front:** Why does Gauba reject force as a complete theory of political obligation?
**Definition (learn):** Obedience grounded in the superior coercive power of the state; fear may secure compliance but cannot secure morally reasoned obedience (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.280–281).
**Breakdown:**
- Explains compliance under domination.
- Cannot explain moral obligation or judgment of unjust commands.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.280–281 (✅ owner 14 adv).
**Links:** PT-3031, PT-3033, PT-3034.
**Trap:** Do not equate force-based compliance with political obligation (✅ owner 14 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Use force theory as the baseline for why consent and common good are needed.
**Back:** *"Fear obeys; reason consents."*

### Flashcard PT-3033 — Divine-Right Obligation
**Front:** What is the divine-right basis of obligation and why does Gauba reject it?
**Definition (learn):** Authority is sacred and obedience is owed to God-given rule; it sanctifies authority without allowing rational scrutiny of unjust rule (✅ owner 14, PDF p.281).
**Breakdown:**
- Explains traditional reverence.
- Denies critical judgment and secular legitimacy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.281 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3031, PT-3032, PT-3034.
**Trap:** Divine right is "unlimited", not "limited by religion" (see PT-3031 spectrum).
**Mains hook:** Use it as the classical example of obligation that cannot be questioned.
**Back:** *"Sacred rule, unquestioned obedience."*

### Flashcard PT-3034 — Conservative Prudence: Order over Ideal Justice
**Front:** Why does conservative thought lean toward obedience?
**Definition (learn):** Conservatism appeals to prudence, continuity and legitimacy, and prefers obedience because disorder is judged worse than many defects of government (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.281–282).
**Breakdown:**
- Values: order, continuity, legitimacy.
- Warns against reckless revolution.
- Weakness: tolerates too much injustice for the sake of order.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.281–282 (✅ owner 14 adv).
**Links:** PT-3031, PT-3046, PT-3006 (Locke's trust).
**Trap:** Conservatism here is not the same as divine right; it is prudential.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — conservatism as the strongest case against resistance; then show its limit.
**Back:** *"Order first, but not at any price."*

### Flashcard PT-3035 — Hobbes: Strong Obligation through Consent to Sovereign
**Front:** Why is Hobbes's obligation strong?
**Definition (learn):** People consent to a sovereign for self-preservation and cannot safely recover natural powers without anarchy; obligation is therefore broad (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.283–284).
**Breakdown:**
- Basis: consent given to escape insecurity.
- Scope: broad; revolt reopens anarchy.
- Contrast: Locke (conditional) and Rousseau (general will).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.283–284 (✅ owner 14 and adv).
**Links:** PT-3005 (Hobbes state), PT-3036 (Locke), PT-3037 (Rousseau), PT-3069 (trap).
**Trap:** Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau are not the same consent model.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — Hobbes vs Locke, with Rousseau added to show consent does different work.
**Back:** *"Hobbes: consent once, obey always."*

### Flashcard PT-3036 — Locke: Conditional Obligation and Resistance
**Front:** Why is Locke's obligation limited, and what does Gauba prefer?
**Definition (learn):** Government is a trust for life, liberty and property; when it fails, people may resist and replace it. Gauba judges Locke's version the clearest limited form because it ties government to ongoing consent and rights (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.283–284; adv PDF p.284).
**Breakdown:**
- Consent: continuous, not one decisive surrender.
- Limit: breach of trust legitimises replacement.
- Context: Locke's text published 1689 (see PT-3023).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.283–284 (✅ owner 14); Locke's publication date (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3006, PT-3035, PT-3037, PT-3087 (no).
**Trap:** Do not attach a 1688 "written to celebrate" claim to Locke (see PT-3023).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — "Why Locke is more sustainable for modern democracy than Hobbes or Rousseau" (adv prompt).
**Back:** *"Locke: obey while the trust holds."*

### Flashcard PT-3037 — Rousseau: Obligation to the General Will
**Front:** How does Rousseau frame political obligation?
**Definition (learn):** Obligation is owed to the general will, so obedience is obedience to one's higher, civic self; strict, self-imposed obligation (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.283–284; adv).
**Breakdown:**
- Sovereignty in the people, not an external ruler.
- Civic freedom replaces raw natural liberty.
- Risk: general will can absorb individual dissent.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.283–284 (✅ owner 14 adv).
**Links:** PT-3007, PT-3035, PT-3036.
**Trap:** Rousseau's self-imposed obligation is not Locke's limited, rights-based obligation.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — Rousseau as the "strict but self-imposed" model.
**Back:** *"Rousseau: obey yourself as citizen."*

### Flashcard PT-3038 — Hegel: Unlimited Obligation through Divine Reason
**Front:** Why does Hegel tend toward unlimited political obligation?
**Definition (learn):** By identifying obedience with the realisation of divine reason in the state, Hegel collapses moral freedom into obedience and risks unconditional statism (✅ owner 14, PDF p.284; adv).
**Breakdown:**
- State is divine reason embodied.
- Moral freedom = obedience.
- Gauba warns this can be recruited by fascistic demands for blind obedience (PDF p.284).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.284 (✅ owner 14 adv).
**Links:** PT-3004, PT-3026, PT-3039.
**Trap:** Hegel is not a "limited" idealist; compare Green.
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — Hegel as the unlimited end of the idealist spectrum.
**Back:** *"Hegel: state as reason, obedience as freedom."*

### Flashcard PT-3039 — T.H. Green: Obligation to the Common Good
**Front:** How does Green limit political obligation?
**Definition (learn):** Obligation is owed only to laws that serve the common good; unjust commands need not bind conscience; society, not government alone, is the moral pivot (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.284–286).
**Breakdown:**
- Idealist moderation of Hegel.
- Denies unconditional government claims.
- Limit: presupposes a unified moral consciousness hard to find in plural societies (PDF pp.285–286) (✅ owner 14 adv).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.284–286 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3009 (Green welfare), PT-3038, Green rights.
**Trap:** Green is not Hegel; the owner separates them as radical vs moderate idealism.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — Hegel vs Green as two idealist answers.
**Back:** *"Green: common good, not command."*

### Flashcard PT-3040 — Marxist Anti-obligation: Duty to the Working Class
**Front:** Why do Marxists deny obligation to the existing state?
**Definition (learn):** In a class-divided society, obligation is not owed to the capitalist state but to the working class and its emancipatory struggle (✅ owner 14, PDF p.286).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: class-state.
- Duty: revolutionary solidarity.
- Critique reveals class bias in "public" authority; weakness: underplays plural moral communities.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.286 (✅ owner 14 and adv).
**Links:** PT-3011, PT-3041, PT-3013.
**Trap:** Do not present Marxists as anarchists; both deny obligation for different reasons.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — Marxist vs anarchist anti-obligation; what replaces authority.
**Back:** *"Marx denies the class-state its claim."*

### Flashcard PT-3041 — Anarchist Anti-obligation: No Obedience to Organised Coercion
**Front:** Why do anarchists deny obligation to organised authority?
**Definition (learn):** All organised coercive authority is illegitimate; the individual owes justice and cooperation, not obedience to the state (✅ owner 14, PDF p.286).
**Breakdown:**
- Keeps coercion under relentless suspicion.
- Weakness: hard to institutionalise stable large-scale order.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.286 (✅ owner 14 adv).
**Links:** PT-3040, PT-3025, PT-3016.
**Trap:** Anarchism's "justice without government" is not Gandhi's disciplined civil disobedience (PT-3042).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — anarchist critique plus the institutional-order problem.
**Back:** *"No master, no duty to obey."*

### Flashcard PT-3042 — Gandhian Civil Disobedience: Conditions of Principled Resistance
**Front:** What conditions make disobedience of an unjust law principled, according to Gandhi as reconstructed by Gauba?
**Definition (learn):** Civil disobedience is deliberate, public and non-violent disobedience of an unjust law or policy, with willingness to accept penalty; the law is broken openly, after persuasion fails, without sectional selfishness, aiming at change of heart (✅ owner 14, PDF p.287; adv PDF p.287).
**Breakdown:**
- Open (public), non-violent, penalty-accepting.
- Last resort: after persuasion fails.
- Presupposes respect for law in general; opposes only specific injustice.
- No sectional selfishness; aims at change of heart, not destruction.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.287 (✅ owner 14 and adv).
**Links:** PT-3043 (Thoreau), PT-3045 (salt satyagraha), PT-3046 (comparison), PT-3047 (caution), PT-3016 (Gandhi state).
**Trap:** "Any illegal act done for a cause" is not civil disobedience (PT-3067).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — "Is civil disobedience justified?" applying each condition as a test.
**Back:** *"Open, peaceful, last, penalty-accepting, selfless."*

### Flashcard PT-3043 — Thoreau: Conscience and Tax Refusal
**Front:** What is Thoreau's example in Gauba's account of principled disobedience?
**Definition (learn):** Thoreau refuses to pay taxes, is imprisoned, and makes conscience a direct source of justified non-compliance (✅ owner 14, PDF p.287; adv).
**Breakdown:**
- Act: personal refusal to support injustice.
- Consequence: imprisonment, accepted.
- Gandhi shares the model of principled disobedience.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.287 (✅ owner 14). Owner gives no year for Thoreau's act; do not add one.
**Links:** PT-3042, PT-3044 (conscientious objection).
**Trap:** Thoreau's refusal is conscience-led; do not describe it as secret or violent.
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — Thoreau as the individual-conscience model of civil disobedience.
**Back:** *"Thoreau: the tax refusal that goes to jail."*

### Flashcard PT-3044 — Conscientious Objection
**Front:** What is conscientious objection, and what example does Gauba give?
**Definition (learn):** Refusal to comply with a legal duty because conscience forbids participation, as in objection to military service (✅ owner 14, PDF p.287).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: a particular legal duty.
- Method: personal refusal; accepts punishment rather than compliance.
- Example: conscientious objection to military service during the First World War (PDF p.287).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** First World War example (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3042, PT-3043, PT-3046.
**Trap:** Conscientious objection targets one duty; civil disobedience targets an unjust law; revolution targets the whole order (PT-3046).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — legal duty vs conscience.
**Back:** *"Conscience refuses the duty, not the state."*

### Flashcard PT-3045 — Gandhi's 1930 Salt-Law Defiance
**Front:** What is Gauba's clearest illustration of public non-violent civil disobedience against colonial law?
**Definition (learn):** Gandhi's 1930 salt-law defiance (salt march) against colonial salt law (✅ owner 14, PDF p.287).
**Breakdown:**
- Public, non-violent, penalty-accepting.
- Against a colonial law.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1930 (✅ owner 14). Do not extend to unverified participant numbers or later legal aftermath (⚠️ owner 14 §13G).
**Links:** PT-3042, PT-3016.
**Trap:** Do not invent salt-march numbers or outcomes.
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — Gandhi's salt satyagraha as a textbook case of the four conditions.
**Back:** *"Salt in 1930: small law, large defiance."*

### Flashcard PT-3046 — Resistance vs Revolution vs Civil Disobedience vs Conscientious Objection
**Front:** How do the four modes of resistance differ by target, method and relation to law?
**Definition (learn):** Resistance targets a specific unjust act or law; revolution targets the wider order; civil disobedience is public, non-violent, penalty-accepting; conscientious objection refuses a particular duty (✅ owner 14 §8B, PDF pp.287–288).
**Breakdown:**
- Resistance: specific command or abuse; may remain within law or move against it.
- Revolution: whole political/social order; goes beyond ordinary legal correction; highest risk.
- Civil disobedience: unjust law or policy; breaks specific law while respecting law generally.
- Conscientious objection: a particular duty; accepts punishment.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.287–288 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3031, PT-3042, PT-3044, PT-3047.
**Trap:** Keep the four analytically separate; "resistance = revolution" is the common confusion.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — "Distinguish resistance from revolution" using the target/method/relation grid.
**Back:** *"Target, method, law: four modes, not one."*

### Flashcard PT-3047 — Gauba's Caution: Resistance Must Be Disciplined
**Front:** What limits does Gauba place on resistance?
**Definition (learn):** Resistance must be publicly reasoned, proportionate, constitutionally preceded and not so destructive that it unleashes chaos worse than the grievance (✅ owner 14 adv, PDF pp.287–288).
**Breakdown:**
- Failure to deliver full employment, universal education or medical care alone is not sufficient ground for resistance (PDF p.288).
- Political authority is encountered through fallible officials; caution is needed (✅ owner 14 §5).
- Laski reminds that claims of state pre-eminence are often claims of fallible governments (adv, PDF p.288).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.287–288 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3042, PT-3046, PT-3031.
**Trap:** Do not treat every policy failure as a licence to resist.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — "Is civil disobedience justified?" with Gauba's caution as the limit.
**Back:** *"Grievance is not yet a warrant."*

### Flashcard PT-3048 — Descriptive vs Prescriptive Law
**Front:** What is the difference between descriptive (scientific) law and prescriptive law?
**Definition (learn):** Descriptive law describes regularities in nature or social science and can be tested as true or false; prescriptive law is a humanly made rule, backed by sanctions, telling people what to do or refrain from doing (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.289–290; adv PDF p.289).
**Breakdown:**
- Descriptive: discovered regularity.
- Prescriptive: humanly made, sanction-backed; central to politics and jurisprudence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.289–290 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3049 (schools), PT-3050 (Austin).
**Trap:** Prescriptive law is not a scientific law; do not confuse them in a "nature of law" answer.
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — define prescriptive law first.
**Back:** *"Describe the regularity; prescribe the rule."*

### Flashcard PT-3049 — Natural Law School
**Front:** What does the natural law school claim about law's authority?
**Definition (learn):** Law draws authority from a higher moral order discoverable by reason; valid law answers to higher justice (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.290–291).
**Breakdown:**
- Key names: Stoics, Roman jurists, Kant-style rational morality (✅ owner 14 §8C).
- Strength: preserves moral criticism of law.
- Risk: too abstract if detached from institutions; lacks procedural precision.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.290–291 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3050, PT-3051, PT-3068.
**Trap:** Natural law does not mean "all law is natural"; it asks whether law is just.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — natural law vs positivism on legal validity and justice.
**Back:** *"Natural law asks: is it just?"*

### Flashcard PT-3050 — Austin: Command Theory of Law
**Front:** What is Austin's command theory, and why does it exclude international law from "law"?
**Definition (learn):** Law is the command of a determinate sovereign, promulgated in a political society and backed by sanction; disobedience must be punishable. International law is at best "positive morality" because it lacks a promulgating sovereign and enforcing superior (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.291–292; adv PDF p.291).
**Breakdown:**
- Command + sovereign + sanction.
- Austin denied only a legal superior, not all moral limits (✅ owner 14 §13B).
- Hart's objection: model based on criminal law (PT-3052).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.291–292 (✅ owner 14). Austin's date not given in owner; do not add one.
**Links:** PT-3051, PT-3052, PT-3065 (trap).
**Trap:** "Austin's command theory and Hart's rule theory are the same" is wrong.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — Austin's command model and Hart's critique.
**Back:** *"Command plus sanction equals law, says Austin."*

### Flashcard PT-3051 — Kelsen: Basic Norm and Hierarchy of Norms
**Front:** How does Kelsen ground legal validity?
**Definition (learn):** Validity comes from proper promulgation and conformity to a basic norm (Grundnorm) within a hierarchy in which each lower norm derives validity from a higher one; Kelsen stays a positivist by separating validity from morality (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.291–292).
**Breakdown:**
- Hierarchy of norms terminating in Grundnorm.
- Positivist: basic norm is not a moral standard.
- Book: *General Theory of Law and State*, first English publication 1945 (Harvard, tr. Anders Wedberg) (✅ owner 14 §6, ⚠️ not fetched).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1945 English edition (✅ owner 14); not attested by fetch (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-3050, PT-3052, PT-3064 (trap).
**Trap:** Kelsen's book is not a 1961 work; 1961 is Hart's (PT-3064).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — Austin → Kelsen as positivist refinement.
**Back:** *"Kelsen: validity climbs to the Grundnorm."*

### Flashcard PT-3052 — Hart: Structure of Rules and Primary/Secondary Rules
**Front:** How does Hart replace Austin's command model?
**Definition (learn):** Law is a structure of rules; civil law confers powers rather than only commanding and punishing; Hart's technical vocabulary distinguishes primary rules (duties on conduct) from secondary rules (rule of recognition, rules of change, rules of adjudication) (✅ owner 14 §6; primary/secondary ✅ Wikipedia: The Concept of Law).
**Breakdown:**
- Critique: Austin's model too narrow, built on criminal law.
- Rule of recognition: identifies valid law, rests on social fact.
- Rules of change and adjudication.
- Dworkin's critique follows (PT-3053).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *The Concept of Law*, first published 1961 (✅ Wikipedia: The Concept of Law). Primary/secondary vocabulary is a precision addition (⚠️ owner 14 §6).
**Links:** PT-3050, PT-3051, PT-3053, PT-3064.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the primary/secondary vocabulary word-for-word to Gauba's paraphrase.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — Austin vs Hart; add Dworkin for 20 marks.
**Back:** *"Hart: primary duties, secondary rules about rules."*

### Flashcard PT-3053 — Dworkin: Principles in Hard Cases
**Front:** What is Dworkin's challenge to positivism?
**Definition (learn):** Source-based rules and a rule of recognition do not exhaust the standards judges use in hard cases; judges rely on principles (e.g., a man should not benefit from his own wrong), which carry weight, can conflict and are not hierarchically ordered; judicial discretion is bounded, not free legislation (✅ owner 14 §6, PDF p.292).
**Breakdown:**
- Principles are part of law (not extra-legal).
- Unlike rules: weight, conflict, no strict hierarchy.
- Judicial reasoning constrained by legal fit and principled justification.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *Taking Rights Seriously* (1977) (✅ owner 14, not fetched).
**Links:** PT-3052, PT-3050, PT-3065.
**Trap:** "Dworkin says judges can decide however they like" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — Austin → Kelsen/Hart → Dworkin as the full sequence.
**Back:** *"Dworkin: principles bind hard cases."*

### Flashcard PT-3054 — Historical Jurisprudence: Savigny and Maine
**Front:** What is historical jurisprudence?
**Definition (learn):** Law must be traced to social history, custom and legal evolution; law evolves from the life of the people (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.292–293).
**Breakdown:**
- Key names: Savigny, Maine.
- Strength: law grounded in social growth.
- Risk: over-romanticises inherited norms.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.292–293 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3055, PT-3056, PT-3058.
**Trap:** Historical jurisprudence does not validate every custom; longevity is not justification (see Burke the citizenship and rights cards (PT-3201 to PT-3278)).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — history vs command as the source of law.
**Back:** *"Custom made the law's roots."*

### Flashcard PT-3055 — Sociological Jurisprudence: Law as Social Engineering
**Front:** What is sociological jurisprudence?
**Definition (learn):** Law is an instrument of social purpose and social engineering, not merely sovereign command; judged by social effects (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.293–294).
**Breakdown:**
- Key names: Duguit, Pound, Laski.
- Strength: law linked to social ends.
- Risk: formal validity underplayed if used carelessly.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.293–294 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3054, PT-3056, PT-3019 (Duguit in pluralism).
**Trap:** Pound and Laski are not the same as Savigny; different schools.
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — law judged by social effects.
**Back:** *"Law is a tool for social purpose."*

### Flashcard PT-3056 — Four-School Grid and Its Classification Caution
**Front:** What is the four-school grid, and how should it be used?
**Definition (learn):** Natural law, analytical/positivist, historical and sociological schools organise jurisprudence in Gauba's teaching structure; it is one defensible scheme, not the only or final classification (✅ owner 14 §8C, ⚠️ classification caution).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural: higher justice.
- Analytical: source, form, sanction.
- Historical: evolution.
- Sociological: social purpose.
- Other courses add legal realism, critical legal studies, and treat Dworkin as interpretivist.
