**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 14 §8C (✅); classification caution (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-3049 to PT-3055, PT-3053.
**Trap:** Presenting the grid as the only classification of jurisprudence.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — "critically examine schools of jurisprudence": use the grid, then caution.
**Back:** *"Four schools, one scheme, not the final map."*

### Flashcard PT-3057 — Rule of Law: Known, General, Prospective, Restrained
**Front:** What is the rule of law, and what operational tests does it imply?
**Definition (learn):** Governance through known, general, non-retrospective law and legally restrained power, not arbitrary will (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.298–300; owner 14 adv §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Tests: notification, generality, prospectivity, restrained power.
- Gauba reads Dicey more broadly than procedure alone; rights-protection included (✅ owner 14 adv §12).
- Traffic example shows generality does not exclude differentiated regulation (PDF p.300).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.298–300 (✅ owner 14 and adv). Dicey named in owner (✅ owner 14 adv).
**Links:** PT-3058, PT-3047, PT-3068.
**Trap:** "Rule of law means identical rights and duties everywhere" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — list operational tests, not slogans.
**Back:** *"Known, general, forward-looking, restrained."*

### Flashcard PT-3058 — Law, Morality and Liberty: Fourfold Reasoning
**Front:** How does Gauba relate law to morality and liberty?
**Definition (learn):** Law restrains some actions to secure equal liberty and other goods; legality and morality are conceptually separable but often overlap; a law may protect equal freedom, resolve conflict, regulate economic abuse or secure welfare (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.296–298; adv §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Examples: child marriage, widow-burning, untouchability, bonded labour reformed by law (PDF p.296).
- Law does restrain liberty but to secure liberty more broadly.
- Caveat: arbitrary or morally stagnant law becomes tyrannical (adv PDF p.300).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.296–300 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3068, PT-3057, PT-3053.
**Trap:** "Law always reduces liberty" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — "Rule of law restrains liberty to secure liberty" evaluated.
**Back:** *"Restraint can protect liberty."*

### Flashcard PT-3059 — Retributive Punishment
**Front:** What justifies punishment on the retributive view?
**Definition (learn):** Backward-looking punishment justified by the offender's culpable wrong, constrained by proportionality; a defensible retributive theory is public and proportionate, not private vengeance (✅ owner 14, *Socio-Political Philosophy* PDF p.164).
**Breakdown:**
- Question: what does this culpable wrong deserve?
- Strong claim: proportion restores moral/legal balance and avoids private revenge.
- Objection: pain may neither repair harm nor reform.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.164 (*Socio-Political Philosophy*) (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3060, PT-3061, PT-3062, PT-3063.
**Trap:** "Retribution means unlimited revenge" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — three theories then synthesis.
**Back:** *"Retribution: just desert, not vengeance."*

### Flashcard PT-3060 — Deterrent Punishment (Specific and General)
**Front:** What is deterrence and how is it divided?
**Definition (learn):** Forward-looking punishment intended to discourage the offender (specific deterrence) or others (general deterrence) from future crime (✅ owner 14, *Socio-Political Philosophy* PDF p.165).
**Breakdown:**
- Specific: the offender.
- General: potential offenders.
- Objection: uses the offender as example; ignores impulsive crime and social causes.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.165 (✅ owner 14 adv).
**Links:** PT-3066 (empirical trap), PT-3059, PT-3063.
**Trap:** Harshness does not prove deterrent effect (PT-3066).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — deterrence as an empirical claim.
**Back:** *"Deter the one, deter the many."*

### Flashcard PT-3061 — Reformative Punishment
**Front:** What is reformative punishment?
**Definition (learn):** Forward-looking treatment aimed at rehabilitation and return to a law-abiding life, addressing social, economic or psychological causes (✅ owner 14, PDF pp.165–166).
**Breakdown:**
- Question: how can the offender return to lawful life?
- Strength: addresses causes and reduces exclusion.
- Objection: not every offence is explained by deprivation or pathology; culpability and public protection remain.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.165–166 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3059, PT-3060, PT-3062, PT-3067.
**Trap:** Reformative theory does not erase responsibility (PT-3063 framework).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — reform with public protection.
**Back:** *"Reform returns the offender to law."*

### Flashcard PT-3062 — Proportionality in Punishment
**Front:** What role does proportionality play in punishment?
**Definition (learn):** Severity should not exceed the seriousness and culpability of the offence; it constrains punishment but does not by itself choose retribution, deterrence or reform (⚠️ owner 14 §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Upper constraint on severity.
- Underdeterminate: does not uniquely identify the humane, effective or just sentence (⚠️ owner 14 adv).
- Juvenile punishment proportionality appears in the 2023 PYQ cluster (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 14 §3 (⚠️ analytical); PYQ cluster (✅).
**Links:** PT-3059 to PT-3061, PT-3063.
**Trap:** Proportionality is not a theory of purpose.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — proportionality as a constraint, not a justification.
**Back:** *"Proportion caps the sentence, not the purpose."*

### Flashcard PT-3063 — Capital Punishment: PYQ Cluster and Answer Framework
**Front:** Which PYQs test punishment theory, and what answer route do they need?
**Definition (learn):** Verified local PYQ transfer: 2022 moral justifications of capital punishment; 2023 proportionality and juvenile punishment; 2024 capital punishment as effective deterrent; 2025 whether the right to life can be absolute with reference to capital punishment (✅ owner 14 §9; source PYQ file cited in owner: Philosophy/paper-2).
**Breakdown:**
- Route: define penal aim → distinguish retribution/deterrence/reform → apply proportionality → test irreversibility and error → separate moral argument from empirical deterrence → conclude conditionally (⚠️ owner 14 frame).
- Capital punishment asymmetry: irreversibility makes correction impossible (⚠️ owner 14 adv §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ years as listed in owner 14 §9 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3059 to PT-3062, PT-3066.
**Trap:** Do not treat the second source's pro/anti lists as settled facts (adv §12).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — conditional conclusion on capital punishment.
**Back:** *"Define aim, test error, conclude with conditions."*

### Flashcard PT-3064 — Trap: Kelsen's Book Is Not 1961
**Front:** Is Kelsen's *General Theory of Law and State* a 1961 work?
**Definition (learn):** No. The first English publication is 1945 (Harvard, tr. Anders Wedberg); 1961 is the year of Hart's *The Concept of Law* (✅ owner 14 §9; Hart 1961 ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Kelsen: 1945 English edition (owner; not fetched ⚠️).
- Hart: 1961 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Dworkin: 1977 (owner, not fetched).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** PT-3051, PT-3052, PT-3053.
**Trap:** Swapped dates in the Austin → Kelsen → Hart → Dworkin sequence; if unsure, name works and sequence without years.
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — name works and sequence.
**Back:** *"Kelsen 1945, Hart 1961, do not swap."*

### Flashcard PT-3065 — Trap: Austin's Command Theory vs Hart's Rules
**Front:** Are Austin's command theory and Hart's rule theory the same?
**Definition (learn):** No. Hart rejects Austin's coercive-command model as too narrow and replaces it with a structure of rules covering duties, powers and procedure (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Austin: sovereign command + sanction.
- Hart: rules; civil law confers powers; rule of recognition.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.291–292 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3050, PT-3052.
**Trap:** Keep Hart's secondary-rule vocabulary attributed to Hart's framework (⚠️).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — Austin vs Hart in one contrast.
**Back:** *"Command is not structure."*

### Flashcard PT-3066 — Trap: Severity Is Not Deterrence
**Front:** Does a severe sentence prove effective deterrence?
**Definition (learn):** No. Deterrence is an empirical proposition; severity alone is neither necessary nor sufficient proof of prevention (⚠️ owner 14 §5; ✅ owner 14 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Claim (sentence severity deters) is empirical.
- Needs independent evidence.
- Moral argument for punishment is separate.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 14 §5 and §9.
**Links:** PT-3060, PT-3063.
**Trap:** Stating "severe sentences are automatically effective" loses the empirical argument.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — separate moral from empirical deterrence.
**Back:** *"Harsh is not proof."*

### Flashcard PT-3067 — Trap: Civil Disobedience Is Not Any Illegal Act for a Cause
**Front:** Why is "any illegal act done for a cause" not civil disobedience?
**Definition (learn):** Civil disobedience is principled, public, non-violent, penalty-accepting and respects law generally; casual illegality is not (✅ owner 14 §9, PDF p.287).
**Breakdown:**
- Public, not secret.
- Non-violent.
- Accepts penalty.
- Targets specific injustice.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.287.
**Links:** PT-3042, PT-3046.
**Trap:** This card is the trap; use Gandhi's conditions.
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — civil disobedience vs ordinary illegality.
**Back:** *"Cause alone is not civil disobedience."*

### Flashcard PT-3068 — Trap: Valid Law Is Not Automatically Just
**Front:** Does legal validity guarantee that a law is just?
**Definition (learn):** No. Legal validity (proper enactment) and moral legitimacy can diverge; natural law, equity and conscience debates depend on this (✅ owner 14 §5, §9, PDF pp.290–296).
**Breakdown:**
- Validity: source, form, sanction.
- Legitimacy: moral justice.
- Separate: conceptual separability does not mean constant real-world divergence (adv §E).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.290–296 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3049, PT-3050, PT-3058.
**Trap:** Equating validity with justice.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — is legal validity enough for obligation? (adv prompt 3).
**Back:** *"Valid is not yet just."*

### Flashcard PT-3069 — Trap: Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau Are Not the Same Consent
**Front:** Do Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau justify the same kind of consent?
**Definition (learn):** No. Hobbes prioritises security; Locke trust and limited government; Rousseau general will and civic freedom (✅ owner 14 §9, PDF pp.283–284).
**Breakdown:**
- Hobbes: strong obligation.
- Locke: conditional.
- Rousseau: self-imposed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.283–284 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** PT-3035, PT-3036, PT-3037, PT-3005.
**Trap:** The same word "consent" masks different sovereignty models (adv §7).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — contrast three consent theories.
**Back:** *"One word, three sovereignties."*

### GROUP — 15 Power, Authority and Legitimacy

### Flashcard PT-3070 — Authority = Power + Legitimacy
**Front:** What is Gauba's formula for the relation between power and authority?
**Definition (learn):** Authority is power regarded as rightful; it is more stable and effective than naked coercion. Gauba's formula: authority = power + legitimacy (✅ owner 15, PDF p.303).
**Breakdown:**
- Power: capacity to secure compliance or intended effects.
- Legitimacy: acceptance of rule as right, good or beneficial.
- Authority: power + legitimacy, producing willing obedience.
- Force used mainly when legitimacy fails (⚠️ owner 15 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.303 (✅ owner 15 and adv).
**Links:** PT-3071, PT-3072, PT-3073, PT-3101.
**Trap:** Power, authority, legitimacy and influence are not synonyms (✅ owner 15 §9).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — state the formula, then add influence and hegemony as neighbours.
**Back:** *"Authority is power that is accepted."*

### Flashcard PT-3071 — Power as Capacity (Russell, Dahl, Wiseman, MacIver)
**Front:** How does Gauba define power?
**Definition (learn):** Power is the ability to produce intended effects or secure compliance, often against resistance; Gauba cites Russell, Dahl, Wiseman and MacIver for its coercive and relational dimensions (✅ owner 15, PDF pp.302–303; adv PDF pp.301–302).
**Breakdown:**
- Capacity, not necessarily coercion.
- Relational: power over others.
- Political science focuses on acquisition, exercise and control of power (adv PDF pp.301–302).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.302–303 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3070, PT-3072, PT-3095.
**Trap:** Power is not automatically rightful (✅ owner 15 §8).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — power as capacity plus legitimacy requirement.
**Back:** *"Power: can make others comply."*

### Flashcard PT-3072 — Influence vs Force
**Front:** How does influence differ from force?
**Definition (learn):** Influence shapes choices or conduct through persuasion, prestige or agenda-shaping without necessarily relying on sanctions; force works by threat or sanction (⚠️ owner 15 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Influence: persuasion, prestige, agenda.
- Force: fear, sanction, compulsion.
- Gauba: force typically used when legitimacy fails (⚠️ owner 15 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 15 §3, §8 (⚠️ analytical distinction).
**Links:** PT-3070, PT-3071.
**Trap:** Do not collapse influence into force (✅ owner 15 §8).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — one-sentence distinction each.
**Back:** *"Influence persuades; force compels."*

### Flashcard PT-3073 — Legitimacy Is Not Legality
**Front:** What is legitimacy, and why is it not the same as legal validity?
**Definition (learn):** Legitimacy is the quality by which rules or decisions are treated as right, good or beneficial, producing willing compliance (✅ owner 15, PDF p.303).
**Breakdown:**
- Sources: belief, respect, social approval.
- Distinct from mere legality (✅ owner 15 §8).
- Connects with rule of law (adv PDF pp.298–300).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.303 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3068 (validity vs justice), PT-3070, PT-3101.
**Trap:** Legitimacy is not identical to legality (✅ owner 15 §8).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — legitimacy as the condition converting power into authority.
**Back:** *"Legal is not automatically legitimate."*

### Flashcard PT-3074 — Weber: Traditional Authority
**Front:** What is traditional authority, and what example does Gauba give?
**Definition (learn):** Rule validated by long-standing custom; hereditary or dynastic rule is Gauba's stock example (✅ owner 15 §7, PDF p.303).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: custom.
- Example: hereditary/dynastic rule.
- Weber did not treat types as pure (see PT-3077).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.303 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3075, PT-3076, PT-3077.
**Trap:** Weber's three authorities do not exist in pure form (✅ owner 15 §9).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — Weber's typology.
**Back:** *"Tradition: custom makes the crown."*

### Flashcard PT-3075 — Weber: Charismatic Authority
**Front:** What is charismatic authority, and what illustration does Gauba use?
**Definition (learn):** Authority flowing from extraordinary personal appeal; Gauba cites Hitler as a historical illustration (✅ owner 15 §7, PDF p.303).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: personal mission or appeal.
- Risk: volatility, dependence on the leader.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Hitler example is historical, book-period (⚠️ owner 15 §12).
**Links:** PT-3074, PT-3076, PT-3077.
**Trap:** Do not present Hitler as an undated present-tense claim (✅ owner 15 §12).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — charisma as a source of authority needing institutional routinisation (⚠️ analytical).
**Back:** *"Charisma: the extraordinary voice."*

### Flashcard PT-3076 — Weber: Legal-Rational Authority
**Front:** What is legal-rational authority, and why is it central to the modern state?
**Definition (learn):** Authority based on accepted valid rules and offices; judge, civil servant or elected office-holder obeyed because office and procedure are accepted as valid (✅ owner 15, PDF p.303).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: impersonal rules and office.
- Central to the modern bureaucratic state (Gauba on Weber).
- Relates to the rule of law (PT-3057).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.303 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3074, PT-3075, PT-3057.
**Trap:** Legal-rational authority is not the same as legal validity alone; it adds acceptance of office.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — bureaucracy and legal-rational authority.
**Back:** *"Office and rule: the modern crown."*

### Flashcard PT-3077 — Weber: Mixed Forms and Democracy as Competition for Leadership
**Front:** How does Weber redefine democracy, and what does Gauba add on mixed forms?
**Definition (learn):** Weber described democracy as competition for political leadership rather than literal government by the people; in his 1918 essay on socialism he argued even a classless society would concentrate power in officials. Gauba notes mixed forms in practice (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF p.314; ⚠️ the 1918 date is owner-stated, not fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Competition model carried forward by Schumpeter (owner 15).
- Inequality of political power is not removed by abolishing economic classes.
- Mixed forms: real regimes blend traditional, charismatic and legal-rational elements (PDF p.303).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.303, 314 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3074 to PT-3076, PT-3080 (Gramsci), PT-3084 (elite).
**Trap:** Weber's types are analytical; do not treat them as empirical boxes (✅ owner 15 adv §12).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — competitive democracy vs participatory accounts.
**Back:** *"Democracy: competition for leaders, not power to the people alone."*

### Flashcard PT-3078 — Political, Economic and Ideological Power
**Front:** What are the three support-bases of power in Gauba's account?
**Definition (learn):** Political power works through formal and informal decision structures (parties, pressure groups, public opinion); economic power rests on control over production and resources; ideological power legitimises rule through beliefs and values (✅ owner 15, PDF pp.304–306; adv §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Political: state and public decision structures.
- Economic: ownership and distribution.
- Ideological: beliefs that make domination acceptable (adv PDF pp.305–306).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.304–306 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3079 (Marx), PT-3080 (Gramsci), PT-3081 (elite).
**Trap:** Gauba also warns against an imprecise power approach that reduces politics to struggle at any price (adv PDF pp.306–307).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — fusion of economic and political power.
**Back:** *"Politics, wealth, belief: three bases."*

### Flashcard PT-3079 — Marx and Engels: Political Power Rooted in Economic Power
**Front:** How do Marx and Engels explain political power?
**Definition (learn):** Political power is traced to economic power rooted in ownership of the means of social production and class domination (✅ owner 15, PDF pp.308–309).
**Breakdown:**
- Base: ownership.
- Politics: inseparable from class domination and struggle.
- Gramsci later adds consent (PT-3080).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.308–309 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3080, PT-3078, PT-3011.
**Trap:** Gramsci is not a repeat of classical Marxism (PT-3099).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — economic base and political superstructure.
**Back:** *"Who owns, rules."*

### Flashcard PT-3080 — Gramsci: Hegemony, Civil Society and Legitimation
**Front:** How does hegemony explain persistence of capitalist rule?
**Definition (learn):** Hegemony is rule apparently exercised with the consent of the ruled through cultural and ideological domination, generated in civil society (✅ owner 15, PDF pp.306, 309–311).
**Breakdown:**
- Relative autonomy of superstructure.
- Civil society: school, family, church teach deference (PDF pp.310–311).
- Coercion + legitimation together.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.306, 309–311 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3012 (political vs civil society), PT-3095, PT-3099, PT-3102 (digital hegemony extension).
**Trap:** Hegemony is subtler than open coercion (✅ owner 15 §8).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — consent and coercion together.
**Back:** *"Hegemony: rule by consent manufactured."*

### Flashcard PT-3081 — Pareto: Governing Elite and Circulation of Elites
**Front:** What are Pareto's elite concepts?
**Definition (learn):** Elite is a minority of superior ability taking society's major decisions; the governing elite is in power, the non-governing elite seeks to replace it; their turnover is the circulation of elites (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF p.312; *The Mind and Society* 1915–19 owner-stated, ⚠️ not fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Lions: courage; suited to maintaining status quo.
- Foxes: cunning; adaptive in change.
- Masses never enter elite ranks.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.312 (✅ owner 15 adv §5, §8).
**Links:** PT-3082 (Mosca), PT-3083 (Michels), PT-3084 (comparison).
**Trap:** Pareto and Mosca are not identical (PT-3084).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — elite theory's circulation.
**Back:** *"Lions hold, foxes adapt."*

### Flashcard PT-3082 — Mosca: Organised Minority
**Front:** What is Mosca's core elite claim?
**Definition (learn):** Every society divides into rulers and ruled; the ruling minority's key strength is organisational capacity ("organised minority" governing an unorganised majority); Mosca judged leadership more open than Pareto (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF pp.312–313; *The Ruling Class* 1896 owner-stated, ⚠️ not fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Organisation, not talent alone.
- Circulation of elites through upper/lower strata competition.
- More democratic openness than Pareto.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.312–313 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3081, PT-3083, PT-3084.
**Trap:** Mosca is not Michels; organisation is the key to his claim, oligarchy to Michels's.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — organisation as the basis of minority rule.
**Back:** *"Organised few, unorganised many."*

### Flashcard PT-3083 — Michels: Iron Law of Oligarchy
**Front:** What is the Iron Law of Oligarchy?
**Definition (learn):** Large organisations, however democratic at founding, drift toward rule by a few because professional experts become difficult to displace (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF pp.313–314). Michels's *Political Parties*, 1911 (✅ Wikipedia: Iron law of oligarchy); summary "Who says organization, says oligarchy" (✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: growth and complexity hand management to experts.
- Leaders may turn over while organisation stays oligarchic.
- Gauba's objection: law cannot be applied uniformly (parties vs trade unions) (⚠️ owner 15 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1911 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3081, PT-3084, PT-3097.
**Trap:** Michels's law is a tendency, not proof of permanent oligarchy everywhere (⚠️ owner 15 §6).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — "turnover of leaders does not democratise rule."
**Back:** *"Organisation breeds oligarchy."*

### Flashcard PT-3084 — Elite Theory Comparison: Circulation vs Organisational Oligarchy
**Front:** How do Pareto/Mosca differ from Michels, and why are they not strict opposites?
**Definition (learn):** Pareto and Mosca explain turnover among ruling minorities; Michels explains why complex organisations tend toward oligarchic leadership even when founded democratically. Elites can circulate while rule remains oligarchic (✅ owner 15 §9, §13C).
**Breakdown:**
- Pareto: superior ability; lions and foxes.
- Mosca: organisation; upper/lower competition.
- Michels: managerial indispensability; iron law.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.312–314 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3081 to PT-3083.
**Trap:** Do not present the three as one doctrine or as logically incompatible on every point.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — "Critically examine elite theory" with the circulation/oligarchy test.
**Back:** *"Circulate the faces; keep the oligarchy."*

### Flashcard PT-3085 — C. Wright Mills: The Power Elite
**Front:** What does Mills's *The Power Elite* (1956) claim?
**Definition (learn):** Mills replaces the Marxian ruling class with a "power elite" of three loosely interlocking groups (captains of industry, military leaders, prominent politicians) occupying pivotal command posts in American society (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF pp.314–315).
**Breakdown:**
- Groups share social origins, culture, mutual reinforcement.
- Not reducible to a single economic class.
- Case study: modern America (PDF pp.315–316, adv).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *The Power Elite* 1956 (✅ owner 15 §6 and §13D).
**Links:** PT-3081, PT-3086.
**Trap:** Mills is not a Marxian "single class" theorist.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — elitism updated for modern institutions.
**Back:** *"Three rooms, one command."*

### Flashcard PT-3086 — Classical Pluralism: Dahl, Polyarchy and Polsby
**Front:** What is classical pluralism's claim, and what does Polsby note?
**Definition (learn):** Resources are widely dispersed and different interests prevail on different issues; Dahl's polyarchy treats government as an honest broker among competing groups; Polsby (1963) noted pluralism's unspoken assumption that "at bottom nobody dominates" (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF pp.317–319).
**Breakdown:**
- Dahl and Lindblom, *Politics, Economics and Welfare* (1953); Dahl, *A Preface to Democratic Theory* (1956); *Who Governs?* (1961) (✅ owner 15).
- Later concessions: Lindblom *Politics and Markets* (1977); Dahl *Dilemmas of Pluralist Democracy* (1982) (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 15, not fetched).
**Links:** PT-3087, PT-3088, PT-3020, PT-3096.
**Trap:** "Pluralism says nobody has unequal power" is wrong (✅ owner 15 §9).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — pluralism's concession and two-step critique.
**Back:** *"Pluralists: many players, nobody dominant, so they say."*

### Flashcard PT-3087 — Non-decision-making: The Second Face of Power
**Front:** What is the non-decision critique of pluralism?
**Definition (learn):** Pluralism examines only decision-making and overlooks issues on which no decision is taken though they may be very important; power is exercised by keeping issues off the agenda (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF p.318). The named framework (Bachrach and Baratz, "Two Faces of Power", 1962) is a standard disciplinary label (⚠️ not verified: fetch returned 404).
**Breakdown:**
- First face: observable decision conflict (pluralism).
- Second face: non-decisions and agenda control.
- Gauba's sentence is the source-grounded claim.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.318 (✅ owner 15); Bachrach & Baratz 1962 (⚠️ owner-stated label, unverified here).
**Links:** PT-3086, PT-3088, PT-3089, PT-3098.
**Trap:** Non-decision and third dimension are not the same idea (PT-3100).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — escalating critique.
**Back:** *"Power also lives in what never reaches the agenda."*

### Flashcard PT-3088 — Lukes: The Third Dimension of Power
**Front:** What is Lukes's third dimension of power?
**Definition (learn):** Power-holders can shape the very wants and preferences of others, suppressing real interests without visible conflict, decision or agenda exclusion (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF p.318; ✅ Wikipedia: Steven Lukes).
**Breakdown:**
- Book: *Power: A Radical View*, first published 1974, London, Macmillan (✅ Wikipedia).
- Wikipedia summary: decision-making; non-decision-making; ideological power (shaping wants) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: third dimension = preference shaping (✅ owner 15 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1974 Macmillan; later editions 2005 and 2021 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3087, PT-3089, PT-3101, PT-3105.
**Trap:** Not an established finding; a hypothesis hard to evidence (adv §13).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — limit: consent does not prove absence of domination.
**Back:** *"Lukes: shape wants, not just votes."*

### Flashcard PT-3089 — The Three-Dimensional Ladder of Power
**Front:** What are the three dimensions or faces of power in the teaching ladder?
**Definition (learn):** First: observable decisions (Dahl's pluralism); second: non-decision-making or agenda control (Bachrach–Baratz type); third: shaping of preferences (Lukes). A standard teaching device layered onto Gauba's own critiques, not a book quotation (⚠️ owner 15 §8a).
**Breakdown:**
| Dimension | Studies | Associated with |
|---|---|---|
| First | who wins visible conflicts | Dahl |
| Second | which issues never reach agenda | Bachrach & Baratz (⚠️) |
| Third | suppression of real interests without visible conflict | Lukes (1974) |
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.317–319 and p.318 (✅ owner 15 §8a).
**Links:** PT-3086, PT-3087, PT-3088, PT-3103 (digital application).
**Trap:** Do not present the ladder as Gauba's own verbatim scheme.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — apply the ladder to an example.
**Back:** *"Decide, agenda, desire."*

### Flashcard PT-3090 — Feminist Theory of Patriarchy: Engels's "World-Historic Defeat"
**Front:** How does Gauba trace patriarchy through Engels?
**Definition (learn):** Patriarchy (rule of the father) is traced through Engels's *The Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State* (1884): the shift from matrilineal reckoning to monogamous patriarchal marriage was, in his phrase, "the world-historic defeat of the female sex" (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF p.315).
**Breakdown:**
- Patriarchy is a structure of male domination.
- Reproduced through reproduction, sexuality, violence, household labour, labour-market segmentation (adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1884 (✅ owner 15); the phrase is the owner's quotation of Engels (⚠️ not fetched, keep as owner-quoted).
**Links:** PT-3091, PT-3017, PT-3024.
**Trap:** Feminist power theory is not only domestic violence (PT-3091).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — structural, not interpersonal, patriarchy.
**Back:** *"Engels: the family's defeat of women."*

### Flashcard PT-3091 — Three Sociological Accounts of Patriarchy
**Front:** What three accounts of patriarchy does Gauba report?
**Definition (learn):** (a) Cultural or psychoanalytic: society's symbols and culture are male-dominated; (b) household: men dominate women economically, sexually and culturally within marriage (an implicit labour contract); (c) Marxist-feminist: patriarchy and capitalism reinforce each other via unpaid domestic labour (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF pp.315–316).
**Breakdown:**
- Read together, not as rivals.
- Gauba cites a 1980 UN report figure as book-period illustration (PDF p.316).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.315–316 (✅ owner 15); 1980 UN figure (⚠️ book-period, not re-verified).
**Links:** PT-3090, PT-3018 (Eisenstein).
**Trap:** Do not reduce feminist power theory to one account (adv §13B).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — three accounts with a unified verdict.
**Back:** *"Symbol, household, market: three doors."*

### Flashcard PT-3092 — Arendt: Power vs Violence
**Front:** How does Arendt distinguish power from violence?
**Definition (learn):** Power is the human ability to act in concert, belongs to a group and lasts only so long as the group holds together; violence is instrumental, can be possessed as equipment, and destroys power; "violence can destroy power; it is utterly incapable of creating it" (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF pp.320–321).
**Breakdown:**
- Power sustains the public realm; violence threatens it.
- Where power is absent, violence may fill the gap (owner quote).
- Book: *On Violence*; owner gives 1969 (⚠️ conflict: standard bibliographies give 1970; the fetched Wikipedia page placed the essay within *Crises of the Republic*, 1972; not resolved).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.320–321 (✅ owner 15); year conflict flagged ⚠️.
**Links:** PT-3093 (Macpherson), PT-3097 (trap), PT-3095.
**Trap:** Arendt does not glorify state force (✅ owner 15 §9).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — power as collective and violence as its destroyer.
**Back:** *"Arendt: power acts together; violence stands alone."*

### Flashcard PT-3093 — Macpherson: Extractive vs Developmental Power
**Front:** What distinguishes extractive from developmental power?
**Definition (learn):** Extractive power is "power over" others (using others' capacities for one's purposes); developmental power is a person's ability to use their own capacities for self-chosen, self-fulfilling goals (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF pp.321–322).
**Breakdown:**
- Book: *Democratic Theory: Essays in Retrieval* (1973) (✅ owner 15).
- Three impediments to developmental power: lack of means of life; lack of access to means of labour; lack of protection against invasion by others (PDF pp.321–322).
- Requires civil liberties with a socialist mode of production (creative freedom) (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1973 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3092, PT-3094, PT-3095, PT-3098.
**Trap:** Macpherson's developmental power is individual; Arendt's power is collective (PT-3098).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — "power to" as empowerment and its market limits.
**Back:** *"Macpherson: extract versus develop."*

### Flashcard PT-3094 — Gandhi's Swaraj as Capacity to Resist Authority
**Front:** How does Gandhi redefine power from the ruled's standpoint?
**Definition (learn):** Swaraj as "the acquisition of the capacity by all to resist authority when it is abused" (*Young India*, 1925); Gauba's illustration of power from the side of the ruled, akin to "speaking truth to power" (✅ owner 15 §6, PDF p.323).
**Breakdown:**
- Power redefined as resistance capacity.
- Indian application kept conceptual (⚠️ owner 15 §13G).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *Young India*, 1925 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3016, PT-3042, PT-3095.
**Trap:** Do not extend to specific modern Indian institutions without verification (⚠️).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — "power to" in Gandhi.
**Back:** *"Swaraj: every citizen can say no."*

### Flashcard PT-3095 — Power Over vs Power To
**Front:** How do "power over" and "power to" differ?
**Definition (learn):** Power over is domination by an agent over a subject; power to is the subject's capacity for resistance, action and self-development (⚠️ owner 15 §5; adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Arendt: collective power to act in concert.
- Macpherson: developmental power.
- Gandhi: resistance capacity.
- Empowerment: reservation, unionisation, local participation read as conversion of power over into power to (⚠️ owner 15 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 15 §5, §7 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** PT-3092, PT-3093, PT-3094.
**Trap:** Do not treat Arendt's power and Macpherson's power as the same (PT-3098).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — "power over" domination and "power to" empowerment.
**Back:** *"Over dominates; to empowers."*

### Flashcard PT-3096 — Trap: Pluralism Does Not Say Nobody Has Unequal Power
**Front:** Does pluralism claim that no one has unequal power?
**Definition (learn):** No. Later pluralists (Lindblom, Dahl) admit business privilege and unequal political resources (✅ owner 15 §9, PDF pp.318–319).
**Breakdown:**
- Early claim: competing groups.
- Concession: business privilege; policies redistributing power (Dahl recommends).
- Non-decision critique remains.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.318–319 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** PT-3086, PT-3020, PT-3087.
**Trap:** This card is the trap; answer with the concession.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — pluralism's self-correction.
**Back:** *"Pluralism concedes privilege."*

### Flashcard PT-3097 — Trap: Arendt Does Not Glorify State Violence
**Front:** Does Arendt treat state force as a form of power?
**Definition (learn):** No. She separates genuine collective power from violence and treats violence as destructive of power (✅ owner 15 §9, PDF pp.320–321).
**Breakdown:**
- Power: acting in concert.
- Violence: instrumental; state violence against people is destructive.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** PT-3092, PT-3093.
**Trap:** Violence is evidence of missing power (adv §7).
**Mains hook: 10-marker** — "violence can destroy power" as thesis.
**Back:** *"Violence does not build power."*

### Flashcard PT-3098 — Trap: Macpherson's Developmental Power Is Not Arendt's Power
**Front:** Are Macpherson's developmental power and Arendt's power the same?
**Definition (learn):** No. Arendt's power is collective and relational; Macpherson's is about an individual's capacity for self-realisation (✅ owner 15 §9, PDF pp.320–322).
**Breakdown:**
- Unit of analysis: group (Arendt), person (Macpherson).
- Both "power to" themes; different foundations.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** PT-3092, PT-3093, PT-3096.
**Trap:** Both are "power to", but not identical.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — constructive power theories.
**Back:** *"Arendt acts together; Macpherson grows alone and together."*

### Flashcard PT-3099 — Trap: Gramsci Is Not Orthodox Marxism
**Front:** Does Gramsci simply repeat classical Marxism on power?
**Definition (learn):** No. He gives relative autonomy to the superstructure and focuses on civil society, culture and consent (✅ owner 15 §9, PDF pp.309–311).
**Breakdown:**
- Classical: economic determinism.
- Gramsci: hegemony and legitimation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.309–311 (✅ owner 15 adv).
**Links:** PT-3012, PT-3080.
**Trap:** Gramsci as "correction or extension" is an answer prompt (⚠️ owner 15 adv §10).
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — correction vs extension.
**Back:** *"Gramsci adds consent to class."*

### Flashcard PT-3100 — Trap: Non-decision and Third Dimension Are Not One Idea
**Front:** How do non-decision-making and Lukes's third dimension differ?
**Definition (learn):** Non-decision-making blocks an issue from the agenda; Lukes's third dimension shapes the very wants and preferences of the subordinate, so no grievance or agenda exclusion is needed (✅ owner 15 §9, PDF p.318).
**Breakdown:**
- Second face: agenda exclusion.
- Third face: preference shaping.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** PT-3087, PT-3088, PT-3089.
**Trap:** This card is the trap.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — escalating ladder.
**Back:** *"Agenda blocks; preference shapes."*

### Flashcard PT-3101 — Legitimacy Economises Rule; Lukes's Limit (adv)
**Front:** Why do regimes invest in legitimacy, and what limit does the third dimension place on consent?
**Definition (learn):** Legitimacy economises the cost of rule (elections, tradition, charisma, ideology); but apparently willing compliance can itself be manufactured by shaping preferences, so consent does not prove absence of domination (✅ owner 15 adv §13E, PDF p.318).
**Breakdown:**
- Benefit: cheaper than coercion.
- Limit: consent may be manufactured.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 15 adv §13E (✅ with ⚠️ analytical inference).
**Links:** PT-3070, PT-3088, PT-3080.
**Trap:** Do not conclude that consent proves rightfulness.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — legitimacy without freedom.
**Back:** *"Consent can be made, not only given."*

### Flashcard PT-3102 — Panopticism: Self-policing Under Uncertain Observation (digital extension)
**Front:** What is panopticism, and why is it a distinct mechanism in power theory?
**Definition (learn):** The disciplinary reading associated with Foucault's *Discipline and Punish* (French 1975; English 1977): Bentham's prison design lets inmates be watched without knowing when; the possibility of observation is internalised, so power becomes cheap, continuous and self-executing (⚠️ owner 15 basic §14.2; ✅ dates in owner as stated).
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: uncertainty plus internalisation.
- Complements Lukes: shapes what people want vs what they dare.
- Caveat: metaphor transfers only analogically to digital contexts; do not quote Foucault.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1975 French; 1977 English (⚠️ owner-stated, not fetched).
**Links:** PT-3088, PT-3103, PT-3106.
**Trap:** Foucault is not a simple entry in the class/elite/pluralist list (owner §14.2).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — panopticism alongside the three dimensions.
**Back:** *"Watched possibly; so you watch yourself."*

### Flashcard PT-3103 — Data and Algorithmic Power: Four Mechanisms (digital extension)
**Front:** What four mechanisms make data and algorithmic power a power relation?
**Definition (learn):** Behavioural data at scale can sort, rank, target and predict, producing compliance or effects without command or sanction (⚠️ owner 15 basic §14.3).
**Breakdown:**
1. Asymmetric knowledge.
2. Ranking and visibility control (agenda power in new medium).
3. Prediction and pre-emption (power shifts from response to anticipation).
4. Automation of discretion (outcome produced by an uninspectable process).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §14.3 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** PT-3102, PT-3104, PT-3105, PT-3089.
**Trap:** Do not name any Indian platform or programme (⚠️ owner §14.8).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — four mechanisms, one legitimacy test.
**Back:** *"Know, rank, predict, decide unseen."*

### Flashcard PT-3104 — Zuboff: Surveillance Capitalism (2019)
**Front:** What does Zuboff's *The Age of Surveillance Capitalism* (2019) argue?
**Definition (learn):** Behavioural data has become a commercial input in its own right, forming a distinctive form of private power (⚠️ owner 15 basic §14.3; cite by title and year only, do not quote).
**Breakdown:**
- Thesis: private power built on behavioural data.
- Contested scholarly position, not settled fact.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** PT-3103, PT-3107.
**Trap:** Do not quote Zuboff.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — private power and public accountability gap.
**Back:** *"Data becomes the commodity of power."*

### Flashcard PT-3105 — Pariser: The Filter Bubble (digital third dimension)
**Front:** How does the filter bubble relate to Lukes's third dimension?
**Definition (learn):** Sustained personalisation can narrow the informational environment in which preferences form; "filter bubble" (Pariser, 2011) and "echo chamber" are the vocabulary (⚠️ owner 15 basic §14.4). The honest claim: personalisation can narrow, and measuring whether it does is disputed.
**Breakdown:**
- Third dimension in digital form.
- Hardest to detect: cannot be read from observed choices.
- Strongest claim, weakest evidence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2011 (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** PT-3088, PT-3089, PT-3100.
**Trap:** Do not state filter bubbles as an established finding.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — contested empirical hypothesis.
**Back:** *"Narrow the feed, shape the wants."*

### Flashcard PT-3106 — Surveillance Asymmetry and Three Consequences
**Front:** Why does surveillance become a power relation, and what three consequences follow?
**Definition (learn):** Surveillance is rarely reciprocal; the watched cannot watch back or see criteria; this asymmetry converts monitoring into power (⚠️ owner 15 basic §14.5).
**Breakdown:**
1. Chilling effects on association and dissent (conceptual claim).
2. Loss of contestability (opaque criteria; domination per owner's cross-reference to Liberty, Equality and Property §22).
3. Aggregation: trivial observations combine into comprehensive profile.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 15 §14.5.
**Links:** PT-3102, PT-3107, PT-3108.
**Trap:** Harmlessness of each item does not settle the whole.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — asymmetry as the core.
**Back:** *"One-way glass, cumulative file."*

### Flashcard PT-3107 — Public vs Private/Platform Power
**Front:** How do classical public power and private/platform power differ on source, accountability and exit?
**Definition (learn):** Public power rests on constitution and statute with elections, legislature, courts and press as checks; private power rests on contract and property, with weaker checks, nominal exit, and often transnational reach (⚠️ owner 15 basic §14.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: constitution vs terms of service.
- Accountability: elections vs market exit and external regulation where it exists.
- Exit: very costly vs nominally cheap but practically costly.
- Hybrid case: public authority relying on private infrastructure is analytically hardest.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §14.7 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** PT-3103, PT-3104, PT-3096 (pluralist business privilege), PT-3109.
**Trap:** Do not force hybrids into one box (owner §14.7).
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — boundary problem with graded verdict.
**Back:** *"Public is elected; private is contracted."*

### Flashcard PT-3108 — Puttaswamy (2017): Privacy under Article 21 (boundary card)
**Front:** What is the Indian conceptual anchor for privacy in the power chapter, and what is its boundary?
**Definition (learn):** *K. S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India* (2017), nine-judge Bench, held privacy a fundamental right under Article 21 and Part III, with legality, legitimate aim and proportionality as safeguards (✅ owner 15 basic §14.8; standard case, not fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Use: informational self-determination as a rights question.
- Structured justification test = non-arbitrariness condition.
- Boundary: no ratio, subsequent litigation or platform position authored here (owner §14.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2017, nine judges, Article 21 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-3106, PT-3109; Polity files (outward link only).
**Trap:** Do not state any current Indian regulatory position from this file.
**Mains hook: 15-marker** — as a conceptual example of structured justification only.
**Back:** *"Privacy is a right; intrusion needs a test."*

### Flashcard PT-3109 — Objection and Reply: Platform Relations Are Voluntary
**Front:** Is platform use a power relation if entered and exited voluntarily?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: voluntariness means no power relation, so calling it power inflates the concept. Reply: power needs only the capacity to secure compliance; where a service is a precondition of work, speech or access, exit is nominal and consent substantively thin. Residual weakness: how costly exit must be is a matter of degree (⚠️ owner 15 basic §14.9).
**Breakdown:**
- Chain 1: voluntariness objection, reply, weakness conceded.
- Chain 2: unfalsifiability of preference-shaping; reply via detectable second-dimension mechanisms.
- Chain 3: state-centrism; reply via pluralism that state is one association among many.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §14.9 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** PT-3107, PT-3088, PT-3105, PT-3106.
**Trap:** Do not say consent to any terms is always power-free or always coerced.
**Mains hook: 20-marker** — objection-reply chain with conceded weakness.
**Back:** *"Voluntary in form; thin in substance."*

### GROUP — 16 Citizenship and Its Critiques (basic + advanced)

> Owners: `Political-Theory/basic/16_Citizenship-and-Its-Critiques.md` and `advanced/16_…` (Gauba Ch.14, PDF pp.324–337). Tags: `(✅ owner 16)` basic; `(✅ owner 16 adv)` advanced; `(✅ Wikipedia: …)` fetched page; `⚠️` memory-only, owner inference or unresolved. Thinker cards that the owner names but does not date are dated only where a fetched page confirms the date.

### Flashcard PT-3201 — Citizenship as Reciprocal Membership (concept card)
**Front:** What is citizenship in the owner's core definition, and why is it more than a legal label?
**Definition (learn):** Citizenship is the status of a full and responsible member of a political community, carrying a two-way relationship: duties, allegiance and obligations on one side; protection, civil, political and social rights on the other (✅ owner 16, PDF pp.324–325).
**Breakdown:**
- Duty side: allegiance, obligations, participation.
- Right side: protection, civil rights, political rights, social rights.
- Status can be only formal (legal) or substantive (lived access) (✅ owner 16 §1 map).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, 5th ed. (2009), Ch.14, PDF pp.324–337 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3202 (citizen vs subject), PT-3203 (formal vs substantive), PT-3231 (answer spine), PT-3212 (negative vs positive rights).
**Trap:** Citizenship is not "only a bundle of rights"; duties are part of the status (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Mains hook:** Open any citizenship answer with the two-way relationship, then ask whether the rights side is merely promised or actually usable.
**Back:** *"Two-way bond: duties given, rights received, and both must be lived."*

### Flashcard PT-3202 — Citizen vs Subject (Gauba's ideal-type contrast)
**Front:** How does Gauba separate a citizen from a subject?
**Definition (learn):** A subject is subservient to a privileged ruler; a citizen belongs to a political community that authorises rule and does not discriminate on irrelevant grounds (✅ owner 16 §3, PDF pp.324–325).
**Breakdown:**
- Subject: obeys a privileged class or ruler.
- Citizen: part of the community that authorises rule procedurally.
- The contrast is an ideal type; historically subjecthood and citizenship overlapped (✅ owner 16 adv §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.324–325 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3201, PT-3204 (democratic ideal of citizen), PT-3036 (Locke conditional obligation).
**Trap:** "Citizen and subject are interchangeable" is wrong (✅ owner 16 §9). Equally, do not present the contrast as an absolute binary in history (✅ owner 16 §3).
**Mains hook:** Use the ideal-type caveat: the contrast is a normative yardstick, not a claim that medieval or colonial subjects had no status at all.
**Back:** *"Subject obeys a master; citizen co-authors the rule."*

### Flashcard PT-3203 — Formal vs Substantive Citizenship
**Front:** What is the difference between formal and substantive citizenship?
**Definition (learn):** Formal citizenship is legal membership without effective enjoyment of its associated rights; substantive citizenship is actual access to the rights, protections and participation the status promises (✅ owner 16, PDF pp.325, 327–329).
**Breakdown:**
- Formal: legal membership, nominal rights.
- Substantive: rights enjoyed and enforceable in lived social and political life (✅ owner 16 adv §4).
- Gap is explained by structural exclusion: under-representation, unequal opportunity (PDF pp.334–336).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba's civil-rights illustration: American civil rights movement (PDF p.328, ✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3220 (feminist), PT-3221 (subaltern), PT-3229 (formal equality objection), PT-3231.
**Trap:** Do not assume legal inclusion means lived equality (✅ owner 16 §8).
**Mains hook:** Formal citizenship is necessary but not sufficient; substantive citizenship needs capability and access.
**Back:** *"Legal in the book, usable in life: formal is a paper, substantive is a power."*

### Flashcard PT-3204 — Gauba's Democratic Ideal of the Citizen
**Front:** What does Gauba's "citizen" ideal require, and what must not be confused with it?
**Definition (learn):** A citizen is a member of a community in which rule is procedurally authorised by the general body and membership carries no discrimination on race, religion or gender (✅ owner 16 §3, PDF p.325).
**Breakdown:**
- Ideal = substantive democratic ideal, not a definition of every legal citizenship regime.
- Rules of acquisition (birthplace, descent) are a separate question from equality after membership is acquired (✅ owner 16 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.325 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3202, PT-3224 (jus soli / sanguinis), PT-3223 (stateless), PT-3233 (genealogy).
**Trap:** Do not read Gauba's three exclusion grounds as a complete legal test of who is a citizen; acquisition and loss belong to Polity (✅ owner 16 §10).
**Mains hook:** Separate "who is admitted" from "what equal membership means once admitted" in every answer on exclusion.
**Back:** *"Ideal: authorised rule, no bar by race, religion or gender."*

### Flashcard PT-3205 — Aristotle's Restricted Polis Citizen
**Front:** How does Aristotle define the citizen, and why is the model not universal?
**Definition (learn):** Aristotle defines the citizen through sharing in deliberative and judicial office within the polis, a restricted body rather than universal membership (✅ owner 16, PDF p.325).
**Breakdown:**
- Citizen = participant in deliberative and judicial office.
- Ancient Greek city-states limited citizenship to a small body of freemen; slaves, women and aliens excluded (✅ owner 16 §7).
- Aristotle's polis reasoning is developed in PT-3003 (the state for the good life).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.325 (✅ owner 16 & 16 adv).
**Links:** PT-3003 (Aristotle state), PT-3222 (Greek exclusion trap), PT-3206 (Rousseau).
**Trap:** Do not romanticise classical citizenship as democratic universalism (✅ owner 16 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Classical citizenship shows participation and exclusion can coexist; the exclusion is the warning.
**Back:** *"Polis citizen judges and deliberates, but only for the few."*

### Flashcard PT-3206 — Rousseau: Citizenship as Autonomy and Common Good
**Front:** What does Rousseau add to citizenship theory?
**Definition (learn):** Rousseau links citizenship with freedom, autonomy and participation in decisions binding on all, privileging the common good over private interest (✅ owner 16, PDF p.326).
**Breakdown:**
- Freedom = obeying a law one has co-authored (PT-3007).
- Warning: private interests can undermine social unity (✅ owner 16 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.326 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3007 (general will), PT-3037 (Rousseau obligation), PT-3205, PT-3213 (Nozick contrast).
**Trap:** Rousseau's citizen is not the same as Locke's rights-holder; do not merge them (PT-3069).
**Mains hook:** Rousseau gives the participatory benchmark against which liberal citizenship is measured.
**Back:** *"Citizen = co-author of the law, serving the common good."*

### Flashcard PT-3207 — Locke as Antecedent of Liberal Citizenship
**Front:** Why is Locke an antecedent but not a complete theorist of citizenship?
**Definition (learn):** Locke grounds rights in natural rights and permits resistance to a rights-violating government, which makes him an antecedent of liberal citizenship, not a full citizenship theorist (✅ owner 16 §6, PDF p.326).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural rights to life, liberty, property.
- Resistance against government that breaches its trust (PT-3036).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.326 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3006 (Locke trust), PT-3036 (Locke conditional obligation), PT-3243 (Locke rights, ch.17).
**Trap:** Locke wrote before the modern rights-based citizenship it anticipates; see PT-3023 for the 1688 trap.
**Mains hook:** Liberal citizenship inherits Locke's protective core but needs Marshall's social rights to become substantive.
**Back:** *"Locke: rights first, citizenship later."*

### Flashcard PT-3208 — Marshall's Triad (1): Civil Rights
**Front:** What are civil rights in Marshall's triad, and in which period does he place them for England?
**Definition (learn):** Civil rights are the rights necessary for individual freedom: liberty of person, speech, thought, faith, property and contract, and equality before law (✅ owner 16 adv §4; ✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
**Breakdown:**
- Content: liberty, speech, property, equality before law (✅ owner 16 §6).
- English period: broadly the eighteenth century (✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Marshall's lecture "Citizenship and Social Class" delivered 1949; published 1950 (✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall). PDF p.327 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3209 (political), PT-3210 (social), PT-3211 (sequence trap), PT-3212 (negative/positive).
**Trap:** Civil rights are not only property; they include speech, faith and equality before law.
**Mains hook:** Civil rights remove barriers to individual standing; they are the first layer, not the whole of citizenship.
**Back:** *"Civil, 18th century: liberty, speech, property, equal before law."*

### Flashcard PT-3209 — Marshall's Triad (2): Political Rights
**Front:** What are political rights in Marshall's triad, and when does Marshall place them?
**Definition (learn):** Political rights secure the right to participate in the exercise of political power: voting and holding office (✅ owner 16 §8; ✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
**Breakdown:**
- Content: voting, office-holding, participation.
- English period: broadly the nineteenth century (✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
- Owner: "formally available" voting can still be blocked by structural exclusion (✅ owner 16 adv §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.327–329 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3208, PT-3210, PT-3240 (democratic rights), PT-3220 (feminist voting critique).
**Trap:** Voting rights alone did not ensure equal representation of women in legislatures (PDF pp.335–336, ✅ owner 16 §7).
**Mains hook:** Political rights enable participation but do not guarantee effective voice.
**Back:** *"Political, 19th century: vote and hold office."*

### Flashcard PT-3210 — Marshall's Triad (3): Social Rights
**Front:** What are social rights in Marshall's triad, and when does he place them?
**Definition (learn):** Social rights secure the right to a modicum of economic welfare and security, and a share in social heritage through welfare institutions (✅ owner 16 adv §4; ✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
**Breakdown:**
- Content: welfare, security, social heritage.
- English period: broadly the twentieth century (✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
- Gauba treats social rights as completing substantive citizenship (PDF pp.327–329).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.327–329 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3009 (welfare liberal state), PT-3208, PT-3211, PT-3248 (Laski social-democratic rights).
**Trap:** Social rights are not "charity added to citizenship" in Marshall's own framing; the owner's argument-move for social rights treats them as constitutive (✅ owner 16 adv §18 move 2).
**Mains hook:** Social rights make the other rights usable by people who lack economic capacity.
**Back:** *"Social, 20th century: welfare and a share of the heritage."*

### Flashcard PT-3211 — Trap: Marshall's Sequence Is an English Historical Model
**Front:** Is Marshall's civil → political → social order a universal chronology?
**Definition (learn):** No. Marshall analysed the English historical extension of citizenship; his sequence is neither a universal law nor a final mature stage (✅ owner 16 §6; ✅ owner 16 adv §8).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: English experience (✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
- Critics: the account applies to England, not Britain generally (✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
- Some scholars (Møller and Skaaning, per Wikipedia) dispute that the sequence holds in the contemporary era (✅ Wikipedia: T. H. Marshall).
- Giddens disputes the smooth, unilinear logic (PT-3218).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Triad periods: 18th / 19th / 20th century (English) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3208–PT-3210, PT-3218 (Giddens), PT-3233 (Western genealogy).
**Trap:** ❌ "Marshall gives a universal, necessary three-stage chronology for all countries" (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Mains hook:** Use Marshall for vocabulary and an English case; add one line that the order is historical, not mandatory.
**Back:** *"Marshall's order: England's story, not the world's law."*

### Flashcard PT-3212 — Negative vs Positive Rights Within Citizenship
**Front:** How do negative and positive rights relate inside citizenship?
**Definition (learn):** Negative rights protect against interference; positive rights claim enabling provision. Modern citizenship combines both; they are not sealed chronological stages (✅ owner 16 §5, PDF pp.326–329).
**Breakdown:**
- Negative: liberty against interference (civil rights).
- Positive: welfare, education, enabling provision (social rights).
- Political rights need both: freedom to organise plus institutions to vote (⚠️ synthesis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.326–329 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3238 (negative/positive, ch.17), PT-3210, PT-3211.
**Trap:** Do not present negative-to-positive as a dated sequence (✅ owner 16 §5).
**Mains hook:** Citizenship's social dimension is positive in form but civil in purpose (protecting dignity).
**Back:** *"Stay out of my way; help me get there — both at once."*

### Flashcard PT-3213 — Nozick: Libertarian Membership
**Front:** What kind of membership does Nozick's libertarian citizenship describe?
**Definition (learn):** Strong individual rights and a minimal protective state, not a generic consumer-of-public-goods image (✅ owner 16 §6, PDF pp.330–331).
**Breakdown:**
- Membership via strong individual rights.
- State limited to protection and enforcement (✅ owner 17 adv §5, PDF p.365).
- Weak social solidarity and public obligation (✅ owner 16 adv §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.330–331 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3008 (laissez-faire night-watchman), PT-3250 (Nozick anti-redistributive), PT-3010 (three strands liberal).
**Trap:** Do not describe Nozick's citizen as a "consumer client" in the generic sense (✅ owner 16 §6); the owner's adv table calls it "rational consumer/client" — read it as libertarian choice-holder.
**Mains hook:** Libertarian citizenship protects choice, but thin social solidarity is its weakness.
**Back:** *"Nozick's citizen: rights strong, state thin."*

### Flashcard PT-3214 — Arendt: Public Freedom and Action in Concert
**Front:** What does Arendt stress about citizenship?
**Definition (learn):** Public freedom and collective action among citizens, not passive receipt of status (✅ owner 16 §6, PDF p.331).
**Breakdown:**
- Active citizenship: acting together in public.
- Shares an active-citizenship concern with Walzer and Barber, but remains distinct (✅ owner 16 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.331 (✅ owner 16). "The right to have rights" and *The Origins of Totalitarianism* (1951) are ⚠️ memory-only: the fetched Wikipedia page confirms the 1951 book but not the phrase (✅ Wikipedia: Hannah Arendt; phrase ⚠️).
**Links:** PT-3092 (Arendt power vs violence), PT-3097 (Arendt not glorifying violence), PT-3215 (Walzer), PT-3216 (Barber).
**Trap:** Arendt's active citizenship is not the same as Rousseau's general will (PT-3206).
**Mains hook:** Arendt shows that without a political place, even formal rights are fragile; citizenship is an act, not only a status.
**Back:** *"Arendt: citizenship is acting together in public."*

### Flashcard PT-3215 — Walzer: Bounded Political Membership
**Front:** What does Walzer's citizenship theory emphasise?
**Definition (learn):** Membership in a bounded political community and the distributive importance of deciding who belongs (✅ owner 16 §6, PDF p.331).
**Breakdown:**
- Community is bounded; boundaries are a substantive choice.
- Boundary-setting is itself a distributive act (✅ owner 16 §6).
- *Spheres of Justice* (1983) is a verified Walzer title (✅ Wikipedia: Spheres of Justice); the membership chapter and its argument are ⚠️ memory-only.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.331 (✅ owner 16). Spheres of Justice, 1983, Basic Books (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3214 (Arendt), PT-3201, PT-3227 (Kymlicka group rights).
**Trap:** Walzer's bounded membership is not a defence of exclusion by default; it is a claim about who may decide membership.
**Mains hook:** Bounded membership is a fact of political life; the question is how boundaries are drawn and justified.
**Back:** *"Walzer: who belongs is a choice that distributes power."*

### Flashcard PT-3216 — Barber: Strong Democracy and Participatory Citizenship
**Front:** What does Barber's citizen do that a representative voter does not?
**Definition (learn):** Barber defends participatory "strong democracy" in which citizens shape common purposes, not merely choose representatives (✅ owner 16 §6, PDF p.331).
**Breakdown:**
- Strong democracy: deliberation and co-decision.
- Contrast with representative voting-only citizenship.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.331 (✅ owner 16). Year of *Strong Democracy* ⚠️ not in owner text; memory-only (⚠️ 1984).
**Links:** PT-3206 (Rousseau), PT-3214 (Arendt), PT-3014 (communitarian).
**Trap:** Do not place Barber in the libertarian camp; he is a participatory democrat (✅ owner 16 §6).
**Mains hook:** Participation deepens citizenship but scale and capacity limit it (see communitarian critique in PT-3014).
**Back:** *"Barber: citizens shape purposes, not just pick rulers."*

### Flashcard PT-3217 — Marx: Political vs Human Emancipation
**Front:** How does Marxist theory explain formal equal citizenship alongside class inequality?
**Definition (learn):** Marx's distinction between political emancipation and human emancipation shows how formally equal citizens can coexist with class and property relations; rights expansion is an achievement of struggle (✅ owner 16 §6, PDF p.331).
**Breakdown:**
- Political emancipation = formal equality before the state.
- Human emancipation = removal of class and property domination.
- Later Marxist accounts see rights as won through struggle, not neutral gifts (✅ owner 16 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no date for the distinction; the essay "On the Jewish Question" (1843) is ⚠️ memory-only.
**Links:** PT-3011 (Marxist class state), PT-3079 (Marx political power economic), PT-3249 (Marx bourgeois rights), PT-3218 (Giddens).
**Trap:** Political emancipation is not human emancipation; do not say Marx opposed rights wholesale (PT-3249).
**Mains hook:** Formal citizenship can coexist with class inequality; the test is whether economic power is also redistributed.
**Back:** *"Political freedom in the state, human freedom from class."*

### Flashcard PT-3218 — Giddens 1982 vs 1985: Keep the Two Arguments Apart
**Front:** What does Giddens argue about Marshall, and which work carries which argument?
**Definition (learn):** In "Class Division, Class Conflict and Citizenship Rights" (1982), Giddens argues rights were won through class conflict and political struggle, not gradual evolution. His surveillance analysis of the modern state belongs to *The Nation-State and Violence* (1985) (✅ owner 16 §12; ⚠️ 1982 title and 1985 title unverified by fetch).
**Breakdown:**
- 1982 essay: against smooth evolutionary logic; unilinear history rejected; class conflict stressed (✅ owner 16 adv §7).
- 1985 book: surveillance and administrative capacity (✅ owner 16 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.331–333 (✅ owner 16 adv).
**Links:** PT-3211 (Marshall sequence), PT-3217 (Marx), PT-3102 (panopticism, surveillance).
**Trap:** ❌ Merging the 1982 conflict argument and the 1985 surveillance argument into one book claim (✅ owner 16 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Giddens corrects a linear reading of Marshall and shows struggle as the engine of rights expansion.
**Back:** *"1982 struggle won the rights; 1985 surveillance runs the state."*

### Flashcard PT-3219 — Held and Turner: Democratic Autonomy and Social Closure
**Front:** How do David Held and B. S. Turner extend citizenship beyond national voting?
**Definition (learn):** Held develops democratic autonomy across interconnected sites of power rather than confining citizenship to periodic national voting (✅ owner 16 §6, PDF pp.333–334). Turner treats citizenship as a contested social institution shaped by stratification, movements and struggles over membership (✅ owner 16 §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Held: multiple sites of power; democratic autonomy.
- Turner: social closure, stratification, struggle over membership.
- They do not share one bundle of claims (✅ owner 16 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.333–334 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3080 (Gramsci civil society), PT-3019 (pluralist), PT-3082 (Mosca).
**Trap:** Do not assign Held and Turner one shared theory of citizenship.
**Mains hook:** Once power sits across global and social sites, citizenship must be measured where decisions are actually made.
**Back:** *"Held: autonomy across sites. Turner: closure and struggle."*

### Flashcard PT-3220 — Feminist Critique: Legal Equality Is Not Equal Participation
**Front:** What does the feminist critique show about formal citizenship?
**Definition (learn):** Legal equality does not exhaust citizenship: under-representation, domestic role expectations and unequal opportunity keep women substantively subordinate despite formal equality (✅ owner 16 §7, PDF pp.334–336).
**Breakdown:**
- Public/private divide: domestic and reproductive labour sit outside the recognised political-economic sphere (⚠️ boundary-respecting strand, owner 16 §13).
- Voting rights did not secure equal legislative representation (PDF pp.335–336).
- Gauba presents reservation for women in panchayats as a starting point toward fuller participation (PDF p.336, ✅ owner 16 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba's country figures are book-period historical data, not current (✅ owner 16 §7).
**Links:** PT-3017 (Millett), PT-3018 (Eisenstein), PT-3090 (patriarchy Engels), PT-3091 (three accounts of patriarchy), PT-3229.
**Trap:** "Once legal equality is granted, feminist critique is over" is wrong (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Mains hook:** The feminist critique moves the question from who is a citizen to who can actually participate.
**Back:** *"Vote granted, seat withheld: formal equality, substantive gap."*

### Flashcard PT-3221 — Subaltern Critique: Broad Grouping, Not Gramsci or Spivak
**Front:** Who counts as "subaltern" in Gauba's citizenship critique, and what must it not be confused with?
**Definition (learn):** Gauba's broad grouping: all groups discriminated against on irrelevant grounds such as caste, gender, religion, language, age, region or vocation, not only class (✅ owner 16 §9, PDF pp.336–337).
**Breakdown:**
- Groups equal in formal law but inferior in social practice (✅ owner 16 adv §5).
- Class is one ground among several.
- Gauba's usage is not Gramsci's vocabulary (PT-3012), not Subaltern Studies, and not Spivak's later argument (✅ owner 16 §9, §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.336–337 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3012 (Gramsci), PT-3015 (postcolonial state), PT-3220 (feminist), PT-3029 (postcolonial paradox).
**Trap:** ❌ "Subaltern critique is only about class" (✅ owner 16 §9). ❌ Treating Gauba's broad "subaltern" as identical to every use of the word.
**Mains hook:** Caste and region can make a citizen formally equal yet socially subordinate; this widens the critique beyond Marx.
**Back:** *"Subaltern here means all discriminated on irrelevant grounds, not class alone."*

### Flashcard PT-3222 — Stateless Person (1954 Convention)
**Front:** Who is a stateless person in international law, and how does this differ from a refugee and a stateless nation?
**Definition (learn):** A person "not considered as a national by any State under the operation of its law" (✅ owner 16 §13; ✅ Wikipedia: Stateless person).
**Breakdown:**
- 1954 Convention relating to the Status of Stateless Persons: the definition (✅ Wikipedia).
- 1961 Convention on the Reduction of Statelessness: adopted 1961; in force 13 December 1975 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Stateless person ≠ refugee (not all refugees are stateless, and vice versa) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Stateless nation (a people without its own state) is categorically different (✅ owner 16 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1954; 1961; in force 13 Dec 1975 (✅ Wikipedia: Stateless person).
**Links:** PT-3223 (citizenship vs nationality), PT-3224 (acquisition principles), PT-3204.
**Trap:** Owner gives the definition without article number; Article 1(1) is ⚠️ (Wikipedia does not name the article).
**Mains hook:** Statelessness shows that formal citizenship is the gate; without it, even universal rights have no state to enforce them.
**Back:** *"Stateless: no state counts you as its national under its law."*

### Flashcard PT-3223 — Citizenship vs Legal Nationality vs National Identity
**Front:** How do citizenship, legal nationality and national identity differ?
**Definition (learn):** Citizenship is domestic legal-political membership; legal nationality is an international-law bond; national identity is cultural or political self-identification (✅ owner 16 §14, PDF p.324).
**Breakdown:**
- Citizenship: domestic constitutional and statutory law; can be formal without effective rights.
- Legal nationality: nationality law as recognised internationally; overlaps with citizenship externally.
- National identity: history, language, culture; not granted or withdrawn by statute.
- Stateless nation ≠ stateless person (✅ owner 16 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner table (✅ owner 16 §14).
**Links:** PT-3222, PT-3224, PT-3201.
**Trap:** Do not treat legal nationality as synonymous with ethnicity; do not infer legal citizenship from cultural identity (✅ owner 16 §14).
**Mains hook:** Three axes give three distinct tests: document, bond, belonging.
**Back:** *"Citizen: domestic law. Nationality: international bond. Identity: belonging."*

### Flashcard PT-3224 — Jus Soli, Jus Sanguinis and Denizenship
**Front:** What are the two birth principles of nationality, and what is a denizen?
**Definition (learn):** Jus soli assigns nationality through birth on a state's territory; jus sanguinis through descent from a national parent. A denizen is a long-term foreign resident with many civil and social rights but no full political membership (⚠️ owner 16 §13; Hammar 1990 unverified).
**Breakdown:**
- Jus soli: territory-based.
- Jus sanguinis: descent-based.
- Denizen (Tomas Hammar): civil and social rights, no full political membership; not one uniform legal status (⚠️).
- Actual regimes must be verified; pure forms are rare (⚠️ owner 16 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Tomas Hammar, *Democracy and the Nation State* (1990) ⚠️ owner-cited; Wikipedia denizen page returned 404 (unverified).
**Links:** PT-3204, PT-3222, PT-3223.
**Trap:** Do not assume any state adopts jus soli or jus sanguinis in pure form without checking the statute (⚠️ owner 16 §13).
**Mains hook:** Birth rules decide who enters the citizenship ledger; denizenship shows the gap between residence and membership.
**Back:** *"Soil makes you; blood makes you; denizens wait at the gate."*

### Flashcard PT-3225 — Iris Marion Young: Differentiated Citizenship
**Front:** What is Young's critique of universal citizenship?
**Definition (learn):** Young criticises universality understood as identical treatment and one allegedly neutral citizen norm that reproduces privileged-group experience; she does not reject universal inclusion or equal moral status (⚠️ owner 16 adv §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Young's "Polity and Group Difference: A Critique of the Ideal of Universal Citizenship", *Ethics*, Jan 1989, vol. 99 no. 2, pp. 250–274 (✅ Wikipedia: Iris Marion Young).
- *Justice and the Politics of Difference*, Princeton University Press, 1990 (✅ Wikipedia: Iris Marion Young).
- Supports group-conscious representation and participation (⚠️ owner 16 adv §12).
- Groups as social positions produced by oppression, not fixed essences (⚠️ owner 16 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1989 essay; 1990 book (✅ Wikipedia). The essay's claim about what universal citizenship reproduces is not confirmed by the fetched page (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-3226 (Kymlicka), PT-3228 (essentialism reply), PT-3220 (feminist), PT-3230 (panchayat illustration).
**Trap:** Young is not a relativist; she accepts universal inclusion and moral equality (⚠️ owner 16 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Young moves the question from "is the content of rights complete" (Marshall) to "do equal institutions reproduce group exclusion".
**Back:** *"Young: universal citizenship, yes; neutral norm, no."*

### Flashcard PT-3226 — Kymlicka: Group-Differentiated Rights and the External/Internal Test
**Front:** What are Kymlicka's group-differentiated rights, and what is his key limiting distinction?
**Definition (learn):** Within a liberal framework, group-differentiated rights include self-government, polyethnic and special-representation claims. External protections against majority decisions are distinguished from internal restrictions on a group's own members (✅ owner 16 adv §12; ✅ Wikipedia: Will Kymlicka).
**Breakdown:**
- *Multicultural Citizenship: A Liberal Theory of Minority Rights*, Oxford University Press, 1995 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Three types: special group representation; self-government; polyethnic rights (✅ Wikipedia: Will Kymlicka).
- External protection: justified against majority decisions, not to permit oppression (✅ Wikipedia).
- Internal restriction: "cannot be justified from a liberal perspective" (✅ Wikipedia), though Wikipedia notes limited allowance for national minorities in some cases (⚠️ owner-vs-source nuance flagged).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1995 (✅ Wikipedia). Polyethnic example: exemption of Sikhs from motorcycle helmet requirements (✅ Wikipedia; as an illustration, not an Indian legal claim).
**Links:** PT-3225, PT-3227, PT-3228.
**Trap:** Do not confuse external protection with internal restriction; the second is the limit where a minority curtails members' basic freedoms (✅ owner 16 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Kymlicka supplies a liberal-compatible defence of group rights, with a built-in limit against intra-group oppression.
**Back:** *"Kymlicka: protect the group from outside; never restrict its members inside."*

### Flashcard PT-3227 — Objection and Reply: Group Rights Undermine Equal Citizenship
**Front:** How does a defender answer the claim that group-differentiated rights create privilege and threaten national unity?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: group rights create privilege and weaken equal citizenship. Reply (Kymlicka's own justification, ⚠️ owner 16 §15): such rights correct disadvantages from majority-culture bias in ordinary institutions and protect members' freedom of choice within their culture.
**Breakdown:**
- Aim: equal opportunity to participate, not a new hierarchy.
- Test: external protection vs internal restriction (PT-3226).
- Check whether the remedy expands participation or entrenches hierarchy (⚠️ owner 16 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 16 §15 (⚠️ synthesis).
**Links:** PT-3226, PT-3215 (Walzer boundaries), PT-3231.
**Trap:** Do not present group rights as automatically justified; the reply holds only under the external/internal test.
**Mains hook:** Equal citizenship is served by group remedies only when those remedies protect members rather than restrict them.
**Back:** *"Privilege? No: remedy for bias, with a limit on restriction."*

### Flashcard PT-3228 — Objection and Reply: Group Identity Essentialises Members
**Front:** Why might group-differentiated citizenship be criticised as essentialist, and how is it answered?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: emphasising group difference assumes internal homogeneity and ignores inequality within the group (e.g., women within a marginalised community). Reply: Young treats groups as social positions produced by oppression, not fixed essences; Kymlicka's internal-restriction limit prevents group leadership dominating members (⚠️ owner 16 §15).
**Breakdown:**
- Young: relational, contestable categories.
- Kymlicka: internal-restriction limit.
- Intersectional refinement sharpens the answer (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 16 §15 (⚠️ synthesis).
**Links:** PT-3225, PT-3226, PT-3220 (feminist).
**Trap:** Group-conscious analysis can essentialise groups unless categories remain relational and open to correction (⚠️ owner 16 §12).
**Mains hook:** A group right is defensible only if it does not lock internal hierarchies in place.
**Back:** *"Essence? No, position. And the internal limit guards the women inside."*

### Flashcard PT-3229 — Objection and Reply: Formal Equality Already Achieved
**Front:** If universal suffrage and non-discrimination law exist, is the citizenship debate settled?
