**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba's own feminist and subaltern material shows persistent under-representation and structural exclusion despite formal equality (✅ owner 16 §15, PDF pp.334–337). Young and the postcolonial and migration strands generalise this into a standing objection (⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Formal equality is necessary, not sufficient.
- Test: is effective participation available?
- Postcolonial strand: colonial classifications can carry hierarchy into formally universal membership unless corrected (⚠️ owner 16 §13).
- Migration strand: residence, nationality and political membership diverge (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.334–337 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3203, PT-3220, PT-3221, PT-3231.
**Trap:** "Once legal equality is granted, feminist critique is over" (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Mains hook:** Formal universality is the start of citizenship, not the end; cite one named critic.
**Back:** *"Formal universality is the floor, not the roof."*

### Flashcard PT-3230 — Indian Illustration Boundary: Citizenship and Panchayat Reservation
**Front:** Which Indian illustration is safe to use in a citizenship answer, and what must be left to Polity?
**Definition (learn):** Use single citizenship as one institutional choice (no separate state citizenship) and women's reservation in panchayats as a starting point toward fuller participation. Article-level and statute-level detail belongs to Polity (⚠️ owner 16 §16).
**Breakdown:**
- Single citizenship: neither proves nor disproves group rights (⚠️ owner 16 §16).
- Panchayat reservation: illustrates Young's group-conscious representation; only analogically related to Kymlicka's special representation (✅ owner 16 §16).
- Documentation-based debates illustrate de jure status vs practical proof of status (⚠️ owner 16 §16).
- Constitutional articles (citizenship provisions in Part II) are Polity material, not reproduced here (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.336 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3225, PT-3226, PT-3224.
**Trap:** Do not present panchayat reservation as derived from Kymlicka's theory (✅ owner 16 §16).
**Mains hook:** One Indian illustration, carefully bounded, earns more than a long list of current-affairs claims.
**Back:** *"Panchayat seats: Young's case in practice; Kymlicka only by analogy."*

### Flashcard PT-3231 — Citizenship Answer Spine: 10/15/20-Mark Qualified Thesis
**Front:** How should a citizenship answer be structured at 10, 15 and 20 marks?
**Definition (learn):** Define citizenship as a two-way status, state Marshall's triad, bring in named critiques, end with a qualified conclusion (⚠️ owner 16 §17–19 scaffold, not a book rule).
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks (~150 words): define → triad line → one named critique (Young or Kymlicka) with its limit → one-line qualified conclusion.
- 15 marks (~200 words): add two critiques and one objection-reply pair (PT-3227 or PT-3228).
- 20 marks (~250 words): three named critiques, full objection-reply chain, one Indian illustration (PT-3230), synthesis conclusion.
- Qualified conclusion: citizenship should combine a universal core of civil-political-social rights with narrowly justified, member-protecting group-differentiated correctives (⚠️ owner 16 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Word counts are repository targets (⚠️ owner 16 §19).
**Links:** PT-3201, PT-3208–PT-3210, PT-3225–PT-3229, PT-3230.
**Trap:** Never end on a flat yes/no; grade the claim as holds / holds under conditions / fails (⚠️ owner 16 §19 move 5).
**Mains hook:** The qualified conclusion is the examiner-grade ending.
**Back:** *"Define, triad, critic, objection, Indian case, qualified verdict."*

### Flashcard PT-3232 — Trap: Citizenship Is Not Only Rights, Nor Only Legal Status
**Front:** Which two one-sided answers does the owner warn against?
**Definition (learn):** Citizenship is neither only a legal label nor only a rights bundle. It is a reciprocal status with duties and allegiance, and its substance depends on lived access (✅ owner 16 §9, §1).
**Breakdown:**
- ❌ Legal label only: ignores substantive access (PT-3203).
- ❌ Rights bundle only: ignores duties and allegiance (PT-3201).
- ✅ Two-way status plus substance test.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 16 §9 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** PT-3201, PT-3203, PT-3231.
**Trap:** Examiners reward the two-sided definition; a one-sided answer loses the central distinction.
**Mains hook:** Use the duty-right symmetry to set up the formal-substantive argument.
**Back:** *"Not just a label, not just a basket: duties and access."*

### Flashcard PT-3233 — Stylised Western Genealogy of Citizenship
**Front:** What historical sequence does Gauba sketch for citizenship, and what caution attaches to it?
**Definition (learn):** Restricted ancient citizenship → Roman expansion with differentiated rights → medieval eclipse → republican revival → liberal rights-based citizenship → welfare-oriented development (✅ owner 16 adv §3, PDF pp.325–329).
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: restricted Greek polis citizenship (PT-3205).
- Stage 2: Roman expansion with differentiated rights.
- Stage 3: medieval eclipse; republican revival.
- Stage 4: liberal rights-based citizenship; then welfare.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.325–329 (✅ owner 16 adv). Dates are not given in owner text (⚠️ none added).
**Links:** PT-3211 (Marshall sequence), PT-3205, PT-3210.
**Trap:** Owner: stylised, Western, not a universal or automatically progressive history (✅ owner 16 §4).
**Mains hook:** Use the genealogy as a sketch, then test each claim against the critiques.
**Back:** *"Polis, Rome, eclipse, republic, liberal rights, welfare: a Western sketch."*

### GROUP — 17 Human Rights, Civil Liberties and Democratic Rights (basic + advanced)

> Owners: `Political-Theory/basic/17_Human-Rights-Civil-Liberties-and-Democratic-Rights.md` and `advanced/17_…` (Gauba Ch.15, PDF pp.338–366; supplementary *Socio-Political Philosophy* PDF pp.69–76). Tags as in Group 16. Fetched confirmations for UDHR, ICCPR, Vienna Declaration, Hohfeld, Dworkin, Gewirth and the 1954/1961 statelessness conventions are noted; owner-only items are tagged `(✅ owner 17)`.

### Flashcard PT-3234 — Human Rights, Civil Liberties and Democratic Rights: Overlapping Branches (concept card)
**Front:** How do human rights, civil liberties and democratic rights relate to one another?
**Definition (learn):** Human rights are the widest umbrella, attaching to human status. Civil liberties are legal protections against arbitrary power; democratic rights organise political participation. The branches overlap and are not a rigid universal hierarchy (✅ owner 17 §1, §5, PDF pp.338, 361–363).
**Breakdown:**
- Human rights: umbrella; attach to persons as such.
- Civil liberties: expression, religion, movement, association, fair trial, personal liberty (✅ owner 17).
- Democratic rights: vote, choose government, influence decisions, seek office (✅ owner 17).
- Socio-economic and cultural rights sit inside the human-rights domain (✅ owner 17 adv §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba Ch.15, PDF pp.338–366 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3235, PT-3239, PT-3240, PT-3273 (objection-reply), PT-3278 (correlativity trap).
**Trap:** "Human rights, democratic rights and civil liberties are interchangeable" (✅ owner 17 §9).
**Mains hook:** Use the three-part map to show that human rights need institutions in each branch; no single branch is enough.
**Back:** *"Human rights: the umbrella. Liberties guard; democratic rights participate."*

### Flashcard PT-3235 — Human Rights: Rights Attached to Human Status
**Front:** What makes a right a human right?
**Definition (learn):** A right to which an individual is entitled by virtue of being human; it remains relevant even where ordinary citizenship rights are absent or exhausted (✅ owner 17 §3, PDF p.338).
**Breakdown:**
- Grounded in dignity, not citizenship (✅ owner 17 adv §4).
- Sets moral standard by which ordinary legal rights may be judged (✅ owner 17 §2).
- Moral priority does not automatically place every human-rights claim legally above a constitution (✅ owner 17 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.338 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3234, PT-3254 (Nuremberg), PT-3255 (UDHR), PT-3270 (UDHR trap).
**Trap:** Human rights are not "the only rights"; they are the widest class (✅ owner 17 adv §4).
**Mains hook:** Human status grounds a claim that outlasts a particular state's grant.
**Back:** *"Human rights: entitled as human, not as member."*

### Flashcard PT-3236 — Rights: Claims Against Arbitrary Power and for a Better Life
**Front:** How does Gauba define rights in general?
**Definition (learn):** Justified normative or legal positions; especially claims restraining arbitrary state power and claims for conditions of a better life (✅ owner 17 §3, PDF pp.339–340).
**Breakdown:**
- Restraint dimension: no arbitrary interference.
- Enabling dimension: claims that require positive provision (PT-3238).
- Rights need legal-constitutional embodiment to function (✅ owner 17 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.339–340 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3237 (Hohfeld), PT-3238, PT-3241 (natural/moral/legal).
**Trap:** Do not reduce rights to non-interference alone (✅ owner 17 §9 trap list).
**Mains hook:** Start the rights answer with the two-sided definition: limit and enable.
**Back:** *"Rights: a shield against power and a claim on conditions."*

### Flashcard PT-3237 — Hohfeld's Jural Relations (Claim, Liberty, Power, Immunity)
**Front:** What are Hohfeld's eight jural concepts, and what are the correlatives?
**Definition (learn):** Hohfeld's analysis: right/duty, privilege/no-right, power/liability, immunity/disability, arranged into four correlative pairs and four opposite pairs (✅ owner 17 §3; ✅ Wikipedia: Hohfeldian analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- Correlatives: right ↔ duty; privilege ↔ no-right; power ↔ liability; immunity ↔ disability (✅ Wikipedia).
- Opposites: right ↔ no-right; privilege ↔ duty; power ↔ disability; immunity ↔ liability (✅ Wikipedia; table read from scraped text, so verify against the book before quoting).
- Owner: claim-rights identify duty-bearers; liberties, powers and immunities need different analysis (✅ owner 17 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Articles 1913 (23 Yale LJ 16) and 1917 (26 Yale LJ 710); posthumous book 1919 (✅ Wikipedia: Hohfeldian analysis).
**Links:** PT-3278 (correlativity trap), PT-3236, PT-3248 (Laski not strict Hohfeld).
**Trap:** Not every right is a claim against the state; Hohfeld shows liberties need no correlative duty (✅ owner 17 adv §4).
**Mains hook:** Use Hohfeld to show that "rights imply duties" is true only for claim-rights.
**Back:** *"Hohfeld: claim binds, liberty frees, power changes, immunity shields."*

### Flashcard PT-3238 — Negative and Positive Dimensions of Rights
**Front:** What is the difference between the negative and positive dimensions of a right?
**Definition (learn):** Negative dimension: duty of restraint (non-interference). Positive dimension: duty to protect or provide. Most rights combine both (✅ owner 17 §3, §5, PDF pp.340–341).
**Breakdown:**
- Negative: non-interference with thought or expression.
- Positive: education, medical care, legal aid (✅ owner 17 §3).
- Speech needs both: no censorship and protection from violent suppression (✅ owner 17 §5).
- Civil liberties also need positive protection against private interference (✅ owner 17 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.340–341 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3212, PT-3236, PT-3262 (generations), PT-3272 (generations trap).
**Trap:** Negative and positive are dimensions of obligation, not sealed classes of rights (✅ owner 17 §3).
**Mains hook:** A right that needs no state action at all is rare; test each right for both dimensions.
**Back:** *"Negative: don't. Positive: do. Most rights need both."*

### Flashcard PT-3239 — Civil Liberties: Legal Freedoms Against Arbitrary Power
**Front:** What are civil liberties and what does their exercise require?
**Definition (learn):** Legal protections such as expression, religion, movement, association, fair trial and personal freedom against arbitrary public power; exercise may need state protection from private coercion (✅ owner 17 §3, PDF pp.361–363).
**Breakdown:**
- Personal liberty, expression, thought, faith, association, movement, fair trial (✅ owner 17 adv §4).
- Not purely negative; not only vertical (✅ owner 17 §8).
- Arbitrary arrest, censorship, coercion are the adversaries (✅ owner 17 adv §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.362–363 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3240, PT-3234, PT-3269 (prisoners).
**Trap:** Civil liberties are not "a smaller subset of democratic rights in every theory" (✅ owner 17 §9).
**Mains hook:** Civil liberties are the protective branch; they can survive where elections do not.
**Back:** *"Civil liberty: freedom from the arbitrary hand of power."*

### Flashcard PT-3240 — Democratic Rights: Participation Under Membership and Eligibility
**Front:** What are democratic rights and what conditions normally attach?
**Definition (learn):** Participation rights enabling eligible citizens to vote, influence public decisions and seek office, under reasonable citizenship, age and procedural conditions (✅ owner 17 §3, PDF pp.361–362).
**Breakdown:**
- Vote, choose government, influence decisions, seek office (✅ owner 17 §3).
- Normally conditioned by citizenship and eligibility (✅ owner 17 adv §4).
- Electoral rights normally require citizenship; some personal liberties apply to non-citizens (⚠️ owner 17 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.361–362 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3209 (Marshall political), PT-3234, PT-3273 (objection-reply).
**Trap:** Electoral rights are not logically necessary for every civil liberty (✅ owner 17 §8).
**Mains hook:** Democratic rights need civil liberties to be meaningful; elections on paper can coexist with repression.
**Back:** *"Democratic right: vote, rule, stand for office, if you qualify."*

### Flashcard PT-3241 — Natural, Moral and Legal Rights Are Not Synonyms
**Front:** How should natural, moral and legal rights be distinguished?
**Definition (learn):** Natural rights are one proposed moral grounding (human nature or reason); moral rights state what ought to be recognised; legal rights are positions recognised by positive law. These categories are not synonyms (✅ owner 17 §3, §5, PDF pp.341–345; categories ⚠️ as analytical framing).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural-rights theory grounds rights in nature or reason (✅ owner 17 §5).
- Moral rights need not rely on natural-law premises (✅ owner 17 §5).
- Legal rights depend on positive-law recognition (✅ owner 17 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.341–345 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3243 (Locke), PT-3246 (Bentham), PT-3242 (Barker), PT-3237.
**Trap:** A natural right is not automatically a legal right; state recognition alone does not confirm moral legitimacy.
**Mains hook:** Use the three-way split to show why both moral justification and enforcement are needed.
**Back:** *"Natural: grounded. Moral: ought. Legal: enacted."*

### Flashcard PT-3242 — Ideal (Personality) Rights: Green and Barker's Dual Source
**Front:** What does Barker say a full right requires, and what does Green contribute?
**Definition (learn):** Green ties rights to socially recognised conditions of moral personality and the common good. Barker: a full right has a double source, development of personality and legal guarantee; either source alone yields only a quasi-right (✅ owner 17 §3, §6; Gauba PDF pp.342–345).
**Breakdown:**
- Green: rights are conditions of moral personality, not atomistic possessions (✅ owner 17 §6).
- Barker: personality source + legal guarantee (✅ owner 17 §6).
- Ideal claim alone is not legally enforceable; legal recognition alone is not moral legitimacy (✅ owner 17 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.342–343, 345 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3245 (Green), PT-3241, PT-3243.
**Trap:** Owner's Barker "full right" needs both sources; one alone is quasi-right (✅ owner 17 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Barker's synthesis is the strongest single answer to "moral or legal?".
**Back:** *"Barker: personality plus guarantee makes a full right; one alone, quasi."*

### Flashcard PT-3243 — Locke: Natural Rights and Resistance
**Front:** What rights does Locke ground, and when may resistance follow?
**Definition (learn):** Life, liberty and property as pre-political entitlements; resistance follows serious breach or forfeiture of governmental trust, not every imperfect performance (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF pp.341–342, 365).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural rights: life, liberty, property (✅ owner 17 §6).
- Resistance threshold: serious breach or forfeiture (✅ owner 17 §6).
- Bentham later attacks the indeterminacy of natural rights (✅ owner 17 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.341–342, 365 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3207 (Locke citizenship), PT-3036 (Locke obligation), PT-3046 (resistance).
**Trap:** Locke's resistance is not a licence for any dissent; it is for serious breach (✅ owner 17 §6).
**Mains hook:** Natural rights supply moral force against unjust power, but their content needs institutions to fix.
**Back:** *"Locke: life, liberty, property; resist when trust is seriously broken."*

### Flashcard PT-3244 — Tom Paine: Civil Rights from Natural Rights
**Front:** How does Tom Paine relate civil rights to natural rights?
**Definition (learn):** Civil rights derive from pre-existing natural rights; natural rights are surrendered or exchanged only insofar as common power is needed (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF p.342).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural rights come first.
- Civil rights are the exchanged portion for common power (✅ owner 17 §6).
- "Teleological" is not the safest label for Paine (✅ owner 17 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.342 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3243, PT-3241.
**Trap:** Do not attribute to Paine a view that all natural rights are surrendered to the state (✅ owner 17 §6).
**Mains hook:** Paine's exchange logic shows civil rights as a bargain that preserves what common power does not need.
**Back:** *"Paine: natural rights first; civil rights are the portion we lend to common power."*

### Flashcard PT-3245 — T. H. Green: Socially Recognised Conditions of Personality
**Front:** How does Green locate rights?
**Definition (learn):** Rights are socially recognised conditions of moral personality and the common good, not atomistic pre-social possessions (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF pp.342–343).
**Breakdown:**
- Personality: the rational self realised in society.
- Rights need recognition by society and law.
- Connects to Barker's personality source (PT-3242).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.342–343 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3242, PT-3039 (Green obligation to common good).
**Trap:** Green's rights are not pre-social possessions; this differs from Locke's natural rights (PT-3243).
**Mains hook:** Green supplies the moral content that legal rights should serve.
**Back:** *"Green: rights are the social conditions of a good self."*

### Flashcard PT-3246 — Bentham: Rights Are Creatures of Law
**Front:** What is Bentham's objection to natural rights?
**Definition (learn):** Bentham attacks natural rights and defends legal rights as creatures of law: real rights exist once the state recognises and enforces them (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF pp.344–345).
**Breakdown:**
- Legal rights: enforceability converts claims into working rights (✅ owner 17 §12).
- Risk: legitimises whatever a state enacts, even if unjust (✅ owner 17 §12).
- Popular epigram "nonsense upon stilts" is attributed to Bentham; provenance not verified here (⚠️ owner 17 §19 caution).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.344–345 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3241, PT-3243, PT-3242.
**Trap:** Do not stake marks on the exact wording of a famous rights epigram (⚠️ owner 17 §19).
**Mains hook:** Bentham gives enforceability; Barker adds moral justification.
**Back:** *"Bentham: no law, no right; natural rights are rhetoric."*

### Flashcard PT-3247 — Burke: Prescriptive Rights and the Limit of Longevity
**Front:** How does Burke ground rights, and what limits his historical theory?
**Definition (learn):** Rights are prescriptive inheritances embodied in evolved institutions; longevity alone cannot validate an oppressive practice (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF pp.346–347).
**Breakdown:**
- Gradualist, precedent-based rights tradition.
- Owner's examples of oppressive customs: sati, devadasi, untouchability cannot be validated by longevity (✅ owner 17 adv §7, PDF pp.346–347).
- Historical theory can preserve oppressive custom (✅ owner 17 adv §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.346–347 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3241, PT-3260 (universality vs relativism), PT-3243.
**Trap:** Burke's tradition is not a licence for custom that subordinates people; longevity is not moral validity (✅ owner 17 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Historical rights are a check on abstraction, not a shield for injustice.
**Back:** *"Burke: rights inherited, but a bad custom stays bad however old."*

### Flashcard PT-3248 — Laski: Social-Democratic Rights and Duties
**Front:** What is Laski's account of rights, and why is it not Hohfeldian correlativity?
**Definition (learn):** Rights are conditions for personality, equality and common welfare, joined to liberty, social justice and social-ethical duties. The relation is social-ethical, not strict Hohfeldian correlativity (✅ owner 17 §6, adv §5, PDF pp.348–353).
**Breakdown:**
- Liberty + equality + social justice + common welfare (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
- Rights and duties are linked in a wider social-ethical sense (⚠️ owner 17 §5).
- Gauba's question: will entrenched power-holders surrender privilege? (✅ owner 17 adv §7, PDF pp.353–354).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.348–353 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3237 (Hohfeld), PT-3210 (social rights), PT-3278 (correlativity trap).
**Trap:** Do not restate Laski's rights-duties thesis as strict Hohfeldian correlativity for every liberty (✅ owner 17 §5).
**Mains hook:** Laski joins liberty and welfare, yet the power question remains open.
**Back:** *"Laski: rights bring duties of the social kind, and power must yield."*

### Flashcard PT-3249 — Marx: Critique of Bourgeois Rights
**Front:** What does Marx criticise in bourgeois rights, and what must not be attributed to him?
**Definition (learn):** Marx criticises the limits of bourgeois rights and the separation of formally equal political citizens from unequal civil society (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF pp.354–358).
**Breakdown:**
- Formal political equality coexists with class inequality in civil society (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
- Leninist theory and records of particular socialist regimes are separate questions (✅ owner 17 §6).
- Socialist systems secured social-economic rights but often made liberal freedoms conditional (✅ owner 17 adv §7, PDF pp.356–357).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.354–358 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3217 (political vs human emancipation), PT-3011 (Marxist state), PT-3079.
**Trap:** Do not attribute to Marx the record of a particular socialist regime (✅ owner 17 §6).
**Mains hook:** Marx exposes class limits of formal rights; the tension with liberty is the trade-off the owner highlights.
**Back:** *"Marx: formal rights, unequal life; judge the regime separately."*

### Flashcard PT-3250 — Nozick: Strong Rights and Anti-Redistributive State
**Front:** What does Nozick defend in rights theory?
**Definition (learn):** Strong individual rights and a minimal anti-redistributive state limited mainly to protection and enforcement (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF p.365).
**Breakdown:**
- Rights as strong side constraints (✅ owner 17 §6).
- Minimal state: protection and enforcement only (✅ owner 17 §6).
- Contrast: Laski's social-democratic account (PT-3248).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.365 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3213 (Nozick citizenship), PT-3008 (laissez-faire), PT-3248.
**Trap:** "Anti-redistributive" describes Nozick's state, not all libertarian thought (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Nozick's strong rights are answered by welfare-rights theorists; the dispute is over the state's positive duties.
**Back:** *"Nozick: rights strong, state small, redistribution refused."*

### Flashcard PT-3251 — MacIntyre: Challenge to Abstract Universal Rights-Talk
**Front:** How does MacIntyre challenge rights, and what does he not do?
**Definition (learn):** MacIntyre challenges the grounding of abstract universal rights-talk; he does not simply argue that community overrides every individual right (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF pp.365–366).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: abstract universal grounding of rights.
- Not: community overrides all rights (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.365–366 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3260 (universality), PT-3014 (communitarian), PT-3233.
**Trap:** Do not place MacIntyre in a flat anti-rights camp (✅ owner 17 §6).
**Mains hook:** MacIntyre pressures the foundations of rights, not their everyday protection.
**Back:** *"MacIntyre: question the grounding, not the whole house."*

### Flashcard PT-3252 — Firestone and Rowbotham: Two Feminist Analyses of Rights
**Front:** How do Firestone and Rowbotham differ in their feminist analysis?
**Definition (learn):** Firestone is a radical feminist centred on reproductive and sex-class power. Rowbotham is a socialist feminist joining patriarchy to class and labour. They are not one interchangeable theory (✅ owner 17 §6, PDF p.366).
**Breakdown:**
- Firestone: reproduction and sex-class power.
- Rowbotham: patriarchy plus class and labour (✅ owner 17 §6).
- Both criticise formal rights under unequal power (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.366 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3017 (Millett), PT-3018 (Eisenstein), PT-3090 (patriarchy), PT-3091.
**Trap:** Do not merge Firestone and Rowbotham into "the feminist theory of rights" (✅ owner 17 §9).
**Mains hook:** Separate the radical and socialist routes when answering a feminist rights question.
**Back:** *"Firestone: sex-class power. Rowbotham: patriarchy plus class."*

### Flashcard PT-3253 — Trap: Pound and Chafee Are Not One Joint Theory
**Front:** Can Roscoe Pound and Zechariah Chafee be cited as co-authors of a single theory of rights?
**Definition (learn):** No. Pound contributes a social-interest argument; Chafee a civil-liberty argument. They should not be presented as one standard jointly held theory (✅ owner 17 §6, adv §5, PDF p.347).
**Breakdown:**
- Pound: social interests.
- Chafee: civil liberties (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
- Use them separately.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.347 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3248, PT-3250, PT-3234.
**Trap:** A combined "Pound-Chafee theory" is invented; mark it as a mistake (✅ owner 17 §6).
**Mains hook:** Cite each only for the specific argument the owner gives.
**Back:** *"Pound: social interests. Chafee: civil liberties. Two voices."*

### Flashcard PT-3254 — Nuremberg: Individual International Criminal Responsibility
**Front:** Why does the Nuremberg legal turn matter for human rights?
**Definition (learn):** The trials established individual international criminal responsibility, including for crimes against humanity; they moved human rights from moral idea to a legal consequence beyond domestic law (✅ owner 17 §7, PDF p.358).
**Breakdown:**
- Individuals, not only states, held accountable.
- Crimes against humanity recognised as an international crime (✅ owner 17 §7).
- Preceded the UDHR (PT-3255).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-Second World War; PDF p.358 (✅ owner 17). Trial dates are not in the owner text and are not added here (⚠️ none).
**Links:** PT-3255, PT-3235, PT-3259.
**Trap:** Nuremberg did not make the UDHR binding; it made individual responsibility a legal fact (✅ owner 17 §7).
**Mains hook:** Nuremberg is the legal bridge from rights talk to accountability.
**Back:** *"Nuremberg: individuals answer for crimes against humanity."*

### Flashcard PT-3255 — Universal Declaration of Human Rights (1948)
**Front:** What is the UDHR, when was it adopted, and what is its legal status?
**Definition (learn):** A declaration and common standard, not a treaty, domestic constitution or directly enforceable global code (✅ owner 17 §7, PDF pp.358–360).
**Breakdown:**
- Adopted 10 December 1948, Palais de Chaillot, Paris (✅ Wikipedia: UDHR).
- 30 articles (✅ Wikipedia).
- Vote: 48 in favour, 0 against, 8 abstentions, 2 not voting (Honduras and Yemen) (✅ Wikipedia; 58 members then).
- Not legally binding; many provisions are widely treated as customary law (✅ Wikipedia).
- Civil-political, socio-economic and cultural claims plus duties to community (✅ owner 17 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 Dec 1948; 30 articles; 48–0–8 (2 not voting) (✅ Wikipedia: UDHR).
**Links:** PT-3254, PT-3256 (ICCPR), PT-3257 (ICESCR), PT-3270 (trap).
**Trap:** "The UDHR is itself a domestic constitution" is wrong (✅ owner 17 §9).
**Mains hook:** The UDHR is the moral and legal standard that later treaties converted into binding obligations.
**Back:** *"UDHR 1948: 30 articles, a standard, not a treaty."*

### Flashcard PT-3256 — ICCPR: Binding Treaty for States Parties
**Front:** What are the key dates and structure of the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights?
**Definition (learn):** A treaty binding States Parties. It combines immediate respect/ensure and remedy duties with permitted restrictions and derogation rules (✅ owner 17 §7).
**Breakdown:**
- Adopted 16 December 1966 by UN General Assembly Resolution 2200A (XXI) (✅ Wikipedia: ICCPR).
- In force 23 March 1976 after 35th ratification (✅ Wikipedia).
- 175 parties as of December 2025 snapshot (✅ Wikipedia; as-of Dec 2025, time-varying).
- First Optional Protocol (individual complaints): 116 parties; Second Optional Protocol (abolition of death penalty): 92 parties (✅ Wikipedia; as-of snapshot).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 16 Dec 1966; 23 Mar 1976; 175 parties (Dec 2025 snapshot) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3255, PT-3257, PT-3258 (India), PT-3266 (Art 4 emergency).
**Trap:** Party counts change; use the as-of date (✅ Wikipedia snapshot).
**Mains hook:** The ICCPR turns the UDHR's standard into enforceable treaty duties with monitoring.
**Back:** *"ICCPR 1966, in force 1976: civil-political treaty, States bound."*

### Flashcard PT-3257 — ICESCR: Progressive Realisation and Immediate Duties
**Front:** How do the ICESCR's obligations differ from the ICCPR's?
**Definition (learn):** The ICESCR requires progressive realisation of socio-economic and cultural rights while also imposing immediate duties such as non-discrimination and good-faith steps (✅ owner 17 §7).
**Breakdown:**
- Progressive realisation (owner-stated).
- Immediate duties: non-discrimination; good-faith steps (✅ owner 17 §7).
- Common Article 1 self-determination in both Covenants (✅ owner 17 §15).
- Adoption year is with the Covenant pair (⚠️ 1966 not separately fetched in this session).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner: no adoption date given; the Covenant pair is a 1966 text (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-3256, PT-3262 (generations), PT-3263 (self-determination).
**Trap:** Second-generation rights are not mere aspirations; they carry immediate duties too (✅ owner 17 §15).
**Mains hook:** Progressive realisation is not an excuse for inaction on immediate duties.
**Back:** *"ICESCR: progressive, but not postponable on non-discrimination."*

### Flashcard PT-3258 — India, the Covenants and the Optional Protocols
**Front:** When did India accede to the Covenants, and what is India's position on individual complaint routes?
**Definition (learn):** India acceded to both Covenants on 10 April 1979. India is not party to the individual-complaint Optional Protocols of the ICCPR or ICESCR; treaty-body reporting is not an individual petition route (✅ owner 17 §7, adv §13; India's accession date ⚠️ not verified by fetch).
**Breakdown:**
- Accession: 10 April 1979 (✅ owner 17).
- Individual complaint route (First Optional Protocol): India absent (✅ owner 17; ⚠️ fetched ICCPR page did not show the list, and the OP page returned 404).
- Reporting to treaty bodies is not petitioning (✅ owner 17 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 April 1979 (✅ owner 17). OP party counts from Wikipedia ICCPR page: 116 (OP1), 92 (OP2) (as-of Dec 2025).
**Links:** PT-3256, PT-3257, PT-3259.
**Trap:** Ratifying a treaty does not automatically mean accepting individual complaints (✅ owner 17 §7).
**Mains hook:** India's human-rights duties run through treaty reporting and domestic law, not direct petition under OP1 (verify before quoting).
**Back:** *"India: Covenants 1979, no individual petition route."*

### Flashcard PT-3259 — Vienna Declaration and Programme of Action (1993)
**Front:** What does the Vienna Declaration say about universality and cultural variation?
**Definition (learn):** The universal nature of human rights is beyond question; national and regional particularities and cultural backgrounds must be borne in mind, but states remain duty-bound to promote and protect all human rights (✅ owner 17 §14; paragraph wording ⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Adopted by consensus at the World Conference on Human Rights, Vienna, 25 June 1993 (✅ Wikipedia: Vienna Declaration).
- Owner cites Part I para 5 (⚠️ paragraph not confirmed by fetch; Wikipedia does not reproduce it).
- Cultural variation cannot excuse breach of binding obligations (✅ owner 17 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 June 1993 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3260 (relativism), PT-3261 (thin floor), PT-3254.
**Trap:** Do not read Vienna as endorsing cultural imperialism or flat relativism (✅ owner 17 §14).
**Mains hook:** Vienna is the cleanest official text for the universality-with-particularity position.
**Back:** *"Vienna 1993: universal, yet particularities matter; duty stays."*

### Flashcard PT-3260 — Objection and Reply: Universality vs Cultural Relativism
**Front:** Are human rights a universal standard or a culture-bound export?
**Definition (learn):** Relativist objection: rights language (autonomy, adversarial due process, property norms) reflects a liberal-Western trajectory and can override other cultures' norms of duty and authority. Two replies: (i) the tradition itself correlates rights with duties to others and the community (Gauba, PDF pp.351–353); (ii) a thin universal floor with thicker variable applications (⚠️ owner 17 §14).
**Breakdown:**
- Universalist claim: human rights attach to persons as such (✅ owner 17 §14).
- Relativist objection (⚠️).
- Reply (i): duty correlation (⚠️ owner 17 §14).
- Reply (ii): minimal-core distinction (⚠️ owner 17 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.351–353, 358–360 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3259, PT-3261, PT-3247 (Burke custom), PT-3251 (MacIntyre).
**Trap:** Avoid flat cultural imperialism and flat relativism (✅ owner 17 §14).
**Mains hook:** Universality attaches to a thin floor; implementation can legitimately vary.
**Back:** *"Universal core, local dress; no flat empire, no flat relativism."*

### Flashcard PT-3261 — Thin Protective Floor vs Thicker Variable Applications
**Front:** What minimal set of rights resists relativist objection most strongly?
**Definition (learn):** A thin universal floor: protection from torture, arbitrary killing and slavery. Thicker applications (specific family or property arrangements) can vary legitimately within binding duties (✅ owner 17 §14, §18).
**Breakdown:**
- Thin floor: torture, arbitrary killing, slavery (✅ owner 17 §14).
- Thick layer: family and property forms (⚠️ owner 17 §14).
- Barker's personality-plus-guarantee logic supports the floor (✅ owner 17 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only synthesis (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-3260, PT-3259, PT-3269 (prisoners' dignity).
**Trap:** Thin does not mean unimportant; the floor is where universality is strongest.
**Mains hook:** Qualify: universal at the floor, variable at the top.
**Back:** *"Floor is universal: no torture, no arbitrary killing, no slavery."*

### Flashcard PT-3262 — Generations of Rights: A Pedagogic Device, Not Gauba's Scheme
**Front:** What are the first, second and third generations of rights, and why must they be labelled a teaching device?
**Definition (learn):** A pedagogic grouping: first generation = civil and political; second = social, economic and cultural; third = contested collective or solidarity claims (⚠️ owner 17 §15 and adv §7).
**Breakdown:**
- First: speech, movement, fair trial, voting (⚠️ owner 17 §15).
- Second: work, education, health, social security (✅ owner 17 §15, PDF pp.340–341, 361, 364–365).
- Third: self-determination, development, environment (⚠️ owner 17 §15).
- Non-linear: first-generation rights need positive institutions; second-generation rights carry immediate duties.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner: pedagogic label only, not Gauba's classification (✅ owner 17 §15).
**Links:** PT-3238, PT-3263, PT-3272 (trap), PT-3257.
**Trap:** "Rights generations is Gauba's own classification" is wrong (✅ owner 17 §9).
**Mains hook:** Use generations as a map, not as a chronology.
**Back:** *"Three generations: a classroom map, not history's order."*

### Flashcard PT-3263 — Self-Determination: Common Article 1 of the Covenants
**Front:** Where is self-determination in the Covenants, and how is it treated in the generations scheme?
**Definition (learn):** Self-determination is a binding right in common Article 1 of the ICCPR and ICESCR; development and environmental claims have different legal histories and must be assessed separately (✅ owner 17 §15).
**Breakdown:**
- Common Article 1 in both Covenants (✅ owner 17 §15).
- Development and environment: separate legal histories (✅ owner 17 §15).
- Third-generation label is contested (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 17 §15 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3256, PT-3257, PT-3262.
**Trap:** Do not lump all third-generation claims into one binding right.
**Mains hook:** Self-determination is binding by treaty; other collective claims need separate assessment.
**Back:** *"Article 1: peoples decide; the rest of the third generation needs care."*

### Flashcard PT-3264 — Four Stages: Moral Validity, Legal Recognition, Justiciability, Effective Realisation
**Front:** What four stages must a claim pass before it works as a right in practice?
**Definition (learn):** Moral validity (is the claim justified?); legal recognition (does positive law acknowledge it?); justiciability/remedy (can an institution enforce it?); effective realisation (can people use it in practice?) (⚠️ owner 17 §16 analytical framing).
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: moral validity (PT-3241).
- Stage 2: legal recognition (Bentham, PT-3246).
- Stage 3: justiciability and remedy (courts, commissions).
- Stage 4: effective realisation (social conditions, PT-3242).
- Domestic rights are produced by legislatures, executives, courts, commissions and movements (⚠️ owner 17 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 (⚠️ synthesis).
**Links:** PT-3241, PT-3242, PT-3246, PT-3265.
**Trap:** International monitoring is not direct domestic judicial enforcement (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Mains hook:** Use the four stages to diagnose where a claimed right fails.
**Back:** *"Justified, recognised, enforceable, usable: four gates for a right."*

### Flashcard PT-3265 — Restriction Test: Law, Permitted Aim, Necessity, Proportionality
**Front:** What must a restriction on a right satisfy?
**Definition (learn):** Restrictions must rest on law, pursue an aim permitted for that particular right, and satisfy necessity and proportionality. "Public order" or "general welfare" is not a free-standing limitation clause (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Breakdown:**
- Legality: must be under law.
- Permitted aim: right-specific.
- Necessity and proportionality (✅ owner 17 §16).
- Generic welfare phrases fail alone (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3266 (emergency), PT-3264, PT-3269.
**Trap:** "Public order" is not a blank cheque for every right (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Mains hook:** Apply the four-part test to any restriction you evaluate.
**Back:** *"Law, aim, necessity, proportion: no blank cheque for order."*

### Flashcard PT-3266 — Emergency: ICCPR Article 4 and India's Articles 358–359
**Front:** How do ICCPR Article 4 and India's emergency provisions treat derogation?
**Definition (learn):** ICCPR Article 4 permits strictly required, officially proclaimed, non-discriminatory derogations from specified obligations, while non-derogable rights remain protected. In India, Articles 358 and 359 operate differently; Articles 20 and 21 cannot be included in an Article 359 suspension order (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Breakdown:**
- ICCPR Art 4: strictly required, proclaimed, non-discriminatory (✅ owner 17).
- Non-derogable rights stay (✅ owner 17 §16).
- India Art 358: Article 19 suspension linked to external aggression or armed rebellion (⚠️ memory; owner says only "operate differently").
- India Art 359: suspension of enforcement of rights; Arts 20 and 21 excluded (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 (✅ owner 17). Article numbers 358/359/20/21 are owner-cited.
**Links:** PT-3256, PT-3265, PT-3268 (fundamental vs constitutional).
**Trap:** Emergency does not suspend every right; Art 21 is protected under Art 359 (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Mains hook:** Derogation is the most serious test of rights; the non-derogable core is the answer.
**Back:** *"Emergency bends some rights; never the Article 20 and 21 core."*

### Flashcard PT-3267 — Horizontal Effect: Private Conduct and Indian Fundamental Rights
**Front:** Which Indian Fundamental Rights directly address private conduct?
**Definition (learn):** Some Fundamental Rights directly address private conduct, notably Articles 15(2), 17, 23 and 24. Elsewhere, private interference is addressed indirectly through legislation, interpretation and positive state duties (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Breakdown:**
- Art 15(2): listed by the owner as a provision addressing private conduct (✅ owner 17 §16; the access-to-shops detail is ⚠️ memory).
- Art 17: untouchability abolished (✅ owner 17).
- Arts 23 and 24: trafficking and child labour (✅ owner 17).
- Other private interference: via statute and positive duty (⚠️ owner 17 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3239 (civil liberties and private coercion), PT-3266, PT-3268.
**Trap:** Not every Fundamental Right applies horizontally (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Mains hook:** Horizontal reach is where civil liberties meet private power.
**Back:** *"Horizontal: 15(2), 17, 23, 24; the rest by law and duty."*

### Flashcard PT-3268 — Fundamental vs Constitutional Rights (Article 300A)
**Front:** Why is every Fundamental Right constitutional but not every constitutional right fundamental?
**Definition (learn):** Every Fundamental Right is a constitutional right; not every constitutional right is Fundamental. Article 300A (right to property as a constitutional right, not a Fundamental Right) is the standard example (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Breakdown:**
- Fundamental: Part III category (✅ owner 17 §16).
- Constitutional: wider, includes Article 300A (✅ owner 17 §16).
- Article 300A: property is not a Fundamental Right but a constitutional right (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §16 (✅ owner 17). Article-level doctrine remains Polity (⚠️ owner 17 §16).
**Links:** PT-3266, PT-3267, PT-3267 (horizontal effect).
**Trap:** "All constitutional rights are Fundamental" is wrong (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Mains hook:** Use the nesting to show that Fundamental Rights are one kind of constitutional right.
**Back:** *"Fundamental is inside constitutional; 300A sits outside Part III."*

### Flashcard PT-3269 — Prisoners Keep Dignity, Bodily Integrity and Procedural Rights
**Front:** What do prisoners keep even when lawful custody restricts liberty?
**Definition (learn):** Lawful custody restricts selected liberties but does not erase dignity, bodily integrity, protection from torture or procedural rights (✅ owner 17 §16, adv §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Restricted: selected liberties (✅ owner 17).
- Retained: dignity, bodily integrity, torture protection, procedural rights (✅ owner 17 adv §9).
- Owner illustrations of abuse (undertrials, custodial violence, fake encounters) are book-period, not current (✅ owner 17 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.363–364 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3261 (thin floor), PT-3265, PT-3239.
**Trap:** Do not present owner's abuse examples as current facts (✅ owner 17 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** A prisoner shows that human rights outlast civic freedom.
**Back:** *"Locked up, not stripped: dignity, body, procedure."*

### Flashcard PT-3270 — Trap: The UDHR Is Not a Domestic Constitution
**Front:** Is the UDHR enforceable as a country's constitution?
**Definition (learn):** No. It is an international declaration and moral-legal standard setting scheme, not a treaty or domestic constitution (✅ owner 17 §9, adv §12, PDF pp.358–360).
**Breakdown:**
- Declaration: standard.
- Treaties (ICCPR, ICESCR) bind States Parties (PT-3256, PT-3257).
- Domestic law binds through national legislation and courts (✅ owner 17 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 Dec 1948 (✅ Wikipedia: UDHR).
**Links:** PT-3255, PT-3256, PT-3264.
**Trap:** Moral universality does not settle domestic legal enforceability (✅ owner 17 §8).
**Mains hook:** The gap between declaration and enforcement is the core of the human-rights debate.
**Back:** *"UDHR: a standard, not a constitution."*

### Flashcard PT-3271 — Trap: Not All Rights Are Absolute, Nor None
**Front:** Which of the two extreme claims about rights absoluteness is the owner's trap?
**Definition (learn):** "All rights are absolute" and "no right is absolute" are both wrong. Rights must be classified: some permit lawful limitation; some permit emergency derogation; some are non-derogable; a few prohibitions, such as torture, are absolute (✅ owner 17 §9, adv §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Lawful limitation: most rights (PT-3265).
- Derogable in emergency (PT-3266).
- Non-derogable (PT-3266).
- Absolute prohibition: torture (✅ owner 17 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §9 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3265, PT-3266, PT-3269.
**Trap:** The owner's test is classification, not a single answer.
**Mains hook:** Answer "absolute?" by sorting the right first.
**Back:** *"Sort first: limited, derogable, non-derogable, absolute."*

### Flashcard PT-3272 — Trap: Rights Generations Is Not Gauba's Classification
**Front:** Who authored the first-second-third generations scheme?
**Definition (learn):** Not Gauba. It is a later pedagogic synthesis and must not be presented as his classification or as a strict historical order (✅ owner 17 §9, §15, adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Gauba's own categories: negative, positive, natural, legal, historical, social-welfare theories (✅ owner 17 §3).
- Generations: pedagogic device (PT-3262).
- Non-linear: generations overlap (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3262, PT-3238, PT-3263.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the generations scheme to Gauba (✅ owner 17 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Label the scheme as your synthesis when you use it.
**Back:** *"Generations: our map, not Gauba's."*

### Flashcard PT-3273 — Objection and Reply: Civil Liberties vs Democratic Rights
**Front:** Are civil liberties and democratic rights two separate categories, and is democracy enough to secure civil liberties?
**Definition (learn):** Objection: electoral rights are exercised through speech and association, so separation is artificial. Reply: they are overlapping branches separated by primary purpose (legal freedom vs political participation). Objection 2: elections can coexist with repression; reply: electoral rights are necessary but not sufficient for civil liberties (✅ owner 17 §13; Gauba PDF pp.361–363).
**Breakdown:**
- Overlap, not strict subset (✅ owner 17 §13).
- Purpose test: protect freedom vs enable participation.
- Democracy helps but does not automatically secure civil liberties (✅ owner 17 §13 via §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.361–363 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3239, PT-3240, PT-3234.
**Trap:** Do not claim electoral rights are logically required for every civil liberty (✅ owner 17 §8).
**Mains hook:** Assess both branches independently; a regular election is a weak proxy for liberty.
**Back:** *"Elections yes, liberty no: assess each branch alone."*

### Flashcard PT-3274 — PYQ Transfer: Rights Questions 2018–2025 (cross-applied)
**Front:** Which rights-related PYQs does the owner list, and who owns them?
**Definition (learn):** Owner's verified local PYQ transfer list covers rights questions in 2018, 2019, 2020, 2021, 2022, 2023 and 2025. Primary ownership is Philosophy Paper II, Individual and State (✅ owner 17 §9; ⚠️ owner-cross-applications).
**Breakdown:**
- 2018: human rights and dignity as a common human aspiration.
- 2019: whether rights make citizens accountable to the state.
- 2020: Indian tradition and individual/human rights.
- 2021: social contract theory and human-rights issues.
- 2022: whether unconditional rights lead to anarchy.
- 2023: priority of duties and accountability over rights.
- 2025: whether duties as obligations and rights as entitlements lack a necessary connection.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Years as listed by owner (✅ owner 17); the Philosophy PYQ file is referenced, not reproduced here.
**Links:** PT-3237 (Hohfeld), PT-3248 (Laski), PT-3278 (correlativity), PT-3231 (answer spine).
**Trap:** Seven PYQs are cross-applications; do not treat them as Political Theory-owned questions (⚠️ owner 17 §9).
**Mains hook:** Route: identify right type, duty-holder, moral vs legal status, equal-rights limit, justified restriction, graded verdict (⚠️ owner 17 §9).
**Back:** *"2018 to 2025: dignity, accountability, duties before rights, entitlements."*

### Flashcard PT-3275 — Answer Spine: 10/15/20-Mark Rights Template
**Front:** How should a rights answer be built at 10, 15 and 20 marks?
**Definition (learn):** Define rights (claims against arbitrary power plus claims for a better life), name theories, add objection-reply and graded conclusion. Word targets are repository conventions (⚠️ owner 17 §19).
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks (~150 words): define → one theory → one named limit → qualified line.
- 15 marks (~200 words): two theories → one objection-reply pair (PT-3273 or PT-3260) → conclusion.
- 20 marks (~250 words): three theories → universality-relativism reply (PT-3260) → generations caution (PT-3262) → Indian enforcement example (PT-3266) → qualified synthesis.
- Five-move method: identify proposition; reconstruct argument; test counter; apply named evidence; grade verdict (✅ owner 17 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §19 (⚠️ repository convention).
**Links:** PT-3236, PT-3242, PT-3260, PT-3262, PT-3264.
**Trap:** Never end on a flat claim; grade it (✅ owner 17 §17).
**Mains hook:** Rights answers are judged on the enforcement gap and the thin floor.
**Back:** *"Define, theorise, object, reply, grade: the rights spine."*

### Flashcard PT-3276 — Dworkin: Rights as Trumps (cross-reference, not owner text)
**Front:** What does Dworkin claim about individual rights against the state?
**Definition (learn):** Individual rights against the state exist beyond written law and act as trumps against majority interests or wishes; he opposes legal positivism (Hart) and utilitarianism (✅ Wikipedia: Taking Rights Seriously).
**Breakdown:**
- Book: *Taking Rights Seriously*, Harvard University Press, 1977; revised edition 1978 with reply to critics (✅ Wikipedia).
- Principles vs policies and the discretion argument: not confirmed by fetched page (⚠️).
- Link to Political Theory: Dworkin's principles and hard cases (PT-3053).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1977; revised 1978 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3053 (Dworkin hard cases), PT-3237 (Hohfeld), PT-3248 (Laski).
**Trap:** Do not present "rights as trumps" as the owner's own claim; it is a cross-reference from another thinker (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Trumps show why rights resist majority arithmetic; critics ask how trumps are identified.
**Back:** *"Dworkin: rights are trumps over majority interest, 1977."*

### Flashcard PT-3277 — Gewirth: Principle of Generic Consistency (cross-reference, not owner text)
**Front:** What is Gewirth's Principle of Generic Consistency, and what does it say about rights?
**Definition (learn):** Every agent must act in accordance with their own and all other agents' generic rights; agents must respect the freedom and well-being of their recipients as well as themselves. Freedom and well-being are necessary conditions of agency, so agents must accept claim-rights to them and grant the same to others (✅ Wikipedia: Alan Gewirth).
**Breakdown:**
- Book: *Reason and Morality* (1978), University of Chicago Press (✅ Wikipedia).
- Argument: dialectical necessity; denying it leads to self-contradiction (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner text does not discuss Gewirth (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1978 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-3236 (rights as claims), PT-3241 (natural/moral rights), PT-3237 (Hohfeld).
**Trap:** Gewirth's PGC is a moral derivation, not a legal list of rights (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Gewirth bases universal rights on agency rather than nature or law, a third route to universality.
**Back:** *"Gewirth: act on your rights and everyone else's, or contradict yourself."*

### Flashcard PT-3278 — Trap: Each Claim-Right Has a Duty; a Liberty Does Not
**Front:** Does every right imply a correlative duty on someone else?
**Definition (learn):** Not in the same way for all rights. Claim-rights have correlative duty-holders; liberties, powers and immunities require different relational analysis. Laski's rights-and-duties thesis is social-ethical, not strict Hohfeldian correlativity (✅ owner 17 §5, §11; PT-3237).
**Breakdown:**
- Claim-right ↔ duty (correlative) (✅ Wikipedia: Hohfeldian analysis).
- Liberty: no correlative duty on others (✅ Wikipedia).
- Laski: social duties, not strict correlativity (✅ owner 17 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner §5 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** PT-3237, PT-3248, PT-3234.
**Trap:** "Rights and duties are always correlative" is an overstatement (✅ owner 17 §5).
**Mains hook:** Name the Hohfeld category before stating the duty-holder.
**Back:** *"Claim has a duty-holder; liberty does not."*

## POLITICAL THEORY — Part 4: Liberty, Equality, Property, Justice and the Common Good


### GROUP — Liberty, Equality and Property (basic/18)

### Flashcard PT-4001 — Liberty in society: regulated freedom
**Front:** What does liberty mean in a social order, and why is "absence of all restraint" the wrong definition?
**Definition (learn):** Liberty in society is the absence of *unreasonable* restraint plus protection of the conditions in which human capacities can develop (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.371–375, 379–381).
**Breakdown:**
- Two strands: (a) non-interference in a protected sphere; (b) enabling conditions for self-development.
- Liberty first appeared as the cry of the oppressed against privilege, so it is linked from the start to equality and social change (✅ owner 18 §4(1)).
- Liberty becomes universal only as *equal* liberty (✅ owner 18 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.367–390 (liberty chapters) (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4002, PT-4004, PT-4005, PT-4034, PT-4036.
**Trap:** "Liberty = absence of restraint" ❌ omits the word *unreasonable*; Gauba rejects both absolute liberty and unconditional authority (✅ owner 18 advanced §3).
**Mains hook:** Define liberty as regulated; show that the reasonable limit is equal liberty for others; conclude that liberty without the social conditions for its use is formal only.
**Back:** *"Liberty has a fence, not a wall: no unreasonable restraint, but room to grow."*

### Flashcard PT-4002 — Liberty versus licence
**Front:** Why does Gauba separate liberty from licence?
**Definition (learn):** Liberty is freedom that does not let one person's action become another's oppression; licence is unchecked action that does exactly that (✅ owner 18 §11 MCQ ledger, PDF pp.369–371).
**Breakdown:**
- Liberty is regulated by equal liberty for all.
- Licence is the case where one person's freedom destroys another's.
- The test: does the act reduce another person's equal freedom?
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.369–371 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4001, PT-4034, PT-4040.
**Trap:** Answering "liberty means doing whatever one wants" confuses liberty with licence.
**Mains hook:** Use the liberty/licence line to argue that restraint on licence is what makes liberty universal, not a betrayal of it.
**Back:** *"Liberty has limits; licence has none."*

### Flashcard PT-4003 — Civil, political and economic liberty
**Front:** Name the three dimensions of liberty and what each protects.
**Definition (learn):** Civil liberty protects personal action; political liberty enables participation; economic liberty concerns material independence and bargaining power (✅ owner 18 §11 ledger, PDF pp.372–375).
**Breakdown:**
- Civil: personal action and expression.
- Political: participation in choosing and checking government.
- Economic: material independence; the sphere where non-interference alone leaves the weak exposed to the strong (✅ owner 18 §4(3)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.372–375 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4004, PT-4014, PT-4020, PT-4041.
**Trap:** Assuming civil and political liberty are enough; economic liberty can be formally available yet unusable.
**Mains hook:** Civil and political rights without economic liberty leave formal freedom hollow for the poor.
**Back:** *"Civil acts, political votes, economic bargains."*

### Flashcard PT-4004 — Negative liberty (non-interference)
**Front:** What is negative liberty and what question does it ask?
**Definition (learn):** Freedom from interference in chosen actions; the state or others abstain from unreasonable restraint on speech, worship, movement and contract (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.375–377).
**Breakdown:**
- Question: is the individual obstructed by the state or other persons within a sphere of action?
- Berlin's wording of the negative question: the area in which a person "is, or should be, left to do or be what he is able to do or be, without interference by other persons" (✅ Wikipedia: Two Concepts of Liberty).
- Gauba treats negative and positive liberty as complementary, though schools differ in priority (✅ owner 18 §5, PDF p.375).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Berlin lecture delivered 31 Oct 1958 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-4005, PT-4007, PT-4010, PT-4032.
**Trap:** Negative liberty does not mean "no state"; non-interference is necessary but not sufficient when economic obstacles bind (✅ owner 18 §5).
**Mains hook:** Concede its value as a protection of choice, then show why it cannot alone deliver substantive freedom.
**Back:** *"Negative asks: is anyone blocking me?"*

### Flashcard PT-4005 — Positive liberty (enabling freedom)
**Front:** What is positive liberty in Gauba's enabling sense?
**Definition (learn):** Freedom secured by removing socially created obstacles, such as poverty, insecurity, unemployment or lack of education, where public policy can alter them (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.379–381).
**Breakdown:**
- Targets alterable social constraints only.
- Complements, rather than replaces, non-interference (✅ owner 18 §5).
- Does not authorise unlimited state command over life (✅ owner 18 §9; see PT-4035).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.379–381 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4006, PT-4008, PT-4035, PT-4034.
**Trap:** "Positive liberty = more government" ❌.
**Mains hook:** Ask which obstacles policy can alter, and where the boundary against paternalism lies.
**Back:** *"Positive removes barriers; it does not write your life plan."*

### Flashcard PT-4006 — Two senses of positive liberty
**Front:** What are the two senses of positive liberty that must not be conflated?
**Definition (learn):** (a) *Self-mastery* — rule by one's reflective or higher self (Berlin's analytic category); (b) *enabling freedom* — removal of socially alterable deprivation (Green and positive liberalism) (✅ owner 18 advanced §4; ⚠️ basic §5 framing).
**Breakdown:**
- Self-mastery asks *who or what rules the self*.
- Enabling freedom asks *whether social conditions make meaningful action possible*.
- Gauba accepts Berlin's self-mastery insight in the moral sphere, but says it is not the welfare-liberal sense (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.378–380).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.378–380 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4004, PT-4005, PT-4007, PT-4008, PT-4031.
**Trap:** Substituting Berlin's self-mastery for Green's enabling freedom ❌ (owner says neither definition may be silently substituted).
**Mains hook:** Two senses, overlapping but not interchangeable; use the distinction to set the argument's terms before evaluating.
**Back:** *"Self-mastery asks who rules; enabling asks what blocks."*

### Flashcard PT-4007 — Isaiah Berlin, "Two Concepts of Liberty"
**Front:** What does Isaiah Berlin argue in "Two Concepts of Liberty", and what is his warning?
**Definition (learn):** Berlin distinguishes negative liberty (non-interference) from positive liberty (self-mastery) and warns that positive liberty's "higher or real self" language can be hijacked to justify coercion (✅ owner 18 §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Negative question: how large is the area of non-interference? (✅ Wikipedia).
- Positive question: what or who is the source of control determining someone to do this rather than that? (✅ Wikipedia).
- Caution: a ruler may claim to free people into their "real" will while coercing actual choices (✅ owner 18 §12). The Wikipedia summary confirms the abuse risk when positive liberty is tied to goals people are told they "ought" to desire; the exact "real self" phrasing is owner-only.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Oxford lecture 31 Oct 1958 (✅ Wikipedia); reprinted in *Four Essays on Liberty* (1969) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-4004, PT-4006, PT-4030, PT-4031.
**Trap:** Berlin's caution concerns misuse of the concept, not a claim that welfare liberalism is totalitarian (✅ owner 18 §12).
**Mains hook:** Use Berlin as the built-in check on any state programme justified by "true freedom"; qualify rather than reject positive liberty.
**Back:** *"Berlin: negative guards the door; positive can be hijacked by a 'real self'."*

### Flashcard PT-4008 — T.H. Green and enabling freedom
**Front:** What does T.H. Green contribute to the liberty debate?
**Definition (learn):** True freedom is moral self-realisation; the state's job is to remove obstacles (poverty, ignorance, exploitative conditions) that block it; political obligation rests on service to the common good (✅ owner 21 §13; ✅ owner 18 advanced §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Property matters so far as it supports development, and must be restricted where it blocks others' self-realisation (✅ owner 18 advanced §5, PDF pp.377–378, 415–416).
- Green's enabling sense is the welfare-liberal foundation Gauba contrasts with Berlin's self-mastery.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only; Green's own publication dates are ⚠️ not in the owner text.
**Links:** PT-4005, PT-4006, PT-4023, PT-4132.
**Trap:** Green's positive freedom is not Berlin's self-mastery (PT-4006).
**Mains hook:** Green gives the moral basis for state action to enlarge freedom without dictating ends.
**Back:** *"Green: freedom is self-realisation that the state clears the path for."*

### Flashcard PT-4009 — J.S. Mill and the harm principle
**Front:** What is Mill's harm principle, and what does it not justify?
**Definition (learn):** Coercion of a competent adult is presumptively justified only to prevent harm to others; offence, dislike and the person's own good are insufficient (✅ owner 18 §6, advanced §5). *On Liberty* (⚠️ 1859 standard date, not in owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Self-regarding vs other-regarding action.
- Burden of proof lies on coercion.
- Mill's support for education, taxation and inheritance reform rests on separate arguments and is not part of the harm principle (✅ owner 18 advanced §5).
- Mill's equality: equal civic standing, women's equality, hostility to inherited status, compatible with differentiated life plans (✅ owner 18 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only for doctrine; year ⚠️.
**Links:** PT-4001, PT-4002, PT-4013, PT-4043.
**Trap:** Presenting Mill's welfare measures as part of the harm principle ❌.
**Mains hook:** Harm principle as the minimal test for coercion, with Mill's equality as a broader social argument.
**Back:** *"Mill: only harm to others licenses coercion."*

### Flashcard PT-4010 — F.A. Hayek on liberty and equality
**Front:** What are Hayek's core positions on liberty, equality and distribution?
**Definition (learn):** Liberty means freedom from coercion; he defends equality before law rather than material equality, and warns that distributive schemes can destroy freedom (✅ owner 18 §6, PDF pp.381–383, 408–412).
**Breakdown:**
- Calls social justice a mirage (✅ owner 18 advanced §5).
- Stresses general rules and market competition.
- Gauba's counter: this divorces liberty from the conditions needed to universalise it (✅ owner 18 advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.381–383, 408–412 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4004, PT-4011, PT-4037.
**Trap:** Hayek's "not a mirage" claim is about rules; confusing him with Friedman or Nozick ❌ (see PT-4011, PT-4028).
**Mains hook:** Hayek's equality-before-law argument is strong against crude outcome-levelling, but underrates how wealth shapes access.
**Back:** *"Hayek: equal before the law; no planned equality of outcome."*

### Flashcard PT-4011 — Milton Friedman and competitive capitalism
**Front:** How does Milton Friedman link capitalism with freedom, and how does Gauba answer him?
**Definition (learn):** Friedman treats competitive capitalism as a condition of freedom and seeks to minimise welfare and regulatory functions of the state (✅ owner 18 §6; PDF pp.383–384).
**Breakdown:**
- Market exchange under freedom of contract is treated as free.
- Gauba: labour without capital does not negotiate from equal power (✅ owner 18 advanced §5).
- Market power itself can coerce labour (✅ owner 18 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.383–384 (✅ owner 18). Book date ⚠️ not in owner.
**Links:** PT-4010, PT-4014, PT-4019, PT-4028.
**Trap:** Friedman's voluntary-exchange view is not Nozick's entitlement theory, though both are market-liberal ❌.
**Mains hook:** Freedom in exchange vs freedom in bargaining power: the central liberal-egalitarian quarrel.
**Back:** *"Friedman: competition is freedom; Gauba: unequal bargaining is not."*

### Flashcard PT-4012 — Four Freedoms versus Atlantic Charter
**Front:** Are the Four Freedoms and the Atlantic Charter the same document?
**Definition (learn):** No. The Four Freedoms (speech, worship, freedom from want, freedom from fear) were announced by F.D. Roosevelt in his State of the Union address on 6 January 1941 (✅ Wikipedia: Four Freedoms). The Atlantic Charter was a joint Roosevelt–Churchill statement issued 14 August 1941 (✅ Wikipedia: Atlantic Charter).
**Breakdown:**
- Atlantic Charter points include no territorial gains, self-determination, freer trade, disarmament, and a world free of want and fear (✅ Wikipedia).
- The Charter does not name the Four Freedoms (✅ Wikipedia).
- No signed version of the Charter exists (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 6 Jan 1941; 14 Aug 1941 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-4001, PT-4004, PT-4005.
**Trap:** Attributing the four freedoms to the Atlantic Charter ❌.
**Mains hook:** Freedom from want and fear as enabling-freedom illustrations; always cite each document with its own date.
**Back:** *"January brought the four freedoms; August brought the charter. Never merge them."*

### Flashcard PT-4013 — Equality as equal worth, not sameness
**Front:** How does Gauba define equality?
**Definition (learn):** Equality is a claim of right and equal moral worth, not a statement that persons are identical in talent, strength or circumstance (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.392–396).
**Breakdown:**
- Equality is a principle of social change against unjust but alterable inequalities (✅ owner 18 §4(5)).
- Permits rational discrimination in favour of need and socially useful excellence (✅ owner 18 §4(6), PDF pp.395–398).
- Develops across legal, political and socio-economic dimensions (✅ owner 18 §4(6)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.392–406 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4014, PT-4015, PT-4016, PT-4017, PT-4040.
**Trap:** Equality = identical treatment or identical shares ❌; Gauba rejects literal equalisation (✅ owner 18 §9).
**Mains hook:** Equal worth is a right-claim that still licenses justified difference; state the difference between worth and sameness at the start.
**Back:** *"Equal worth, never identical shares."*

### Flashcard PT-4014 — Formal versus substantive equality
**Front:** What separates formal from substantive equality?
**Definition (learn):** Formal equality means non-discrimination, equal legal personality, equal vote and open access in principle. Substantive equality means actual reduction of unjust, alterable inequalities, extending real opportunities and safeguards to weaker sections (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.400–407).
**Breakdown:**
- Formal equality removes explicit exclusion.
- Substantive equality asks whether ordinary people can actually use the opportunity (⚠️ owner test).
- Hobhouse's point: "freedom of contract" needs approximate bargaining equality (✅ owner 18 §7 ⚠️ example).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.400–407 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4013, PT-4015, PT-4041, PT-4032.
**Trap:** "Formal equality means equality is achieved" ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use the usable-access test to show that legal equality can coexist with deep practical inequality.
**Back:** *"Formal opens the door; substantive lets people walk through."*

### Flashcard PT-4015 — Equality of opportunity versus equality of outcome
**Front:** How do equality of opportunity and equality of outcome differ, and what is each one's main risk?
**Definition (learn):** Opportunity asks whether offices and advantages are open on fair terms; outcome asks whether the final distribution leaves avoidable deprivation or domination (⚠️ owner 18 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Fair equality of opportunity corrects background barriers, not merely formal openness.
- Outcome-sensitive views can defend a threshold, floor or priority for the least advantaged, not identical shares (⚠️).
- Main risk of opportunity: inherited resources decide the race. Main risk of outcome: identical results can crush choice and plurality (⚠️).
- Rawls orders fair equality of opportunity before the difference principle (✅ Wikipedia: A Theory of Justice; see PT-4050).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rawls, *A Theory of Justice* (1971, Belknap/Harvard) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-4014, PT-4017, PT-4039, PT-4087.
**Trap:** Treating outcome equality as "everyone gets the same" ❌.
**Mains hook:** Position the answer on whether justice asks for fair access, a floor, or both.
**Back:** *"Opportunity asks 'can I compete?'; outcome asks 'am I left deprived?'"*

### Flashcard PT-4016 — Rousseau: natural versus conventional inequality
**Front:** What does Rousseau's distinction between natural and conventional inequality do for equality?
**Definition (learn):** Natural differences (physical or talent) cannot be the main target of reform; conventional privileges of wealth, honour, power and dependence can, and only these are properly political targets (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.394–395, 400).
**Breakdown:**
- Separates unavoidable difference from socially produced rank.
- Makes social inequality the object of reform.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.394–395, 400 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4013, PT-4017, PT-4035.
**Trap:** Treating every natural difference as an injustice ❌.
**Mains hook:** Justifies the alterability test (PT-4017) and grounds reforms against conventional privilege.
**Back:** *"Nature differs; convention ranks."*

### Flashcard PT-4017 — Alterability: necessary but not sufficient
**Front:** Why is alterability necessary but not sufficient to show injustice?
**Definition (learn):** An inequality must be alterable to be a practical reform target, but alterability alone does not prove injustice; relevance, need, function and socially beneficial excellence can still justify it (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.394–397).
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1: Is the inequality alterable by social action?
- Step 2: Is it a relevant difference (need, function, excellence)?
- Step 3: Does it leave avoidable deprivation or domination?
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.394–397 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4013, PT-4016, PT-4018, PT-4042.
**Trap:** "If alterable, then unjust" ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use the three-step test to structure any equality question; it works as a 15-mark spine.
**Back:** *"Alterable is the start of the argument, not its verdict."*

### Flashcard PT-4018 — Affirmative action and the reverse-discrimination objection
**Front:** What are the rationales for affirmative action, and why is "reverse discrimination" not a neutral label?
**Definition (learn):** Preferential treatment for historically deprived sections may serve equality (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.398–399). The main rationales are compensation, anti-subordination, representation and fair opportunity (⚠️ owner analytical synthesis, labelled CARF here).
**Breakdown:**
- "Reverse discrimination" names an objection: merit, identifying deserving cases, fairness to others.
- Critics also note capture of benefits by advantaged sub-groups (✅ owner advanced §7).
- Indian reservation debate is a lens only; constitutional detail belongs to Polity (⚠️ owner boundary).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.398–399 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4013, PT-4017, PT-4043.
**Trap:** Describing affirmative action neutrally as "reverse discrimination" ❌.
**Mains hook:** Separate the four rationales, state the objection, and assess each on its own terms before a verdict.
**Back:** *"CARF: Compensate, Anti-subordinate, Represent, Fair-open. 'Reverse' is an objection."*

### Flashcard PT-4019 — Property as security versus property as power
**Front:** Why is property politically contentious?
**Definition (learn):** Property denotes possession or control of material things and claims over them; it raises the political question of whether control empowers some over others (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.413–414).
**Breakdown:**
- Personal holdings can give security and independence.
- Once property yields monopoly, control of capital or bargaining superiority, it becomes power over others' labour and life-chances (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.414, 420–423).
- Ownership patterns reflect inheritance, markets and prior advantage, not only merit (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.413–416).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.413–431 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4020, PT-4021, PT-4023, PT-4025, PT-4042.
**Trap:** "All property is power" ❌ and "all large holdings are illegitimate" ❌ (✅ owner 18 §13).
**Mains hook:** Property becomes suspect when concentrated and functionless (PT-4024).
**Back:** *"Property secures a home; concentrated property commands other people."*

### Flashcard PT-4020 — Personal property versus means of production
**Front:** How does the Marxist distinction between personal property and means of production work?
**Definition (learn):** Personal property is held for use, security and the fruit of one's own labour and is not the Marxist target. Private property in land, capital and productive assets enables control over others' labour and is the target (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.426–431).
**Breakdown:**
- Marxism does not seek abolition of all possessions (✅ owner 18 §9).
- Gauba notes that a large middle class and widespread shareholding can blur the line in practice (✅ owner 18 advanced §7, PDF pp.430–431).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.426–431 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4019, PT-4025, PT-4026.
**Trap:** "Marxism abolishes every possession" ❌.
**Mains hook:** Rebut the caricature and then ask whether shareholding changes the class structure of control.
**Back:** *"Marx spares the toothbrush and targets the factory that owns the workers."*

### Flashcard PT-4021 — Locke: labour, provisos and money
**Front:** How does Locke ground property, and what limits does he set?
**Definition (learn):** Locke treats life, liberty and property as natural rights. Initial appropriation through labour is subject to leaving "enough and as good" for others and to a spoilage limit. Money bypasses spoilage through consent and enables accumulation (✅ owner 18 §6, PDF pp.415, 417–419).
**Breakdown:**
- Labour mixed with natural resources gives initial title.
- Proviso 1: enough and as good for others.
- Proviso 2: spoilage limit; money removes it.
- Whether proviso 1 survives monetisation is contested; Gauba says the theory accommodates substantial inequality (✅ owner 18 advanced §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.417–419 (✅ owner 18). Locke's *Second Treatise* (⚠️ 1689 standard date).
**Links:** PT-4019, PT-4022, PT-4023, PT-4028, PT-4042.
**Trap:** "Locke proves capitalism just" ❌ (✅ owner 18 §12).
**Mains hook:** Reconstruct Locke's title, show how money stretches it, and note Marx's rejoinder on workers' freedom to apply labour.
**Back:** *"Labour makes title; enough and spoilage limit it; money lets it grow."*

### Flashcard PT-4022 — Hegel and personhood-based property
**Front:** How does Hegel justify property, and what does he not justify?
**Definition (learn):** Property is the external embodiment through which free will gains objective existence within Abstract Right, supporting recognition as a person (⚠️ owner 18 §6; advanced §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Explains why a domain of personhood-supporting property matters.
- Does not justify unlimited accumulation or domination (⚠️ owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only (⚠️ Hegel's *Philosophy of Right* year not in owner).
**Links:** PT-4021, PT-4019.
**Trap:** Reading Hegel as a licence for unlimited accumulation ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use Hegel as a bridge from Locke's title to Marx's structural critique (owner advanced §8).
**Back:** *"Hegel: property makes will visible, not will limitless."*

### Flashcard PT-4023 — Hobhouse, Tawney and Laski: function-regulated property
**Front:** What is the social-democratic position on property?
**Definition (learn):** Property survives only under limits of service, function, social origin and common welfare. Absolute or functionless property is rejected; abolition is not the aim (✅ owner 18 §6, PDF pp.415–416, 420–426).
**Breakdown:**
- Hobhouse: property has social origin, since society secures and helps create it, so it cannot be absolute (✅ owner 18 advanced §5).
- Tawney: property judged by service, not dividend (✅ owner 18 advanced §5).
- Laski: inheritance and concentrated capital need strong limits, a common civic minimum and industrial democratisation (✅ owner 18 advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.415–426 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** PT-4019, PT-4024, PT-4025, PT-4043.
**Trap:** Social democracy equals Marxism ❌ (see PT-4025 and PT-4093).
**Mains hook:** Middle path between absolutism and abolition; compare with Marx on who controls production.
**Back:** *"Function, not dividend: property earns its place by service."*

### Flashcard PT-4024 — Tawney's functionless property
**Front:** What is functionless property, and when does it become politically suspect?
**Definition (learn):** Property that yields income or control without any socially useful service or productive function by its owner, such as pure rent extraction or speculative holding (✅ owner 18, PDF pp.420–426; advanced §4, PDF pp.421–425).
**Breakdown:**
