**Trap:** Using these as current institutional data ❌.
**Mains hook:** Transitional state versus withering away.
**Back:** *"Lenin and Mao: transition by state; Marx: state withers."*

### Flashcard PT-4115 — Marxist realisation weakness: new domination
**Front:** What is Gauba's double-edged verdict on Marxian communism?
**Definition (learn):** Communism is ethically attractive, yet new dominance may reappear through bureaucratic or political power even after private property is abolished (✅ owner advanced 20 §7, PDF p.459; basic 20 §8, PDF p.486).
**Breakdown:**
- Underestimates reappearance of domination through political-bureaucratic power (✅ owner §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.459 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4093, PT-4025, PT-4114.
**Trap:** Presenting communism as automatically just ❌.
**Mains hook:** Abolishing private ownership does not abolish power.
**Back:** *"Abolish private power; political power can remain."*

### Flashcard PT-4116 — Democratic socialism: fragile institutions
**Front:** Why is democratic socialism normatively attractive but fragile?
**Definition (learn):** It depends on strong public opinion, resources and incentives (✅ owner advanced 20 §7, PDF p.461).
**Breakdown:**
- Hard to fund and sustain without coercion or incentive loss (✅ owner basic 20 §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.461 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4094, PT-4105.
**Trap:** Assuming institutional success follows normative appeal ❌.
**Mains hook:** Normative attractiveness is not an institutional guarantee.
**Back:** *"Democratic socialism needs civic will and budget."*

### Flashcard PT-4117 — Anarchism: programmatically weak
**Front:** What is the standard criticism of anarchism?
**Definition (learn):** Fascinating but programmatically weak, overly optimistic about human nature and inattentive to regulatory needs of large societies (✅ owner advanced 20 §7, PDF p.464).
**Breakdown:**
- Lacks a workable large-scale mechanism (✅ owner advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.464 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4095.
**Trap:** Dismissing anarchist values because the programme is weak ❌.
**Mains hook:** Separate the anti-domination insight from the institutional gap.
**Back:** *"Anarchism: sharp on domination, thin on regulation."*

### Flashcard PT-4118 — Walzer: strong morality, weak machinery
**Front:** What is the criticism of Walzer's communitarianism?
**Definition (learn):** Strong moral reasoning but weak political machinery (✅ owner advanced 20 §7, PDF p.473).
**Breakdown:**
- Complex equality needs enforceable institutions (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.473 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4099, PT-4098.
**Trap:** Treating Walzer's spheres as self-enforcing ❌.
**Mains hook:** Ask what institution blocks money's spread.
**Back:** *"Walzer's spheres need guards."*

### Flashcard PT-4119 — Feminist and subaltern blind spot
**Front:** What blind spot do feminist and subaltern critiques expose in older distributive theories?
**Definition (learn):** Persons enter distribution already marked by hierarchy, dependence and damaged status (⚠️ owner advanced 20 §7). "Misrecognition" is later recognition-theory vocabulary, not Gauba's term.
**Breakdown:**
- Hierarchy distorts reward, status and dignity (⚠️ owner advanced 20 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Analytical (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4096, PT-4097, PT-4072.
**Trap:** Presenting misrecognition as Gauba's term ❌.
**Mains hook:** Start with feminist and subaltern cases, then add Fraser or Honneth as an extension.
**Back:** *"Hierarchy enters the starting line before the race."*

### Flashcard PT-4120 — Dated examples: keep book-period illustrations dated
**Front:** Which examples in the diverse-perspectives chapter must not be read as current data?
**Definition (learn):** Soviet Russia, Maoist China, East Europe, UNDP ranking tables and late-20th-century market examples are historical or book-period anchors (✅ owner advanced 20 §12, PDF pp.456–468).
**Breakdown:**
- Feminist numerical claims come from older sources and must stay dated (✅ owner §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Book-period (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4093, PT-4096.
**Trap:** Quoting book figures as present-day facts ❌.
**Mains hook:** Always date empirical examples or avoid them.
**Back:** *"Old data, dated labels."*

### Flashcard PT-4121 — Comparison matrix of seven perspectives
**Front:** How does the advanced matrix compare the perspectives?
**Definition (learn):** Rows: Rawlsian liberal, libertarian, Marxist, democratic socialist, anarchist, feminist, subaltern, communitarian. Columns: distribution rule, state, property, equality, community, critique (✅ owner advanced 20 §8, basic 20 §8).
**Breakdown:**
- Column headings are exam-use synthesis (⚠️ owner advanced 20 §12).
- Rows must be filled with the owner's positions, not guessed (✅ owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.446–473 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4108, PT-4122.
**Trap:** Using the table without citing the owner's row ❌.
**Mains hook:** Compare by axis, not by adjectives.
**Back:** *"Eight rows, six columns: rule, state, property, equality, community, critique."*

### Flashcard PT-4122 — Prompt: compare Rawls and Nozick
**Front:** How should a Rawls-versus-Nozick answer be organised?
**Definition (learn):** Organise by distributive object, method, liberty, property, state and treatment of inequality (⚠️ owner advanced 20 §10).
**Breakdown:**
- Rawls: fair choice behind the veil; Nozick: historical title and rectification (✅ owner advanced 20 §4).
- Shorthand: patterned versus anti-patterned distribution (⚠️ owner advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4087, PT-4090, PT-4121.
**Trap:** Describing one theory and forgetting the other ❌.
**Mains hook:** Two theories, same grid, one verdict.
**Back:** *"Rawls chooses fair rules; Nozick traces the title."*

### Flashcard PT-4123 — Prompt: distribution or domination?
**Front:** Is justice mainly about distribution or about domination?
**Definition (learn):** Start with Rawls and Nozick on distribution, then widen to Marxist, feminist and subaltern critiques of domination (⚠️ owner advanced 20 §10).
**Breakdown:**
- Advanced takeaway: the dispute is about what counts as a person, wrong, good and site of distribution (✅ owner advanced 20 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Synthesis (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4096, PT-4097, PT-4122, PT-4034.
**Trap:** Declaring one answer the only answer ❌.
**Mains hook:** A graded verdict naming what each frame sees.
**Back:** *"Distribution asks who gets; domination asks who commands."*

### GROUP — Common Good and Community (basic/21)

### Flashcard PT-4124 — The common good as a normative ideal
**Front:** What is the common good in Gauba's account?
**Definition (learn):** A normative ideal concerning the welfare of the whole community, transcending the immediate claims of particular individuals or groups. It is related to justice but not reducible to public interest or aggregate private satisfaction (✅ owner basic 21 §1, §3, PDF pp.474–477).
**Breakdown:**
- Common good cannot be identified with the state, the majority or any one faction (✅ owner §1, PDF p.475).
- Requires objectives beyond factional competition and immediate self-interest (✅ owner §4, PDF pp.476–478).
- Aristotle's starting point: the right constitution serves the whole community (✅ owner §4, PDF p.474).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.474–490 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** PT-4125, PT-4126, PT-4127, PT-4150.
**Trap:** Equating common good with aggregated interests ❌.
**Mains hook:** Define the normative ideal, then show why it restrains both atomism and majoritarianism.
**Back:** *"Common good: the whole's welfare, not a faction's win."*

### Flashcard PT-4125 — Common good, common interest and public interest
**Front:** How do common good, common interest and public interest differ?
**Definition (learn):** Common good is normative. Common interest is empirical: the converging interests of persons likely to be affected by a decision. Public interest is a judgment-oriented administrative notion used to justify a public decision (✅ owner basic 21 §3, PDF p.475).
**Breakdown:**
- Every public-interest decision presupposes some common good, but not every common-good sacrifice is administrative public interest (✅ owner §5).
- Public interest is a justificatory judgment by competent authority; common good is the wider horizon it should serve (✅ owner advanced 21 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.475 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4143, PT-4162.
**Trap:** Using "public interest" and "common good" as synonyms ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use the three-way distinction to test a public-policy justification.
**Back:** *"Common good is normative; common interest is observed; public interest is judged."*

### Flashcard PT-4126 — Trap: common good is the good of the state
**Front:** Is the common good the same as the good of the state?
**Definition (learn):** No. A tyranny may act against the common good (✅ owner basic 21 §5, PDF p.475).
**Breakdown:**
- Strengthening a state apparatus may still injure the common good (✅ owner basic 21 §18 ledger).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.475 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4127.
**Trap:** The trap itself ❌.
**Mains hook:** State-good error: the state is an instrument, not the end.
**Back:** *"A tyranny has a state and no common good."*

### Flashcard PT-4127 — Trap: common good is the good of the majority
**Front:** Does "common good" mean whatever the majority wants?
**Definition (learn):** No. It may require protection of minorities against majority injustice, and it may be invoked to stop injustice to a minority (✅ owner basic 21 §5, §9, PDF p.475).
**Breakdown:**
- Majority-good error: numerical majority cannot convert injustice into common good (✅ owner §18 ledger).
- Majoritarian absorption is the main democratic risk (⚠️ owner advanced 21 §7; PT-4157).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.475 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4157, PT-4149.
**Trap:** The trap itself ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use the minority-protection clause to show the common good disciplines majorities.
**Back:** *"Majority count is not common good when a minority pays."*

### Flashcard PT-4128 — Trap: common good is utilitarian greatest happiness
**Front:** Is common good the utilitarian "greatest happiness of the greatest number"?
**Definition (learn):** No. Minority suffering cannot be justified by aggregate gain, and both Rawlsian criticism and Gandhian sarvodaya reject sacrificing some for aggregate benefit (✅ owner basic 21 §5, §9, PDF pp.477, 490).
**Breakdown:**
- Utilitarian-aggregate error (✅ owner §18).
- Gandhi rejects utilitarian arithmetic for sarvodaya (✅ owner advanced 21 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.477, 490 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4045, PT-4057, PT-4141.
**Trap:** The trap itself ❌.
**Mains hook:** Aggregate gain cannot justify expendable minorities.
**Back:** *"Greatest happiness is not greatest good for every person."*

### Flashcard PT-4129 — Consensus as process, not unanimity
**Front:** What is consensus in Gauba's account?
**Definition (learn):** The process through which groups resolve differences and arrive at an arrangement suited to shared needs under existing constraints. It is procedural and partial, not total sameness (✅ owner basic 21 §3, §5, PDF pp.477–479).
**Breakdown:**
- Ongoing accommodation of disagreement (✅ owner §18 ledger).
- Not the absence of disagreement (✅ owner advanced 21 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.477–479 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4149, PT-4160.
**Trap:** Consensus means unanimity ❌.
**Mains hook:** Plural consensus is compatible with dissent.
**Back:** *"Consensus is a continuing bargain, not a silent crowd."*

### Flashcard PT-4130 — Aristotle: the whole community
**Front:** What is Aristotle's contribution to the common good?
**Definition (learn):** The right constitution serves the good life of the whole community; constitutions serving only rulers or one faction are defective (✅ owner basic 21 §4, §13, PDF p.474).
**Breakdown:**
- Polis exists for living well together, not only survival or exchange (✅ owner §13).
- Constitution judged by advantage to the whole (✅ owner §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.474 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4131, PT-4132.
**Trap:** Treating the polis as a modern state ❌ (⚠️ owner reconstruction).
**Mains hook:** Start the common-good answer with Aristotle's whole.
**Back:** *"Aristotle: judge a constitution by the whole it serves."*

### Flashcard PT-4131 — Rousseau: the general will
**Front:** What is Rousseau's general will, and what is its risk?
**Definition (learn):** The general will aims always at the public good, unlike the will of all, which is only the sum of private wills. Sovereignty requires citizens to submit particular interest to it through the social contract. Partial associations and factions can corrupt it (✅ owner basic 21 §13, §6, PDF p.475).
**Breakdown:**
- Rousseau's phrase "forced to be free" is widely cited from the *Social Contract*, Book I (⚠️ not a Gauba page citation).
- The risk: the general will used to justify coercive homogeneity (✅ owner basic 21 §14 objection 2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Social Contract (⚠️ year 1762 not in owner).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4130, PT-4146, PT-4159.
**Trap:** Treating the general will as majority counting ❌ (owner: not majority preference-counting alone).
**Mains hook:** General will is public-regarding, but its enforcement can crush dissent.
**Back:** *"General will: public-minded, not the head-count."*

### Flashcard PT-4132 — Green and the common good
**Front:** How does T.H. Green ground political obligation in the common good?
**Definition (learn):** The state exists to promote the common good, and political obligation rests on it. Green's common good is the set of conditions enabling each person's moral self-realisation, partly secured through state action (✅ owner basic 21 §6, §13, PDF pp.475, 481–482).
**Breakdown:**
- Only laws promoting it claim moral authority (✅ owner advanced 21 §5).
- Links to enabling freedom (PT-4008).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.475, 481–482 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4008, PT-4005, PT-4131.
**Trap:** Treating Green's state as a command power ❌ (enabling conditions only).
**Mains hook:** Green's obligation depends on service to self-realisation.
**Back:** *"Green: obey laws that enable everyone's self-realisation."*

### Flashcard PT-4133 — Liberal reconciliation of interests
**Front:** What is the liberal account of the common good?
**Definition (learn):** Rational individuals adjust conflicting claims under civil rules, and the point of reconciliation may be called common good or public interest. Locke, Smith, Bentham and J.S. Mill are presented as exponents (✅ owner basic 21 §6, §8, PDF p.479).
**Breakdown:**
- Civil rules adjust interests while rights protect persons from mutual oppression (✅ owner §18).
- Gauba accepts it fully only when corrected by welfare-state concern (✅ owner §11, PDF pp.479–480).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.479–480 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4134, PT-4124, PT-4137.
**Trap:** Treating liberal reconciliation as sufficient ❌.
**Mains hook:** Reconciliation needs welfare correction.
**Back:** *"Liberals reconcile claims; Gauba adds welfare."*

### Flashcard PT-4134 — Macpherson: market valuation and human capacity
**Front:** What does Macpherson add to the liberal common good?
**Definition (learn):** Demand-and-supply valuation can undervalue socially necessary but low-priced talents and stunt human capacities, so market society needs welfare correction (✅ owner basic 21 §6, §18, PDF p.480).
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal market weakness (✅ owner §18).
- Developmental power link (PT-4027).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.480 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4027, PT-4133.
**Trap:** Thinking markets reward all socially valuable work ❌.
**Mains hook:** Market prices are not a full measure of social value.
**Back:** *"Markets can undervalue what society cannot do without."*

### Flashcard PT-4135 — MacIntyre: practices and traditions
**Front:** What do MacIntyre's practices contribute to the common good?
**Definition (learn):** Traditions and cooperative practices generate internal goods, virtues and duties. Rights detached from social obligation become morally thin (✅ owner basic 21 §6, PDF pp.481–482).
**Breakdown:**
- Persons flourish within practices (✅ owner advanced 21 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.481–482 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4136, PT-4098, PT-4137.
**Trap:** Treating MacIntyre as anti-rights ❌.
**Mains hook:** Rights need duties and practices behind them.
**Back:** *"MacIntyre: goods come from practices, not from detached rights."*

### Flashcard PT-4136 — Sandel and Taylor: the communitarian bridge
**Front:** How do Sandel and Taylor extend the common-good debate?
**Definition (learn):** Sandel: constitutive attachments partly form the self, so the unencumbered chooser understates them. Taylor: identity is dialogical, so recognition and shared horizons matter to membership (⚠️ owner basic 21 §6; supplementary).
**Breakdown:**
- Sandel: PT-4100.
- Taylor: exercise concept, PT-4031.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only supplementary (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4100, PT-4031, PT-4135.
**Trap:** Treating supplementary bridges as Gauba's own chapter claims ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use the bridge to argue for shared horizons without majoritarian absorption.
**Back:** *"Sandel: selves are embedded. Taylor: identity is dialogical."*

### Flashcard PT-4137 — Walzer: sphere-specific goods in the common good
**Front:** Why does Walzer matter to the common good?
**Definition (learn):** Goods carry social meanings and should be distributed by sphere-specific criteria, not by one master good such as money (✅ owner basic 21 §6, PDF pp.482–483; ✅ Wikipedia: *Spheres of Justice*, 1983).
**Breakdown:**
- Walzerian spheres (✅ owner §18 ledger).
- See the full complex-equality card (PT-4099).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.482–483 (✅ owner); 1983 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-4099, PT-4118, PT-4135.
**Trap:** Reading Walzer as anti-equality ❌.
**Mains hook:** Money should not colonise every sphere.
**Back:** *"Walzer: keep money in its own sphere."*

### Flashcard PT-4138 — Marxian class barrier
**Front:** Why does Marxism deny a genuine common good under capitalism?
**Definition (learn):** Antagonistic class society organises each class around class interest, so a genuine common good is blocked until a classless communist society (✅ owner basic 21 §6, PDF pp.482–486).
**Breakdown:**
- State is a class instrument until class abolition (✅ owner §8).
- Marxian realisation weakness: new domination can appear (PT-4115).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.482–486 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4093, PT-4115, PT-4147.
**Trap:** Marxian common good already exists in capitalism ❌.
**Mains hook:** Class conflict is structural, not moral failure.
**Back:** *"Marx: no common good until class goes."*

### Flashcard PT-4139 — Gandhi: trusteeship
**Front:** What is Gandhian trusteeship?
**Definition (learn):** The rich should treat possessions not as absolute personal property but as a trust held for the service of humanity (✅ owner basic 21 §3, PDF pp.487–488).
**Breakdown:**
- Achieved through moral conversion, not coercive confiscation (✅ owner §9).
- Gauba: morally elevating but abstract to implement (✅ owner advanced 21 §7, PDF pp.487–488).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.487–488 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4140, PT-4141, PT-4148.
**Trap:** Trusteeship means violent redistribution ❌.
**Mains hook:** Trusteeship relies on sustained change of heart.
**Back:** *"Trusteeship: wealth held in service, not in fear."*

### Flashcard PT-4140 — Gandhi: bread labour
**Front:** What is bread labour and what does it achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Everyone should perform physical labour sufficient to justify consumption, upholding equality and dignity of work (✅ owner basic 21 §3, §13, PDF pp.488–489).
**Breakdown:**
- Equality achieved through shared work, not legal proclamation (✅ owner §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.488–489 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4139, PT-4141.
**Trap:** Bread labour as a punishment ❌.
**Mains hook:** Dignity of labour as a common-good practice.
**Back:** *"Bread labour: earn what you eat, honour all work."*

### Flashcard PT-4141 — Sarvodaya
**Front:** What is sarvodaya?
**Definition (learn):** Gandhian ideal of uplift or awakening of all, especially the last and neglected person, through a non-violent moral order (✅ owner basic 21 §3, PDF pp.489–490). The companion term antyodaya is widely used in the tradition but is not asserted as Gauba's wording (⚠️ owner §13).
**Breakdown:**
- Rejects utilitarian arithmetic; self-sacrifice for all may be required (✅ owner advanced 21 §4, PDF p.490).
- Normative, not an implemented design (✅ owner advanced 21 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.489–490 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4128, PT-4139, PT-4140.
**Trap:** Presenting sarvodaya as a realised institution ❌.
**Mains hook:** Sarvodaya is a moral aim with an institutional gap.
**Back:** *"Sarvodaya: uplift all, last first."*

### Flashcard PT-4142 — Excluded-public bargain
**Front:** Why is an employer–labour settlement not necessarily a common-good settlement?
**Definition (learn):** If consumers or displaced groups bear hidden costs, a bargain that satisfies employer and labour is not common good (✅ owner basic 21 §7, advanced 21 §9, PDF p.477).
**Breakdown:**
- Excluded-public bargain error (✅ owner §18 ledger).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.477 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4125, PT-4143.
**Trap:** Bargaining equals common good ❌.
**Mains hook:** Ask who pays outside the bargain.
**Back:** *"A deal between two parties can hide a third party's bill."*

### Flashcard PT-4143 — Soldier's sacrifice and quarantine law
**Front:** What examples does Gauba use to separate common good from narrow public interest?
**Definition (learn):** A soldier's self-sacrifice in war illustrates action for common good broader than narrow public-interest calculation. Quarantine laws show a public-interest action that also serves the common good (✅ owner basic 21 §7, PDF p.475).
**Breakdown:**
- Examples show that common good exceeds arithmetic addition of private gains (✅ owner §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.475 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4125, PT-4124.
**Trap:** Treating every public-interest law as common good by default ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use the two examples to show overlap and difference.
**Back:** *"The soldier and the quarantine: duty beyond the ledger."*

### Flashcard PT-4144 — Displaced tribals and villagers: defending the common good
**Front:** Why are rehabilitation movements not divisive by definition?
**Definition (learn):** Movements seeking rehabilitation of displaced tribals or villagers may defend the common good against unjust "development" (✅ owner basic 21 §7, PDF p.478).
**Breakdown:**
- Opposition to a project is not automatically anti-common-good (✅ owner §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.478 (✅ owner); no current-case data asserted (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4142, PT-4149.
**Trap:** Treating every protest as divisive ❌.
**Mains hook:** Common good can defend the excluded.
**Back:** *"Protest can be the common good speaking for the displaced."*

### Flashcard PT-4145 — Trap: common good is the sum of private interests
**Front:** Is the common good simply the total of private interests?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gauba distinguishes it from bargaining outcomes and factional aggregation (✅ owner basic 21 §9, PDF pp.475–477).
**Breakdown:**
- Common good requires objectives beyond claims and counterclaims (✅ owner advanced 21 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.475–477 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4125.
**Trap:** The trap itself ❌.
**Mains hook:** Bargaining is not the same as the good.
**Back:** *"Summed private wants are not the common good."*

### Flashcard PT-4146 — Trap: common-good politics silences dissent
**Front:** Can the common good justify silencing dissent in the name of unity?
**Definition (learn):** A defensible common-good answer must preserve minority rights, disagreement and plural voices within the community (⚠️ owner basic 21 §9 ❌ trap; advanced 21 §8).
**Breakdown:**
- Common-good language must not erase pluralism (✅ owner advanced 21 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Synthesis (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4127, PT-4157, PT-4131.
**Trap:** The trap itself ❌.
**Mains hook:** Rights and dissent are conditions of the common good.
**Back:** *"Unity without dissent is uniformity, not common good."*

### Flashcard PT-4147 — Trap: Marxian common good exists under capitalism
**Front:** Is a Marxian common good already available in capitalist society?
**Definition (learn):** No. Class society is organised by antagonistic class interests, not genuine common good (✅ owner basic 21 §9, PDF pp.482–486).
**Breakdown:**
- Realisable only in classless community (✅ owner §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.482–486 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4138.
**Trap:** The trap itself ❌.
**Mains hook:** Class structure blocks it.
**Back:** *"Under class rule, the common good waits."*

### Flashcard PT-4148 — Trap: Gandhian common good is violent levelling
**Front:** Does Gandhian common good mean violent levelling of rich and poor?
**Definition (learn):** No. Gandhi relies on trusteeship, ahimsa, bread labour and moral regeneration, not coercive confiscation (✅ owner basic 21 §9, PDF pp.487–490).
**Breakdown:**
- Non-violent transformation (✅ owner §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.487–490 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4139, PT-4141.
**Trap:** The trap itself ❌.
**Mains hook:** Gandhian method is moral and non-coercive.
**Back:** *"Gandhi levels hearts, not households."*

### Flashcard PT-4149 — Common good as a restraint on both atomism and majoritarianism
**Front:** What is the best one-line answer on the common good?
**Definition (learn):** Common good is valid only when it restrains both selfish atomism and majoritarian domination (✅ owner basic 21 §11 ⚠️ best answer line).
**Breakdown:**
- Atomism: summed private interests (PT-4145).
- Majoritarian domination: head-count as good (PT-4127).
- Plural consensus (PT-4129).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Synthesis (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4127, PT-4145, PT-4146.
**Trap:** Declaring the common good simply majoritarian or simply individualist ❌.
**Mains hook:** Close with the dual restraint.
**Back:** *"Not atoms alone, not the mob alone: the whole, with dissent."*

### Flashcard PT-4150 — PYQ route: common good and community
**Front:** Which verified PYQs route to this chapter?
**Definition (learn):** Verified cross-applied PYQs in the owner: 2018 (10 marks, liberal democracy and social cohesion); 2018 (10 marks, multiculturalism and Indian cultural identity); 2020 (10 marks, liberal democracies and minorities); 2020 (20 marks, Gandhian social development); 2023 (15 marks, Ambedkar's annihilation of caste); 2024 (15 marks, tolerance and multicultural societies) (✅ owner basic 21 §18C).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner primary sources are Philosophy Paper II files (✅ owner).
- Ambedkar and caste route to the Caste file (✅ owner §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ years as listed (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4124, PT-4139, PT-4149, PT-4152.
**Trap:** Answering multicultural PYQs with only the common good ❌ (needs minority rights).
**Mains hook:** Map each PYQ onto a theorist and a safeguard.
**Back:** *"Map the PYQ to a theorist, then to the minority safeguard."*

### Flashcard PT-4151 — Common good, public health and the minority cost
**Front:** How do you test a public-health restriction against the common good?
**Definition (learn):** Public interest explains the measure administratively; common good asks whether the burden is fairly justified to the whole community, including minorities and dissenters (⚠️ owner advanced 21 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Quarantine example: PT-4143.
- Ask who bears the cost (PT-4142).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Illustration (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4125, PT-4143, PT-4142.
**Trap:** Treating the authority's justification as proof of common good ❌.
**Mains hook:** Justification must include those who pay.
**Back:** *"Public health speaks for all; burdens must be shared fairly."*

### Flashcard PT-4152 — Indian parallel: fraternity and village self-rule (cautious)
**Front:** What Indian parallels can a common-good answer use without asserting Polity content?
**Definition (learn):** Fraternity in the Preamble and directive principles on welfare can be read as gestures toward a common good distinct from majority preference (⚠️ owner basic 21 §15). Gandhian village self-reliance has a loose echo in decentralised local self-government (⚠️ owner §15).
**Breakdown:**
- Illustrations only; no statutory or amendment detail (⚠️ owner §15).
- Contemporary dissent-branding illustrates homogeneity danger in general terms (⚠️ owner §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None asserted (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4141, PT-4146, PT-4150.
**Trap:** Quoting article numbers from memory in a common-good answer ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use as a conceptual parallel, labelled as such.
**Back:** *"Preamble fraternity and village self-rule: parallels, not proofs."*

### GROUP — Common Good and Community (advanced/21)

### Flashcard PT-4153 — Four traditions of common good
**Front:** What four traditions does the advanced common-good chapter compare?
**Definition (learn):** Liberal (reconciliation under civil rules), communitarian (shared good as source of personal good), Marxian (impossible in class society, realisable in classless order) and Gandhian (moral uplift of all through trusteeship, bread labour, ahimsa and sarvodaya) (✅ owner advanced 21 §1, §3, PDF pp.474–490).
**Breakdown:**
- Each guards a different value: liberal guards plurality, communitarian solidarity, Marxian structural critique, Gandhian moral transformation (⚠️ owner advanced 21 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.474–490 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4133, PT-4135, PT-4138, PT-4139, PT-4154.
**Trap:** Absolutising one tradition ❌.
**Mains hook:** Compare by unit of concern, constitution of good, conflict, state, property, weakness.
**Back:** *"Liberal plural, communitarian solidarity, Marxian structure, Gandhian conscience."*

### Flashcard PT-4154 — Liberal view: welfare correction
**Front:** Why does Gauba accept liberal reconciliation only with welfare correction?
**Definition (learn):** Market reward may undervalue talents and social functions indispensable to civilisation, so the common good needs welfare-state correction (✅ owner advanced 21 §7, PDF p.480).
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal main weakness: market reconciliation may ignore deeper inequality (✅ owner advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.480 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4133, PT-4134.
**Trap:** Liberal reconciliation without correction ❌.
**Mains hook:** Correction is part of the liberal argument.
**Back:** *"Liberals need welfare to make reconciliation fair."*

### Flashcard PT-4155 — Communitarian weakness: moral richness without machinery
**Front:** What is the communitarian weakness in Gauba's account?
**Definition (learn):** Strong moral appeal but weak institutional machinery to secure commitment (✅ owner advanced 21 §7, PDF p.482).
**Breakdown:**
- Moral richness does not automatically supply accountable institutions (✅ owner §18 ledger).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.482 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4135, PT-4136, PT-4118.
**Trap:** Thinking shared values enforce themselves ❌.
**Mains hook:** Pair shared purpose with institutions.
**Back:** *"Communitarians have the vision; institutions must hold it."*

### Flashcard PT-4156 — Gandhian weakness: voluntarism and renunciation
**Front:** What is the main weakness of Gandhian common good?
**Definition (learn):** Heavy dependence on moral transformation and voluntary renunciation; trusteeship is elevating but difficult to institutionalise (✅ owner advanced 21 §7, §8, PDF pp.487–490).
**Breakdown:**
- Sustained moral conversion required (✅ owner basic 21 §18 ledger).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.487–490 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4139, PT-4141, PT-4148.
**Trap:** Assuming trusteeship scales automatically ❌.
**Mains hook:** Acknowledge the voluntarist gap as a fair residual criticism.
**Back:** *"Gandhi's gift depends on the giver's conscience."*

### Flashcard PT-4157 — Majoritarian absorption
**Front:** What is the major contemporary democratic risk of common-good rhetoric?
**Definition (learn):** If community good is defined only by dominant voices, minorities are asked to disappear into an alleged whole (⚠️ owner advanced 21 §7).
**Breakdown:**
- Must be disciplined by rights, constitutional dissent and plural representation (⚠️ owner §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Analytical (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4127, PT-4146, PT-4149.
**Trap:** Equating majority voice with common good ❌.
**Mains hook:** Name the majority risk and the safeguards.
**Back:** *"When the whole is only the loudest part, minorities vanish."*

### Flashcard PT-4158 — Group rights and internal domination
**Front:** Does protecting a cultural community always serve the common good?
**Definition (learn):** Not necessarily. Protecting a community may strengthen dominant elites within it and silence women, dissenters or lower-status members. A defensible common good protects external coexistence without giving groups unchecked power over members (⚠️ owner basic 21 §14, objection 4).
**Breakdown:**
- Tests: voice, exit, equal status and review (⚠️ owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Analytical (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4096, PT-4146, PT-4159.
**Trap:** Treating group protection as automatically good ❌.
**Mains hook:** External coexistence yes, internal domination no.
**Back:** *"Protect the group from outside, not from its own members' voice."*

### Flashcard PT-4159 — Constitutional morality against social consensus
**Front:** Can inherited social consensus justify a norm?
**Definition (learn):** Not alone. Inherited consensus may reproduce caste, gender or religious hierarchy. Constitutional morality disciplines popular and customary morality through equal citizenship, rights and public justification (⚠️ owner basic 21 §14, objection 5; §18 ledger).
**Breakdown:**
- Plural communities may pursue shared goods within these limits (⚠️ owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Analytical (⚠️). Constitutional text itself is Polity content.
**Links:** PT-4131, PT-4158, PT-4152.
**Trap:** Treating social consensus as the final test ❌.
**Mains hook:** Equal citizenship disciplines the community.
**Back:** *"Consensus can be wrong; constitutional morality is the check."*

### Flashcard PT-4160 — Rights, dissent and minorities across the four traditions
**Front:** How does the advanced matrix treat rights and minorities?
**Definition (learn):** Liberal: strongest native safeguard, though solidarity may thin out. Communitarian: must be explicitly safeguarded. Marxian: risks subsuming dissent into class teleology. Gandhian: moral consensus must not erase those outside the dominant idiom (⚠️ owner advanced 21 §8).
**Breakdown:**
- Row added as synthesis (⚠️ owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Synthesis (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4153, PT-4146, PT-4157.
**Trap:** Reading the row as Gauba's text ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use the row to check each tradition's blind spot.
**Back:** *"Each tradition needs a dissent check."*

### Flashcard PT-4161 — Dated examples in the common-good chapter
**Front:** Which examples in the common-good chapter are book-period illustrations?
**Definition (learn):** Cigarettes, broadcasting restrictions, East Europe, the Soviet experience and Gandhian publications are historical or book-period anchors and should stay dated (✅ owner advanced 21 §12, PDF pp.478–490).
**Breakdown:**
- Gandhi's sarvodaya is normative, not an implemented design (✅ owner §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.478–490 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4120, PT-4141.
**Trap:** Quoting book examples as current evidence ❌.
**Mains hook:** Date the example or drop it.
**Back:** *"Old examples keep old dates."*

### Flashcard PT-4162 — Prompt: common good in a plural society
**Front:** How should an answer discuss common good in a plural society?
**Definition (learn):** Use liberal rights, communitarian belonging and an explicit warning against majoritarian absorption (⚠️ owner advanced 21 §10).
**Breakdown:**
- Prompt: can common good justify restrictions on liberty? Distinguish it from majority will, then insist on rights and minority safeguards (⚠️ owner §10).
- Prompt: why common good is not the sum of private interests (⚠️ owner §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Synthesis (⚠️).
**Links:** PT-4149, PT-4157, PT-4125.
**Trap:** Using "community" to silence minorities ❌ (owner: self-defeating).
**Mains hook:** Qualify the common good with rights and dissent.
**Back:** *"Plural common good: rights first, belonging second."*

### Flashcard PT-4163 — Application: public-health restrictions and sacrifice
**Front:** How does the advanced file apply common-good tests to public policy?
**Definition (learn):** In a public-health restriction, public interest explains the measure; common good asks about fair burdens. In an ethics question on service, sarvodaya supplies the moral vocabulary of self-restraint for all (✅ owner advanced 21 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Welfare taxation: liberal and democratic-welfare arguments can be separated; Gauba does not accept pure market common good (✅ owner §9, PDF p.480).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.480 (✅ owner).
**Links:** PT-4151, PT-4141, PT-4154.
**Trap:** Treating the authority's rationale as enough ❌.
**Mains hook:** Separate public-interest justification from common-good test.
**Back:** *"Justify the measure, then share the burden."*

### GROUP — Cross-owner Indian property anchor (basic/18 §16; basic/20 §15)

### Flashcard PT-4164 — Property: from fundamental right to legal right (44th Amendment, 1978)
**Front:** How did the 44th Amendment change the status of property, and why does the Political Theory owner use it?
**Definition (learn):** The Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978 reduced property's constitutional protection: it was made a legal right, not a fundamental right, and is now protected through Article 300-A (✅ Wikipedia: Act title and 1978 enactment; ✅ owner basic 20 §15 and basic 18 §16 for the shift and Article 300-A).
**Breakdown:**
- Act enacted in 1978; sections took effect on 30 April 1979, 1 August 1979 and 6 September 1979 (✅ Wikipedia).
- The owner uses it only as an analytical parallel to the liberal-versus-welfare-state tension, not as Gauba's content (✅ owner basic 20 §15).
- Article 300-A's insertion and effective date are not on the fetched page (⚠️ not verified in this pass).
- Article-wise and land-reform detail belongs to Polity (✅ owner basic 18 §16 boundary).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Enacted 1978; effective 30 April 1979 (✅ Wikipedia). Article 300-A (⚠️ owner-cited; not verified externally here).
**Links:** PT-4019, PT-4034, PT-4042, PT-4075.
**Trap:** Saying property is "abolished" as a right ❌; it remains a legal right under Article 300-A (⚠️ owner-cited). Quoting article numbers from memory in a Political Theory answer ❌.
**Mains hook:** Use as a cautious Indian parallel for the claim that property is a conditional, socially regulable right, labelled as an analytical bridge.
**Back:** *"1978 moved property from fundamental to legal: still protected, now regulable."*

## POLITICAL THEORY — Part 5: Democracy, Representation, Contemporary Democracy and Development

Source owners: `upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Political-Theory/basic/` and `advanced/`, files 22 and 23. Verification tags: (✅ owner NN) = owner text; (✅ Wikipedia: Page) = fetched page; ⚠️ = owner synthesis or unverified memory. Edition grounding: O. P. Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, 5th ed. (2009), Ch.22–25.

### GROUP — Democracy, Representation and Liberal Democracy (owner 22)

### Flashcard PT-5001 — Concept: Democracy as rule of the people and the liberal-democratic threshold
**Front:** What is democracy at its core, and why does "elections were held" not prove a regime is democratic?
**Definition (learn):** Democracy is rule of the people: ultimate governing authority rests with ordinary people, and public policy should reflect their will and interests (✅ owner 22 §3, PDF p.491).
**Breakdown:**
- Classical/direct: citizens themselves discuss and decide public affairs (✅ owner 22 §1).
- Modern/representative: citizens choose representatives at intervals (✅ owner 22 §1).
- Liberal democracy adds consent, accountability, majority rule, minority rights, constitutional government, civil liberties, party competition, regular elections and an independent judiciary (✅ owner 22 §1).
- One-line test: democracy concerns who rules, under what restraints, and with what protection for dissenters and minorities (⚠️ owner 22 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner source: Gauba 5th ed. (2009), Ch.22, PDF pp.491–512 (✅ owner 22 header). Owner declares no post-2009 fact assumed.
**Links:** PT-5002, PT-5003, PT-5004, PT-5007, PT-5019, PT-5044.
**Trap:** "Elections held, therefore democracy" is wrong; Gauba notes non-democratic systems may hold elections (✅ owner 22 §9, PDF p.504).
**Mains hook:** Define the threshold first, separate necessary electoral conditions from sufficient liberal safeguards, and conclude that democracy is both an institution and a social ethic (⚠️ owner 22 §9 answer frame).
**Back:** *"Rule of the people, restrained by rights, tested by real choice."*

### Flashcard PT-5002 — Direct democracy
**Front:** Define direct democracy and state where it is feasible.
**Definition (learn):** Citizens themselves participate in managing public affairs and law-making, as in small-scale political communities (✅ owner 22 §3, PDF pp.491, 500).
**Breakdown:**
- Locus: face-to-face discussion and law-respecting participation (✅ owner 22 §8).
- Scale condition: small communities only (✅ owner 22 §8; advanced §4 "feasible only in small political communities").
- Ancient reference: Greek model of equal participation by freemen, public discussion and respect for law (✅ owner 22 adv §3, PDF p.491).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba places direct democracy in the Greek and small-commune frame (PDF pp.491, 500).
**Links:** PT-5001, PT-5003, PT-5017, PT-5029 (delegate view), PT-5042 (radical/direct theory).
**Trap:** Do not present direct democracy as a template for large modern states; scale is the constraint (✅ owner 22 §5).
**Mains hook:** Direct democracy maximises immediacy of self-rule but fails the scale test; representation is a response to scale, not a betrayal of popular sovereignty (⚠️ owner 22 adv §6 table).
**Back:** *"Direct = everyone decides; works in villages, not in nations."*

### Flashcard PT-5003 — Representative democracy
**Front:** What is representative democracy and why does modern democracy rely on it?
**Definition (learn):** Citizens do not decide every issue directly; they choose representatives who govern on their behalf for limited periods (✅ owner 22 §3, PDF p.500).
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: elections, legislatures, parties, committees translate public choice into policy (✅ owner 22 §8).
- Justification: large modern states cannot govern through constant assembly (✅ owner 22 §5).
- Characteristic danger: distance between people and rulers (✅ owner 22 §8, PDF p.500).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF p.500 is the anchor for the representative-democracy definition.
**Links:** PT-5002, PT-5004, PT-5028, PT-5029, PT-5030, PT-5031.
**Trap:** "Representation means citizens disappear after voting" is a trap; the way-of-life thesis says democratic habits must continue after polling day (⚠️ owner 22 §9).
**Mains hook:** Representation solves scale but creates the representation problem; the answer should name a model (trustee, delegate, mandate) and its democratic risk (⚠️ owner 22 §12).
**Back:** *"Vote for a voice, not a verdict on every law."*

### Flashcard PT-5004 — Liberal democracy: definition and the five-principle package
**Front:** Define liberal democracy and list the principles Gauba attributes to it.
**Definition (learn):** A representative system combining popular authorisation with accountability, constitutional government, civil liberties, political competition and minority safeguards (✅ owner 22 §3, PDF pp.494–499).
**Breakdown:**
- Five principles: government by consent, public accountability, majority rule, recognition of minority rights, constitutional government (✅ owner 22 adv §3, PDF pp.495–497).
- Institutional mechanism: multi-party competition, open access to office, periodic elections on universal franchise, civil liberties, independent judiciary (✅ owner 22 adv §3, PDF pp.498–499).
- Historical fusion: liberalism and democracy were initially antithetical, later fused under mass-politics pressure (✅ owner 22 adv §3, PDF pp.494–495).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauba PDF pp.494–499 (owner 22 §3). Owner notes capitalism's historical association is not a defining test (⚠️ owner 22 §5).
**Links:** PT-5005, PT-5006, PT-5020, PT-5021, PT-5041, PT-5024.
**Trap:** A free market is neither necessary nor sufficient for liberal democracy; defining tests are consent, competition, liberties, accountability and constitutional restraint (⚠️ owner 22 §5 and adv §4 risk table).
**Mains hook:** Liberal democracy is a compound: democracy supplies popular rule; liberalism supplies limits, rights and restraint (⚠️ Sartori framing, see PT-5041).
**Back:** *"Consent, Accountability, Majority, Minority, Constitution — the five-part package."*

### Flashcard PT-5005 — Majority rule and the one-person-one-vote principle
**Front:** What does majority rule mean in a democratic body, and what is its limit?
**Definition (learn):** Decisions in democratic bodies are settled by voting under political equality, one person one vote (✅ owner 22 §3, PDF p.497).
**Breakdown:**
- Decision rule: numerical majority settles outcomes (✅ owner 22 §3).
- Limit: majority rule does not license crushing minorities; legal safeguards remain essential (✅ owner 22 §5, PDF p.497).
- Necessary decision rule but not sufficient theory (✅ owner 22 adv §4 audit table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.497 (owner 22 §3, §5).
**Links:** PT-5004, PT-5018, PT-5041, PT-5043.
**Trap:** "Majority rule means minorities must simply obey" is wrong (✅ owner 22 §9).
**Mains hook:** Can majority rule be reconciled with minority rights? Answer: yes, if rights and liberal constitutionalism bound the majority (⚠️ owner 22 §16 directive).
**Back:** *"One head, one vote, but never one head to crush."*

### Flashcard PT-5006 — Constitutional government (rule of law vs arbitrary rule)
**Front:** Define constitutional government and state how it differs from "rule by men".
**Definition (learn):** Government by laws and prescribed procedures rather than arbitrary personal rule; procedures bind rulers as well as citizens (✅ owner 22 §3, §5, PDF p.497).
**Breakdown:**
- Binds rulers and ruled alike (✅ owner 22 §5).
- Enforced through independent judiciary, fundamental rights, checks and balances (⚠️ owner 22 §15 and §17).
- Liberal-democratic constraint on majority rule (✅ owner 22 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.497.
**Links:** PT-5004, PT-5005, PT-5022, PT-5041.
**Trap:** Constitutional government is not the same as popular sovereignty; it restrains the majority as well as protecting individuals (⚠️ owner 22 §15).
**Mains hook:** A regime may be constitutional yet illiberal, or popular yet lawless; liberal democracy needs both (⚠️ synthesis, owner 22 §5).
**Back:** *"Procedures rule the rulers too."*

### Flashcard PT-5007 — Elections are necessary but not sufficient (necessary/sufficient audit)
**Front:** Why does Gauba treat genuine electoral choice as necessary but not sufficient for democracy?
**Definition (learn):** Genuine electoral choice, broad franchise and periodic contestation are necessary institutional conditions; a one-candidate ballot or censorship can coexist with voting, so elections alone are not sufficient (✅ owner 22 §5; ❌ owner 22 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Necessary: real alternatives; free and secret ballot (✅ owner 22 adv §4, PDF pp.504–505).
- Necessary for will-formation: civil liberties and opposition (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
- Not sufficient: one-candidate ballot, censorship, arbitrary detention, permanent exclusion of minorities (✅ owner 22 §5).
- Democratic culture: supportive, often practically necessary, but not alone sufficient (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.504–505.
**Links:** PT-5001, PT-5004, PT-5024, PT-5043, PT-5099 (democratisation).
**Trap:** "If elections are held, democracy exists" is the headline trap (✅ owner 22 §9).
**Mains hook:** Use the audit table (periodic elections, real alternatives, liberties, majority rule, market, democratic culture) to structure a 20-mark answer (⚠️ owner 22 adv §4).
**Back:** *"Votes are the door, liberties are the house."*

### Flashcard PT-5008 — Plato's critique of democracy (PYQ 2025, 10 marks)
**Front:** What is Plato's central objection to democracy, and how does it appear in PYQs?
**Definition (learn):** Democracy may empower persuasive, selfish or incompetent leaders because mass judgment may be poor, so the wisest are not chosen (✅ owner 22 §6, PDF p.492; adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: eloquence over wisdom; demagogy (✅ owner 22 §6).
- Instability risk under unchecked popular rule (✅ owner 22 adv §3).
- PYQ: "Comment on Plato's critique of Democracy" (2025, 10 marks; ✅ owner 22 §9 verified PYQ).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Plato — PDF p.492 (owner 22 §6).
**Links:** PT-5009 (Aristotle), PT-5027 (propaganda PYQ), PT-5024.
**Trap:** Do not present Plato as a simple anti-democrat without noting his concern is with judgment and competence; contrast Aristotle, who sees collective wisdom in the many (✅ owner 22 §6).
**Mains hook:** Plato's critique anticipates the competence and manipulation problems that modern democracies answer through elections, parties, courts and information freedom (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Eloquence is not wisdom; the crowd can be seduced."*

### Flashcard PT-5009 — Aristotle: legal equality, mixed constitution
**Front:** How does Aristotle qualify democracy, and what constitution does he prefer?
**Definition (learn):** Aristotle sees some collective wisdom in the many but criticises democracy for mistaking legal (numerical) equality for equality in all respects; he prefers a mixed constitution (✅ owner 22 §6, PDF p.492).
**Breakdown:**
- Recognises collective judgment of many citizens (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
- Critique: turning one type of equality into universal equality (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
- Remedy: mixed constitution (✅ owner 22 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.492.
**Links:** PT-5008, PT-5042, PT-5017.
**Trap:** Aristotle is not simply anti-democratic; his objection is about equality being over-generalised (✅ owner 22 §6).
**Mains hook:** Legal equality is necessary but not an account of all merit; a balanced constitution answers Aristotle's worry (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Equal before law is not equal in everything."*

### Flashcard PT-5010 — Dicey: democracy as majority-shaped legislation
**Front:** How does A. V. Dicey define democracy in the owner's classical-to-modern survey?
**Definition (learn):** Democracy is identified with legislation shaped by majority opinion (✅ owner 22 §6, PDF p.492).
**Breakdown:**
- Modern classical exponent: democracy as majority-guided lawmaking (✅ owner 22 adv §3).
- Contrasts with literal day-to-day rule by the people (✅ owner 22 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.492.
**Links:** PT-5011, PT-5005, PT-5001.
**Trap:** Dicey's democracy is about the link between opinion and law, not about every citizen legislating (✅ owner 22 §4).
**Mains hook:** Modern defenders shift democracy from participation to responsiveness of law to opinion; test whether responsiveness includes minority protection (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Democracy = law that follows majority opinion."*

### Flashcard PT-5011 — Bryce: comparative defence of democracy
**Front:** Why does Bryce defend democracy despite its defects?
**Definition (learn):** Democracy can secure welfare and educate citizens through participation, even though it suffers from money power and party manipulation (✅ owner 22 §6, PDF pp.493–494).
**Breakdown:**
- Positive: performs governmental functions and broadens citizens (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
- Defects: money, vote-seeking, party evils (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.493–494.
**Links:** PT-5010, PT-5016, PT-5062 (Gramsci/consent).
**Trap:** Bryce is a qualified defender; do not cite him as an uncritical champion (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Democracy's justification is comparative and educative; money power is a standing test of liberal democracy's integrity (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Democracy: imperfect teacher, better than the alternatives."*

### Flashcard PT-5012 — Locke: government as trustee answerable to the people
**Front:** What is Locke's contribution to the accountability strand of liberal democracy?
**Definition (learn):** Government is a trustee that must remain answerable to vigilant people (✅ owner 22 adv §5, PDF pp.495–496).
**Breakdown:**
- Accountable government as liberal democratic principle (✅ owner 22 §6).
- Vigilance against abuse of power (✅ owner 22 §6).
- Later usage in trustee/delegate debate: Locke's agency logic supports delegate-type representation (✅ owner 22 adv §5, PDF p.503).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.495–496.
**Links:** PT-5013, PT-5014, PT-5028, PT-5029.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Locke's trustee-of-people (accountability) with Burke's trustee model of representation (independent judgment) (✅ owner 22 adv §5; ⚠️ see PT-5028).
**Mains hook:** Accountability depends on vigilant citizens and revocable office; that is the liberal core of consent (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Locke: the ruler is a trustee; the people keep watch."*

### Flashcard PT-5013 — Bentham: accountability through frequent checks
**Front:** Why does Bentham justify accountability of governors?
**Definition (learn):** Governors may abuse power unless frequently checked by the governed (✅ owner 22 adv §5, PDF p.496).
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: frequent checks, periodic accountability (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
- Liberal-democratic strand in the package (✅ owner 22 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.496.
**Links:** PT-5012, PT-5004, PT-5014.
**Trap:** Bentham's accountability is about governors' abuse, not about the people's wisdom; do not merge it with Rousseau's sovereignty view (PT-5015).
**Mains hook:** Accountability is the check that turns consent into continuing control (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Bentham: rulers must be checked often."*

### Flashcard PT-5014 — J. S. Mill: liberty and representative democracy
**Front:** How does J. S. Mill join liberty with representative democracy?
**Definition (learn):** Free discussion and accountable government produce wiser citizens and protect individuality (✅ owner 22 adv §5, PDF p.496).
**Breakdown:**
- Liberty as condition of democratic citizenship (✅ owner 22 §6, PDF pp.495–496).
- Representative democracy as educative and protective of individuality (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.496.
**Links:** PT-5013, PT-5023, PT-5041 (tyranny of majority).
**Trap:** Mill is associated with the tyranny-of-majority worry; do not read him as defending unlimited majority power (⚠️ owner 22 §15 cites the Tocqueville–Mill concern).
**Mains hook:** Liberty and participation reinforce each other; minority safeguards protect individuality from majorities (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Mill: free talk makes wiser citizens."*

### Flashcard PT-5015 — Rousseau: sovereignty cannot be fully represented
**Front:** Why is Rousseau cited as a critic of representation?
**Definition (learn):** Sovereignty resides continuously in the people and cannot be fully represented; deputies are agents, not sovereign substitutes (✅ owner 22 adv §5, PDF p.496).
**Breakdown:**
- Sovereignty inalienable; representation a partial substitute (✅ owner 22 §6).
- Precursor of participatory democracy (✅ owner 23 §6, see PT-5057).
- Not to be flattened into ordinary representative democracy (✅ owner 22 adv §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.496.
**Links:** PT-5002, PT-5042, PT-5057, PT-5011.
**Trap:** Do not present Rousseau as an ordinary representative democrat (✅ owner 22 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Rousseau shows the radical objection: representation dilutes sovereignty; liberal systems answer with accountability and rights (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Rousseau: sovereignty can be lent, never given away."*

### Flashcard PT-5016 — Dewey: democracy as associated living and communication
**Front:** What wider meaning of democracy does Dewey add?
**Definition (learn):** Democracy is collective inquiry, communication and associated living, beyond elections and office-holding (✅ owner 22 adv §5, PDF pp.510–512).
**Breakdown:**
- Democracy as a way of life across social levels (✅ owner 22 §4, PDF pp.510–512).
- Communication and consultation as democratic habits (✅ owner 22 §11).
- Conceptual extension, not substitute for liberal institutions (✅ owner 22 adv §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.510–512.
**Links:** PT-5019 (form vs way of life), PT-5064 (Habermas public sphere), PT-5065 (deliberation).
**Trap:** Dewey's extension is not a reason to downgrade institutions (✅ owner 22 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** End with Dewey: democracy needs habits of consultation in social life, not only ballots (⚠️ owner 22 §11).
**Back:** *"Dewey: democracy is how we live and talk together."*

### Flashcard PT-5017 — Pair: direct vs representative democracy
**Front:** Distinguish direct and representative democracy on the owner's comparison axes.
**Definition (learn):** Direct democracy: citizens decide collectively; representative democracy: elected intermediaries govern because large states cannot be run by constant assembly (✅ owner 22 §5, PDF p.500).
**Breakdown:**
- Basis: direct deliberation vs choice of representatives (✅ owner 22 §8).
- Scale: small communities vs large modern states (✅ owner 22 §8).
- Danger: demagogy/instability vs distance between people and rulers (✅ owner 22 §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.491, 500.
**Links:** PT-5002, PT-5003, PT-5008.
**Trap:** Representation is not automatically anti-democratic; the trap is treating it as the final form of popular rule (✅ owner 22 adv §6; radical theory).
**Mains hook:** Representative democracy is a scale solution with a control problem; the answer should show what linkage (elections, parties, accountability) closes the gap (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Direct: everyone governs. Representative: some govern for all."*

### Flashcard PT-5018 — Pair: minority rights vs majority suppression
**Front:** How does liberal democracy answer the question whether majority rule can coexist with minority rights?
**Definition (learn):** Majority rule must be restrained by minority rights and constitutional limits; recognition and protection of minorities are part of liberal democracy's own principles (✅ owner 22 §9, PDF p.497; adv §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Majority rule = decision rule (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
- Minority rights = protection (✅ owner 22 §5).
- Tension with the tyranny-of-majority concern (⚠️ owner 22 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.497.
**Links:** PT-5005, PT-5041, PT-5037, PT-5040.
**Trap:** Do not equate majority rule with unrestricted majority domination (✅ owner 22 adv §12).
**Mains hook:** Can majority rule be reconciled with minority rights? Yes, through a fusion of democracy and liberal constraints (⚠️ owner 22 §16).
**Back:** *"Majority decides; rights decide what majority cannot decide."*

### Flashcard PT-5019 — Form of government vs way of life
**Front:** Distinguish democracy as a form of government from democracy as a way of life.
**Definition (learn):** Form of government concerns institutions of rule; way of life concerns habits of consultation, communication and anti-authoritarian social relations (⚠️ owner 22 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Form: elections, legislature, courts (✅ owner 22 §4).
- Way of life: Dewey's associated living (✅ owner 22 adv §4 and §5).
- Bridge: institutions need civic habits to function (⚠️ owner 22 adv §4 audit table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 adv PDF pp.510–512.
**Links:** PT-5016, PT-5001, PT-5064.
**Trap:** Do not treat "democratic culture" as sufficient alone: consultation without enforceable institutions cannot restrain arbitrary power (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
**Mains hook:** Form without way of life hollows out; way of life without form lacks enforceability (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Form makes rules; way of life keeps them honest."*

### Flashcard PT-5020 — Liberalism and democracy: historical fusion
**Front:** Why were liberalism and democracy initially antithetical, and how did they fuse?
**Definition (learn):** Classical liberalism defended property qualification and market inequality, so liberalism and democracy were initially opposed; under mass-politics pressure they fused into liberal democracy (✅ owner 22 adv §3, PDF pp.494–495).
**Breakdown:**
- Phase 1: liberal property-based inequality (✅ owner 22 adv §3).
- Phase 2: universal franchise under pressure (✅ owner 22 adv §7).
- Result: compromise, not conceptual purity (✅ owner 22 adv §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.494–495.
**Links:** PT-5004, PT-5021, PT-5041.
**Trap:** Fusion was historical; the two commitments remain analytically separable (⚠️ owner 22 §15, Sartori framing).
**Mains hook:** Liberal democracy is an historical compromise whose tensions remain live (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Liberty first, franchise later, fused by pressure."*

### Flashcard PT-5021 — Trap: a market economy does not prove liberal democracy
**Front:** Why is "market economy proves liberal-democratic state" a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Market exchange does not establish free political choice, minority rights, accountable office or civil liberty (✅ owner 22 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Market = economic test; liberal democracy = political tests (⚠️ owner 22 adv §4).
- Historical association does not establish legitimacy (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 adv §4 audit table, "Market economy: neither necessary nor sufficient."
**Links:** PT-5004, PT-5020, PT-5074 (market development path).
**Trap:** Conflating market-led development with democracy (✅ owner 22 §9).
**Mains hook:** Separate the economic and political tests; this matters for Indian and comparative debates on economic liberalisation (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Markets trade goods; democracy trades votes and rights."*

### Flashcard PT-5022 — Trap: independent judiciary is not anti-democratic
**Front:** Why is the claim "independent judiciary is anti-democratic because judges are unelected" wrong?
**Definition (learn):** Gauba treats judicial independence as a safeguard for freedom and continuity under democracy (✅ owner 22 §9, PDF p.499).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: restrains majority and executive (✅ owner 22 §15).
- Part of the institutional package (✅ owner 22 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.499.
**Links:** PT-5006, PT-5004, PT-5041.
**Trap:** Counter-majoritarian institutions are conditions of accountable rule, not elite obstructions by default (⚠️ owner 23 adv §21.5 populism critique).
**Mains hook:** Judicial independence reconciles majority rule with constitutional restraint (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Unelected does not mean undemocratic."*

### Flashcard PT-5023 — Trap: civil liberties are core, not optional
**Front:** Why are civil liberties core to democracy?
**Definition (learn):** Freedom of expression, association and personal liberty are core because public will cannot be formed or communicated without them (✅ owner 22 §9, PDF p.499).
**Breakdown:**
- Will-formation requires expression (✅ owner 22 §9).
- Accountability requires association and opposition (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
- Political competition needs liberties to function (✅ owner 22 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.499.
**Links:** PT-5007, PT-5014, PT-5041.
**Trap:** Treating liberties as "extras" to be suspended in emergencies (⚠️ owner 22 §9).
**Mains hook:** A regime without liberties can hold elections but cannot have consent; liberties are the precondition of informed consent (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"No free speech, no formed will."*

### Flashcard PT-5024 — PYQ route: liberal democracy and minorities (2018 and 2020)
**Front:** What do the 2018 (10 marks) and 2020 (10 marks) liberal-democracy PYQs test?
**Definition (learn):** 2018: "What is meant by liberal democracy? Does it require deeper principles for social cohesion to balance its own strong affirmation of individual rights? Give reasons from the Indian context." 2020: "How far do the liberal democracies safeguard the interests of minorities? Evaluate critically." (✅ owner 22 §9, verified PYQ; primary owner Philosophy Paper II).
**Breakdown:**
- 2018: definition → social-cohesion principles → Indian context (✅ owner 22 §9).
- 2020: safeguard evaluation → minority rights → critical verdict (✅ owner 22 §9).
- Route: definition, necessary/sufficient audit, minority safeguard, qualified conclusion (⚠️ owner 22 §9 frame).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ source: `Philosophy/paper-2/_PYQ-SocioPolitical-2018-2025.md` (✅ owner 22 §19).
**Links:** PT-5004, PT-5007, PT-5018, PT-5020.
**Trap:** Answering the 2020 question only with rights, without evaluating safeguards in practice (⚠️ synthesis).
**Mains hook:** Qualify: rights exist on paper; safeguards depend on independent courts, opposition and minority participation (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Define, then deliver safeguards that work."*

### Flashcard PT-5025 — PYQ route: liberty and equality as democratic features (2018, 20 marks)
**Front:** How should a 20-mark answer on "how far can liberty and equality be distinctive features of democracy" proceed?
**Definition (learn):** The verified 2018 20-mark PYQ asks how far liberty and equality are distinctive democratic features; primary owner Philosophy Paper II, Social and Political Ideals (✅ owner 22 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Liberty: civil liberties, opposition and free discussion (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
- Equality: political equality through one person one vote (✅ owner 22 §3).
- Tension: formal equality versus substantive equality (⚠️ owner 22 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018, 20 marks (✅ owner 22 §9).
**Links:** PT-5005, PT-5023, PT-5008 (Aristotle formal equality, PT-5009).
**Trap:** Treating equality as only numerical; Aristotle's warning about legal equality (✅ owner 22 §6).
**Mains hook:** Liberty and equality are distinctive only when institutionalised together; a graded verdict names the residual tension (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Liberty protects voice; equality counts it."*

### Flashcard PT-5026 — PYQ route: sovereignty questions (Austin and Kautilya, 2021)
**Front:** What do the 2021 Austin (10 marks) and Kautilya (15 marks) sovereignty PYQs test?
**Definition (learn):** 2021 10 marks: "Is Austin's theory of sovereignty compatible with democracy?" 2021 15 marks: "Discuss Kautilya's contribution regarding the concept of sovereignty. Is it applicable in a democratic form of government?" (✅ owner 22 §9; primary owner Philosophy Paper II, Sovereignty).
**Breakdown:**
- Austin: sovereign as determinate commander; test compatibility with popular sovereignty (⚠️ owner 22 cross-link to file 11).
- Kautilya: sovereignty within an ancient state; test applicability to democracy (✅ owner 22 §9).
- Link to ultimate authority (✅ owner 22 §10, cross-ref to `basic/11_Sovereignty-and-Pluralism.md`).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021, 10 and 15 marks.
**Links:** PT-5015 (Rousseau sovereignty), PT-5001, PT-5016.
**Trap:** Do not answer as if Kautilya were a democrat; test applicability rather than assume (⚠️ synthesis).
**Mains hook:** Democracy relocates ultimate authority to the people and its institutions; compatibility depends on whether the sovereign is determinate or constrained (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Austin commands; democracy must answer to the people."*

### Flashcard PT-5027 — PYQ route: propaganda (2022) and challenges to democratic states (2023)
**Front:** How do the 2022 (20 marks) propaganda PYQ and 2023 (15 marks) challenges PYQ test democracy?
**Definition (learn):** 2022: "Discuss propaganda as a challenge to democratic form of government." 2023: "Explain the challenges faced by a democratic state and the ways to overcome them." (✅ owner 22 §9; primary owner Philosophy Paper II, Forms of Government.)
**Breakdown:**
- Propaganda: information systematically manipulated; formal choice insufficient (⚠️ owner 22 adv §7).
- Challenges: necessary-vs-sufficient gap, elite control, inequality (✅ owner 22 adv §7).
- Remedies: liberties, plural media, independent courts (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022, 20 marks; 2023, 15 marks.
**Links:** PT-5007, PT-5023, PT-5063, PT-5100.
**Trap:** Treating propaganda as only a fringe problem; it targets the will-formation condition (⚠️ owner 22 adv §7).
**Mains hook:** Formal choice without informed opinion is hollow consent; propaganda is a test of the consent condition (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Propaganda hollows the choice before the ballot."*

### Flashcard PT-5028 — Trustee model of representation (Burke, 1774)
**Front:** What is the trustee model and who is its classic source?
**Definition (learn):** A representative should exercise independent, deliberative judgment on constituents' true interest, even against immediate instructions (⚠️ owner 22 §12; ✅ Wikipedia: Edmund Burke).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: Burke's *Speech to the Electors of Bristol* (1774) (✅ Wikipedia: Edmund Burke).
- Core claim: "Your Representative owes you, not his industry only, but his judgment" (✅ Wikipedia: Edmund Burke).
- Owner: trustee reading associated with conservative theory, Gauba PDF pp.502–503 (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1774 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** PT-5029, PT-5030, PT-5042, PT-5012 (Locke's trustee).
**Trap:** Burke's trustee is about judgment in representation, not Locke's trusteeship of power (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Trustee leadership filters passion but can slide into elite paternalism; answer with accountability at election time (⚠️ owner 22 adv §6).
**Back:** *"Burke: judgment, not just instructions."*

### Flashcard PT-5029 — Delegate model of representation
**Front:** What is the delegate model and why is it the opposite pole of Burke's trustee?
**Definition (learn):** A representative acts strictly on constituents' instructions or expressed wishes, with little independent judgment (⚠️ owner 22 §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Liberal/delegate theory: representative as the people's agent carrying constituent judgment (✅ owner 22 adv §4 and §6, PDF p.503).
- Associated with Locke and Jefferson in Gauba's typology (✅ owner 22 adv §5).
- Strength: strongest link to popular control (✅ owner 22 adv §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p.503.
**Links:** PT-5028, PT-5030, PT-5015.
**Trap:** Delegate does not mean mere messenger in complex policy domains; the owner flags oversimplification risk (⚠️ owner 22 adv §6).
**Mains hook:** Delegate models maximise responsiveness but limit deliberation; a 10-mark answer should state the trade-off (⚠️ owner 22 §16).
**Back:** *"Delegate carries the message; trustee weighs it."*

### Flashcard PT-5030 — Mandate model of representation
**Front:** What is the mandate model and where is it most associated?
**Definition (learn):** A party or representative seeks authorisation to implement a stated programme placed before voters, and is expected to act on it once elected (⚠️ owner 22 §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Authority comes from an electoral programme (⚠️ owner 22 §12).
- Most associated with party-manifesto politics in parliamentary systems (⚠️ owner 22 §12).
- Link to Schumpeter's competitive vote-seeking (✅ owner 23 §6; PT-5046).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Standard democratic-theory vocabulary; not verified against Gauba pages (⚠️ owner 22 §12 provenance note).
**Links:** PT-5028, PT-5029, PT-5046.
**Trap:** A manifesto mandate is not blanket authorisation for every decision (⚠️ synthesis).
**Mains hook:** Mandate links representation to accountability through the programme; test whether promises were kept (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Mandate: promise at the poll, delivery in office."*

### Flashcard PT-5031 — Descriptive vs substantive representation (Pitkin, 1967)
**Front:** Distinguish descriptive from substantive representation.
**Definition (learn):** Descriptive: a body is representative if it mirrors the social characteristics of the population. Substantive: a representative acts in the interests of the represented, whether or not they share those characteristics (⚠️ owner 22 §12; ✅ Wikipedia: Hanna Fenichel Pitkin confirms the 1967 book covers both).
**Breakdown:**
- Descriptive asks about composition: gender, caste, region, religion, class (⚠️ owner 22 §12).
- Substantive asks about actions and outcomes (⚠️ owner 22 §12).
- Source: Hanna Pitkin, *The Concept of Representation* (1967) (✅ Wikipedia: Hanna Fenichel Pitkin).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1967.
**Links:** PT-5032, PT-5033, PT-5038 (consociational proportionality).
**Trap:** Descriptive representation alone does not guarantee substantive outcomes; the two are conceptually separate (⚠️ owner 22 §12).
**Mains hook:** Use Pitkin's split to assess reservations and proportionality: composition may not deliver interests (⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Descriptive = who sits; substantive = what they do."*

### Flashcard PT-5032 — Territorial vs functional representation
**Front:** Define territorial and functional representation and give the owner's Indian illustration limits.
**Definition (learn):** Territorial: representatives elected from geographic constituencies (ordinary basis, including Lok Sabha). Functional: representatives speak for occupational or sectoral interests (labour, industry, professions) (⚠️ owner 22 §13; ✅ owner 22 adv §4, PDF pp.501–502).
**Breakdown:**
- Territorial: near-equal population constituencies; simple and locally intelligible (✅ owner 22 adv §8A).
- Functional: specialised interests voiced directly (✅ owner 22 adv §8A).
- Associated debate: G. D. H. Cole's guild-socialist writings (⚠️ owner 22 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.501–502.
**Links:** PT-5031, PT-5033, PT-5042.
**Trap:** Functional representation was used largely in corporatist or totalitarian settings, creating democratic suspicion (✅ owner 22 adv §7 and §12, PDF pp.501–502).
**Mains hook:** Functional seats address expertise and interests but risk capture; territorial seats risk neglecting national interests (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Territory votes you; function speaks for your trade."*

### Flashcard PT-5033 — First-Past-The-Post (plurality)
**Front:** Define FPTP and state its trade-off.
**Definition (learn):** The candidate with the largest vote total in a single-member constituency wins without securing 50% (⚠️ owner 22 §14; ✅ owner 22 adv §4, PDF pp.504–505).
**Breakdown:**
- Clear accountability and often stable government (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
- Can convert modest vote share into large seat share and waste votes (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
- Under-represents minorities and distorts vote-seat ratios (✅ owner 22 adv §7, PDF pp.505–507).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.504–507.
**Links:** PT-5034, PT-5035, PT-5039.
**Trap:** FPTP is a plurality rule, not a majoritarian rule; a winner may have far below 50% (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
**Mains hook:** FPTP trades inclusiveness for decisiveness; the 38% four-cornered example shows the legitimacy problem (⚠️ owner 22 adv §9).
**Back:** *"Most votes wins, even under half."*

### Flashcard PT-5034 — Majoritarian systems (absolute majority)
**Front:** How does a majoritarian system differ from plurality?
**Definition (learn):** The winner must secure an absolute majority directly or through runoff or preference transfer (⚠️ owner 22 §14; ✅ owner 22 adv §4, PDF pp.505–506).
**Breakdown:**
- Broader numerical support for the winner (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
- Constituency distortions and minority exclusion may persist (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
- Alternative vote and second ballot are the usual devices (✅ owner 22 adv §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.505–506.
**Links:** PT-5033, PT-5036, PT-5035.
**Trap:** Majoritarian ≠ proportional; a majoritarian system can still exclude minorities (✅ owner 22 adv §8B).
**Mains hook:** Majoritarian legitimacy is numerical; minority protection requires additional devices (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Over half or runoff."*

### Flashcard PT-5035 — List Proportional Representation
**Front:** Define List PR and its trade-off.
**Definition (learn):** Parties present ranked lists; seats allocated roughly in proportion to vote share (⚠️ owner 22 §14; ✅ owner 22 adv §4, PDF pp.506–507).
**Breakdown:**
- Seat shares track vote shares; small and minority parties gain representation (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
- Coalitions often needed; decisiveness and stability may suffer (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
- Can produce fragmented legislatures or sectional parties (✅ owner 22 adv §7 and §8B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp.506–507.
**Links:** PT-5033, PT-5036, PT-5039, PT-5038.
**Trap:** PR can weaken direct constituency linkage; do not state that PR always stabilises (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** PR improves inclusion but may weaken government formation; a qualified preference depends on social cleavages (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Lists share seats like votes; coalitions follow."*

### Flashcard PT-5036 — Single Transferable Vote (STV)
**Front:** How does STV work and what does it trade off?
**Definition (learn):** Voters rank candidates in multi-member constituencies; surplus and eliminated votes transfer by preference until seats fill (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
**Breakdown:**
- Retains individual candidate choice while achieving rough proportionality (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
- Counting is complex; constituency linkage weaker than FPTP (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
- Illustrations (⚠️ owner 22 §17): Republic of Ireland Dáil; Australian Senate.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Standard comparative illustrations, unverified against Gauba (⚠️ owner 22 §17).
**Links:** PT-5035, PT-5034, PT-5033.
**Trap:** Do not confuse STV with List PR; the voter ranks individuals, not party lists (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
**Mains hook:** STV blends candidate choice with proportionality; complexity is its practical cost (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Rank them, transfer them, fill the seats."*

### Flashcard PT-5037 — Concurrent majority (Calhoun, 1851)
**Front:** What is the concurrent majority and what is its historical limitation?
**Definition (learn):** Government requires concurrence of each major interest or section, giving a mutual veto, rather than a head-count majority (⚠️ owner 22 §14; ✅ Wikipedia: Concurrent majority).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: John C. Calhoun, *A Disquisition on Government*, published posthumously in 1851 (✅ Wikipedia: Concurrent majority).
- Purpose: prevent tyranny of the majority (✅ Wikipedia: Concurrent majority).
- Limitation: deployed to shield the slaveholding South; linked to 1832 nullification (✅ Wikipedia: Concurrent majority).
- Analytical risk: deadlock when every interest holds a veto (⚠️ owner 22 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1851 (posthumous publication); Nullification Crisis 1832.
**Links:** PT-5038 (Lijphart), PT-5040 (objection chain), PT-5018.
